## The Valley Was Still / 山谷寂然

===Original===
Wind touched the pines on the ridge, and stirred the thicket forest on the hills opposite; but the grassy valley between, with its red and white houses at the bottom, was as still as a painted backdrop in a theater. Not even a grasshopper sang in it.

Two cavalrymen sat their mounts at the edge of the pines. The one in the torn butternut blouse hawked and spat, and the sound was strangely loud at the brink of that silence.

“I’d reckoned the Yanks was down in that there little town,” he said. “Channow, it’s called. Joe, you look like a Yank yourself in them clothes.”

===Chinese===
风拂过山脊上的松林，摇动着对面群丘间的密林；可夹在当中的那片青草山谷，连同谷底红白相间的房舍，却静得像剧院里一幅画成的布景。谷里连一只蚱蜢都不曾鸣叫。

两名骑兵勒马停在松林边上。穿一件破烂灰褐色上衣的那个咳了咳嗓子，啐了一口，那声音在这片寂静的边缘响起，竟出奇地响亮。

“俺还当北佬就窝在下边那座小镇里呢，”他说，“那地方叫查诺（Channow）。乔，你穿这身行头，自己看着就像个北佬。”

===Original===
His mate, who wore half-weathered blue, did not appear complimented. The garments had been stripped from an outraged sergeant of Pennsylvania Lancers, taken prisoner at the Seven Days. They fitted their new wearer’s lean body nicely, except across the shoulders. His boots were likewise trophies of war—from the Second Manassas, where the Union Army had learned that lightning can strike twice in the same place; and his saddlecloth, with its U.S. stamp, had also been unwillingly furnished by the Federal army. But the gray horse had come from his father’s Virginia farm, and had lived through a year of fierce fighting and fiercer toil. The rider’s name was Joseph Paradine, and he had recently declined, with thanks, the offer of General J. E. B. Stuart to recommend him for a commission.

He preferred to serve as a common trooper. He was a chivalric idealist, and a peerless scout.

“You’d better steal some Yankee blues yourself, Dauger,” he advised. “Those homespun pants would drop off of you if you stood up in your stirrups. … Yes, the enemy’s expected to take up a position in Channow Valley. But if he had done so, we’d have run into his videttes by now, and that town would be as noisy as a county fair.”

===Chinese===
他的同伴穿一身半旧的蓝军装，听着并不觉得这是恭维。这身衣裳是从宾夕法尼亚枪骑兵团一名怒不可遏的中士身上扒下来的，那人在七日战役中当了俘虏。衣裳穿在新主人精瘦的身板上相当合体，只是肩头紧了些。他的靴子同样是战争的战利品——得自第二次马纳萨斯，在那一仗里，联邦军领教了闪电可以在同一个地方劈两回；他那条印着“U.S.”字样的鞍鞯，也是北军不大情愿地奉送的。唯独那匹灰马来自他父亲的弗吉尼亚农庄，熬过了一年的恶战和更苦的劳役。骑手名叫约瑟夫·帕拉丁（Joseph Paradine），前不久刚带着谢意婉拒了J·E·B·斯图尔特将军举荐他受衔任官的好意。

他宁愿当一名普通骑兵。他是个骑士般的理想主义者，也是个无与伦比的斥候。

“道格（Dauger），你自己也最好去偷一套北佬的蓝军装穿，”他劝道，“你那条家织布裤子，你要是踩着马镫站起来，它就得往下掉。……不错，敌人料想会在查诺谷占据阵地。可他们真要布下了，这会儿咱们早撞上他们的哨骑了，那镇子也该吵得像县里赶集一样了。”

===Original===
He rode from among the pines and into the open on the lower slope.

“You’re plumb exposin’ yourself, Joe,” warned Dauger anxiously.

“And I’m going to expose myself more,” returned Paradine, his eyes on the valley. “We’ve been told to find the Yankees, establish their whereabouts. Then our people will tackle them.” He spoke with the confidence of triumph that in the summer of 1862 possessed Confederates who had driven the Union’s bravest and best all through Virginia. “I’m going all the way down.”

“There’ll be Yanks hidin’,” suggested Dauger pessimistically. “They’ll plug you plumb full of lead.”

“If they do,” called Paradine, “ride back and tell the boys, because then you’ll know the Yankees actually are in Channow.” He put his horse to the slope, feeling actually happy at the thought that he might suffer for the sake of his cause. It is worthy of repetition that he was a chivalric idealist.

Dauger, quite as brave but more practical, bode where he was. Paradine, riding downhill, passed out of reach of any more warnings.

---

===Chinese===
他纵马穿出松林，驶向下坡的开阔地。

“乔，你这是把自己整个亮给人家看呢，”道格提心吊胆地警告。

“我还打算亮得更彻底些，”帕拉丁应道，眼睛盯着山谷。“上头吩咐咱们找到北军，摸清他们的位置，然后咱们的人再去收拾他们。”他说话时带着全胜在握的自信——1862 年那个夏天，把联邦军最勇最好的部队撵着打过整个弗吉尼亚的邦联军人，个个都有这股自信。“我要一直下到谷底去。”

“保准有北佬猫着呢，”道格悲观地咕哝，“他们会把你打成个铅疙瘩。”

“他们要是真打，”帕拉丁回头喊道，“你就骑回去报告弟兄们，因为那会儿你就知道，北佬确实是在查诺了。”他策马下坡，一想到自己或许能为事业受苦，心里竟真觉得快活。这里不妨再说一遍：他是个骑士般的理想主义者。

道格的胆气一点也不小，只是更讲实际，便原地守着不动。帕拉丁顺坡而下，走出了任何劝告所能及的范围。

---

===Original===
Paradine’s eyes were kept on the village as he descended deep into silence as into water. He had never known such silence, not even at the frequent prayings of his very devout regiment. It made him nervous, a different nervousness from the tingling elation brought by battle thunders, and it fairly daunted his seasoned and intelligent horse. The beast tossed its head, sniffed, danced precariously, and had to be urged to the slope’s foot and the trail that ran there.

From the bottom of the slope, the village was a scant two miles away. Its chimneys did not smoke, nor did its trees stir in the windless air. Nor was there sign or motion upon its streets and among its houses of red brick and white wood—no enemy soldiers, or anything else.

Was this a trap? But Paradine smiled at the thought of a whole Yankee brigade or more, lying low to capture one lone Southerner.

More likely they thought him a friend, wearing blue as he did; but why silence in that case, either?

===Chinese===
帕拉丁一路下行，目光始终锁定那座村庄，自己则像沉入水中一般，一寸寸沉入寂静的深处。他从不曾领教这样的寂静，就连他那支极其虔诚的团频频举行的祷告会上也没有过。这寂静叫他心慌——一种不同于战场惊雷带来的酥麻亢奋的心慌——连他那匹久经战阵、通晓人意的马也分明怯了。牲口甩着头，打着响鼻，脚下踉跄地蹦跳，非得连催带逼，才肯挪到坡底，踏上坡底那条小径。

从坡底望去，村子离着不到两英里。烟囱不冒烟，无风的空气里，树梢也一动不动。街上和那些红砖白木的房舍之间，也不见半点迹象、半分动静——没有敌兵，也没有别的任何东西。

这是个圈套？可一想到整整一个北佬旅、还不止，埋伏着就为活捉他一个孤零零的南方人，帕拉丁不禁笑了。

更可能的是，他们把他当成了自己人，谁让他穿着蓝军装呢；可若是那样，又为什么要这么静？

===Original===
He determined to make noise. If there were hostile forces in and among the houses of Channow, he would draw their attention, perhaps their musket fire. Spurring the gray so that it whickered and plunged, he forced it to canter at an angle toward the nearest houses. At the same time he drew his saber, whetted to a razor-edge contrary to regulations, and waved it over his head. He gave the rebel yell, high and fierce.

“*Yee-hee!*”

Paradine’s voice was a strong one, and it could ring from end to end of a brigade in line; but, even as he yelled, that yell perished—dropped from his lips, as though cut away.

He could not have been heard ten yards. Had his throat dried up? Then, suddenly, he knew. There was no echo here, for all the ridge lay behind, and the hills in front to the north. Even the galloping hoofs of the gray sounded muffled, as if in cotton. Strange … there was no response to his defiance.

===Chinese===
他打定主意要弄出点声响。要是查诺的房舍里里外外藏有敌军，他就要把他们的注意力引过来，没准连排枪也一并引来。他猛夹灰马，马儿嘶鸣着往前蹿跳，他逼着它斜刺里朝最近的几栋房子小跑过去。与此同时，他抽出军刀——那刀磨得剃刀般锋利，全然违反军规——在头顶挥舞，发出一声高亢凶悍的反叛吶喊。

“*咿——嗨！*”

帕拉丁的嗓门是洪亮的，全旅列队时一嗓子能从队首响到队尾；可他这一嗓子才喊出口，便当场夭折——像被什么东西齐根剪断，从唇边跌落下去。

十码开外就没人听得见他了。是嗓子干了吗？突然间，他明白了。这里没有回声——山脊整个落在身后，北面的群丘又横在眼前。连灰马奔驰的蹄声听着也发闷，像裹在棉花里。怪了……他这一番叫阵，竟没换来半点回应。

===Original===
That was more surprising still. If there were no enemy troops, what about the people of the town? Paradine felt his brown neck-hair, which needed cutting badly, rise and stiffen. Something sinister lay yonder, and warned him away. But he had ridden into this valley to gather intelligence for his officers. He could not turn back, and respect himself thereafter, as a gentleman and a soldier. Has it been noted that Paradine was a chivalric idealist?

But his horse, whatever its blood and character, lacked such selfless devotion to the cause of State’s Rights. It faltered in its gallop, tried first to turn back, and then to throw Paradine. He cursed it feelingly, fought it with bit, knee and spur, and finally pulled up and dismounted. He drew the reins forward over the tossing gray head, thrust his left arm through the loop, and with his left hand drew the big cap-and-ball revolver from his holster. Thus ready, with shot or saber, he proceeded on foot, and the gray followed him protestingly.

“Come on,” he scolded, very loudly—he was sick of the silence. “I don’t know what I’m getting into here. If I have to retreat, it won’t be on foot.”

===Chinese===
这才更叫人吃惊。镇上要是没有敌军，那镇上的老百姓呢？帕拉丁觉得后颈上那圈早该修剪的棕色头发根根竖起，硬硬地扎着。那边横着某种凶邪的东西，正警告他走开。可他骑进这条山谷，是来为长官们搜集情报的。他不能掉头回去，往后再把自己当绅士、当军人看。前头说过没有？——帕拉丁是个骑士般的理想主义者。

可他那匹马，无论血统多高贵、性子多刚烈，对州权大业都没有这般无私的奉献精神。它的奔跑乱了章法，先是想掉头往回跑，接着又想把帕拉丁甩下鞍去。他掏心挖肺地咒骂它，用嚼口、膝盖和马刺同它搏斗，最后总算勒住它，翻身下马。他把缰绳从甩动不休的灰脑袋上绕过去，左臂套进缰圈，左手从枪套里拔出那杆大火帽转轮手枪。就这样，刀枪两便，他徒步前进，灰马一路不情愿地跟着。

“走吧，”他大声呵斥——这份寂静他受够了。“我不知道自己这是在往什么里头闯。要是得撤退，总不能靠两条腿。”

===Original===
Half a mile more, at a brisk walk. A quarter-mile beyond that, more slowly; for still there was no sound or movement from the village. Then the trail joined a wagon track, and Paradine came to the foot of the single street of Channow.

He looked along it, and came to an abrupt halt.

The street, with its shaded yards on either side, was littered with slack blue lumps, each the size of a human body.

The Yankee army, or its advance guard, was there—but fallen and stony still.

“Dead!” muttered Paradine, under his breath.

But who could have killed them? Not his comrades, who had not known where the enemy was. Plague, then? But the most withering plague takes hours, at least, and these had plainly fallen all in the same instant.

---

===Chinese===
又走了半英里，脚步轻快。再往前四分之一英里，放慢了；因为村子里依旧没有一丝声响、一点动静。随后小径并上一道车辙路，帕拉丁来到查诺镇那条独街的街口。

他顺着街望过去，猛地站住了。

街道两侧绿荫遮蔽的院落之间，横七竖八地散落着一摊摊瘫软的蓝色东西，每一摊都有一个人身子大小。

北军，或者他们的先头部队，就在这儿——只是倒下了，僵石般一动不动。

“死了！”帕拉丁低声咕哝。

可谁能杀了他们？不会是自己人——弟兄们根本不知道敌军的下落。那是瘟疫？可再狠的瘟疫，少说也得折磨几个钟头，而这些人分明是在同一个瞬间齐齐倒下的。

---

===Original===
Paradine studied the scene. Here had been a proper entry of a strange settlement—first a patrol, watchful and suspicious; then a larger advance party, in two single files, each file hugging one side of the street with eyes and weapons commanding the other side; and, finally, the main body—men, horses and guns, with a baggage train—all as it should be; but now prone and still, like tin soldiers strewn on a floor after a game.

The house at the foot of the street had a hitching-post, cast from iron to represent a Negro boy with a ring in one lifted hand. To that ring Paradine tethered the now almost unmanageable gray. He heard a throbbing roll, as of drums, which he identified as the blood beating in his ears. The saber-hilt was slippery with the sweat of his palm.

He knew that he was afraid, and did not relish the knowledge. Stubbornly he turned his boot-toes forward, and approached the fallen ranks of the enemy. The drums in his ears beat a cadence for his lone march.

He reached and stood over the nearest of the bodies. A blue-bloused infantryman this, melted over on his face, his hands slack upon the musket lying crosswise beneath him. The peaked forage cap had fallen from rumpled, bright hair. The cheek, what Paradine could see of it, was as downy as a peach. Only a kid, young to die; but was he dead?

===Chinese===
帕拉丁细细打量这幅景象。这儿本该是一支军队开进陌生村落的规矩阵势——先是巡逻队，警觉而多疑；随后是人数更多的先遣队，分成两路单列，各贴街道一侧行进，目光和枪口封锁着对侧；最后是主力——人员、马匹和火炮，外加辎重车队——一切有条不紊，合乎章法；只是此刻全数伏地不动，像一场战局收场后撒了一地的锡兵。

街口那栋房子前立着一根系马桩，铁铸的，是个黑人男孩的模样，举起的一只手里攥着个铁环。帕拉丁把此刻几乎控制不住的灰马拴到那铁环上。他听见一阵擂动般的轰响，好似鼓声，随即辨认出那是自己耳中奔流的血。军刀柄被掌心的汗浸得发滑。

他知道自己怕了，也不喜欢知道这一点。他执拗地把靴尖转向前方，朝敌人倒伏的队列走去。耳中的鼓声为他这孤身一人的行进敲着步点。

他走到最近的一具尸身旁，居高俯视。这是个穿蓝上衣的步兵，脸朝下瘫伏着，双手松松搭在横压在身下的火枪上。尖顶的军便帽从那头蓬乱的亮发上滚落下来。看得见的那半边脸颊，绒毛细软得像只桃子。不过是个娃娃，死还嫌小的年纪——可他真死了吗？

===Original===
There was no sign of a wound. Too, a certain waxy finality was lacking in that slumped posture. Paradine extended the point of his saber and gingerly prodded a sun-reddened wrist.

No response. Paradine increased the pressure. A red drop appeared under the point, and grew. Paradine scowled. The boy could bleed. He must be alive, after all.

“Wake up, Yankee,” said Joseph Paradine, and stirred the blue flank with his foot. The flesh yielded, but did not stir otherwise. He turned the body over. A vacant pink face stared up out of eyes that were fixed, but bright. Not death—and not sleep.

Paradine had seen men in a swoon who looked like that. Yet even swooners breathed, and there was not a hair’s line of motion under the dimmed brass buttons.

===Chinese===
身上看不出伤痕。再者，那瘫软的姿势里也欠着一点蜡似的终局之感。帕拉丁伸出刀尖，小心翼翼地捅了捅一只晒得发红的手腕。

没有反应。帕拉丁加了点力。刀尖下渗出一滴红珠，越鼓越大。帕拉丁皱起眉。这孩子能流血。这么说，他到底还活着。

“醒醒，北佬。”约瑟夫·帕拉丁开口说话，用脚拨了拨那蓝色的身侧。皮肉随脚陷了陷，却没有别的动静。他把尸体翻了过来。一张空茫的粉红脸孔仰面朝天，一双眼睛直勾勾的，却亮着。不是死——也不是睡。

帕拉丁见过昏死过去的人，就是这副模样。可再怎么昏死的人也要喘气，而那几粒发乌的铜纽扣底下，连一根头发丝宽的起伏都没有。

===Original===
“Funny,” thought Paradine, not meaning that he was amused. He walked on, because there was nothing left to do. Just beyond that first fallen lad lay the rest of the patrol, still in the diamond-shaped formation they mast have held when awake and erect. One man lay at the right side of the street, another opposite him at the left. The corporal was in the center and, to his rear, another private.

The corporal was, or had been, an excitable man. His hands clutched his musket firmly, his lips drew back from gritted teeth, his eyes were narrow instead of staring. A bit of awareness seemed to remain upon the set, stubbly face. Paradine forbore to prod him with the saber, but stooped and twitched up an eyelid. It snapped back into its squint. The corporal, too, lived but did not move.

“Wake up,” Paradine urged him, as he had urged the boy. “You aren’t dead.” He straightened up, and stared at the more distant and numerous blue bodies in their fallen ranks. “None of you are dead!” he protested at the top of his lungs, unable to beat down his hysteria. “Wake up, Yankees!”

He was pleading with them to rise, even though he would be doomed if they did.

===Chinese===
“怪事。”帕拉丁心想，却丝毫不觉得有趣。他继续往前走，因为再没有别的可做。头一个倒下的小伙子再往前一点，躺着巡逻队其余的人，仍保持着他们清醒站立时想必摆下的菱形队形。一人躺在街右侧，另一人正对着他躺在左侧。下士居中，他身后还有一名列兵。

那下士生前——或者说从前——是个急性子。他双手死死攥着火枪，嘴唇从咬紧的牙关往后咧，眼睛不是圆瞪，而是眯缝着。那张僵住的胡茬脸上，似乎还残存着一丝知觉。帕拉丁不忍心用刀尖去捅他，只俯下身，挑起他一边眼皮。眼皮啪地弹回眯缝里。这下士也一样：活着，却动弹不得。

“醒醒。”帕拉丁催他，像方才催那孩子一样。“你没有死。”他直起身，望着更远处成片倒伏的蓝色身影。“你们谁都没有死！”他扯开嗓子喊了起来，压不住心头的癔症。“醒醒，北佬们！”

他在哀求他们起来，尽管他们真起来了，他就完了。

===Original===
“*Yee-hee!*” he yelled. “You’re all my prisoners! Up on your feet!”

“Yo’re wastin’ yore breath, son.”

Paradine whirled like a top to face this sudden quiet rebuke.

A man stood in the front yard of a shabby house opposite, leaning on a picket fence. Paradine’s first impression was of noble and vigorous old age, for a mighty cascade of white beard covered the speaker’s chest, and his brow was fringed with thick cottony hair. But next moment Paradine saw that the brow was strangely narrow and sunken, that the mouth in the midst of its hoary ambush hung wryly slack, and that the eyes were bright but empty, like cheap imitation jewels.

===Chinese===
“*咿——嗨！*”他高喊，“你们全都是我的俘虏！都给我站起来！”

“儿子，你这是白费嗓子。”

帕拉丁陀螺似的猛转身，去面对这句突然冒出来的平静训斥。

对面一栋破房子的小院里站着个人，倚在尖桩栅栏上。帕拉丁的第一印象是：好一位高贵矍铄的老者——说话人胸前垂挂着一大道白胡子瀑布，额前覆着一圈厚实的棉白头发。可下一刻帕拉丁便看清：那额头窄得出奇、向里塌陷，掩在白须埋伏中央的那张嘴歪斜地松垂着，那双眼睛倒是发亮，却空洞无物，像廉价的假宝石。

===Original===
The stranger moved slowly along the fence until he came to a gate. He pushed it creakily open, and moved across the dusty road toward Paradine. His body and legs were meager, even for an old man, and he shook and shuffled as though extremely feeble. His clothing was a hodgepodge of filthy tatters.

At any rate, he was no soldier foe. Paradine holstered his revolver, and leaned on his saber. The bearded one came close, making slow circuit of two fallen soldiers that lay in his path. Close at hand, he appeared as tall and gaunt as a flagstaff, and his beard was a fluttering white flag, but not for truce.

“I spoke to ’em,” he said, quietly but definitely, “an’ they dozed off like they was drunk.”

“You mean these troops?”

“Who else, son? They come marchin’ from them hills to the north. The folks scattered outa here like rabbits—all but me. I waited. An’—I put these here Yanks to sleep.”

---

===Chinese===
那陌生人顺着栅栏慢慢挪到院门跟前，吱呀一声推开小门，穿过尘土飞扬的大路，朝帕拉丁走来。他身子精瘦，两腿细弱，即便在老头子堆里也算瘦的，走起来哆哆嗦嗦、蹭蹭挨挨，仿佛衰弱到了极点。身上那行头是各色脏破布片的大杂烩。

无论如何，他不是军中之敌。帕拉丁把转轮枪插回枪套，拄住军刀。大胡子老头走近来，绕开挡道的两个倒地士兵，缓缓兜了半圈。到了近处，他显得又高又瘦像根旗杆，那部胡子则是一面飘拂的白旗——却不是为了求和。

“俺跟他们念叨了几句，”他说，声音平静却笃定，“他们就跟灌醉了似的，一头栽过去睡着了。”

“你是说这些部队？”

“不是他们还能是谁，儿子？他们从北边那些山里开进来。乡亲们四散奔逃，跟炸了窝的兔子似的——就剩俺没跑。俺等着。然后——俺让这帮北佬睡了过去。”

---

===Original===
Hhe reached under his veil of beard, apparently fumbling in the bosom of his ruined shirt. His brown old fork of a hand produced a dingy book, bound in gray paper.

“This does it,” he said.

Paradine looked at the front cover. It bore the woodcut of an owl against a round moon.

The title was in black capitals:

> John George Hohman’s

Powwows

or

Long Lost Friend

“Got it a long time back, from a Pennsylvany witch-man.”

===Chinese===
他把手伸进胡须的帷幕底下，像是在那件破衬衫的怀里摸索。一只枯叉似的棕色老手摸出一本灰纸皮的书，脏乎乎的。

“就是它管用的。”他说。

帕拉丁看了看封面。上面是一幅木刻：一轮圆月衬托着一只猫头鹰。

书名是黑色大写字母：

> 约翰·乔治·霍曼

咒语法典

或

失传的友人

“老早以前弄到手的，从一个宾夕法尼亚来的巫术师（witch-man）手里。”

===Original===
Paradine did not understand, and was not sure that he wanted to. He still wondered how so many fighting-men could lie stunned.

“I thought ye was a Yank, an’ I’d missed ye somehow,” the quiet old voice informed him. “That’s a Yank sojer suit, hain’t it? I was goin’ to read ye some sleep words, but ye give the yell, an’ I knew ye was secesh.”

Paradine made a gesture, as though to brush away a troublesome fly. He must investigate further. Up the street he walked, among the prone soldiers.

===Chinese===
帕拉丁听不明白，也拿不准自己想不想明白。他仍在纳闷，这么多能征惯战的汉子，怎么会这样躺着昏死过去。

“俺还当你是北佬，不知怎么偏偏把你漏下了，”那把平静的老嗓音向他解释，“那是套北佬军装，是不是？俺本来正要给你念几句催眠的话，可你先喊了那一嗓子，俺就知道你是南军了。”

帕拉丁摆了摆手，像赶一只缠人的苍蝇。他还得再查探一番。他顺着街往上走，走在倒伏的士兵中间。

===Original===
It took him half an hour to complete his survey, walking from end to end of that unconscious host. He saw infantry, men and officers sprawling together in slack comradeship; three batteries of Parrott guns, still coupled to their limbers, with horses slumped in their harness and riders and drivers fallen in the dust beneath the wheels; a body of cavalry—it should have been scouting out front, thought Paradine professionally—all down and still, like a whole parkful of equestrian statues overturned; wagons; and finally, last of the procession save for a prudently placed rearguard, a little clutter of men in gold braid. He approached the oldest and stoutest of these, noting the two stars on the shoulder straps—a major general.

Paradine knelt, unbuttoned the frock coat, and felt in the pockets. Here were papers. The first he unfolded was the copy of an order:

> General T. F. Kottler,

Commanding ——— Division, U.S.A.
> General:
> You will move immediately, with your entire force, taking up a strong defensive position in the Channow Valley. …

===Chinese===
他花了半个钟头，从这失去知觉的大军的这头走到那头，查了个遍。他看见步兵——官兵们不分你我，瘫作一堆；三连帕罗特炮，炮身还挂在前车上，马匹颓倒在挽具里，骑手和驭手栽在车轮下的尘土中；一支骑兵——照行家的眼光，这队人马本该撒到前头去侦察，帕拉丁职业性地想道——也全数倒地不动，像满园骑马雕像齐齐翻倒；还有大车队；最后，在这条长龙的末尾、只隔着一小段谨慎布防的后卫，是一小簇缀着金绦的人。他朝其中最年长、最壮实的那位走过去，一眼看到肩章上的两颗星——一位少将。

帕拉丁单膝跪下，解开大衣，伸手到口袋里摸索。里面是文件。他展开的第一份，是一道命令的抄件：

> T·F·科特勒（Kottler）将军，

美国陆军某师师长
> 将军：
> 着即率所部全部兵力即刻开拔，于查诺谷占据一处坚固的防御阵地。……

===Original===
This, then, was Kottler’s Division. Paradine estimated the force as five thousand bluecoats, all veterans by the look of them, but nothing that his own comrades would have feared. He studied the wagon-train hungrily. It was packed with food and clothing, badly needed by the Confederacy. He would do well to get back and report his find. He turned, and saw that the old man with the white beard had followed him along the street. “I reckon,” he said to Paradine, in tones of mild reproach, “ye think I’m a-lyin’ about puttin’ these here Yanks to sleep.”

Paradine smiled at him, as he might have smiled at an importunate child. “I didn’t call you a liar,” he temporized, “and the Yankees are certainly in dreamland. But I think there must be some natural explanation for—”

“Happen I kin show ye better’n tell ye,” cut in the dotard. His paper-bound book was open in his scrawny hands. Stooping close to it, he began rapidly to mumble something. His voice suddenly rose, sounded almost young:

“Now, stand there till I tell ye to move!”

===Chinese===
那么，这就是科特勒师了。帕拉丁估计这股兵力约有五千蓝衣官兵，瞧那模样全是老兵，可在自家弟兄眼里也算不得可怕的对手。他眼馋地研究起那支辎重车队来。车上装满了粮食和衣被，都是邦联急需的东西。他最好赶紧回去报告这趟发现。他转过身，看见白胡子老头已一路跟到了街边。“俺估摸，”老头对帕拉丁说，语调里带着温和的责备，“你是觉着俺撒谎，说没把这些北佬弄睡。”

帕拉丁朝他笑了笑，像对一个缠人的孩子那样。“我没有说你是骗子，”他敷衍着，“北佬们确实是在梦乡里。不过我想，这背后总该有个合乎自然的解释，解释——”

“说不准俺做给你看，比你听俺干说更灵。”老家伙插嘴道。他那本纸皮书已经摊在皮包骨的手里。他弓身凑近书页，飞快地咕哝起什么来。嗓音陡然扬起，听着几乎年轻了：

“站着别动，等俺叫你动再动！”

===Original===
Paradine, standing, fought for explanations. What was happening to him could be believed, was even logical. Mesmerism, scholars called it, or a newer name, hypnotism.

As a boy he, Paradine, had amused himself by holding a hen’s beak to the floor and drawing a chalk line therefrom. The hen could never move until he lifted it away from that mock tether. That was what now befell him, he was sure. His muscles were slack, or perhaps tense; he could not say by the feel. In any case, they were immovable. He could not move eye. He could not loosen grip on his saber-hilt. Yes, hypnotism. If only he rationalized it, he could break the spell.

But he remained motionless, as though he were the little iron figure to which his horse was tethered, yonder at the foot of the street.

The old man surveyed him with a flicker of shrewdness in those bright eyes that had seemed foolish.

===Chinese===
帕拉丁站在原地，拼命想找出个解释。落到他身上的这件事，是可以相信的，甚至合乎逻辑。学者们管它叫麦斯麦术（Mesmerism），或者用一个新近的名字：催眠术（hypnotism）。

小时候，帕拉丁玩过一个把戏：把母鸡的喙按在地板上，再从喙尖用粉笔画出一道线。不把他从那道假系绳上拎开，母鸡就动弹不得。他确信，如今这情形轮到了自己。他的肌肉是松弛还是紧绷，凭感觉竟说不出来。总之就是动不了。眼睛转不动。攥住刀柄的手也松不开。没错，就是催眠术。只要他把道理想通想透，就能破了这个咒。

可他依旧纹丝不动，仿佛他自己就是街那头拴着他坐骑的那尊小铁人。

老头上下打量他，那双方才还显得痴傻的亮眼珠里，闪过一丝精明。

===Original===
“I used only half power. Happen ye kin still hear me. So listen:

“My name’s Teague. I live down yon by the crick. I’m a witch-man, an’ my pappy was a witch-man afore me. He was the seventh son of a seventh son—an’ I was *his* seventh son. I know conjer stuff—black an’ white, forrard an’ back’ard. It’s my livin’.

“Folks in Channow make fun o’ me, like they did o’ my pappy when he was livin’ but they buy my charms. Things to bring love or hate, if they hanker fer ’em. Cures fer sick hogs an’ calves. Sayin’s to drive away fever. All them things. I done it fer Channow folks all my life.”

---

===Chinese===
“俺只使了一半道行。兴许你还听得见俺说话。那就听着：

“俺名叫蒂格（Teague），就住在那边小河湾。俺是个巫术师，俺爹在俺前头也是个巫术师。他是第七个儿子的第七个儿子——俺又是*他*的第七个儿子。俺懂召魂术的道行——黑的白的，正着来反着来都行。俺就靠这个糊口。

“查诺的乡亲们拿俺取笑，像俺爹在世时他们取笑他一样；可他们照样买俺的符咒。招爱的、招恨的物件，只要他们稀罕。治病猪病牛犊的方子，驱热退烧的口诀。诸如此类，样样都有。俺给查诺人干了一辈子。”

---

===Original===
It was a proud pronouncement, Paradine realized. Here was the man diligent in business, who could stand before kings. So might speak a statesman who had long served his constituency, or the editor of a paper that had built respectful traditions, or a doctor who had guarded a town’s health for decades, or a blacksmith who took pride in his lifetime of skilled toil. This gaffer who called himself a witch-man considered that he had done service, and was entitled to respect and gratitude. The narrator went on, more grimly:

“Sometimes I been laffed at, an’ told to mind my own bizness. Young ’uns has hooted, an’ throwed stones. I coulda cursed ’em—but I didn’t. Nossir. They’s my friends an’ neighbors—Channow folks. I kep’ back evil from ’em.”

The old figure straightened, the white beard jutted forward. An exultant note crept in.

“But when the Yanks come, an’ everybody run afore ’em but me, I didn’t have no scruples! Invaders! Tyrants! Thievin’ skunks in blue!” Teague sounded like a recruiting officer for a Texas regiment. “I didn’t owe them nothin’—an’ here in the street I faced ’em. I dug out this here little book, an’ I read the sleep words to ’em. See,” and the old hands gestured sweepingly, “they sleep till I tell ’em to wake. *If* I ever tell ’em!”

Paradine had to believe this tale of occult patriotism. There was nothing else to believe in its place. The old man who called himself Teague smiled twinklingly.

===Chinese===
帕拉丁这才省悟，这是一番骄傲的宣言。眼前这位，正是《箴言》里那种勤勉于本业、足以侍立于君王面前的人物。一位长期服务乡梓的政治家，一位立下体面传统的报纸主笔，一位守护全镇人健康几十年的医生，或是一位以毕生手艺自豪的铁匠，说起话来都会是这副腔调。这个自称巫术师的老头认定自己于人有功，理当受敬重、受感激。讲述者接着往下说，语气更加阴沉：

“有时候俺也挨人笑话，叫俺少管闲事。小崽子们朝俺喝倒彩，扔石头。俺本可以咒死他们——可俺没有。没有，先生。他们是俺的朋友乡亲——查诺人。俺一直替他们挡着灾祸。”

老迈的身板挺直起来，白胡子朝前翘着。话音里悄悄透出一股得意。

“可北佬一来，人人都在他们前头逃光了，就俺没逃，俺就再没什么顾忌了！侵略者！暴君！穿蓝衣裳的偷儿臭鼬！”蒂格那腔调，活像给得克萨斯团募兵的招兵官。“俺不欠他们一个铜子儿——就在这条街上，俺跟他们照了面。俺掏出这本小书，对他们念了催眠的话。瞧，”两只老手四下里豪迈地一挥，“他们要睡到俺叫醒为止。*要是*俺肯叫的话！”

这通关于玄门爱国心的话，帕拉丁不得不信。再没有别的说法能取而代之。自称蒂格的老头眨着眼笑了。

===Original===
“Yo’re secesh. Ye fight the Yanks. If ye’ll be good, an’ not gimme no argyments, blink yore left eye.”

Power of blinking returned to that lid, and Paradine lowered it submissively.

“Now ye kin move again—I’ll say the words.”

He leafed through the book once more, and read out; “Ye horsemen an’ footmen, conjered here at this time, ye may pass on in the name of …” Paradine did not catch the name, but it had a sound that chilled him. Next instant, motion was restored to his arms and legs. The blood tingled sharply in them, as if they had been asleep.

===Chinese===
“你是南军的人，你打北佬。你要是乖乖听话，不跟俺犟嘴，就眨一下左眼。”

那片眼皮恢复了眨动的力气，帕拉丁乖乖把它垂下。

“现在你又能动了——俺来念词儿。”

他把书又翻了一遍，念出声来：“你们这些骑兵步兵，此时此刻被召魂至此，可奉……之名继续前行。”那个名字帕拉丁没有听清，只觉得那声音里透着一股寒气。下一瞬，他的胳膊和腿重新听他使唤了。血在四肢里针扎似的发麻，仿佛它们方才睡了一觉。

===Original===
Teague offered him a hand, and Paradine took it. That hand was froggy cold and soft, for all its boniness.

“Arter this,” decreed Teague, “do what I tell ye, or I’ll read ye somethin’ ye’ll like less.” And he held out the open book significantly.

Paradine saw the page—it bore the number 60 in one corner, and at its top was a heading in capitals: **To release spellbound persons**. Beneath were the lines with which Teague had set him in motion again, and among them were smudged inky marks.

“You’ve crossed out some words,” Paradine said at once.

“Yep. An’ wrote in others.” Teague held the book closer to him.

===Chinese===
蒂格向他伸出一只手，帕拉丁握了。那只手再怎么瘦骨嶙峋，摸上去也像青蛙皮一样又冷又软。

“打这往后，”蒂格发下话来，“俺叫你干啥你就干啥，不然俺就给你念点你更不爱听的。”说着，他把摊开的书意味深长地递了过来。

帕拉丁看清了那一页——一角印着页码 60，顶上是一行大写标题：**为受咒所困者解禁**。标题底下，正是蒂格方才使他重新动弹的那几句，句子中间夹着一道道涂抹的墨痕。

“你划掉了一些字眼。”帕拉丁立刻指出。

“对。又添上了别的。”蒂格把书朝自己怀里拢了拢。

===Original===
Paradine felt yet another chill, and beat down a desire to turn away. He spoke again, because he felt that he should.

“It’s the name of God that you’ve cut out, Teague. Not once, but three times. Isn’t that blasphemy? And you’ve written in—”

“The name of somebody else.” Teague’s beard ruffled into a grin. “Young feller, ye don’t understand. This book was wrote full of the name of God. That name is good—fer some things. But fer curses an’ deaths an’ overthrows, sech as this ’un—well, I changed the names an’ spells by puttin’ in that other name ye saw. An’ it works fine.” He grinned wider as he surveyed the tumbled thousands around them, then shut the book and put it away.

Paradine had been wwell educated. He had read Marlowe’s *Dr. Faustus*, at the University of Virginia, and some accounts of the New England witchcraft cases. He could grasp, though he had never been called upon to consider, the idea of an alliance with evil. All he could reply was:

“I don’t see more than five thousand Yankees in this town. Our boys can whip that many and more, without any spells.”

===Chinese===
帕拉丁又是一阵发冷，硬压下扭头就走的念头。他再度开口，因为他觉得理应如此。

“蒂格，你划掉的是上帝之名。不是一处，是三处。这不算渎神吗？你还添上了——”

“添上了另外一位的名号。”蒂格的胡子抖开一个咧嘴笑。“年轻人，你不懂。这书原先写满了上帝之名。那名号是好使——对某些事。可对付诅咒、索命、倾覆这路活计，就好比这一桩——俺把你瞧见的那个名号添了进去，把名字和咒语都改了。好使得很。”他环视四下里倒伏的几千号人，咧嘴笑得更开了，随即合上书，收了起来。

帕拉丁受过良好的教育。他在弗吉尼亚大学读过马洛的《浮士德博士》，还读过几篇新英格兰巫案的记述。与邪恶结盟这回事，他虽从不曾被迫细想，却也完全能够领会。他能回答的只有一句：

“我看这镇里的北军超不过五千。咱们的小伙子不用半句咒语，也能收拾下这个数还有富余。”

===Original===
Teague shook his old head. “Come on, let’s go an’ set on them steps,” he invited, pointing.

The two walked back down the street, entered a yard and dropped down upon a porch. The shady leaves above them hung as silent as chips of stone. Through the fence-pickets showed the blue lumps of quiet that had been a fighting division of Federals. There was no voice, except Teague’s.

“Ye don’t grasp what war means, young feller. Sure, the South is winnin’ now—but to win, men must die. Powder must burn. An’ the South hain’t got men an’ powder enough to keep it up.”

If Paradine had never thought of that before, neither had his superiors, except possibly General Lee. Yet it was plainly true.

Teague extended the argument:

===Chinese===
蒂格摇了摇他那张苍老的头。“来，咱们去那边台阶上坐坐。”他抬手一指，相邀道。

两人顺街往回走，进了一处院子，在门廊里落了座。头顶荫凉的树叶悬垂着，静得像一片片石头。透过尖桩栅栏的缝隙，望得见外面一堆堆安静的蓝色——那本是一支能征惯战的联邦军师。四下没有一点人声，除了蒂格的。

“年轻人，你没弄明白打仗是怎么回事。不错，眼下南方正赢着——可要赢，就得死人。火药就得烧。可南方的人手和火药，撑不起这么打下去。”

这些话，帕拉丁从前没想过；他的长官们也没想过——兴许李将军除外。可这明明白白是实情。

蒂格把话头又推进了一层：

===Original===
“But if every Yank army was put to sleep, fast’s it got in reach—what then? How’d ye like to lead yore own army into Washington an’ grab ole Abe Lincoln right outen the White House? How’d ye like to be the second greatest man o’ the South?”

“Second greatest man?” echoed Paradine breathlessly, forgetting to fear. He was being tempted as few chivalric idealists can endure. “Second only to—Robert E. Lee!”

The name of his general trembled on his lips. It trembles to this day, on the lips of those who remember. But Teague only snickered, and combed his beard with fingers like skinny sticks.

“Ye don’t ketch on yet. Second man, not to Lee, but to—me, Teague! Fer I’d be a-runnin’ things!”

===Chinese===
“可要是北佬的军队一支接一支，一开到跟前就被催眠放倒——那会怎么样？你想不想亲自领着你的兵开进华盛顿，把老亚伯·林肯从白宫里一把揪出来？想不想当南方的第二号大人物？”

“第二号大人物？”帕拉丁气都喘不匀了，应声重复，忘了害怕。这是没几个骑士理想主义者经受得住的诱惑。“仅次于——罗伯特·E·李！”

他那位将军的尊名，在他唇边颤动。直到今天，这名字仍在记得他的人们唇边颤动。可蒂格只是嗤笑一声，用几根细柴棒似的手指梳着胡子。

“你还没转过弯来。第二人，不是仅次于李，是仅次于——俺，蒂格！因为说了算的得是俺！”

===Original===
Paradine, who had seen and heard so much to amaze him during the past hour, had yet the capacity to gasp. His saber was between his knees, and his hands tightened on the hilt until the knuckles turned pale. Teague gave no sign. He went on:

“I hain’t never got no respect here in Channow. Happen it’s time I showed ’em what I can do.” His eyes studied the windrows of men he had caused to drop down like sickled wheat. Creases of proud triumph deepened around his eyes. “We’ll do all the Yanks this way, son. Yore gen’rals hain’t never done nothing like it, have they?”

His generals—Paradine had seen them on occasion. Jackson, named Stonewall for invincibility, kneeling in unashamed public prayer; Jeb Stuart, with his plume and his brown beard, listening to the clang of Sweeney’s banjo; Hood, who outcharged even his wild Texans; Polk blessing the soldiers in the dawn before battle, like a prophet of brave old days; and Lee, the gray knight, at whom Teague had laughed. No, they had never done anything like it. And, if they could, they would not.

“Teague,” said Paradine, “this isn’t right.”

“Not right? Oh, I know what ye mean. Ye don’t like them names I wrote into the *Powwows*, do ye? But ain’t everything fair in love an’ war?”

---

===Chinese===
这一个钟头里，帕拉丁见过的听过的惊人之事够多了，此刻却仍倒抽得上一口气。军刀夹在他两膝之间，双手在刀柄上越攥越紧，指节都攥白了。蒂格毫无表示，只管往下说：

“俺在查诺从来没得过半分敬重。兴许到了让他们瞧瞧俺本事的时候了。”他的眼睛扫过一垄垄被他放倒的人，像镰刀割倒的麦子。得意的皱纹在他眼角越刻越深。“咱们就这么把北佬一支支全办了，儿子。你的将军们可从来没干成过这么一票，对不对？”

他的将军们——帕拉丁是见过几面的。杰克逊，因战无不胜得名“石墙”，当众跪下坦然祷告，毫无愧色；杰布·斯图尔特，顶着羽饰、蓄着棕须，听斯威尼的班卓琴铮铮作响；胡德，冲锋起来连他手下那帮狂野的得克萨斯兵都得甘拜下风；波尔克在开战前的黎明为士兵们祝福，宛如古时英烈年代的先知；还有李，那位灰衣骑士——蒂格方才嗤笑的就是他。没有，他们从没干过这样的事。而且，纵然干得成，他们也不会去干。

“蒂格，”帕拉丁说，“这不是正道。”

“不正大？噢，俺知道你的意思。你不喜欢俺写进《失传的友人》的那些名号，对不对？可情场也好，战场也好，什么招数不都算光明正大吗？”

---

===Original===
Teague laid a persuasive claw on the sleeve of Paradine’s looted jacket. “Listen this oncet. Yore idee is to win with sword an’ gun. Mine’s to win by conjurin’. Which is the quickest way? The easiest way? The only way?”

“To my way of thinking, the only way is by fair fight. God,” pronounced Paradine, as stiffly as Leonidas Polk himself, “watches armies.”

“An’ so does somebody else,” responded Teague. “Watches—an’ listens. Happen he’s listenin’ this minit. Well, lad, I need a sojer to figger army things fer me. You joinin’ me?”

Not only Teague waited for Paradine’s answer. … The young trooper remembered, from Pilgrim’s Progress, what sort of dealings might be fatal. Slowly he got to his feet.

===Chinese===
蒂格把一只劝说用的爪子搭上帕拉丁那身缴来的军装袖子。“听俺说这一回。你的主意，是靠刀枪取胜。俺的主意，是靠召魂术取胜。哪条道最快？最省力？是唯一的路？”

“照我的想法，唯一的路是堂堂正正地打。上帝，”帕拉丁一字一板地宣布，那副严正像足了利奥尼达斯·波尔克本人，“在注视着大军。”

“另有哪一位也在瞧着呢。”蒂格应道。“瞧着——还听着。说不定这会儿就正听着。好了小子，俺要个大兵替俺摆弄军队里的事。你入伙不入？”

等着帕拉丁回答的，不止蒂格一个。……这年轻的骑兵从《天路历程》里想起，有一种交易是会送命的。他慢慢站起身来。

===Original===
“The South doesn’t need that kind of help,” he said flatly.

“Too late to back out,” Teague told him.

“What do you mean?”

“The help’s been asked fer already, son. An’ it’s been given. A contract, ye might call it. If the contract’s broke—well, happen the other party’ll get mad. They can be worse enemies ’n Yanks.”

Teague, too, rose to his feet. “Too late,” he said again. “That power can sweep armies away fer us. But if we say no—well, it’s been roused up, it’ll still sweep away armies—Southern armies. Ye think I shouldn’t have started sech a thing? But I’ve started it. Can’t turn back now.”

===Chinese===
“南方不需要那种帮助。”他斩钉截铁地说。

“想抽身，晚啦。”蒂格对他说。

“你这是什么意思？”

“那份帮助已经求过了，儿子。人家也已经给了。你可以说，这是一纸契约。契约要是毁了——唔，说不定那一位就要动怒。他们当起仇家来，能比北佬还凶。”

蒂格也站起身来。“晚啦。”他又说了一遍。“那股力量能替咱们把千军万马扫荡干净。可咱们要是说个不字——唔，它已经被惊起来了，照样要扫荡大军——南方的大军。你觉得俺不该招惹这路东西？可俺已经招惹了。如今收不回了。”

===Original===
Victory through evil—what would it become in the end? Faust’s story told, and so did the legend of Gilles de Retz, and the play about Macbeth. But there was also the tale of the sorcerer’s apprentice, and of what befell him when he tried to reject the force he had thoughtlessly evoked.

“What do you want me to do?” he asked, through lips that muddled the words.

“Good lad, I thought ye’d see sense. First off, I want yore name to the bargain. Then me ’n’ you can lick the Yanks.”

Lick the Yankees! Paradine remembered a gayly profane catchphrase of the Confederate camp: “Don’t say Yankee, say damned Yankee.” But what about a damned Confederacy? Teague spoke of the day of victory; what of the day of reckoning?

What payment would this ally ask in the end?

===Chinese===
靠邪恶换来的胜利——到头来会变成什么？浮士德的故事给过答案，吉尔·德·雷（Gilles de Retz）的传说给过，写麦克白的那出戏也给过。可还有那个巫师学徒的故事——他不假思索唤起一股力量，待到想把它推开时，落了个什么下场。

“你要我做什么？”他问，嘴唇发木，字句都念得含混了。

“好小子，俺就知道你会想通。头一件，俺要你把名字签到这份买卖上。然后咱爷俩就能痛揍北佬了。”

痛揍北佬！帕拉丁想起邦联军营里一句快活的浑话：“别说北佬，要说该死的北佬。”可要是换成一个该死的邦联呢？蒂格满口都是胜利之日；那清算之日呢？

这位盟友到头来会索要什么报酬？

===Original===
Again Faust popped into his mind. He imagined the Confederacy as a Faust among the nations, devil-lifted, devil-nurtured—and devil-doomed, by the connivance of one Joseph Paradine.

Better disaster, in the way of man’s warfare.

The bargain was offered him for all the South. For all the South he must reject, completely and finally.

Aloud he said: “My name? Signed to something?”

===Chinese===
浮士德又跳进他脑海。他想象邦联成了万国之中的浮士德，被魔鬼托举，被魔鬼滋养——也被魔鬼断送，而促成这一切的，是约瑟夫·帕拉丁的一手纵容。

宁可要一场灾难，走人类自己的战争之路。

这份买卖，是替整个南方递到他手里的。为了整个南方，他必须回绝——回绝得干干净净、彻彻底底。

他出声问道：“我的名字？签在什么东西上？”

===Original===
“Right here’ll do.”

Once more Teague brought forth the *Powwows* book which he had edited so strangely. “Here, son, on this back page—in blood.”

Paradine bowed his head. It was to conceal the look in his eyes, and he hoped to look as though he acquiesced. He drew his saber, passed it to his left hand. Upon its tip he pressed his right forefinger. A spot of dull pain, and a drop of blood creeping forth, as had appeared on the wrist of the ensorcelled boy lying yonder among the Yankees in the street.

“That’ll be enough to sign with,” approved Teague.

He flattened out the book, exposing the rear flyleaf. Paradine extended his reddened forefinger. It stained the rough white paper.

“J for Joseph,” dictated Teague. “Yep, like that—”

---

===Chinese===
“就签在这儿，成。”

蒂格又一次掏出那本被他改得邪里邪气的《失传的友人》。“这儿，儿子，就这最后一页上——用血签。”

帕拉丁低下头去。为的是遮住眼里的神色，他还指望自己看上去像是从了。他抽出军刀，换到左手。右手食指抵住刀尖。一点钝痛，一滴血慢慢渗将出来，就像街上那堆北佬当中躺着的、中了咒的孩子，手腕上渗出的那滴一样。

“这点血够签的了。”蒂格点头认可。

他把书摊平，露出后面的衬页。帕拉丁伸出染红的食指，在粗糙的白纸上洇下一道血痕。

“J，约瑟夫的J。”蒂格口授道。“对，就那么写——”

---

===Original===
Paradine galvanized into action. His bloody right hand seized the book, wrenching it from the trembling fingers. With the saber in his left hand, he struck.

A pretty stroke for even a practised swordsman; the honed edge of the steel found the shaggy side of Teague’s scrawny neck. Paradine felt bone impeding his powerful drawing slash. Then he felt it no longer. The neck had sliced in two, and for a moment Teague’s head hung free in the air, like a lantern on a wire.

The bright eyes fixed Paradine’s, the mouth fell open in the midst of the beard, trying to speak a word that would not come. Then it fell, bounced like a ball, and rolled away. The headless trunk stood on braced feet, crumpling slowly. Paradine stepped away from it, and it collapsed upon the steps of the house.

Again there was utter silence in the town and valley of Channow. The blue soldiers did not budge where they lay. Paradine knew that he alone moved and breathed and saw—no, not entirely alone. His horse was tethered at the end of the street.

He flung away his saber and ran, ashamed no more of his dread. Reaching the gray, he found his fingers shaky, but he wrenched loose the knotted reins. Flinging himself into the saddle, he rode away across the level and up the slope.

The pines sighed gently, and that sound gave him comfort after so much soundlessness.

===Chinese===
帕拉丁如遭电击般暴起。染血的右手一把攥住那本书，从颤巍巍的手指间夺了过来。左手军刀，挥刀便劈。

这一刀干净利落，即便出自老练剑客之手也算得上漂亮；磨利的钢刃咬进蒂格细脖子上胡须蓬乱的一侧。帕拉丁觉得骨头挡住了他大力回拖的刀锋；随即便觉不着了。脖子断成了两截，有一瞬间，蒂格的头颅悬空荡着，像挂在铁丝上的一盏灯笼。

那双亮眼睛定定地盯住帕拉丁的眼睛，大胡子中央那张嘴张开着，想说出一个字，却说不出来。随后它落了下去，皮球似的弹了一下，滚开了。无头的躯干叉开双脚立在原地，缓缓地瘫软下去。帕拉丁闪身让开，它便扑倒在房子的台阶上。

查诺镇和查诺谷又一次陷入彻底的寂静。蓝色的士兵们在倒下的地方一动没动。帕拉丁知道，此刻只有他一个人在动、在呼吸、在看——不，也不全是独自一个。他的马还拴在街尾。

他甩掉军刀，撒腿就跑，再不为自己的恐惧害臊。跑到灰马跟前，手指直打颤，可他还是解开了打结的缰绳。他翻身上鞍，一路狂奔，越过平地，冲上坡顶。

松林轻轻叹息，这一丝声响，让他在经历了那么多无声之后感到安慰。

===Original===
He dismounted, his knees swaying as though their tendons had been cut, and studied the earth. Here were the footprints of Danger’s horse. Here also was a cleft stick, and in it a folded scrap of paper, a note. He lifted it, and read the penciled scrawl:

> Dear frend Joe, you ant com back so I left like you said to bring up the boys. I hope your alright & if the Yankies have got you well get you back.
> L. Dauger.

His comrades were coming, then, with gun and sword. They expected to meet Union soldiers. Paradine gazed back into the silence-brimmed valley, then at what he still held in his right hand. It was the Poiu-Wows book, marked with a wet capital J in his own blood.

What had Teague insisted? The one whose name had been invoked would be fatally angry if his help were refused. But Paradine was going to refuse it.

He turned to Page 60. His voice was shaky, but be managed to read aloud:

===Chinese===
他翻身下马，膝盖直打晃，像腿筋被挑断了一样，然后细看地面。这儿有道格坐骑的蹄印。这儿还有一根劈开的木棍，夹着一张折起来的纸片，一张字条。他拾起来，读那铅笔涂的歪字：

> 亲爱的朋友乔：你没回来，我就照你说的回去把弟兄们带来了。希望你没事。要是北佬把你拿了，我们会把你救回来的。
> L. 道格。

这么说，弟兄们正带着枪炮刀剑在路上了。他们一心指望撞上北军。帕拉丁回头望了望那寂静满溢的谷地，又看看自己右手仍攥着的东西。是那本《失传的友人》，上面有他亲笔用血写下的、尚未干透的大写字母J。

蒂格方才一口咬定什么来着？被唤了名号的那一位，一旦相助遭拒，便会发起致命的怒火。可帕拉丁偏要回绝。

他翻到第 60 页。嗓音发颤，可他还是念出了声：

===Original===
“Ye horsemen and footmen, conjured here at this time, ye may pass on in the name of”—he faltered, but disregarded the ink-blotting, and the substituted names—“of Jesus Christ, and through the word of God.”

Again he gulped, and finished. “Ye may now ride on and pass.”

From under his feet burst a dry, startling thunder of sound, a partridge rising to the sky. Farther down the slope a crow took wing, cawing querulously. Wind wakened in the Channow Valley; Paradine saw the distant trees of the town stir with it. Then a confused din came to his ears, as though something besides wind was wakening.

After a moment he heard the notes of a bugle, shrill and tremulous, sounding an alarm.

===Chinese===
“你们这些骑兵与步兵，此时此刻被召魂至此，可奉——”他打了个顿，随即不管那些墨污和被换掉的名号，径直往下念——“奉耶稣基督之名，凭上帝之言，继续前行。”

他又咽了口气，念完末句：“你们如今可以上马前行，一路无阻。”

脚边猛地炸起一声干爽惊人的响雷，一只鹧鸪冲天而起。再往坡下，一只乌鸦振翅飞起，聒噪着抱怨。查诺谷里醒了风；帕拉丁望见远处镇上的树梢随风摇动。跟着，一片嘈杂的声浪涌进他耳朵，仿佛醒来的不止是风。

片刻之后，他听见了军号的音符，尖利而发颤，吹的是警报。

===Original===
Paradine struck fire, and built it up with fallen twigs. Into the hottest heart of it he thrust Teague’s book of charms. The flame gnawed eagerly at it, the pages crumpled and fanned and blackened with the heat. For a moment he saw, standing out among charred fragments, a bloodred J, his writing, as though it fought for life. Then it, too, was consumed, and there were only ashes. Before the last red tongue subsided, his ears picked up a faint rebel yell, and afar into the valley rode Confederate cavalry.

He put his gray to the gallop, got down the slope and joined his regiment before it reached the town. On the street a Union line was forming. There was hot, fierce fighting, such as had scattered and routed many a Northern force.

But, at the end of it, the Southerners ran like foxes before hounds, and those who escaped counted themselves lucky.

---

===Chinese===
帕拉丁打出火来，用枯枝把火堆架旺。他把蒂格那本符咒书捅进火最烫的心窝。火苗贪婪地啃咬它，书页蜷曲、翻卷，在热浪里焦黑。有一瞬，他看见焦黑的碎屑当中凸现出一个血红的J，他自己的笔迹，仿佛还在挣扎求生。随后它也被烧尽了，只剩下一堆灰。在最后一条红舌熄灭之前，他耳朵里捕到一声隐约的反叛吶喊——南军骑兵远远地驰进了山谷。

他催灰马放蹄疾驰，赶下坡去，赶在团队进镇之前并入了队列。街上，一条北军战线正在结成。战斗打得炽烈凶悍，一如曾多少次冲垮、击溃北军的那些恶战。

可打到末了，南方人像猎犬面前的狐狸一样逃了，逃出去的都暗自庆幸命大。

---

===Original===
In his later garrulous years, Joseph Paradine was apt to say that the war was lost, not at Antietam or Gettysburg, but at a little valley hamlet called Channow. Refusal of a certain alliance, he would insist, was the cause; that offered ally fought thenceforth against the South.

But nobody paid attention, except to laugh or to pity. So many veterans go crazy.

===Chinese===
在后来絮叨的晚年岁月里，约瑟夫·帕拉丁动不动就说，这场仗不是败在安提塔姆，也不是败在葛底斯堡，而是败在一个叫查诺的小山谷村落。他会一口咬定，祸根就在回绝了某一场结盟；那位被回绝的盟友，从此处处与南方为敌。

可谁也不当回事，要么一笑，要么可怜他。老兵疯癫的，见得还少么。

## The Witch’s Cat / 女巫的猫

===Original===

Old Jael Bettiss, who lived in the hollow among the cypresses, was not a real witch.

It makes no difference that folk thought she was, and walked fearfully wide of her shadow. Nothing can be proved by the fact that she was as disgustingly ugly without as she was wicked within. It is quite irrelevant that evil was her study and profession and pleasure. She was no witch; she only pretended to be.

Jael Bettiss knew that all laws providing for the punishment of witches had been repealed, or at the least forgotten. As to being feared and hated, that was meat and drink to Jael Bettiss, living secretly alone in the hollow.

===Chinese===

住在柏树丛生山坳里的老杰尔·贝蒂斯（Jael Bettiss），并不是真正的女巫。

乡亲们认定她是女巫，走路都心惊胆战地绕开她的影子——这算不得数。她外表丑得骇人、内里毒得彻骨，里外一般坏——这也证明不了什么。恶，是她的学问、营生和乐趣——这同样不相干。她不是女巫，她只是装成女巫罢了。

杰尔·贝蒂斯心里有数：惩治女巫的种种法规早已废除，或者至少早已被人遗忘。至于遭人惧怕、遭人憎恨，那对独居山坳、深居简出的杰尔·贝蒂斯来说，不啻酒肉饭食。

===Original===

The house and the hollow belonged to a kindly old villager, who had been elected marshal and was too busy to look after his property. Because he was easygoing and perhaps a little daunted, he let Jael Bettiss live there rent-free. The house was no longer snug; the back of its roof was broken in, the eaves drooped slackly. At some time or other the place had been painted brown, before that with ivory black. Now both coats of color peeled away in huge flakes, making the clapboards seem scrofulous. The windows had been broken in every small, grubby pane, and mended with coarse brown paper, so that they were like cast and blurred eyes. Behind was the muddy, bramble-choked back yard, and behind that yawned the old quarry, now abandoned and full of black water. As for the inside﻿—but few ever saw it.

Jael Bettiss did not like people to come into her house. She always met callers on the old cracked doorstep, draped in a cloak of shadowy black, with gray hair straggling, her nose as hooked and sharp as the beak of a buzzard, her eyes filmy and sore-looking, her wrinkle-bordered mouth always grinning and showing her yellow, chisel-shaped teeth.

===Chinese===

这房子连同这片山坳，属于村里一位和善的老人。他当选了治安官（marshal），公务繁忙，无暇照看自己的产业。因为他脾气随和——或许也有点心虚——便由着杰尔·贝蒂斯白白住下去。这房子早已谈不上安逸严实：后半边屋顶塌了，屋檐疲塌塌地耷拉着。这地方不知哪年漆过一回棕色，更早以前漆的是象牙黑。如今两层漆皮都大块大块剥落，墙板瞧着像害了瘰疬。窗户上每一块小而脏的玻璃都碎过，用粗糙的棕纸糊着，活像一双生了翳、雾蒙蒙的眼睛。屋后是泥泞的、塞满荆棘的后院；再往后，老采石场张着大口——如今废弃了，积着一坑黑水。至于屋里头嘛——见识过的人可没几个。

杰尔·贝蒂斯不喜欢人家进她的屋。有客上门，她总是裹着一件影影绰绰的黑斗篷，在开裂的旧门阶上迎候：灰白的头发披散着，鼻子又钩又尖，像兀鹫的喙，眼睛蒙着翳子、一副病恹恹的样子，皱纹镶边的嘴巴永远咧着，露出凿子般的一口小黄牙。

===Original===

The nearby village was an old-fashioned place, with stone flags instead of concrete for pavements, and the villagers were the simplest of men and women. From them Jael Bettiss made a fair living, by selling love philtres, or herbs to cure sickness, or charms to ward off bad luck. When she wanted extra money, she would wrap her old black cloak about her and, tramping along a country road, would stop at a cow-pen and ask the farmer what he would do if his cows went dry. The farmer, worried, usually came at dawn next day to her hollow and bought a good-luck charm. Occasionally the cows would go dry anyway, by accident of nature, and their owner would pay more and more, until their milk returned to them.

Now and then, when Jael Bettiss came to the door, there came with her the gaunt black cat, Gib.

Gib was not truly black, any more than Jael Bettiss was truly a witch. He had been born with white markings at muzzle, chest and forepaws, so that he looked to be in full evening dress. Left alone, he would have grown fat and fluffy. But Jael Bettiss, who wanted a fearsome pet, kept all his white spots smeared with thick soot, and underfed him to make him look rakish and lean.

===Chinese===

附近的村子是个老派地方，人行道铺的是石板而不是水泥，村民是最淳朴不过的男男女女。杰尔·贝蒂斯靠他们过上了一份不坏的日子：卖爱情魔药（love philtre），卖治病的草药，卖避邪的护符。想额外捞点钱的时候，她就裹上那件旧黑斗篷，沿着乡间土路长途跋涉，在牛栏边停下脚，问农场主：要是您的奶牛断了奶，您打算怎么办？农场主心里一慌，第二天清早多半就会赶到山坳来，买一个讨好运气的护符。偶尔奶牛仍会断奶——天生天养出了岔子——主人便一次又一次地加钱，直到牛奶重新下来为止。

时不时地，杰尔·贝蒂斯走到门口的时候，身边还会跟出来那只精瘦的黑猫，吉布（Gib）。

吉布并非真正的黑猫，正如杰尔·贝蒂斯并非真正的女巫。他生来口鼻、胸口和前爪就带着白斑，瞧着像穿了一身笔挺的燕尾服。要是随他自便，本会长得肥肥胖胖、毛蓬蓬的。可杰尔·贝蒂斯要的是一只吓人的宠物，便把他身上的白点子统统抹上厚厚的烟灰，还让他吃不饱，好叫他显得瘦削而不驯。

===Original===

On the night of the full moon, she would drive poor Gib from her door. He would wander to the village in search of food, and would wail mournfully in the yards. Awakened householders would angrily throw boots or pans or sticks of kindling. Often Gib was hit, and his cries were sharpened by pain. When that happened, Jael Bettiss took care to be seen next morning with a bandage on head or wrist. Some of the simplest villagers thought that Gib was really the old woman, magically transformed. Her reputation grew, as did Gib’s unpopularity. But Gib did not deserve mistrust﻿—like all cats, he was a practical philosopher, who wanted to be comfortable and quiet and dignified. At bottom, he was amiable. Like all cats, too, he loved his home above all else; and the house in the hollow, be it ever so humble and often cruel, was home. It was unthinkable to him that he might live elsewhere.

In the village he had two friends﻿—black-eyed John Frey, the storekeeper’s son, who brought the mail to and from the county seat, and Ivy Hill, pretty blond daughter of the town marshal, the same town marshal who owned the hollow and let Jael Bettiss live in the old house. John Frey and Ivy Hill were so much in love with each other that they loved everything else, even black-stained, hungry Gib. He was grateful; if he had been able, he would have loved them in return. But his little heart had room for one devotion only, and that was given to the house in the hollow.

===Chinese===

每逢月圆之夜，她就把可怜的吉布赶出门去。他便溜达到村里找吃的，在各家院子里凄声哀叫。被吵醒的主人气冲冲地扔靴子、扔锅盆、扔劈柴。吉布常常挨砸，疼得叫声更尖。每逢这种时候，杰尔·贝蒂斯必定留神让人瞧见，第二天早上她头上或手腕上准缠着绷带。村里顶单纯的一些人当真以为，吉布就是那老太婆施法变的。她名头越发响，吉布也越发招人嫌。可吉布并不该受这份猜忌——跟天下所有的猫一样，他是个讲实际的哲学家，只求安逸、清静、体面。骨子里，他是和善的。也跟天下所有的猫一样，他把家看得比什么都重；山坳里那座屋子，再怎么破败、再怎么经常受罪，也是家。要他到别处去住，那是他想都不敢想的事。

村里他有两个朋友——一个是黑眼睛的约翰·弗赖（John Frey），店主的儿子，替店里在县城和村子之间收发邮件；一个是艾薇·希尔（Ivy Hill），镇治安官的漂亮金发女儿——那位治安官，正是山坳的主人，白让杰尔·贝蒂斯住进旧屋的也正是他。约翰·弗赖和艾薇·希尔彼此爱得深，连带着爱起了世上一切旁的东西，连熏得一身黑、饿着肚子的吉布也在内。吉布领情；他若是有本事，也想回敬他们一份爱。可他那颗小心脏只搁得下一份忠心，而那份忠心，早交给山坳里的那座房子了。

---

===Original===

One day, Jael Bettiss slouched darkly into old Mr. Frey’s store, and up to the counter that served for postoffice. Leering, she gave John Frey a letter. It was directed to a certain little-known publisher, asking for a certain little-known book. Several days later, she appeared again, received a parcel, and bore it to her home.

In her gloomy, secret parlor, she unwrapped her purchase. It was a small, drab volume, with no title on cover or back. Sitting at the rickety table, she began to read. All evening and most of the night she read, forgetting to give Gib his supper, though he sat hungrily at her feet.

At length, an hour before dawn, she finished. Laughing loudly and briefly, she turned her beak-nose toward the kerosene lamp on the table. From the book she read aloud two words. The lamp went out, though she had not blown at it. Jael Bettiss spoke one commanding word more, and the lamp flamed alight again.

===Chinese===

一天，杰尔·贝蒂斯一身阴气地溜达到老弗赖先生的店里，晃到那权充邮柜的柜台跟前。她挤眉弄眼地递给约翰·弗赖一封信。信是写给一家没多少人知道的出版商的，讨购一本没多少人知道的书。几天之后她又露面了，收到一个包裹，抱回了家。

在那间幽暗、不见外人的堂屋里，她拆开买来的东西。那是小小一册灰扑扑的书，封面封底都没有书名。她坐到那张摇摇晃晃的桌子前，读了起来。一整晚，又搭上大半夜，她读个不停，忘了给吉布开饭——尽管他饿着肚子蹲在她脚边。

终于，天亮前一个钟头，她读完了。她放声大笑，笑得短促而响亮，鹰钩鼻尖直指桌上那盏煤油灯。她对着书高声念出两个词。灯灭了——她根本没吹它。杰尔·贝蒂斯又喝令了一个字，灯重新亮了起来。

===Original===

“At last!” she cried out in shrill exultation, and grinned down at Gib. Her lips drew back from her yellow chisels of teeth. “At last!” she crowed again. “Why don’t you speak to me, you little brute?﻿ ﻿… Why don’t you, indeed?”

She asked that final question as though she had been suddenly inspired. Quickly she glanced through the back part of the book, howled with laughter over something she found there, then sprang up and scuttled like a big, filthy crab into the dark, windowless cell that was her kitchen. There she mingled salt and malt in the palm of her skinny right hand. After that, she rummaged out a bundle of dried herbs, chewed them fine and spat them into the mixture. Stirring again with her forefinger, she returned to the parlor. Scanning the book to refresh her memory, she muttered a nasty little rime. Finally she dashed the mess suddenly upon Gib.

===Chinese===

“成了！”她尖声狂喜地喊道，低头朝吉布咧嘴一笑，嘴唇掀开，露出凿子般的一口黄牙。“成了！”她又得意洋洋地叫了一遍。“你干吗不跟我说话，小畜生？……你倒是干吗不说话呢，嗯？”

问出最后那句时，她像是突然得了天启。她飞快地把书后半本翻检了一遍，对着查到的东西哈哈狂笑，随即一跃而起，像只邋遢的大螃蟹似的横着窜进那间没有窗子的黑屋子——她的厨房。在那里，她枯瘦的右掌心里把盐和麦芽混作一处。接着又翻出一捆干草药，嚼碎了吐进那混合物里。她用食指又搅了一通，回到堂屋。她照着书温习了一遍，咕哝出一小段下作的小韵文。末了，她把那摊东西猛地泼向吉布。

===Original===

He retreated, shaking himself, outraged and startled. In a corner he sat down, and bent his head to lick the smeared fragments of the mixture away. But they revolted his tongue and palate, and he paused in the midst of this chore, so important to cats; and meanwhile Jael Bettiss yelled, “Speak!”

Gib crouched and blinked, feeling sick. His tongue came out and steadied his lips. Finally he said: “I want something to eat.”

His voice was small and high, like a little child’s, but entirely understandable. Jael Bettiss was so delighted that she laughed and clapped her bony knees with her hands, in self-applause.

“It worked!” she cried. “No more humbug about me, you understand? I’m a real witch at last, and not a fraud!”

===Chinese===

他连连后退，抖着身子，又惊又怒。他缩进墙角坐下，低头去舔毛上粘的药渣。可那味道实在冲，舌头口腔都受不了，这项猫族要紧的活计干到一半便停了嘴；这当口，杰尔·贝蒂斯大喝一声：“说话！”

吉布蜷着身子直眨眼，只觉恶心。他伸出舌头舔了舔，好稳住嘴唇。终于开了口：“我想吃点东西。”

他的嗓音又细又高，像个小孩子，可字字听得明白。杰尔·贝蒂斯乐坏了，双手拍着瘦骨嶙峋的膝盖，自己给自己喝彩。

“灵了！”她嚷道。“往后谁也别想再说我装神弄鬼，听见没有？我终于是个真女巫了，不是骗子了！”

===Original===

Gib found himself able to understand all this, more clearly than he had ever understood human affairs before. “I want something to eat,” he said again, more definitely than before. “I didn’t have any supper, and it’s nearly﻿—”

“Oh, stow your gab!” snapped his mistress. “This book, crammed with knowledge and strength, that made me able to do it. I’ll never be without it again, and it’ll teach me all the things I’ve only guessed at and mumbled about. I’m a real witch now, I say. And if you don’t think I’ll make those ignorant sheep of villagers realize it﻿—”

Once more she went off into gales of wild, cracked mirth, and threw a dish at Gib. He darted away into a corner just in time, and the missile crashed into blue-and-white china fragments against the wall. But Jael Bettiss read aloud from her book an impressive gibberish, and the dish reformed itself on the floor; the bits crept together and joined and the cracks disappeared, as trickling drops of water form into a pool. And finally, when the witch’s twig-like forefinger beckoned, the dish floated upward like a leaf in a breeze and set itself gently back on the table. Gib watched warily.

“That’s small to what I shall do hereafter,” swore Jael Bettiss.

===Chinese===

吉布发现自己全听懂了，比以往听懂过的任何人事都清楚。“我想吃点东西。”他又说了一遍，比先前更斩钉截铁。“我晚饭还没吃上呢，眼看就快——”

“闭嘴，少啰嗦！”他的女主人厉声道。“是这本书办成的，它满肚子学问和法力。我再也不离开它半步了，它要把我从前只能瞎猜、只能干嘟囔的那些事，一样一样教给我。我说了，我如今是真女巫。你要是以为我不能叫村里那帮无知的蠢羊明白这一点——”

她又爆发出一阵狂野嘶哑的大笑，随手朝吉布摔过去一只盘子。他及时窜进墙角，那飞盘撞在墙上，摔成蓝白瓷片。可杰尔·贝蒂斯照着书高声念了一串煞有介事的咒文胡话，那盘子便在地板上自行复原；碎片彼此爬拢、拼合，裂纹尽数消隐，好比涓涓水珠汇成一泓。最后，女巫枯枝般的食指一勾，那盘子像微风里的一片落叶，悠悠飘起，轻轻落回桌上。吉布警惕地瞧着。

“这跟往后的作为比起来，不过雕虫小技。”杰尔·贝蒂斯赌咒发誓似的说。

---

===Original===

When next the mail was distributed at the general store, a dazzling stranger appeared.

She wore a cloak, an old-fashioned black coat, but its drapery did not conceal the tall perfection of her form. As for her face, it would have stirred interest and admiration in larger and more sophisticated gatherings than the knot of letter-seeking villagers. Its beauty was scornful but inviting, classic but warm, with something in it of Grecian sculpture and Oriental allure. If the nose was cruel, it was straight; if the lips were sullen, they were full; if the forehead was a suspicion low, it was white and smooth. Thick, thunder-black hair swept up from that forehead, and backward to a knot at the neck. The eyes glowed with strange, hot lights, and wherever they turned they pierced and captivated.

People moved away to let her have a clear, sweeping pathway forward to the counter. Until this stranger had entered, Ivy Hill was the loveliest person present; now she looked only modest and fresh and blond in her starched gingham, and worried to boot. As a matter of fact. Ivy Hill’s insides felt cold and topsy-turvy, because she saw how fascinated was the sudden attention of John Frey.

===Chinese===

下一次杂货店里分发邮件的时候，来了一位光彩夺目的陌生女子。

她身披斗篷，一件老式黑外套，可那衣料的垂坠掩不住她高挑完美的身段。至于她的容貌，便是比这群来取信的乡民盛大得多、老练得多的场合，也照样要为之轰动倾倒。那张脸美得倨傲又撩人，典雅又温热，兼有希腊雕塑的韵味和东方情调的魅惑。鼻子纵然透着狠辣，却挺直；嘴唇纵然透着阴郁，却丰满；额头若嫌略低了一分，却白皙光洁。乌黑浓密的头发雷云般从额上一掠而过，向后绾成脑后一个髻。那双眼睛燃着奇异的灼灼光焰，扫到哪儿，哪儿便被看穿、被迷住。

人们纷纷让开，给她空出一条直通柜台的宽绰道儿。在这位陌生女子进门之前，艾薇·希尔原是在场最动人的人；如今她那身浆挺的格纹裙，只显得素净、鲜嫩、一头金发，外加满心忐忑。说实在的，艾薇·希尔只觉五脏六腑冰凉翻腾，因为她看得分明，约翰·弗赖这突如其来的注意，是何等痴迷。

===Original===

“Is there,” asked the newcomer in a deep, creamy voice, “any mail for me?”

“Wh-what name, ma’am?” asked John Frey, his brown young cheeks turning full crimson.

“Bettiss. Jael Bettiss.”

He began to fumble through the sheaf of envelopes, with hands that shook. “Are you,” he asked, “any relation to the old lady of that name, the one who lives in the hollow?”

“Yes, of a sort.” She smiled a slow, conquering smile. “She’s my﻿—aunt. Yes. Perhaps you see the family resemblance?” Wider and wider grew the smile with which she assaulted John Frey. “If there isn’t any mail,” she went on, “I would like a stamp. A one-cent stamp.”

===Chinese===

“有没有，”新来的人开口，嗓音低沉圆润，“我的信？”

“您……您贵姓，太太？”约翰·弗赖问，年轻棕红的脸颊涨得通红。

“贝蒂斯。杰尔·贝蒂斯。”

他动手在一叠信封里翻找，双手直打颤。“您，”他问，“跟住在山坳里的那位同姓老太太，是亲戚吗？”

“嗯，沾点儿亲。”她绽开一个缓缓的、志在必得的笑。“她是我——姨妈。对。兴许您瞧出来我们家的相像之处了？”她用来向约翰·弗赖进攻的那个笑容，越咧越大。“要是没有我的信，”她接着说，“我想买张邮票。一张一分的邮票。”

===Original===

Turning to his little metal box on the shelf behind, John Frey tore a single green stamp from the sheet. His hand shook still more as he gave it to the customer and received in exchange a copper cent.

There was really nothing exceptional about the appearance of that copper cent. It looked brown and a little worn, with Lincoln’s head on it, and a date﻿—1917. But John Frey felt a sudden glow in the hand that took it, a glow that shot along his arm and into his heart. He gazed at the coin as if he had never seen its like before. And he put it slowly into his pocket, a different pocket from the one in which he usually kept change, and placed another coin in the till to pay for the stamp. Poor Ivy Hill’s blue eyes grew round and downright miserable. Plainly he meant to keep that copper piece as a souvenir. But John Frey gazed only at the stranger, raptly, as though he were suddenly stunned or hypnotized.

The dark, sullen beauty drew her cloak more tightly around her, and moved regally out of the store and away toward the edge of town.

===Chinese===

约翰·弗赖转过身，从身后架子上的小铁盒里，从整版邮票上撕下一张绿色的一分票。递给顾客、接过一枚铜板的时候，他的手抖得更厉害了。

那枚铜板的样子实在没什么特别。棕褐色，略有磨损，一面是林肯头像，还有一个年份——1917。可约翰·弗赖接它的那只手蓦地一阵发热，那股热顺着手臂一路窜进心口。他盯着这枚铜板，仿佛生平头一回见到这等物件。他慢吞吞把它放进衣袋——放进另一个衣袋，不是他平日装零钱的那只——又另拿一枚硬币放进钱匣，抵那邮票的钱。可怜的艾薇·希尔一双蓝眼睛越睁越圆，委屈得要命。明摆着，他要把这枚铜板当纪念品留下了。而约翰·弗赖的眼睛只顾盯着那陌生女子，如痴如醉，像是蓦地被砸懵了，或是被催眠了。

那位阴郁的黑衣美人把斗篷裹紧了些，雍容地走出店门，朝镇子边上去了。

===Original===

As she turned up the brush-hidden trail to the hollow, a change came. Not that her step was less young and free, her figure less queenly, her eyes dimmer or her beauty short of perfect. All these were as they had been; but her expression became set and grim, her body tense and her head high and truculent. It was as though, beneath that young loveliness, lurked an old and evil heart﻿—which was precisely what did lurk there, it does not boot to conceal. But none saw except Gib, the black cat with soot-covered white spots, who sat on the doorstep of the ugly cottage. Jael Bettiss thrust him aside with her foot and entered.

In the kitchen she filled a tin basin from a wooden bucket, and threw into the water a pinch of coarse green powder with an unpleasant smell. As she stirred it in with her hands, they seemed to grow skinny and harsh. Then she threw great palmfuls of the liquid into her face and over her head, and other changes came.﻿ ﻿…

The woman who returned to the front door, where Gib watched with a cat’s apprehensive interest, was hideous old Jael Bettiss, whom all the village knew and avoided.

===Chinese===

她一拐上那条灌木掩映、通往山坳的小径，变化就来了。倒不是脚步不如方才轻盈自在，身段不如方才高贵，眼睛黯了，容貌差了——这些都跟先前一样；变的是她的神情，变得僵硬狠戾，浑身绷紧，头颅高高昂起，一副寻衅的架势。仿佛那年轻的丽质底下，潜伏着一颗老而邪恶的心——那正是潜伏在那儿的东西，瞒也无益。只是没人瞧见，除了吉布——那只白斑上涂满烟灰的黑猫，正蹲在那丑屋子门前的台阶上。杰尔·贝蒂斯一脚把他拨开，走了进去。

在厨房里，她从木桶里打水盛满一只白铁盆，往水里撒了一撮气味难闻的粗绿药粉。她双手在水里搅动，那双手眼看着变得枯瘦粗糙。接着她掬起大捧大捧的水，泼向自己的脸和头，别的变化也随之而来……

回到前门口的那个女人——吉布正怀着猫特有的惴惴兴味瞧着——正是全村人人认识、人人躲着的丑老婆子杰尔·贝蒂斯。

===Original===

“He’s trapped,” she shrilled triumphantly. “That penny, the one I soaked for three hours in a love-philtre, trapped him the moment he touched it!” She stumped to the table, and patted the book as though it were a living, lovable thing.

“You taught me,” she crooned to it. “You’re winning me the love of John Frey!” She paused, and her voice grew harsh again. “Why not? I’m old and ugly and queer, but I can love, and John Frey is the handsomest man in the village!”

===Chinese===

“他上钩了，”她得意地尖声嚷。“那枚铜板——那枚我在爱情魔药里泡了三个钟头的铜板——他一碰就给套牢了！”她咚咚地走到桌边，拍拍那本书，好像那是个活生生、招人疼的物件。

“是您教我的，”她对着书柔声哼唱。“您正在替我赢得约翰·弗赖的爱！”她顿了顿，嗓音又凶起来。“有什么不该？我又老又丑又古怪，可我也能爱，而约翰·弗赖是全村最俊的男人！”

---

===Original===

The next day she went to the store again, in her new and dazzling person as a dark, beautiful girl. Gib, left alone in the hollow, turned over in his mind the things that he had heard. The new gift of human speech had brought with it, of necessity, a human quality of reasoning; but his viewpoint and his logic were as strongly feline as ever.

Jael Bettiss’ dark love that lured John Frey promised no good to Gib. There would be plenty of trouble, he was inclined to think, and trouble was something that all sensible cats avoided. He was wise now, but he was weak. What could he do against danger? And his desires, as they had been since kittenhood, were food and warmth and a cozy sleeping-place, and a little respectful affection. Just now he was getting none of the four.

He thought also of Ivy Hill. She liked Gib, and often had shown it. If she won John Frey despite the witch’s plan, the two would build a house all full of creature comforts﻿—cushions, open fires, probably fish and chopped liver. Gib’s tongue caressed his soot-stained lips at the savory thought. It would be good to have a home with Ivy Hill and John Frey, if once he was quit of Jael Bettiss.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

第二天，她又以黑发美人的那副崭新炫目身姿去了店里。吉布独自留在山坳，把听到的事情在心里翻来覆去地掂量。新得的人语这份天赋，势必也带来了人的推理能力；可他的立场和逻辑，一如既往，全是猫的立场、猫的逻辑。

杰尔·贝蒂斯那勾住约翰·弗赖的阴暗爱情，对吉布没什么好兆头。多半要出乱子，他这么琢磨，而乱子这种东西，凡明白事理的猫一概躲着走。他如今有了智慧，可还是弱小。遇上危险，他能怎么样？再说他的心愿，从猫崽时候起就没变过：吃的，暖和，一处安适的睡窝，外加一点点受人敬重的疼爱。这四样，眼下他一样也没沾着。

他也想到艾薇·希尔。她喜欢吉布，还常常表露出来。要是她能不理会女巫的算计，赢回约翰·弗赖，两个人就会起一座房子，里头满满当当全是安适享受——软垫、敞亮的炉火，多半还有鱼和剁碎的肝脏。想到这般美味，吉布的舌头把熏黑的嘴唇舔了又舔。只要能甩脱杰尔·贝蒂斯，跟艾薇·希尔和约翰·弗赖一起有个家，倒真不赖……

===Original===

But he put the thought from him. The witch had never held his love and loyalty. That went to the house in the hollow, his home since the month that he was born. Even magic had not taught him how to be rid of that cat-instinctive obsession for his own proper dwelling-place. The sinister, strife-sodden hovel would always call and claim him, would draw him back from the warmest fire, the softest bed, the most savory food in the world. Only John Howard Payne could have appreciated Gib’s yearnings to the full, and he died long ago, in exile from the home he loved.

===Chinese===

可他把这个念头撵开了。女巫从来不曾占有过他的爱与忠心。那些都属于山坳里的房子，他从出生那个月起的家。连魔法也没教会他，怎样摆脱那种猫类与生俱来的、对自己真正居所的痴念。那座阴森、浸透争斗的破屋，永远会召唤他、索要他，会把他从世上最暖的炉火、最软的床铺、最香的食物跟前拽回去。这份眷恋，怕只有约翰·霍华德·佩恩（John Howard Payne）能体会个十足，而他已经死了很久了，死在他所爱的家以外的流亡之中。

===Original===

When Jael Bettiss returned, she was in a fine trembling rage. Her real self shone through the glamor of her disguise, like murky fire through a thin porcelain screen.

Gib was on the doorstep again, and tried to dodge away as she came up, but her enchantments, or something else, had made Jael Bettiss too quick even for a cat. She darted out a hand and caught him by the scruff of the neck.

“Listen to me,” she said, in a voice as deadly as the trickle of poisoned water. “You understand human words. You can talk, and you can hear what I say. You can do what I say, too.” She shook him, by way of emphasis. “Can’t you do what I say?”

“Yes,” said Gib weakly, convulsed with fear.

===Chinese===

杰尔·贝蒂斯回来时，正气得浑身乱颤。她的真身从幻化的艳光里透出来，像薄瓷屏后透出的浑浊火光。

吉布又蹲在门阶上，见她走近便想溜开，可她的法术也好，别的什么也好，把杰尔·贝蒂斯弄得比猫还迅捷。她一把探出手，揪住了他的后颈皮。

“听我说，”她开口，嗓音像毒水滴漏一样，阴狠致命。“你听得懂人话。你会说话，也听得见我说什么。我叫你做什么，你就得做什么。”她把他晃了晃，算是强调。“我叫你做什么，你做不做？”

“做。”吉布有气无力地说，吓得浑身抽紧。

===Original===

“All right, I have a job for you. And mind you do it well, or else﻿—” She broke off and shook him again, letting him imagine what would happen if he disobeyed.

“Yes,” said Gib again, panting for breath in her tight grip. “What’s it about?”

“It’s about that little fool. Ivy Hill. She’s not quite out of his heart.﻿ ﻿… Go to the village tonight,” ordered Jael Bettiss, “and to the house of the marshal. Steal something that belongs to Ivy Hill.”

“Steal something?”

“Don’t echo me, as if you were a silly parrot.” She let go of him, and hurried back to the book that was her constant study. “Bring me something that Ivy Hill owns and touches﻿—and be back here with it before dawn.”

===Chinese===

“好，我给你派个活儿。你给我办得妥妥当当的，要不然——”她顿住话头，又晃了晃他，由着他自己去想象不听话的下场。

“好。”吉布又说了一遍，被她攥得直喘。“什么活儿？”

“是那个小傻丫头的事。艾薇·希尔。她还没有完全从他心里头出去……你今儿夜里进村去，”杰尔·贝蒂斯吩咐道，“到治安官家去。偷一件艾薇·希尔的东西。”

“偷东西？”

“别学舌，跟只傻鹦鹉似的。”她撒开手，急忙回到那本让她手不释卷的书跟前。“给我拿一件艾薇·希尔所有、艾薇·希尔摸过的东西来——天亮前带回来。”

===Original===

Gib carried out her orders. Shortly after sundown he crept through the deepened dusk to the home of Marshal Hill. Doubly black with the soot habitually smeared upon him by Jael Bettiss, he would have been almost invisible, even had anyone been on guard against his coming. But nobody watched; the genial old man sat on the front steps, talking to his daughter.

“Say,” the father teased, “isn’t young Johnny Frey coming over here tonight, as usual?”

“I don’t know, daddy,” said Ivy Hill wretchedly.

“What’s that daughter?” The marshal sounded surprised. “Is there anything gone wrong between you two young ’uns?”

===Chinese===

吉布照办了。日头刚落，他便趁着渐浓的暮色，一路潜行到治安官希尔家的宅子跟前。杰尔·贝蒂斯平素给他抹的那层烟灰使他黑上加黑，就算真有人提防着他来，他也几乎瞧不见。可根本没人守着；和气的老先生坐在门前台阶上，正跟女儿说话。

“我说，”当爹的打趣道，“小约翰尼·弗赖今儿晚上还照常过来不？”

“不知道，爸爸。”艾薇·希尔可怜巴巴地说。

“怎么啦，闺女？”治安官听出一惊。“你们两个小年轻闹别扭了？”

===Original===

“Perhaps not, but﻿—oh, daddy, there’s a new girl come to town﻿—”

And Ivy Hill burst into tears, groping dolefully on the step beside her for her little wadded handkerchief. But she could not find it.

For Gib, stealing near, had caught it up in his mouth and was scampering away toward the edge of town, and beyond to the house in the hollow.

===Chinese===

“也许没有，可是——唉，爸爸，镇上来了位新姑娘——”

话没说完，艾薇·希尔放声大哭，凄凄惶惶地在身旁台阶上摸索她那块小小的软布手帕。可她摸不着。

因为吉布已经蹑着脚凑近，一口叼住那手帕，撒腿朝镇子边上跑，一直跑回山坳里的那座房子。

---

===Original===

Meanwhile, Jael Bettiss worked hard at a certain project of wax-modeling. Any witch, or student of witchcraft, would have known at once why she did this.

After several tries, she achieved something quite interesting and even clever﻿—a little female figure, that actually resembled Ivy Hill.

Jael Bettiss used the wax of three candles to give it enough substance and proportion. To make it more realistic, she got some fresh, pale-gold hemp, and of this made hair, like the wig of a blond doll, for the wax head. Drops of blue ink served for eyes, and a blob of berry-juice for the red mouth. All the while she worked, Jael Bettiss was muttering and mumbling words and phrases she had gleaned from the rearward pages of her book.

===Chinese===

与此同时，杰尔·贝蒂斯正埋头苦干一桩蜡模手艺。随便哪个女巫，或者研究巫术的人，一眼就能看出她干这个的用意。

试了好几回，她做出一件颇为有趣、甚至称得上灵巧的东西——一具小小的女人蜡像，当真有几分像艾薇·希尔。

杰尔·贝蒂斯用三支蜡烛的蜡，给它凑足了身量与比例。为求逼真，她弄来些新鲜的淡金色麻丝，替蜡脑袋做成头发，像金发娃娃的假发。几滴蓝墨水权当眼睛，一粒浆果汁当红嘴巴。干活的当儿，她嘴里一直念念有词，嘟哝着从书后半本里捡来的字句。

===Original===

When Gib brought in the handkerchief, Jael Bettiss snatched it from his mouth, with a grunt by way of thanks. With rusty scissors and coarse white thread, she fashioned for the wax figure a little dress. It happened that the handkerchief was of gingham, and so the garment made all the more striking the puppet’s resemblance to Ivy Hill.

“You’re a fine one!” tittered the witch, propping her finished figure against the lamp. “You’d better be scared!”

For it happened that she had worked into the waxen face an expression of terror. The blue ink of the eyes made wide round blotches, a stare of agonized fear; and the berry-juice mouth seemed to tremble, to plead shakily for mercy.

===Chinese===

吉布叼着手帕进屋，杰尔·贝蒂斯一把从他嘴里夺过去，哼了一声算是道谢。她用生锈的剪刀和粗白线，给蜡像缝了一条小裙子。赶巧那手帕是格纹布的，这条裙子上身，小蜡人越发像足了艾薇·希尔。

“你真俊呐！”女巫吃吃地笑，把完工的蜡像往灯边一靠。“你还是怕一怕的好！”

原来她恰好在那张蜡脸上捏出了一副惊骇的表情。蓝墨水的眼睛成了两个又圆又大的墨斑，瞪出一脸痛极的恐惧；浆果汁的嘴巴仿佛在哆嗦，摇摇晃晃地哀告求饶。

===Original===

Again Jael Bettiss refreshed her memory of goetic spells by poring over the back of the book, and after that she dug from the bottom of an old pasteboard box a handful of rusty pins. She chuckled over them, so that one would think triumph already hers. Laying the puppet on its back, so that the lamplight fell full upon it, she began to recite a spell.

“I have made my wish before,” she said in measured tones. “I will make it now. And there was never a day that I did not see my wish fulfilled.” Simple, vague﻿—but how many have died because those words were spoken in a certain way over images of them?

The witch thrust a pin into the breast of the little wax figure, and drove it all the way in, with a murderous pressure of her thumb. Another pin she pushed into the head, another into an arm, another into a leg; and so on, until the gingham-clad puppet was fairly studded with transfixing pins.

“Now,” she said, “we shall see what we shall see.”

===Chinese===

杰尔·贝蒂斯又埋头把书的后半部翻了一通，把那些召魔邪咒（goetic spells）温习了一遍，随后从一只旧纸盒底翻出一把生锈的大头针。她对着针咯咯直乐，瞧那架势，倒像大功已经告成。她让蜡像仰面躺下，好教灯光正照在它身上，开始念一道咒语。

“我的心愿从前许下过，”她一字一顿地说。“如今再许一回。没有一天，我不曾见我的心愿应验。”话虽简单，意思虽含糊——可曾有多少人，只因这几句话被人以某种方式对着他们的塑像念出来，就丢了性命？

女巫把一枚大头针扎进小蜡像的胸口，大拇指带着杀气一按，直没至底。又一枚扎进脑袋，一枚扎进胳膊，一枚扎进腿；如此这般，直到那穿格纹裙的小蜡人周身钉满了穿透的大头针。

“好了，”她说，“咱们就等着看个分明吧。”

===Original===

Morning dawned, as clear and golden as though wickedness had never been born into the world. The mysterious new paragon of beauty﻿—not a young man of the village but mooned over her, even though she was the reputed niece and namesake of that unsavory old vagabond, Jael Bettiss﻿—walked into the general store to make purchases. One delicate pink ear turned to the gossip of the housewives.

Wasn’t it awful, they were agreeing, how poor little Ivy Hill was suddenly sick almost to death﻿—she didn’t seem to know her father or her friends. Not even Doctor Melcher could find out what was the matter with her. Strange that John Frey was not interested in her troubles; but John Frey sat behind the counter, slumped on his stool like a mud idol, and his eyes lighted up only when they spied lovely young Jael Bettiss with her market basket.

When she had heard enough, the witch left the store and went straight to the town marshal’s house. There she spoke gravely and sorrowfully about how she feared for the sick girl, and was allowed to visit Ivy Hill in her bedroom. To the father and the doctor, it seemed that the patient grew stronger and felt less pain while Jael Bettiss remained to wish her a quick recovery; but, not long after this new acquaintance departed. Ivy Hill grew worse. She fainted, and recovered only to vomit.

And she vomited﻿—pins, rusty pins. Something like that happened in old Salem Village, and earlier still in Scotland, before the grisly cult of North Berwick was literally burned out. But Doctor Melcher, a more modern scholar, had never seen or heard of anything remotely resembling Ivy Hill’s disorder.

===Chinese===

清晨来了，澄澈金黄，仿佛世间从来不曾生过邪恶。那位神秘的新科绝色美人儿——村里的年轻人个个为她害相思，哪怕人家都说，她是那个名声不堪的老流浪婆子杰尔·贝蒂斯的外甥女，还承了她姨的名字——走进杂货店采买。一只粉嫩纤巧的耳朵，转向了主妇们的闲话。

可不是邪门嘛，主妇们正议论纷纷：可怜的艾薇·希尔怎么突然病得快死了——好像连她爹、她的朋友都认不得了。连梅尔彻医生（Doctor Melcher）都查不出她得了什么病。更怪的是，约翰·弗赖竟不把她放在心上；约翰·弗赖坐在柜台后头，蔫在凳子上活像一尊泥胎偶像，只有瞧见可爱的年轻杰尔·贝蒂斯挎着菜篮子进来，眼里才放光。

闲话听得够多了，女巫离开杂货店，径直去了镇治安官家。她言辞恳切哀戚，念叨着放心不下病中的姑娘，获准到卧室探望艾薇·希尔。当着父亲和医生的面，杰尔·贝蒂斯留下祝她早日康复的当儿，病人似乎精神好了些、疼也轻了些；可这位新交一走，艾薇·希尔便又沉了下去。她晕了过去，醒过来只管呕吐。

她吐出来的是——大头针，生锈的大头针。这般事情，旧时的塞勒姆村（Salem Village）出过，更早还在苏格兰出过，在北贝里克（North Berwick）那伙骇人邪教被一把火烧绝之前。可梅尔彻医生是更新派的学者，从没见过、也没听说过有哪样病，跟艾薇·希尔这怪症有半分相像。

===Original===

So it went, for three full days. Gib, too, heard the doleful gossip as he slunk around the village to hunt for food and to avoid Jael Bettiss, who did not like him near when she did magic. Ivy Hill was dying, and he mourned her, as for the boons of fish and fire and cushions and petting that might have been his. He knew, too, that he was responsible for her doom and his loss﻿—that handkerchief that he had stolen had helped Jael Bettiss to direct her spells.

But philosophy came again to his aid. If Ivy Hill died, she died. Anyway, he had never been given the chance to live as her pensioner and pet. He was not even sure that he would have taken the chance﻿—thinking of it, he felt strong, accustomed clamps upon his heart. The house in the hollow was his home forever. Elsewhere he’d be an exile.

Nothing would ever root it out of his feline soul.

===Chinese===

就这样挨了整整三天。吉布在村里东躲西藏地找食吃、躲着杰尔·贝蒂斯——她做法的时候不喜欢他待在近旁——也听够了这些凄惨的闲话。艾薇·希尔眼看要死了，他为她难过，也为那些本可能归他的好处难过：鱼、炉火、软垫、爱抚。他还知道，她的死和他失去这一切，都得算他一份责任——他偷来的那块手帕，帮着杰尔·贝蒂斯对准了她的咒语。

可哲学又来给他解围了。艾薇·希尔要死，便由她死。反正他从没捞着机会做她的食客和宠儿。他甚至拿不准，自己会不会要那个机会——一想到这个，他就觉得心头给几道熟悉的铁箍紧紧箍住。山坳里的房子才是他永久的家。去了别处，他就是流亡。

什么也休想把这念想，从他猫的心魂里刨出去。

---

===Original===

On the evening of the third day, witch and cat faced each other across the tabletop in the old house in the hollow.

“They’ve talked loud enough to make his dull ears hear,” grumbled the fearful old woman﻿—with none but Gib to see her, she had washed away the disguising enchantment that, though so full of lure, seemed to be a burden upon her. “John Frey has agreed to take Ivy Hill out in his automobile. The doctor thinks that the fresh air, and John Frey’s company, will make her feel better﻿—but it won’t. It’s too late. She’ll never return from that drive.”

She took up the pin-pierced wax image of her rival, rose and started toward the kitchen.

===Chinese===

第三天晚上，山坳旧屋里，女巫和猫隔着桌面相对。

“他们把话嚷得够响了，响得他那对木头耳朵都听见了，”那骇人的老太婆咕哝道——眼前除了吉布没第二个观众，她已把那副幻化艳妆洗去了；那幻妆纵然魅惑十足，瞧着却像压在她身上的一副担子。“约翰·弗赖答应了，开车带艾薇·希尔出去兜风。医生觉得，新鲜空气，再加上约翰·弗赖作伴，能让她好受些——好受不了。太迟了。她这一趟兜风，去了就回不来。”

她拿起那具钉满大头针的情敌蜡像，站起身朝厨房走去。

===Original===

“What are you going to do?” Gib forced himself to ask.

“Do?” repeated Jael Bettiss, smiling murderously. “I’m going to put an end to that baby-faced chit﻿—but why are you so curious? Get out, with your prying!”

And, snarling curses and striking with her claw-like hands, she made him spring down from his chair and run out of the house. The door slammed, and he crouched in some brambles and watched. No sound, and at the half-blinded windows no movement; but, after a time, smoke began to coil upward from the chimney. Its first puffs were dark and greasy-looking. Then it turned dull gray, then white, then blue as indigo. Finally it vanished altogether.

===Chinese===

“你要干什么？”吉布逼着自己问。

“干什么？”杰尔·贝蒂斯重复了一遍，笑里带着杀气。“我去结果了那个娃娃脸的小丫头——你打听得这么细干什么？滚，少管闲事！”

她一边骂骂咧咧，一边挥动那双爪子似的手，逼得他从椅子上窜下来，逃出屋子。门砰地关上，他蹲进荆棘丛里张望。没有声音，瞎了一半的窗后也不见动静；只是过了一阵，烟囱里升起烟来，盘旋而上。头几股又黑又油腻，随后转成死灰，转成白，转成靛蓝。末了，烟干脆没了。

===Original===

When Jael Bettiss opened the door and came out, she was once more in the semblance of a beautiful dark girl. Yet Gib recognized a greater terror about her than ever before.

“You be gone from here when I get back,” she said to him.

“Gone?” stammered Gib, his little heart turning cold. “What do you mean?”

She stooped above him, like a threatening bird of prey.

“You be gone,” she repeated. “If I ever see you again. I’ll kill you﻿—or I’ll make my new husband kill you.”

He still could not believe her. He shrank back, and his eyes turned mournfully to the old house that was the only thing he loved.

===Chinese===

杰尔·贝蒂斯开门出来时，又是一副黑发美人的模样。可吉布认得出，她身上透着一股比以往更甚的凶焰。

“等我回来的时候，你给我滚得远远的，”她对他说。

“滚？”吉布结结巴巴，小小的心凉了半截。“你是什么意思？”

她朝他俯下身来，像一只临头威胁的猛禽。

“你给我滚，”她重复道。“我要是再瞧见你，就杀了你——要不，就叫我未来的新丈夫来杀你。”

他还是不敢信她这话。他往后缩，眼睛哀哀地望向那座旧屋——他唯一爱的东西。

===Original===

“You’re the only witness to the things I’ve done,” Jael Bettiss continued. “Nobody would believe their ears if a cat started telling tales, but anyway, I don’t want any trace of you around. If you leave, they’ll forget that I used to be a witch. So run!”

She turned away. Her mutterings were now only her thoughts aloud:

“If my magic works﻿—and it always works﻿—that car will find itself idling around through the hill road to the other side of the quarry. John Frey will stop there. And so will Ivy Hill﻿—forever.”

Drawing her cloak around her, she stalked purposefully toward the old quarry behind the house.

===Chinese===

“你是我那些事儿的唯一见证，”杰尔·贝蒂斯接着说。“一只猫要是开口讲古，没人会信自己的耳朵。不过不管怎样，我不想让你在我跟前留下一点踪迹。你要是走了，他们就会忘了我从前当过女巫。所以，跑吧！”

她转过身去。此刻她的喃喃自语，不过是心里想的说出了声：

“只要我的法术灵——它从来不失灵——那辆车自会鬼使神差，顺着山道慢悠悠绕到采石场另一头。约翰·弗赖会在那儿停车。艾薇·希尔也会——永远停在那儿。”

她裹紧斗篷，大步流星，直奔屋后的老采石场而去。

---

===Original===

Left by himself, Gib lowered his lids and let his yellow eyes grow dim and deep with thought. His shrewd beast’s mind pawed and probed at this final wonder and danger that faced him and John Frey and Ivy Hill.

He must run away if he would live. The witch’s house in the hollow, that had never welcomed him, now threatened him. No more basking on the doorstep, no more ambushing wood-mice among the brambles, no more dozing by the kitchen fire. Nothing for Gib henceforth but strange, forbidding wilderness, and scavenger’s food, and no shelter, not on the coldest night. The village? But his only two friends, John Frey and Ivy Hill, were being taken from him by the magic of Jael Bettiss and her book.﻿ ﻿…

That book had done this. That book must undo it. There was no time to lose.

The door was not quite latched, and he nosed it open, despite the groans of its hinges. Hurrying in, he sprang up on the table.

===Chinese===

只剩下自己一个，吉布垂下眼帘，让那双黄眼睛随沉思变得幽深。他那颗精明的兽类头脑，一遍遍拨弄、探问着眼前这桩最后的奇事与凶险——它悬在他、约翰·弗赖和艾薇·希尔三个的头顶上。

想活命就得逃。女巫在山坳里的这座房子，从来没给过他半点温暖，如今更要他的命了。再不能趴在门阶上晒太阳，再不能在荆棘丛里伏击林鼠，再不能偎着厨房的炉火打盹。往后等着吉布的，只有陌生凶险的荒野，只有野猫拾荒的残羹，没有遮蔽，再冷的夜里也没有。村子呢？可他仅有的两个朋友，约翰·弗赖和艾薇·希尔，正被杰尔·贝蒂斯和她那本书的法术，从他身边夺走……

是那本书造的孽。就得那本书来解。一刻也耽误不得。

门没有闩死，他不顾合页的呻吟抗议，用鼻子拱开了它。他一溜烟进去，蹿上桌子。

===Original===

It was gloomy in that tree-invested house, even for Gib’s sharp eyes. Therefore, in a trembling fear almost too big for his little body, he spoke a word that Jael Bettiss had spoken, on her first night of power. As had happened then, so it happened now; the dark lamp glowed alight.

Gib pawed at the closed book, and contrived to lift its cover. Pressing it open with one front foot, with the other he painstakingly turned leaves, more leaves, and more yet. Finally he came to the page he wanted.

Not that he could read; and, in any case, the characters were strange in their shapes and combinations. Yet, if one looked long enough and levelly enough﻿—even though one were a cat, and afraid﻿—they made sense, conveyed intelligence.

And so into the mind of Gib, beating down his fears, there stole a phrase:

Beware of mirrors.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

那座掩在树影里的屋子黑黢黢的，纵有吉布的利眼也吃力。于是，他怀着一份几乎要把小身子撑破的战栗，念出一个词——杰尔·贝蒂斯在她得法力的头一夜念过的那个词。当年怎样，眼下便怎样：黑黢黢的灯亮了起来。

吉布扒拉那本合拢的书，设法掀开封面。一只前爪按住摊开的书页，另一只费劲地翻，一页，又一页，再一页。终于翻到了他要的那一页。

倒不是他识字；何况那些字符，字形与拼法都古怪。然而，只要盯得够久、够稳——哪怕盯的是只猫，还是只吓坏了的猫——它们便显出条理，传出消息来。

就这样，压下满心恐惧，一句话潜入了吉布的头脑：

当心镜子。……

===Original===

So that was why Jael Bettiss never kept a mirror﻿—not even now, when she could assume such dazzling beauty.

Beware of mirrors, the book said to Gib, for they declare the truth, and truth is fatal to sorcery. Beware, also, of crosses, which defeat all spells.﻿ ﻿…

That was definite inspiration. He moved back from the book, and let it snap shut. Then, pushing with head and paws, he coaxed it to the edge of the table and let it fall. Jumping down after it, he caught a corner of the book in his teeth and dragged it to the door, more like a retriever than a cat. When he got it into the yard, into a place where the earth was soft, he dug furiously until he had made a hole big enough to contain the volume. Then, thrusting it in, he covered it up.

===Chinese===

原来杰尔·贝蒂斯从不备镜子，是为了这个——哪怕如今她能幻出那样耀眼的美貌，也不备。

当心镜子，书对吉布说，因为镜子道出真相，而真相于巫术是致命的。还要当心十字，十字能破一切咒语。……

这可是确凿的启示。他从书跟前退开，任书页啪地合上。接着他脑袋爪子一齐上，把书一点点拱到桌沿，任它摔下去。他跟着跳下去，一口咬住书角，把它拖向门口——那架势不像猫，倒像条叼物的猎犬。到了院里一块软土上，他发疯似的刨起来，刨出一个容得下这本书的坑。然后把它塞进去，埋上。

===Original===

Nor was that all his effort, so far as the book was concerned. He trotted a little way off to where lay some dry, tough twigs under the cypress trees. To the little grave he bore first one, then another of these, and laid them across each other, in the form of an X. He pressed them well into the earth, so that they would be hard to disturb. Perhaps he would keep an eye on that spot henceforth, after he had done the rest of the things in his mind, to see that the cross remained. And, though he acted thus only by chance reasoning, all the demonologists, even the Reverend Montague Summers, would have nodded approval. Is this not the way to foil the black wisdom of the *Grand Albert*? Did not Prospero thus inter his grimoires, in the fifth act of *The Tempest*?

Now back to the house once more, and into the kitchen. It was even darker than the parlor, but Gib could make out a basin on a stool by the moldy wall, and smelled an ugly pungency﻿—Jael Bettiss had left her mixture of powdered water after last washing away her burden of false beauty.

Gib’s feline nature rebelled at a wetting; his experience of witchcraft bade him be wary, but he rose on his hind legs and with his forepaws dragged at the basin’s edge. It tipped and toppled. The noisome fluid drenched him. Wheeling, he ran back into the parlor, but paused on the doorstep. He spoke two more words that he remembered from Jael Bettiss. The lamp went out again.

And now he dashed around the house and through the brambles and to the quarry beyond.

===Chinese===

说到这本书，他的工夫还不止这些。他小跑几步，到柏树底下衔来些又干又韧的细枝，一趟一趟衔回那个小坟上，一根根横着搭起来，搭成一个 X 形的十字。他把细枝牢牢压进土里，教人轻易动不得。往后等他办完心里剩下的那些事，他还要常来照看这个地方，瞧着那十字还在不在。别看他这全是瞎打误撞式的推理，天下的恶魔学家——连蒙塔古·萨默斯牧师（Reverend Montague Summers）算在内——都得点头称许。对付《大阿尔伯特》（Grand Albert）那门黑学问，不正是这个法子吗？普洛斯彼罗（Prospero）当年安葬他的魔典，不也正是这般光景——就在《暴风雨》（The Tempest）第五幕里？

现在再回屋，进厨房。厨房比堂屋还黑，可吉布辨认得出发霉墙边凳子上那只盆，还闻见一股呛人的怪味——杰尔·贝蒂斯上回洗去那副假美貌的担子后，把那盆药粉水剩下了。

吉布的猫性死活不肯沾水；他的巫术见闻又劝他小心。可他后腿立起，前爪扳住盆沿。盆一歪，翻了。那股恶臭的液体把他浇了个透。他一转身窜回堂屋，却在门槛上停住，又念了两个词——也是从杰尔·贝蒂斯嘴里记下的。灯又灭了。

接着他绕过屋子，钻出荆棘丛，直奔后面的采石场。

===Original===

It lay amid uninhabited wooded hills, a wide excavation from which had once been quarried all the stones for the village houses and pavements. Now it was full of water, from many thaws and torrents.
