Almost at its lip was parked John Frey’s touring-car, with the top down, and beside it he lolled, slack-faced and dreamy. At his side, cloak-draped and enigmatically queenly, was Jael Bettiss, her back to the quarry, never more terrible or handsome. John Frey’s eyes were fixed dreamily upon her, and her eyes were fixed commandingly on the figure in the front seat of the car﻿—a slumped, defeated figure, hard to recognize as poor sick Ivy Hill.

“Can you think of no way to end all this pain. Miss Ivy?” the witch was asking. Though she did not stir, nor glance behind her, it was as though she had gestured toward the great quarry-pit, full to unknown depths with black, still water. The sun, at the very point of setting, made angry red lights on the surface of that stagnant pond.

“Go away,” sobbed Ivy Hill, afraid without knowing why. “Please, please!”

“I’m only trying to help,” said Jael Bettiss. “Isn’t that so, John?”

“That’s so, Ivy,” agreed John, like a little boy who is prompted to an unfamiliar recitation. “She’s only trying to help.”

Gib, moving silently as fate, crept to the back of the car. None of the three human beings, so intent upon each other, saw him.

===Chinese===

采石场卧在无人烟的林山之间，一片开阔的大坑，村里房屋和路面用的石头，早年都取自这里。如今它叫历年的融雪和山洪灌满了水。约翰·弗赖那辆游览车几乎停在大坑唇边，篷顶敞着；他倚在车旁，一脸松垮的痴相，恍恍惚惚。他身边站着裹斗篷的杰尔·贝蒂斯，王后般气度、莫测高深，背朝采石场，从没有这么美，也从没有这么可怕。约翰·弗赖痴痴地望着她，她的眼睛则威严地盯住车里前座上的身影——那身影瘫作一团、败军之将似的，几乎认不出就是病恹恹的可怜艾薇·希尔。

“你就想不出个法子，了结这一切苦楚吗，艾薇小姐？”女巫正问着。她身子一动没动，眼也不朝后瞟一下，却像朝那口大坑比划了一下似的——坑里灌满不知多深的死黑的水。太阳正卡在沉落的当口，给那汪死水敷上一层怒红的光。

“走开，”艾薇·希尔呜咽着说，不知为何怕得慌。“求求你，求求你！”

“我不过是想帮你，”杰尔·贝蒂斯说。“对不对，约翰？”

“是啊，艾薇，”约翰应声，活像个被人提词、背不上课文的小学生。“她不过是想帮你。”

吉布像命运一样悄没声地贴到车后。三个人彼此盯着彼此，谁也没瞧见他。

===Original===

“Get out of the car,” persisted Jael Bettiss. “Get out, and look into the water. You will forget your pain.”

“Yes, yes,” chimed in John Frey, mechanically. “You will forget your pain.”

===Chinese===

“下车来，”杰尔·贝蒂斯步步紧逼。“下来，看看这水。你会忘掉你的苦楚。”

“是啊，是啊，”约翰·弗赖机械地跟着说。“你会忘掉你的苦楚。”

---

===Original===

Gib scrambled stealthily to the running-board, then over the side of the car and into the rear seat. He found what he had hoped to find. Ivy Hill’s purse﻿—and open.

He pushed his nose into it. Tucked into a little side-pocket was a hard, flat rectangle, about the size and shape of a visiting-card. All normal girls carry mirrors in their purse﻿—all mirrors show the truth. Gib clamped the edge with his mouth, and struggled to drag the thing free.

“Miss Ivy,” Jael Bettiss was commanding, “get out of this car, and come and look into the water of the quarry.”

===Chinese===

吉布蹑手蹑脚爬上踏板，翻过车帮，钻进后座。他找到了他指望找到的东西：艾薇·希尔的手提包——开着口。

他把鼻子拱进包里。侧兜里插着一个扁硬的长方形，大小厚薄像张名片。寻常姑娘的提包里都装着镜子——镜子都照真相。吉布咬住那物件的边，使劲往外拖。

“艾薇小姐，”杰尔·贝蒂斯正发号施令，“下车来，到采石场这儿来，看看这水。”

===Original===

No doubt what would happen if once Ivy Hill should gaze into that shiny black abyss; but she bowed her head, in agreement or defeat, and began slowly to push aside the catch of the door.

Now or never, thought Gib. He made a little noise in his throat, and sprang up on the side of the car next to Jael Bettiss. His black-stained face and yellow eyes were not a foot from her.

She alone saw him; Ivy Hill was too sick, John Frey too dull. “What are you doing here?” she snarled, like a bigger and fiercer cat than he; but he moved closer still, holding up the oblong in his teeth. Its back was uppermost, covered with imitation leather, and hid the real nature of it. Jael Bettiss was mystified, for once in her relationship with Gib. She took the thing from him, turned it over, and saw a reflection.

She screamed.

The other two looked up, horrified through their stupor. The scream that Jael Bettiss uttered was not deep and rich and young; it was the wild, cracked cry of a terrified old woman.

===Chinese===

艾薇·希尔只要朝那片油亮的黑色深渊望上一眼，会有什么下场，不言自明；可她垂下头，不知是应承还是认栽，慢慢去拨车门的闩。

机不可失，吉布想。他喉咙里挤出一声细响，纵身跳上靠杰尔·贝蒂斯那一侧的车帮。那张染黑的脸和那双黄眼睛，离她不到一英尺。

只有她瞧见了他；艾薇·希尔病糊涂了，约翰·弗赖呆滞了。“你上这儿来干什么？”她低吼，像一只比他更大更凶的猫；可他凑得更近，嘴里高高举着那个长方物件。背面朝上，包着仿皮，看不出真身。杰尔·贝蒂斯跟吉布打交道，这还是头一回摸不着头脑。她接过那东西，翻过来，看见了一幅映像。

她尖叫起来。

另外两人从迷瞪里惊起，骇然抬头。杰尔·贝蒂斯发出的那声尖叫，不再是低沉、圆润、年轻；那是一个吓破了胆的老太婆狂乱嘶哑的嚎叫。

===Original===

“I don’t look like that,” she choked out, and drew back from the car. “Not old﻿—ugly﻿—”

Gib sprang at her face. With all four claw-bristling feet he seized and clung to her. Again Jael Bettiss screamed, flung up her hands, and tore him away from his hold; but his soggy fur had smeared the powdered water upon her face and head.

Though he fell to earth, Gib twisted in midair and landed upright. He had one glimpse of his enemy. Jael Bettiss, no mistake﻿—but a Jael Bettiss with hooked beak, rheumy eyes, hideous wry mouth and yellow chisel teeth﻿—Jael Bettiss exposed for what she was, stripped of her lying mask of beauty!

And she drew back a whole staggering step. Rocks were just behind her. Gib saw, and flung himself. Like a flash he clawed his way up her cloak, and with both forepaws ripped at the ugliness he had betrayed. He struck for his home that was forbidden him﻿—Marco Bozzaris never strove harder for Greece, nor Stonewall Jackson for Virginia.

===Chinese===

“我不是这副模样，”她哽着嗓子挤出一句，直往后缩。“不老——不丑——”

吉布朝她的脸扑去。四只爪子毛刺乍起，他攀住她死死不放。杰尔·贝蒂斯又是一声尖叫，双手乱挥，把他从自己身上扯了下来；可他湿透的毛，已把那药粉水抹上了她的脸和头。

吉布虽然跌向地面，却在半空拧过身子，四脚落地。他朝仇家望了一眼。没错，是杰尔·贝蒂斯——却是生着鹰钩喙、黏糊泪眼、狰狞歪嘴和凿子般黄牙的杰尔·贝蒂斯——原形毕露、那副撒谎的美貌假面被剥了个精光的杰尔·贝蒂斯！

她跌跌撞撞地整退了一步。她脚后就是乱石。吉布瞧见了，一纵身扑上去。他闪电般顺着她的斗篷攀上去，两只前爪一齐撕抓他亲手揭破的那张丑脸。他为他那被禁归的家而扑击——当年马尔科·博扎里斯（Marco Bozzaris）为希腊拼争，石墙杰克逊（Stonewall Jackson）为弗吉尼亚力战，也不比这更狠。

===Original===

Jael Bettiss screamed yet again, a scream loud and full of horror. Her feet had slipped on the edge of the abyss. She flung out her arms, the cloak flapped from them like frantic wings. She fell, and Gib fell with her, still tearing and fighting.

The waters of the quarry closed over them both.

===Chinese===

杰尔·贝蒂斯又一声尖叫，尖利而充满恐怖。她的脚已在深渊边上打了滑。她双臂乱张，斗篷在臂上扑打，像一对疯狂的翅膀。她坠了下去，吉布跟她一起坠下，还在又撕又打。

采石场的黑水，在他们两个头上合拢了。

---

===Original===

Gib thought that it was a long way back to the surface, and a longer way to shore. But he got there, and scrambled out with the help of projecting rocks. He shook his drenched body, climbed back into the car and sat upon the rear seat. At least Jael Bettiss would no longer drive him from the home he loved. He’d find food some way, and take it back there each day to eat.﻿ ﻿…

With tongue and paws he began to rearrange his sodden fur.

John Frey, clear-eyed and wide awake, was leaning in and talking to Ivy Hill. As for her, she sat up straight, as though she had never known a moment of sickness.

“But just what did happen?” she was asking.

===Chinese===

吉布觉得，游回水面是一段老长的路，游到岸边更长。可他到底游到了，攀着凸出的石头爬上了岸。他抖了抖透湿的身子，爬回车里，蹲坐在后座上。至少，杰尔·贝蒂斯再也不能把他从心爱的家里赶出去了。吃的他总归有法子弄到，每天叼回那儿去吃。……

他动起舌头和爪子，理起那身湿透的毛来。

约翰·弗赖目光清明、神完气足，正探身跟艾薇·希尔说话。她呢，坐得笔直，仿佛从没病过一天。

“可刚才到底出了什么事？”她正问。

===Original===

John Frey shook his head, though all the stupidity was gone from his face and manner. “I don’t quite remember. I seem to have wakened from a dream. But are you all right, darling?”

“Yes, I’m all right.” She gazed toward the quarry, and the black water that had already subsided above what it had swallowed. Her eyes were puzzled, but not frightened. “I was dreaming, too,” she said. “Let’s not bother about it.”

She lifted her gaze, and cried out with joy. “There’s that old house that daddy owns. Isn’t it interesting?”

John Frey looked, too. “Yes. The old witch has gone away﻿—I seem to have heard she did.”

Ivy Hill was smiling with excitement. “Then I have an inspiration. Let’s get daddy to give it to us. And we’ll paint it over and fix it up, and then﻿—” She broke off, with a cry of delight. “I declare, there’s a cat in the car with me!”

===Chinese===

约翰·弗赖摇头，脸上眼里那股呆气已一扫而空。“我记不大清。像是做了场梦刚醒。你没事吧，亲爱的？”

“嗯，我没事。”她朝采石场那边望去，黑水早已把吞下的东西盖得平平的。她眼里有困惑，却没有惧意。“我也在做梦来着，”她说。“咱们别去想它了。”

她抬起目光，欢叫出声。“那不是爸爸名下的老房子嘛。多有意思！”

约翰·弗赖也望过去。“是啊。那个老女巫走了——我像是听说她走了。”

艾薇·希尔兴奋得直笑。“那我可有了个主意。咱们求爸爸把它给了咱们。里里外外刷一遍，修整修整，然后——”她说不下去了，一声欢呼。“哎呀，你们看，车里还有只猫呢！”

===Original===

It was the first she had known of Gib’s presence.

John Frey stared at Gib. He seemed to have wakened only the moment before. “Yes, and isn’t he a thin one? But he’ll be pretty when he gets through cleaning himself. I think I see a white shirtfront.”

Ivy Hill put out a hand and scratched Gib behind the ear. “He’s bringing us good luck, I think. John, let’s take him to live with us when we have the house fixed up and move in.”

“Why not?” asked her lover. He was gazing at Gib. “He looks as if he was getting ready to speak.”

===Chinese===

这是她头一回察觉吉布在场。

约翰·弗赖盯着吉布瞧。他像是前一刻才睡醒。“可不，瞧这瘦劲儿！不过等它把自己拾掇干净，准漂亮。我好像瞧见它胸口有一片白，衬衫前襟似的。”

艾薇·希尔伸出手，在吉布耳后挠了挠。“我看它是要给咱们带来好运的。约翰，等房子修好搬进去，咱们带它一起住吧。”

“有何不可？”她的爱人反问。他正端详吉布。“它那样子，像是要开口说话了。”

===Original===

But Gib was not getting ready to speak. The power of speech was gone from him, along with Jael Bettiss and her enchantments. But he understood, in a measure, what was being said about him and the house in the hollow. There would be new life there, joyful and friendly this time. And he would be a part of it, forever, and of his loved home.

He could only purr to show his relief and gratitude.

===Chinese===

可吉布并不打算开口。说话的本事，已随着杰尔·贝蒂斯和她的法术一同去了。可人家说的关于他和山坳里那座房子的话，他大体听得明白。那儿将有一场新的生活，这一回，是快活而友善的。而他将成为其中的一份子，直到永远，也永远属于他心爱的家。

他只会呼噜呼噜地念，好表明他的宽心与感激。

## The Golgotha Dancers / 各各他舞者

===Original===

I had come to the Art Museum to see the special show of Goya prints, but that particular gallery was so crowded that I could hardly get in, much less see or savor anything; wherefore I walked out again. I wandered through the other wings with their rows and rows of oils, their Greek and Roman sculptures, their stern ranks of medieval armors, their Oriental porcelains, their Egyptian gods. At length, by chance and not by design, I came to the head of a certain rear stairway. Other habitués of the museum will know the one I mean when I remind them that Arnold Böcklin’s *The Isle of the Dead* hangs on the wall of the landing.

I started down, relishing in advance the impression Böcklin’s picture would make with its high brown rocks and black poplars, its midnight sky and gloomy film of sea, its single white figure erect in the bow of the beach-nosing skiff. But, as I descended, I saw that *The Isle of the Dead* was not in its accustomed position on the wall. In that space, arresting even in the bad light and from the up-angle of the stairs, hung a gilt-framed painting I had never seen or heard of in all my museum-haunting years.

I gazed at it, one will imagine, all the way down to the landing. Then I had a close, searching look, and a final appraising stare from the lip of the landing above the lower half of the flight. So far as I can learn﻿—and I have been diligent in my research﻿—the thing is unknown even to the best-informed of art experts. Perhaps it is as well that I describe it in detail.

===Chinese===

我到美术馆来，本是想看戈雅（Goya）版画特展，无奈那间展厅挤得水泄不通，我几乎挤不进去，更谈不上细看细品；于是我退了出来。我在别的展馆里信步闲逛，看过一排又一排的油画、希腊罗马的雕塑、一列列森然肃立的中世纪甲胄、东方的瓷器、埃及的神祇。末了，纯属偶然而非有意，我走到了一道后楼梯的顶端。常来美术馆的老主顾，只消我提醒一句——楼梯平台上挂着阿诺德·勃克林（Arnold Böcklin）的《死之岛》（The Isle of the Dead）——便知道我说的是哪一道了。

我顺梯而下，一路预先回味着勃克林那幅画给人的印象：高耸的褐色岩壁，黑色的杨树，午夜的天空，海面上阴郁的薄翳，还有那孤零零一身白衣的身影，笔直立在船头——小艇的船首正探向滩头。可是下到半途，我发现《死之岛》并不在墙上惯常的位置。那片空处挂着一幅装金框的画，光线昏暗，又隔着楼梯的仰角，它依然夺人眼目——我常年在美术馆里流连，这幅画却从未见过，也从未听说过。

可以想见，我一路下到平台，目光始终没有离开它。接着我凑近细细端详，最后又站在平台边沿、俯临下半截楼梯的地方，把它评判地盯了个够。据我所能查知的——而在查证上我素来勤勉——就连消息最灵通的艺术专家，也不知道有这么一件东西。那么，由我来把它详详细细描述一遍，或许倒是一件好事。

===Original===

It seemed to represent action upon a small plateau or table rock, drab and bare, with a twilight sky deepening into a starless evening. This setting, restrainedly worked up in blue-grays and blue-blacks, was not the first thing to catch the eye, however. The front of the picture was filled with lively dancing creatures, as pink, plump and naked as cherubs and as patently evil as the meditations of Satan in his rare idle moments.

I counted those dancers. There were twelve of them, ranged in a half-circle, and they were cavorting in evident glee around a central object﻿—a prone cross, which appeared to be made of two stout logs with some of the bark still upon them. To this cross a pair of the pink things﻿—that makes fourteen﻿—kneeling and swinging blocky-looking hammers or mauls, spiked a human figure.

I say “human” when I speak of that figure, and I withhold the word in describing the dancers and their hammer-wielding fellows. There is a reason. The supine victim on the cross was a beautifully represented male body, as clear and anatomically correct as an illustration in a surgical textbook. The head was writhed around, as if in pain, and I could not see the face or its expression; but in the tortured tenseness of the muscles, in the slaty white sheen of the skin with jagged streaks of vivid gore upon it, agonized nature was plain and doubly plain. I could almost see the painted limbs writhe against the transfixing nails.

By the same token, the dancers and hammerers were so dynamically done as to seem half in motion before my eyes. So much for the sound skill of the painter. Yet, where the crucified prisoner was all clarity, these others were all fog. No lines, no angles, no muscles﻿—their features could not be seen or sensed. I was not even sure if they had hair or not. It was as if each was picked out with a ray of light in that surrounding dusk, light that revealed and yet shimmered indistinctly; light, too, that had absolutely nothing of comfort or honesty in it.

===Chinese===

画面上似乎是一小片高台，或者叫桌状岩石上的情景：荒秃单调，暮色四合的天空正沉入一个无星的夜晚。这处背景以蓝灰与蓝黑的色调含蓄地铺陈出来，却不是最先攫住目光的东西。画面前方挤满了欢腾起舞的生灵，粉嫩、肥胖、赤条条，像小天使一般，又像撒旦难得闲下来的片刻所转的念头一般，露骨地邪恶。

我数了数那些舞者。一共十二个，排成半圈，围着正中一件东西欢蹦乱跳、乐不可支——那是一具平放的十字架，看去是用两根粗壮的圆木捆成的，木头上还留着些树皮。有两个粉嫩的生灵——这就凑成十四个了——跪在架旁，抡着样子笨重的锤子，或者叫木槌，正往架上钉一个人形。

说到那个人形，我用了一个“人”字；而形容那些舞者和抡锤的家伙时，我却不用这个字。这自有缘故。仰面钉在十字架上的受难者，是一具画得极精美的男人的躯体，清晰而合于解剖，像外科教科书里的插图一样一丝不差。头颅痛苦地扭向一旁，脸和脸上的表情我看不见；可是那备受煎熬、紧绷欲裂的肌肉，那泛着石板白光的皮肤、皮上一道道蜿蜒刺目的鲜血——痛苦万状的神态明明白白，再明白不过。我几乎能看见那画出来的四肢，在贯穿它们的铁钉上挣动。

同样地，那些舞者和抡锤者也画得动感十足，仿佛就在我眼前半动半停。画家的功力扎实，仅此可见一斑。然而，钉在十字架上的囚徒通体清晰，这些家伙却通体是雾。没有轮廓，没有棱角，没有肌肉——他们的五官眉目，看不见也辨不出。我甚至说不准他们有没有头发。仿佛在那四合的昏暝之中，每一个都被一束光挑亮；那光勾出了形体，却闪烁模糊；那光里也绝对没有半分温暖或诚实的成分。

---

===Original===

“Hold on, there!” came a sharp challenge from the stairs behind and below me. “What are you doing? And what’s that picture doing?”

I started so that I almost lost my footing and fell upon the speaker﻿—one of the Museum guards. He was a slight old fellow and his thin hair was gray, but he advanced upon me with all the righteous, angry pluck of a beefy policeman. His attitude surprised and nettled me.

“I was going to ask somebody that same question,” I told him as austerely as I could manage. “What about this picture? I thought there was a Böcklin hanging here.”

The guard relaxed his forbidding attitude at first sound of my voice. “Oh, I beg your pardon, sir. I thought you were somebody else﻿—the man who brought that thing.” He nodded at the picture, and the hostile glare came back into his eyes. “It so happened that he talked to me first, then to the curator. Said it was art﻿—great art﻿—and the Museum must have it.” He lifted his shoulders, in a shrug or a shudder. “Personally, I think it’s plain beastly.”

So it was, I grew aware as I looked at it again. “And the Museum has accepted it at last?” I prompted.

===Chinese===

“喂，站住！”我身后楼梯下方传来一声厉喝。“你在干什么？那幅画又在干什么？”

我吓了一跳，险些失足栽到说话人身上——他是美术馆的一名警卫。瘦小的老头儿，稀疏的头发已经花白，可他朝我逼近时那股正气冲冲的胆气，活像个膀大腰圆的警察。他这副架势叫我又是吃惊又是冒火。

“我正要找人问这句话呢，”我板起脸，尽量端着架子对他说。“这幅画是怎么回事？我记得这儿挂的原本是一幅勃克林。”

一听我开口，警卫那副凛然拒人的架势便松弛下来。“哦，失敬失敬，先生。我还当您是另外那位——把这东西送来的那个人。”他朝那幅画点了点头，眼里的敌意又冒了上来。“他凑巧先撞见我，跟我搭话，后来才去找的馆长。说这是艺术——伟大的艺术——美术馆非收藏不可。”他耸起两肩，不知是耸肩还是打颤。“要依我说，这玩意儿简直就是畜生一般的可憎。”

我又看了一眼那幅画，发觉确实如此。“这么说，美术馆最后还是把它收下了？”我追问。

===Original===

He shook his head. “Oh, no, sir. An hour ago he was at the back door, with that nasty daub there under his arm. I heard part of the argument. He got insulting, and he was told to clear out and take his picture with him. But he must have got in here somehow, and hung it himself.” Walking close to the painting, as gingerly as though he expected the pink dancers to leap out at him, he pointed to the lower edge of the frame. “If it was a real Museum piece, we’d have a plate right there, with the name of the painter and the title.”

I, too, came close. There was no plate, just as the guard had said. But in the lower left-hand corner of the canvas were sprawling capitals, pale paint on the dark, spelling out the word **Golgotha**. Beneath these, in small, barely readable script:

I sold my soul that I might paint a living picture.

No signature or other clue to the artist’s identity.

===Chinese===

他摇了摇头。“哦，没有，先生。一个钟头前他还在后门口，胳膊底下夹着那块破烂货。争吵的话我听见了几句。他出口伤人，人家叫他带着他的画滚蛋。可他准是想法子混了进来，自己把它挂上了。”他小心翼翼地凑近那幅画，活像防着那些粉红舞者会朝他扑出来似的，指了指画框的下沿。“要是正经的馆藏品，这里本该有一块铭牌，写着画家的姓名和画题。”

我也凑上前去。果然如警卫所说，没有铭牌。但在画布左下角，有几个张牙舞爪的大写字母，暗底上一道浅色的颜料，拼出 **各各他（Golgotha）** 一词。字母底下，还有一行小得勉强能认的字迹：

我出卖我的灵魂，为的是画一幅活的画。

没有署名，也没有任何能透露画家身份的线索。

===Original===

The guard had discovered a great framed rectangle against the wall to one side. “Here’s the picture he took down,” he informed me, highly relieved. “Help me put it back, will you, sir? And do you suppose,” here he grew almost wistful, “that we could get rid of this other thing before someone finds I let the crazy fool slip past me?”

I took one edge of *The Isle of the Dead* and lifted it to help him hang it once more.

“Tell you what,” I offered on sudden impulse; “I’ll take this *Golgotha* piece home with me, if you like.”

“Would you do that?” he almost yelled out in his joy at the suggestion. “Would you, to oblige me?”

“To oblige myself,” I returned. “I need another picture at my place.”

And the upshot of it was, he smuggled me and the unwanted painting out of the Museum. Never mind how. I have done quite enough as it is to jeopardize his job and my own welcome up there.

===Chinese===

警卫已在旁边墙边找到了那幅被卸下来的大框子。“他取下来的画在这儿，”他告诉我，如释重负。“帮我把它挂回去，好吗，先生？还有，您看，”说到这里，他几乎露出恳求的神色，“咱们能不能趁旁人还没发觉我放过了那个疯疯癫癫的傻瓜，先把这另一件东西弄走？”

我托住《死之岛》的一边，帮着他把画重新挂了上去。

“这么办吧，”我一时心血来潮，出了个主意；“您若愿意，我把这幅《各各他》带回我家里去。”

“您当真肯？”听了这提议，他欢喜得几乎喊出声来。“您肯吗，就当帮我个忙？”

“是帮我自己个儿的忙，”我答道。“我家里正需要再添一幅画。”

事情到头来是这样：他把我连同那幅没人要的画，偷偷摸摸送出了美术馆。怎么送出去的，姑且不谈。光是我已经说出来的这些，就够害他丢差事，也够叫我往后在那儿不受待见了。

---

===Original===

It was not until I had paid off my taxi and lugged the unwieldy parallelogram of canvas and wood upstairs to my bachelor apartment that I bothered to wonder if it might be valuable. I never did find out, but from the first I was deeply impressed.

Hung over my own fireplace, it looked as large and living as a scene glimpsed through a window or, perhaps, on a stage in a theater. The capering pink bodies caught new lights from my lamp, lights that glossed and intensified their shape and color but did not reveal any new details. I pored once more over the cryptic legend: I sold my soul that I might paint a living picture.

A living picture﻿—was it that? I could not answer. For all my honest delight in such things, I cannot be called expert or even knowing as regards art. Did I even like the Golgotha painting? I could not be sure of that, either. And the rest of the inscription, about selling a soul; I was considerably intrigued by that, and let my thoughts ramble on the subject of Satanist complexes and the vagaries of half-crazy painters. As I read, that evening, I glanced up again and again at my new possession. Sometimes it seemed ridiculous, sometimes sinister. Shortly after midnight I rose, gazed once more, and then turned out the parlor lamp. For a moment, or so it seemed, I could see those dancers, so many dim-pink silhouettes in the sudden darkness. I went to the kitchen for a bit of whisky and water, and thence to my bedroom.

I had dreams. In them I was a boy again, and my mother and sister were leaving the house to go to a theater where﻿—think of it!﻿—Richard Mansfield would play *Beau Brummell*. I, the youngest, was told to stay at home and mind the troublesome furnace. I wept copiously in my disappointed loneliness, and then Mansfield himself stalked in, in full Brummell regalia. He laughed goldenly and stretched out his hand in warm greeting. I, the lad of my dreams, put out my own hand, then was frightened when he would not loosen his grasp. I tugged, and he laughed again. The gold of his laughter turned suddenly hard, cold. I tugged with all my strength, and woke.

===Chinese===

直到付掉出租车的车钱、把那块画布木框拼成的笨重平行四边形拖上楼、搬进我的单身寓所，我才想起来琢磨这画值不值钱。这个问题始终没有弄清，可是打从第一眼起，它就给了我极深的印象。

挂在我自家壁炉上方，它显得又宏大又鲜活，像隔窗望见的一景，或者像戏院舞台上映出的一景。那些欢腾雀跃的粉红躯体从我的灯光里映出新的光亮，那光给它们的形体和颜色上了釉、加了浓，却再照不出任何新的细节。我又一遍细读那行谜样的题词：我出卖我的灵魂，为的是画一幅活的画。

一幅活的画——是这么回事吗？我答不上来。尽管我对这类东西怀着一片老老实实的喜爱，说到艺术，我却称不上行家，甚至连懂行也说不上。那我究竟喜欢不喜欢这幅《各各他》？这我也拿不准。至于题词的后半句，关于出卖灵魂的那半句，我倒是大大地起了兴味，任凭思绪在撒旦崇拜的情结和半疯画家的怪癖上信马由缰。那天晚上读书的时候，我一次又一次抬头，望我新到手的这件东西。它时而显得可笑，时而显得凶邪。午夜刚过，我起身又望了它一眼，然后熄掉了客厅的灯。恍惚有一瞬——或者说仿佛有一瞬——我还能看见那些舞者，骤然的黑暗里一团一团暗粉色的剪影。我到厨房喝了点掺水的威士忌，然后就进了卧室。

我做了梦。梦里我又成了个孩子，母亲和姐姐正要出门上戏院——想想吧！——理查德·曼斯菲尔德（Richard Mansfield）要演《纨绔子布鲁梅尔》（Beau Brummell）。我年纪最小，被打发留在家里看管那台爱闹毛病的炉子。我孤零零一个人，失望之下哭了个痛快，随后曼斯菲尔德本人竟大步踱了进来，一身布鲁梅尔的全副行头。他笑得金光灿灿，热情地伸出手来见礼。我，梦里那个少年，也伸出手去，却在他不肯松手的时候吓了一跳。我拽，他又笑。他笑声里那金子般的成色，忽然变得又冷又硬。我使出全身的力气一挣——醒了。

===Original===

Something held me tight by the wrist.

In my first half-moment of wakefulness I was aware that the room was filled with the pink dancers of the picture, in nimble, fierce-happy motion. They were man-size, too, or nearly so, visible in the dark with the dim radiance of fox-fire. On the small scale of the painting they had seemed no more than babyishly plump; now they were gross, like huge erect toads. And, as I awakened fully, they were closing in, a menacing ring of them, around my bed. One stood at my right side, and its grip, clumsy and rubbery-hard like that of a monkey, was closed upon my arm.

I saw and sensed all this, as I say, in a single moment. With the sensing came the realization of peril, so great that I did not stop to wonder at the uncanniness of my visitors. I tried frantically to jerk loose. For the moment I did not succeed and as I thrashed about, throwing my body nearly across the bed, a second dancer dashed in from the left. It seized and clamped my other arm. I felt, rather than heard, a wave of soft, wordless merriment from them all. My heart and sinews seemed to fail, and briefly I lay still in a daze of horror, pinned down crucifix-fashion between my two captors.

Was that a *hammer* raised above me as I sprawled?

===Chinese===

有什么东西紧紧攥住了我的手腕。

醒来的头半霎，我便觉察满屋都是画中那些粉红舞者，一个个动作轻捷，凶悍而快活。他们也成了人的个头，或者说差不多了，在黑暗里凭着鬼火般的幽光显出形来。在画上小小的一团，瞧着不过婴孩似的肥嫩；此刻却臃肿粗大，像一只只直立起来的巨蟾。而等我完全醒透，他们已经围拢上来，在我床四围收成一个凶险的环。一个立在我右手边，那只爪子像猴子的爪子一样，笨拙而又橡皮似的硬，正牢牢扣在我的胳膊上。

如我所说，这一切，我在一瞬之间全都看在眼里、感在身上。知觉一到，对险境的了悟便随之而至，那险境大得让我顾不上惊怪这班不速之客的邪门。我发了狂似的拼命想挣脱。一时没能挣开；我翻腾挣扎，身子几乎横甩过床去，左边又扑上来第二个舞者，一把攥住、扣死了我的另一条胳膊。我从他们全体身上感到了——与其说是听到，不如说是感到——一阵软绵绵、无声无息的欢腾。我的心和筋力仿佛一齐垮了，一时之间，我只直挺挺躺着，吓呆了，被两个捉住我的人钉成十字架的姿势。

我这样四仰八叉摊在那儿，头顶上方高高举起的，是一柄锤子吗？

===Original===

There rushed and swelled into me the sudden startled strength that sometimes favors the desperate. I screamed like any wild thing caught in a trap, rolled somehow out of bed and to my feet. One of the beings I shook off and the other I dashed against the bureau. Freed, I made for the bedroom door and the front of the apartment, stumbling and staggering on fear-weakened legs.

One of the dim-shining pink things barred my way at the very threshold, and the others were closing in behind, as if for a sudden rush. I flung my right fist with all my strength and weight. The being bobbed back unresistingly before my smash, like a rubber toy floating through water. I plunged past, reached the entry and fumbled for the knob of the outer door.

They were all about me then, their rubbery palms fumbling at my shoulders, my elbows, my pajama jacket. They would have dragged me down before I could negotiate the lock. A racking shudder possessed me and seemed to flick them clear. Then I stumbled against a stand, and purely by good luck my hand fell upon a bamboo walking-stick. I yelled again, in truly hysterical fierceness, and laid about me as with a whip. My blows did little or no damage to those unearthly assailants, but they shrank back, teetering and dancing, to a safe distance. Again I had the sense that they were laughing, mocking. For the moment I had beaten them off, but they were sure of me in the end. Just then my groping free hand pressed a switch. The entry sprang into light.

On the instant they were not there.

===Chinese===

一股惊起的蛮力猛然涌遍我的全身、涨满我的四肢——走投无路的人，有时会得到这样的眷顾。我像落进陷阱的野兽一样尖声嘶喊，连滚带爬翻下床，站了起来。一个被我甩脱了，另一个被我撞向衣柜。脱了身，我便直奔卧室门、直奔寓所前半间，两条吓得发软的腿跌跌撞撞、踉踉跄跄。

卧室门槛上，正正挡着一个幽幽发亮的粉红东西，其余的在我身后步步收紧，眼看就要合力猛扑。我用上全身的力气和分量，右拳狠狠砸了过去。那家伙毫不抗拒地往后一荡，像一件漂过水面的橡皮玩具。我从旁边硬闯过去，奔到门厅里，伸手乱摸外门的门把手。

这时他们已经把我团团围住，一只只橡皮似的手掌在我肩膀上、胳膊肘上、睡衣上衣上胡乱摸索。不等我把门锁拧开，他们就会把我拖倒在地。一阵撕扯肺腑的战栗攫住了我，倒像把他们从身上抖落了个干净。接着我绊在一张小桌上，全凭运气，那只手落处摸着一根竹手杖。我又喊叫起来，声嘶力竭，抡起手杖像使鞭子一样四面抽打。我的打击落在那些非人的凶徒身上，几乎不能伤他们分毫，可他们还是缩了回去，摇摇晃晃、连蹦带舞地退到一个安全的距离。我又一次觉出他们在笑，在嘲弄。眼下我是把他们打退了，可他们笃定，到头来我总逃不出他们的手心。正在这时，我那只空着乱摸的手按到了一个开关。门厅里霎时大放光明。

一眨眼，他们没了。

---

===Original===

Somebody was knocking outside, and with trembling fingers I turned the knob of the door. In came a tall, slender girl with a blue lounging-robe caught hurriedly around her. Her bright hair was disordered as though she had just sprung from her bed.

“Is someone sick?” she asked in a breathless voice. “I live down the hall﻿—I heard cries.” Her round blue eyes were studying my face, which must have been ghastly pale. “You see, I’m a trained nurse, and perhaps﻿—”

“Thank God you did come!” I broke in, unceremoniously but honestly, and went before her to turn on every lamp in the parlor.

It was she who, without guidance, searched out my whisky and siphon and mixed for me a highball of grateful strength. My teeth rang nervously on the edge of the glass as I gulped it down. After that I got my own robe﻿—a becoming one, with satin facings﻿—and sat with her on the divan to tell of my adventure. When I had finished, she gazed long at the painting of the dancers, then back at me. Her eyes, like two chips of the April sky, were full of concern and she held her rosy lower lip between her teeth. I thought that she was wonderfully pretty.

“What a perfectly terrible nightmare!” she said.

===Chinese===

外面有人敲门，我用哆哆嗦嗦的手指拧开门把手。进来一位高挑苗条的姑娘，身上急急匆匆裹着一件蓝色晨衣。她一头亮发乱蓬蓬的，像刚从床上跳下来。

“有人病了吗？”她上气不接下气地问。“我就住在走廊那头——我听见了喊叫。”她圆圆的蓝眼睛端详着我的脸，想必我那张脸白得吓人。“是这样，我是受过训练的护士，也许——”

“谢天谢地，亏得您来了！”我插嘴道，虽说失礼，却出于真心；说罢抢到她前头，把客厅里的灯一盏盏全都拧亮。

是她，不用人指点，自己找出了我的威士忌和苏打水瓶，给我调了一杯威士忌苏打，酒力之足，正叫我感激。我一饮而尽，牙齿紧张得在杯沿上磕得叮叮响。随后我取来自己的晨衣——一件体面的、缎子滚边的——和她一同坐在长沙发上，讲了我的遭遇。讲完，她久久地凝望那幅舞者图，然后又回望我。她那双眼睛，宛如两小片四月的天空，盛满了关切，玫瑰色的下唇咬在牙齿中间。我觉得她漂亮极了。

“好可怕的一场噩梦啊！”她说。

===Original===

“It was no nightmare,” I protested.

She smiled and argued the point, telling me all manner of comforting things about mental associations and their reflections in vivid dreams.

To clinch her point she turned to the painting.

“This line about a ‘living picture’ is the peg on which your slumbering mind hung the whole fabric,” she suggested, her slender fingertip touching the painted scribble. “Your very literal subconscious self didn’t understand that the artist meant his picture would live only figuratively.”

“Are you sure that’s what the artist meant?” I asked, but finally I let her convince me. One can imagine how badly I wanted to be convinced.

She mixed me another highball, and a short one for herself. Over it she told me her name﻿—Miss Dolby﻿—and finally she left me with a last comforting assurance. But, nightmare or no, I did not sleep again that night. I sat in the parlor among the lamps, smoking and dipping into book after book. Countless times I felt my gaze drawn back to the painting over the fireplace, with the cross and the nail-pierced wretch and the shimmering pink dancers.

===Chinese===

“那不是梦，”我抗议道。

她微微一笑，跟我辩驳起来，讲了许多宽心的话，什么联想作用啦，它们在鲜活梦境里的投影啦。

为了把话坐实，她转向那幅画。

“‘活的画’这一行题词，就是根挂钩，你那沉睡的头脑把整套东西全挂在了上面，”她提示道，纤长的指尖点着画上那行潦草的小字。“你那个过分拘泥字面的潜意识，没能明白画家原是打比方——他的画只是在比喻里活着。”

“您能肯定画家是这个意思？”我问。可最后还是让她把我说服了。可想而知，我多么想被人说服。

她又给我调了一杯威士忌苏打，也给自己调了小半杯。就着酒，她告诉了我她的名字——多尔比小姐（Miss Dolby）——末了她留下最后一句宽心的话走了。可是，无论是不是噩梦，那一夜我再没有合眼。我坐在满屋灯光的客厅里，抽着烟，一本接一本地翻书。无数次，我觉出自己的目光又被拽回壁炉上方那幅画上——十字架，被钉穿的可怜人，还有那些莹莹微亮的粉红舞者。

===Original===

After the rising sun had filled the apartment with its honest light and cheer I felt considerably calmer. I slept all morning, and in the afternoon was disposed to agree with Miss Dolby that the whole business had been a bad dream, nothing more. Dressing, I went down the hall, knocked on her door and invited her to dinner with me.

It was a good dinner. Afterward we went to an amusing motion picture, with Charles Butterworth in it as I remember. After bidding her good night, I went to my own place. Undressed and in bed, I lay awake. My late morning slumber made my eyes slow to close. Thus it was that I heard the faint shuffle of feet and, sitting up against my pillows, saw the glowing silhouettes of the Golgotha dancers. Alive and magnified, they were creeping into my bedroom.

I did not hesitate or shrink this time. I sprang up, tense and defiant.

“No, you don’t!” I yelled at them. As they seemed to hesitate before the impact of my wild voice, I charged frantically. For a moment I scattered them and got through the bedroom door, as on the previous night. There was another shindy in the entry; this time they all got hold of me, like a pack of hounds, and wrestled me back against the wall. I writhe even now when I think of the unearthly hardness of their little gripping paws. Two on each arm were spread-eagling me upon the plaster. The cruciform position again!

I swore, yelled and kicked. One of them was in the way of my foot. He floated back, unhurt. That was their strength and horror﻿—their ability to go flabby and nonresistant under smashing, flattening blows. Something tickled my palm, pricked it. The point of a spike.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

朝阳升起，把老老实实的光亮和暖意灌满寓所，我心境平静了许多。我睡了一整个上午，到了下午，已经倾向于同意多尔比小姐的说法：这整桩事不过是一场噩梦，如此而已。我穿戴整齐，沿走廊走过去，敲了她的门，请她和我共进晚餐。

那是一顿很不错的晚餐。饭后我们去看了一场滑稽电影，记得里面有查尔斯·巴特沃思（Charles Butterworth）。同她道过晚安，我回到自己的住处。脱了衣上了床，却毫无睡意。上午那一觉睡到很晚，此刻眼皮迟迟合不拢。就这样，我听见了窸窸窣窣的脚步声，撑着枕头坐起身，看见了各各他舞者发着微光的剪影。他们活了，放大了，正悄悄爬进我的卧室。

这一回我没有迟疑，也没有畏缩。我一跃而起，浑身绷紧，摆出对抗的架势。

“休想！”我朝他们大吼。他们似乎被我狂野的吼声震得迟疑了一霎，我乘势疯狂冲杀过去。一时之间被我冲散，闯出了卧室门，跟前一晚一样。门厅里又是一场混战；这一回他们全体一齐抓上了我，像一群猎狗，把我往墙上扭摁。如今一想他们那小小攫爪里非人的硬劲，我还要浑身扭动。我每条胳膊上各压着两个，正把我大字式地摁在墙皮上。又是钉十字架的姿势！

我咒骂，喊叫，踢蹬。有一个正撞在我脚前。他荡了回去，毫发无伤。这正是他们的力气和可怕之处——任你拳脚交加、要砸扁他们，他们都能变得软塌塌的，毫不受力。有什么东西在我的掌心里挠动，刺了我一下。一枚尖钉的钉尖。……

===Original===

“Miss Dolby!” I shrieked, as a child might call for its mother. “Help! Miss D﻿—﻿—”

The door flew open; I must not have locked it. “Here I am,” came her unafraid reply.

She was outlined against the rectangle of light from the hall. My assailants let go of me to dance toward her. She gasped but did not scream. I staggered along the wall, touched a light-switch, and the parlor just beyond us flared into visibility. Miss Dolby and I ran in to the lamp, rallying there as stone-age folk must have rallied at their fire to face the monsters of the night. I looked at her; she was still fully dressed, as I had left her, apparently had been sitting up. Her rouge made flat patches on her pale cheeks, but her eyes were level.

===Chinese===

“多尔比小姐！”我尖声喊叫，像小孩子喊妈妈那样。“救命！多尔比——”

门一下子开了；想必是我没锁门。“我来了，”传来她毫不畏惧的回答。

走廊里那方亮光照出她的身形。袭击我的那些家伙放开我，朝她舞去。她倒抽一口气，却没有尖叫。我扶着墙跌跌撞撞往前挪，摸到了一个电灯开关，我们面前的客厅霎时亮堂起来。多尔比小姐和我奔到灯下，在那儿站定会合，就像石器时代的人想必曾在火堆旁站定，面对黑夜里的怪物。我看了看她；她还是一身齐全的衣裙，跟我送她回去时一样，显然一直没睡。胭脂在她苍白的脸颊上敷成平塌塌的两块，可她的目光平平稳稳。

---

===Original===

This time the dancers did not retreat or vanish; they lurked in the comparative gloom of the entry, jigging and trembling as if mustering their powers and resolutions for another rush at us.

“You see,” I chattered out to her, “it wasn’t a nightmare.”

She spoke, not in reply, but as if to herself. “They have no faces,” she whispered. “No faces!” In the half-light that was diffused upon them from our lamp they presented the featurelessness of so many huge gingerbread boys, covered with pink icing. One of them, some kind of leader, pressed forward within the circle of the light. It daunted him a bit. He hesitated, but did not retreat.

===Chinese===

这一次，舞者们没有退却，也没有消散；他们埋伏在门厅相对昏暗的地方，蹦跳着、颤动着，像是在攒力气、下狠心，准备再一次朝我们猛扑。

“您瞧见了吧，”我牙齿打战地对她说，“那不是梦。”

她开了口，不是答我的话，倒像自言自语。“他们没有脸，”她低声说。“没有脸！”我们的灯光漫洒在他们身上，半明半暗里，他们显出一个个没有眉目的巨大姜饼人的样子，浑身浇满粉红的糖霜。其中一个，像是领头之类的，朝前挤进了光圈。那光让他有几分发怵。他迟疑着，却没有退。

===Original===

From my center table Miss Dolby had picked up a bright paper-cutter. She poised it with the assurance of one who knows how to handle cutting instruments.

“When they come,” she said steadily, “let’s stand close together. We’ll be harder to drag down that way.”

I wanted to shout my admiration of her fearless front toward the dreadful beings, my thankfulness for her quick run to my rescue. All I could mumble was, “You’re mighty brave.”

She turned for a moment to look at the picture above my dying fire. My eyes followed hers. I think I expected to see a blank canvas﻿—find that the painted dancers had vanished from it and had grown into the living ones. But they were still in the picture, and the cross and the victim were there, too. Miss Dolby read aloud the inscription:

“ ‘A living picture﻿ ﻿…’ The artist knew what he was talking about, after all.”

“Couldn’t a living picture be killed?” I wondered.

===Chinese===

多尔比小姐已从我那张屋子中央的小桌上拿起一把锃亮的裁纸刀。她持刀的姿态沉稳笃定，一看便知是个会使利器的人。

“他们扑上来的时候，”她声音沉着地说，“咱们紧紧靠在一起站着。这样他们更难把咱们拖倒。”

我真想大声赞叹她面对那些可怕东西时的毫无惧色，大声感谢她闻声赶来救我的迅疾。可我嘴里只咕哝出一句：“您真够勇敢的。”

她转过身，看了一眼我那奄奄一息的炉火上方的那幅画。我的目光随着她望去。我想，我原是盼着看见一块空白的画布——发现画上的舞者已从画里消失，长成了眼前这些活的。可他们仍在画里，十字架和受难者也还在原处。多尔比小姐把题词高声念了出来：

“‘一幅活的画……’说到底，画家还是知道自己在说什么的。”

“一幅活的画，难道就没法子杀死吗？”我疑惑地说。

===Original===

It sounded uncertain, and a childish quibble to boot, but Miss Dolby exclaimed triumphantly, as at an inspiration.

“Killed? Yes!” she shouted. She sprang at the picture, darting out with the paper-cutter. The point ripped into one of the central figures in the dancing semicircle.

All the crowd in the entry seemed to give a concerted throb, as of startled protest. I swung, heart racing, to front them again. What had happened? Something had changed, I saw. The intrepid leader had vanished. No, he had not drawn back into the group. He had vanished.

Miss Dolby, too, had seen. She struck again, gashed the painted representation of another dancer. And this time the vanishing happened before my eyes, a creature at the rear of the group went out of existence as suddenly and completely as though a light had blinked out.

===Chinese===

这话听着没底气，还带着孩子气的强辩，可多尔比小姐一声欢呼，得意洋洋，像得了神启。

“杀死？对呀！”她喊道。她扑向那幅画，裁纸刀疾刺而出。刀尖豁进舞蹈半圈正中一个人物的身上。

门厅里那一群像是齐齐一颤，仿佛是受了惊的抗议。我心头狂跳，霍地转过身，再度面对他们。出了什么事？有什么变了，我看了出来。那个大胆的领头者不见了。不，他并没有缩回群里。他是消失了。

多尔比小姐也看见了。她再次出手，划开了画上另一个舞者的形象。而这一次，消失就发生在我的眼前——队伍末尾的一个生灵倏忽灭了存在，又突然又干净，就像一盏灯眨了一下便熄灭了。

===Original===

The others, driven by their danger, rushed.

I met them, feet planted. I tried to embrace them all at once, went over backward under them. I struck, wrenched, tore. I think I even bit something grisly and bloodless, like fungoid tissue, but I refuse to remember for certain. One or two of the forms struggled past me and grappled Miss Dolby. I struggled to my feet and pulled them back from her. There were not so many swarming after me now. I fought hard before they got me down again. And Miss Dolby kept tearing and stabbing at the canvas﻿—again, again. Clutches melted from my throat, my arms. There were only two dancers left. I flung them back and rose. Only one left. Then none.

They were gone, gone into nowhere.

“That did it,” said Miss Dolby breathlessly.

She had pulled the picture down. It was only a frame now, with ragged ribbons of canvas dangling from it.

===Chinese===

其余的，被险境逼急了，一拥而上。

我双脚扎稳，迎了上去。我想把他们一股脑儿全抱住，却被压得仰面朝天。我拳打、扭拽、撕扯。我想我甚至咬到了什么可怖的无血之物，像真菌一类的组织，但我拒绝记清那到底是什么。有一两个挣脱了我，缠住多尔比小姐。我挣扎着爬起来，把他们从她身上拽开。这会儿朝我蜂拥而上的已经不多了。我又苦斗了一场，才被他们再度按倒。而多尔比小姐只顾一撕再撕、一刺再刺那画布——一下，又一下。扼在我喉咙上、胳膊上的爪子一只只化了、没了。只剩两个舞者了。我把他们甩开，站了起来。只剩一个。然后一个也不剩了。

他们没了，没了，消失得无影无踪。

“这一下成了，”多尔比小姐喘着气说。

她已把画拽了下来。如今那只是一副框子，框上垂挂着一条条撕破的画布。

===Original===

I snatched it out of her hands and threw it upon the coals of the fire.

“Look,” I urged her joyfully. “It’s burning! That’s the end. Do you see?”

“Yes, I see,” she answered slowly. “Some fiend-ridden artist﻿—his evil genius brought it to life.”

“The inscription is the literal truth, then?” I supplied.

===Chinese===

我从她手里一把夺过来，扔进了炉膛的煤火上。

“看，”我欣喜地催她。“烧起来了！这就是结局。您看见了吗？”

“是的，我看见了，”她缓缓地回答。“哪个被恶灵附身的画家——是他身上的邪才把它弄活了。”

“那么，那行题词是字字属实的了？”我接口道。

===Original===

“Truth no more.” She bent to watch the burning. “As the painted figures were destroyed, their incarnations faded.”

We said nothing further, but sat down together and gazed as the flames ate the last thread of fabric, the last splinter of wood. Finally we looked up again and smiled at each other.

All at once I knew that I loved her.

===Chinese===

“如今不实了。”她俯下身去看火。“画上的形象被毁掉几个，它们的化身就消散几个。”

我们没有再说什么，只是一同坐下，望着火焰吞掉最后一缕布丝、最后一片木屑。最后我们又抬起头来，相视而笑。

霎时间，我知道了我爱她。

## I The Sacrifice / 一 献祭

===Original===
Enid Mandifer tried to stand up under what she had just heard. She managed it, but her ears rang, her eyes misted. She felt as if she were drowning.

The voice of Persil Mandifer came through the fog, level and slow, with the hint of that foreign accent which nobody could identify:

“Now that you know that you are not really my daughter, perhaps you are curious as to why I adopted you.”

Curious … was that the word to use? But this man who was not her father after all, he delighted in understatements. Enid’s eyes had grown clearer now. She was able to move, to obey Persil Mandifer’s invitation to seat herself. She saw him, half sprawling in his rocking-chair against the plastered wall of the parlor, under the painting of his ancient friend Aaron Burr. Was the rumor true, she mused, that Burr had not really died, that he still lived and planned ambitiously to make himself a throne in America? But Aaron Burr would have to be an old, old man—a hundred years old, or more than a hundred.

===Chinese===
埃涅德·曼迪弗（Enid Mandifer）竭力想撑住，不被方才听到的话击倒。她做到了，可耳朵里嗡嗡直响，眼前一片迷蒙。她觉得自己像是在往下沉，溺水一般。

珀西尔·曼迪弗（Persil Mandifer）的声音穿过这片迷雾传来，平稳而缓慢，带着一丝谁也说不出来路的异国口音：

“如今你知道了自己并非我亲生女儿，也许你会好奇，我为什么收养你。”

好奇……用这个词合适吗？可这个毕竟不是她父亲的男人，最爱的就是把话说得轻描淡写。埃涅德的眼睛此刻清楚些了。她能动弹了，便遵从珀西尔·曼迪弗的招呼坐了下来。她看见他半躺半坐地在摇椅里，椅子抵着客厅抹灰泥的墙，墙上挂着他那位故交亚伦·伯尔（Aaron Burr）的画像。她暗自琢磨：传言莫非是真的——伯尔并没有真死，人还活着，还野心勃勃地盘算着在美洲给自己挣一顶宝座？可那样的话，亚伦·伯尔该是个老而又老的老人了——一百岁，或者不止一百岁。

===Original===
Persil Mandifer’s own age might have been anything, but probably he was nearer seventy than fifty. Physically he was the narrowest of men, in shoulders, hips, temples and legs alike, so that he appeared distorted and compressed. White hair, like combed thistledown, fitted itself in ordered streaks to his high skull. His eyes, dull and dark as musket-balls, peered expressionlessly above the nose like a stiletto, the chin like the pointed toe of a fancy boot. The fleshlessness of his legs was accentuated by tight trousers, strapped under the insteps. At his throat sprouted a frill of lace, after a fashion twenty-five years old.

At his left, on a stool, crouched his enormous son Larue. Larue’s body was a collection of soft-looking globes and bladders—a tremendous belly, round-kneed short legs, puffy hands, a gross bald head between fat shoulders. His white linen suit was only a shade paler than his skin, and his loose, faded-pink lips moved incessantly. Once Enid had heard him talking to himself, had been close enough to distinguish the words. Over and over he had said: “I’ll kill you. I’ll kill you. I’ll kill you.”

These two men had reared her from babyhood, here in this low, spacious manor of brick and timber in the Ozark country. Sixteen or eighteen years ago there had been Indians hereabouts, but they were gone, and the few settlers were on remote farms. The Mandifers dwelt alone with their slaves, who were unusually solemn and taciturn for Negroes.

===Chinese===
珀西尔·曼迪弗本人的年纪看不出个准数，多半是年近七十而非年近五十。论身形，他是天下最窄的一个男人：肩膀、胯骨、太阳穴、两条腿，一概窄窄的，整个人像是被压扁了、挤拧了。一头白发如同梳理过的蓟花冠毛，一绺绺服服帖帖地贴在高耸的头颅上。他那双眼睛晦暗乌沉，像两颗火枪弹丸，毫无表情地盯视着；眼睛底下是匕首也似的一根鼻子，下巴像时髦靴子的尖头。两条腿瘦得没有四两肉，又被紧绷绷的细腿裤子一裹，更显枯瘦，裤脚下还用带子绷在脚背上。喉咙口蓬出一圈蕾丝花边，那式样已经旧了整整二十五年。

他左手边的一只凳子上，蹲着他那身躯庞大的儿子拉吕（Larue）。拉吕的身体是一堆看着松软的圆球和气囊——好大一副肚皮，膝盖滚圆的短腿，虚胖的手，夹在肥肩之间一颗粗大的秃头。他那身白亚麻衣裳只比皮肤白上一色，松垮的、褪成粉色的嘴唇动个不停。有一回埃涅德听见他自言自语，离得近，字字听得真切。他翻来覆去说的是：“我要杀了你。我要杀了你。我要杀了你。”

从襁褓时起，把她抚养长大的就是这两个男人，就在欧扎克（Ozark）乡间这座低矮宽敞的砖木庄园里。十六八年前，这一带还有印第安人，如今已走得干干净净，寥寥几户拓荒者也都在偏远的田庄上。曼迪弗一家只同他们的奴隶住在一起——那些黑人一个个异常阴沉寡言，在黑人里头算得上少见的。

===Original===
Persil Mandifer was continuing: “I have brought you up as a gentleman would bring up his real daughter—for the sole and simple end of making her a good wife. That explains, my dear, the governess, the finishing-school at St. Louis, the books, the journeys we have undertaken to New Orleans and elsewhere. I regret that this distressing war between the states,” and he paused to draw from his pocket his enameled snuffbox, “should have made recent junkets impracticable. However, the time has come, and you are not to be despised. Your marriage is now to befall you.”

“Marriage,” mumbled Larue, in a voice that Enid was barely able to hear. His fingers interlaced, like fat white worms in a jumble. His eyes were for Enid, his ears for his father.

Enid saw that she must respond. She did so: “You have—chosen a husband for me?”

Persil Mandifer’s lips crawled into a smile, very wide on his narrow blade of a face, and he took a pinch of snuff. “Your husband, my dear, was chosen before ever you came into this world,” he replied. The smile grew broader, but Enid did not think it cheerful. “Does your mirror do you justice?” he teased her. “Enid, my foster-daughter, does it tell you truly that you are a beauty, with a face all lustrous and oval, eyes full of tender fire, a cascade of golden-brown curls to frame the whole?” His gaze wandered upon her body, and his eyelids drooped. “Does it convince you, Enid, that your figure combines rarely those traits of fragility and rondure that are never so desirable as when they occur together? Ah, Enid, had I myself met you, or one like you, thirty years ago—”

===Chinese===
珀西尔·曼迪弗接着说下去：“我把你养大，是照绅士抚养亲生女儿的法子养的——为的只有一个简单明白的目的：把你调教成一个好妻子。亲爱的，这就解释了为什么有家庭女教师，为什么送你去圣路易斯（St. Louis）上女子精修学校，为什么有那些书，为什么我们带着你跑新奥尔良（New Orleans），跑别处。我很遗憾，这场令人痛心的州际战争，”说到这里他停了停，从口袋里掏出那只珐琅鼻烟盒，“害得近来出门游历不成。不过，时候到了，你也不是可以小瞧的人。你的婚事，如今要临到你头上了。”

“结婚，”拉吕咕哝了一声，声音低得埃涅德勉强才听得见。他的手指交叉在一起，活像一堆乱作一团的肥白蠕虫。他的眼睛盯着埃涅德，耳朵却听候着他父亲。

埃涅德明白自己总得应个声。她应了：“您已经——替我挑好丈夫了？”

珀西尔·曼迪弗的嘴唇爬出一个笑容，在他那刀片似的窄脸上咧得极宽，他捏了一撮鼻烟。“亲爱的，你的丈夫，早在你来到人世之前就选定好了。”他答道。笑容咧得更宽了，可埃涅德并不觉得那笑有什么喜气。“你的镜子待你可公道？”他打趣她道，“埃涅德，我的养女，它可如实告诉了你：你是个美人，一张脸莹润的鹅蛋脸，一双眼睛盛满温柔的火，一头金棕色的鬈发像瀑布一般，把这一切框在当中？”他的目光在她身上逡巡，眼皮耷拉了下来。“它可让你信了，埃涅德：你的身段把纤弱与丰润罕见地集于一身，而这两种好处，再没有比凑在一处更叫人动心的了？啊，埃涅德，倘若三十年前，我自己遇见的是你，或是跟你一样的人——”

===Original===
“Father!” growled Larue, as though at sacrilege. Persil Mandifer chuckled. His left hand, white and slender with a dark cameo upon the forefinger, extended and patted Larue’s repellent bald pate, in superior affection.

“Never fear, son,” crooned Persil Mandifer. “Enid shall go a pure bride to him who waits her.” His other hand crept into the breast of his coat and drew forth something on a chain. It looked like a crucifix.

“Tell me,” pleaded the girl, “tell me, fa—” She broke off, for she could not call him father. “What is the name of the one I am to marry?”

===Chinese===
“父亲！”拉吕低吼了一声，像是有人亵渎了神圣。珀西尔·曼迪弗轻声笑了。他左手伸出来——那只手白皙修长，食指上戴着一枚深色浮雕玉石——拍了拍拉吕那颗令人生厌的秃头，带着居高临下的怜爱。

“别怕，儿子，”珀西尔·曼迪弗柔声哄道，“埃涅德会以纯洁新娘之身，去到那位等着她的人跟前。”他另一只手探进外衣前襟，拽出一件拴着链子的东西。看模样，像是一具十字架。

“告诉我，”姑娘恳求道，“告诉我，父——”她说不下去，因为她实在叫不出“父亲”两个字。“我要嫁的那个人，他叫什么名字？”

===Original===
“His name?” said Larue, as though aghast at her ignorance.

“His name?” repeated the lean man in the rocking-chair. The crucifix-like object in his hands began to swing idly and rhythmically, while he paid out chain to make its pendulum motion wider and slower. “He has no name.”

===Chinese===
“他的名字？”拉吕说，像是被她的无知惊呆了。

“他的名字？”摇椅里的瘦男人重复了一遍。他手里那件十字架似的东西开始悠悠地、合着节拍地摆荡，他一面放出链子，好让那钟摆似的动作荡得更宽、更慢。“他没有名字。”

---

===Original===
Enid felt her lips grow cold and dry. “He has no—”

“He is the Nameless One,” said Persil Mandifer, and she could discern the capital letters in the last two words he spoke.

“Look,” said Larue, out of the corner of his weak mouth that was nearest his father. “She thinks that she is getting ready to run.”

===Chinese===
埃涅德觉得自己的嘴唇变得又冷又干。“他没有——”

“他是无名者（the Nameless One）。”珀西尔·曼迪弗说。他末尾说出的那几个字，她简直能听出大写的字形来。

“瞧，”拉吕从那张绵软的嘴巴上靠近他父亲的一侧嘴角里挤出话来，“她当是打算跑呢。”

===Original===
“She will not run,” assured Persil Mandifer. “She will sit and listen, and watch what I have here in my hand.” The object on the chain seemed to be growing in size and clarity of outline. Enid felt that it might not be a crucifix, after all.

“The Nameless One is also ageless,” continued Persil Mandifer. “My dear, I dislike telling you all about him, and it is not really necessary. All you need know is that we—my fathers and I—have served him here, and in Europe, since the days when France was Gaul. Yes, and before that.”

The swinging object really was increasing in her sight. And the basic cross was no cross, but a three-armed thing like a capital T. Nor was the body-like figure spiked to it; it seemed to twine and clamber upon that T-shape, like a monkey on a bracket. Like a monkey, it was grotesque, disproportionate, a mockery. That climbing creature was made of gold, or of something gilded over. The T-shaped support was as black and bright as jet.

Enid thought that the golden creature was dull, as if tarnished, and that it appeared to move; an effect created, perhaps, by the rhythmic swinging on the chain.

===Chinese===
“她不会跑的。”珀西尔·曼迪弗一口担保，“她会坐好，听着，看着我手里这样东西。”链子上那件东西似乎在越变越大，轮廓越来越清楚。埃涅德觉得，它毕竟也许并不是一具十字架。

“无名者也是不老不死的。”珀西尔·曼迪弗接着说，“亲爱的，我不愿意把他的一切都讲给你听，也实在没有必要。你只需知道：我们——我历代的先父与我——自法兰西还是高卢的年代起，就在这里，也在欧洲，侍奉着他。是的，比那还早。”

那件摆荡的东西在她眼里当真越来越大了。底下那个十字并不是十字，而是一件三臂之物，像大写字母 T。那个人形也不是被钉在上面的，倒像是在那 T 字形上缠绕攀爬，如同一只猴子攀在壁架上。又像猴子一样，它怪诞、比例失调，是一副嘲弄人的丑态。那攀爬的小东西是金的，或者是镀了金的东西。那 T 字形的支架则黑亮黑亮，如同煤玉。

埃涅德觉得那金色的小东西晦暗无光，像是失了光泽，而且似乎还在动——这效果，多半是链子合着节拍的摆荡造出来的。

===Original===
“Our profits from the association have been great,” Persil Mandifer droned. “Yet we have given greatly. Four times in each hundred years must a bride be offered.”

Mist was gathering once more, in Enid’s eyes and brain, a thicker mist than the one that had come from the shock of hearing that she was an adopted orphan. Yet through it all she saw the swinging device, the monkey-like climber upon the T. And through it all she heard Mandifer’s voice:

“When my real daughter, the last female of my race, went to the Nameless One, I wondered where our next bride would come from. And so, twenty years ago, I took you from a foundling asylum at Nashville.”

===Chinese===
“这场结交，我们得的利处一直不小。”珀西尔·曼迪弗拖长了声调，念经似的说，“可我们也付出了很多。每一百年，必得献上新娘四回。”

迷雾又在埃涅德的眼睛里、脑子里聚拢起来，比先前听说自己是收养的孤儿时那阵雾更浓。可透过这一切，她仍看得见那件摆荡的东西，看得见 T 字上那个猴子似的攀爬者。也透过这一切，她听得见曼迪弗的声音：

“我的亲生女儿——我这一族最后一个女性——去到无名者那里之后，我就犯愁：我们的下一任新娘从哪里来。于是，二十年前，我从纳什维尔（Nashville）一所育婴堂里把你抱了来。”

===Original===
It was becoming plausible to her now. There was a power to be worshipped, to be feared, to be fed with young women. She must go—no, this sort of belief was wrong. It had no element of decency in it, it was only beaten into her by the spell of the pendulum-swinging charm. Yet she had heard certain directions, orders as to what to do.

“You will act in the manner I have described, and say the things I have repeated, tonight at sundown,” Mandifer informed her, as though from a great distance. “You will surrender yourself to the Nameless One, as it was ordained when first you came into my possession.”

“No,” she tried to say, but her lips would not even stir. Something had crept into her, a will not her own, which was forcing her to accept defeat. She knew she must go—where?

“To Fearful Rock,” said the voice of Mandifer, as though he had heard and answered the question she had not spoken. “Go there, to that house where once my father lived and worshipped, that house which, upon the occasion of his rather mysterious death, I left. It is now our place of devotion and sacrifice. Go there, Enid, tonight at sundown, in the manner I have prescribed. …”

===Chinese===
这一切此刻在她听来，竟渐渐像是真的了。有那么一个大能，要人敬拜，要人畏惧，要用年轻女子喂养。她必须去——不，这种念头是错的。它没有半分体面可言，它不过是那枚钟摆般来回摆荡的符咒（charm）施咒，一下一下敲进她心里的。可她已经听见了某些吩咐，某些叫她如何行事的命令。

“今晚日落时分，你要照我讲过的样子行事，把我交代过的话念出来。”曼迪弗吩咐她道，声音像是从极远的地方传来，“你要把自己交给无名者——打你头一天落到我手里时，这一切就已注定了。”

“不。”她想说，可嘴唇动也不肯动。有什么东西已经钻进了她身体里——一股不属于她自己的意志，正逼着她认输。她知道自己必须去——去哪里？

“去骇人岩（Fearful Rock）。”曼迪弗的声音说，像是听见了她没问出口的问题，并作了回答，“去那里，去我父亲生前居住、供奉的那座房子——他死得相当蹊跷，他一死，我就离开了那地方。如今那是我们虔修和献祭的所在。去吧，埃涅德，今晚日落时分，照我吩咐的样子去。……”

### Foreword / 序言

===Original===
> To Whom It May Concern:
> Few words are best, as Sir Philip Sidney once wrote in challenging an enemy. The present account will be accepted as a challenge by the vast army of skeptics of which I once made one. Therefore I write it brief and bald. If my story seems unsteady in spots, that is because the hand that writes it still quivers from my recent ordeal.
> Shifting the metaphor from duello to military engagement, this is but the first gun of the bombardment. Even now sworn statements are being prepared by all others who survived the strange and, in some degree, unthinkable adventure I am recounting. After that, every great psychic investigator in the country, as well as some from Europe, will begin researches. I wish that my friends and brother-magicians, Houdini and Thurston, had lived to bear a hand in them.
> I must apologize for the strong admixture of the personal element in my narrative. Some may feel that I err against good taste. My humble argument is that I was not merely an observer, but an actor, albeit a clumsy one, throughout the drama.
> As to the setting forth of matters which many will call impossible, let me smile in advance. Things happen and have always happened, that defy the narrow science of test-tube and formula. I can only say again that I am writing the truth, and that my statement will be supported by my companions in the adventure.
> Talbot Wills.
> November 15, 1937.

===Chinese===
> 敬启者：
> 正如菲利普·锡德尼爵士（Sir Philip Sidney）当年向敌人下战书时写下的那句：“话以简为贵。”眼下这份记述，会被怀疑论者的浩荡大军当作一封挑战书——我自己从前也是那大军中的一员。因此我把它写得简短而不加修饰。倘若我的故事在某些地方读来发颤不稳，那是因为执笔的这只手新遭劫难，至今犹自颤抖。
> 且容我把比方从决斗换作交兵——这不过是这场炮击的第一炮。就在此刻，凡在我所讲述的这场离奇——而且在某种程度上堪称匪夷所思——的历险中幸存的其余诸人，都在各自准备具结宣誓的证词。此后，本国的每一位灵学调查名家，连同欧洲的几位，都将着手调查研究。但愿我的朋友、魔术师同仁胡迪尼（Houdini）与瑟斯顿（Thurston）尚在人世，能在这件事上搭一把手。
> 我的叙述里掺入了浓重的个人成分，必须为此致歉。有人或许会觉得我有伤大雅。我的浅见是：在这场戏里，我并非仅仅是个旁观者，而是一名演员——尽管是个笨手笨脚的演员。
> 至于陈说那些许多人会斥为不可能之事，且容我预先一笑。世上总有种种事情发生，而且从来都在发生，它们全然不把试管与公式的狭隘科学放在眼里。我只能说，我写下的是真相，而且我的陈述将得到同历此险的伙伴们的佐证。
> 塔尔博特·威尔斯（Talbot Wills）
> 1937 年 11 月 15 日

### Foreword / 序言

===Original===
> Unlike most actors, I do not consider my memoirs worth the attention of the public. Even if I did so consider them, I have no desire to carry my innermost dear secrets to market. Often and often I have flung aside the autobiography of some famous man or woman, crying aloud: “Surely this is the very nonpareil of bad taste!”
> Yet my descendants—and, after certain despairful years, again I have hope of descendants—will want to know something about me. I write this record of utterly strange happenings while it is yet new and clear in my mind, and I shall seal it and leave it among my important possessions, to be found and dealt with at such time as I may die. It is not my wish that the paper be published or otherwise brought to the notice of any outside my immediate family and circle of close friends. Indeed, if I thought that such a thing would happen I might write less frankly.
> Please believe me, you who will read; I know that part of the narrative will strain any credulity, yet I am ready with the now-threadbare retort of Lord Byron, of whose works more below: “Truth is stranger than fiction.” I have, too, three witnesses who have agreed to vouch for the truth of what I have set down. Their only criticism is that I have spoken too kindly of them. If anything, I have not spoken kindly enough.
> Like Peter Quince in *A Midsummer Night’s Dream*, I have rid my prolog like a rough colt. Perhaps, like Duke Theseus, you my readers will be assured thereby of my sincerity.
> Signed, 

 **Gilbert Connatt**
> New York City 

 August 1, 1938

===Chinese===
> 与大多数演员不同，我并不认为自己的回忆录值得公众垂顾。即便当真这么认为，我也不愿把心底最珍贵的秘密拿到市面上去叫卖。不知多少回，我把某位名人的自传一摔了之，放声嚷道：“这简直是坏品位的登峰造极！”
> 然而我的后人——在捱过若干绝望的年头之后，如今我又重新有了后嗣可盼——总有一天会想要知道一些我的事。趁着这段离奇至极的遭遇在脑海中还新鲜清晰，我把它记录下来，加以密封，与其他紧要物件存放在一起，待我去世之时由人发现处置。我不愿这份手稿付梓刊行，也不愿它以任何方式引起至亲和挚友圈子之外任何人的注意。说实在的，倘若我以为这种事当真会发生，我下笔或许就不会这般坦白了。
> 将来读到此文的诸位，请相信我；我知道这段叙述里有一部分，任凭怎样肯轻信的人也难以置信。不过拜伦勋爵（Lord Byron）那句如今已用得发旧的回敬正合我用——他的作品下文还会详谈：“真实比虚构更离奇。”此外，我还有三位证人，他们已同意为我在此记下的一切担保属实。他们唯一的批评，是说我把他们写得太好了。若说有什么不妥，那便是我写得还不够好。
> 就像《仲夏夜之梦》（*A Midsummer Night’s Dream*）里的彼得·昆斯（Peter Quince）那样，我这篇开场白也如一匹未驯的劣马驹，撒开蹄子不知收束。但愿我的读者们能像忒修斯公爵（Duke Theseus）那样，由此确信我的诚意。
> 谨此署名，

 **吉尔伯特·康纳特（Gilbert Connatt）**
> 纽约市

 1938 年 8 月 1 日

===Original===
> We, the undersigned, having read the appended statement of Gilbert Connatt, do hereby declare it to be true in substance.
> Signed, 

 **Sigrid Holgar** 

 **Keith Hilary Pursuivant** 

 **Jacob A. Switz**

===Chinese===
> 我们，以下署名诸人，已读毕吉尔伯特·康纳特所附的陈述，兹声明其所记实质属实。
> 谨此署名，

 **西格丽德·霍尔加（Sigrid Holgar）**

 **基思·希拉里·普瑟文特（Keith Hilary Pursuivant）**

 **雅各布·A·斯威茨（Jacob A. Switz）**

## I The Cursed Damozel / 一 被诅咒的少女

===Original===

Wasn’t Shiloh supposed to be named after an angel or a devil? Angels and devils were both there, sorting the two armies through for who should live and who die, who go to heaven and who go to hell. We Southerners won the first day and part of the second, even after they’d killed General Albert Sidney Johnston. When I say he was about as great as General Lee, I expect to be believed. When we fell back, Bedford Forrest sent some of us to save a field piece that Bragg’s artillery left behind. But the Yankees got there fustest with the mostest men. They carried off the gun, and two or three of us Tennessee cavalry with it.

They were bivouacking on the field—sundown, April 7, 1862. I was marched far back. Passing a headquarters, I saw a fateful little man with a big cigar—General Grant. With him was a taller, red-whiskered man, who was crying. Someone said he was Sherman, but Sherman never seemed to me like a man who would cry over any sorrow, his own or another’s.

This introduction is jumbled. So was my mind at the time. I must have looked forlorn, a skinny gray-clad trooper plundered of saber, carbine and horse. One of the big blue cavalrymen who escorted the prisoners, leaned down from his saddle and rubbed the heel of his hand on my feebly fuzzy cheek.

===Chinese===

人家不是说，夏伊洛（Shiloh）这名字，是从哪位天使或魔鬼那儿得来的吗？天使和魔鬼那天果然都在场，把两军人马细细分拣：谁该活，谁该死；谁上天堂，谁下地狱。我们南方人打赢了头一天，还捎带赢下第二天的一截——这还是在他们打死了阿尔伯特·西德尼·约翰斯顿将军以后的事。我说他差不多同李将军一样伟大，是指望人家肯信的。我们后撤的时候，贝德福德·福雷斯特（Bedford Forrest）派了我们几个人去抢救一门布拉格（Bragg）的炮兵落在后头的野战炮。可北军顶早赶到，人手又顶多。他们把炮拖走了，还捎上了我们两三个田纳西骑兵。

他们就在战场上露宿——1862 年 4 月 7 日，日落时分。我被押到了很后面。路过一处指挥部，我看见一个叼着大雪茄的小个子，一副主掌命数的模样——格兰特将军。他身边站着个高些的红胡子汉子，正在哭。有人说那是谢尔曼，可依我看，谢尔曼不像是个会为什么伤心掉泪的人，不管那伤心是他自己的，还是别人的。

这段开场白乱七八糟。我当时脑子里也一样乱。我想必是一副可怜相：一个瘦精精的灰衣骑兵，马刀、卡宾枪、坐骑全叫人缴了去。押送俘虏的一个高大的蓝衣骑兵从马背上俯下身，用手掌根蹭了蹭我腮帮上那层软塌塌的绒毛。

===Original===

“Little Johnny Reb’s growing some nice black whiskers to surprise his sweetheart,” he said, laughing.

“I haven’t got a sweetheart,” I snapped, trying to sound like a big soldier. But he laughed the louder.

“Hear that, boys?” he hailed the others of the escort. “This little feller never had a sweetheart.” They mingled their cackles with his, and I wished I’d not spoken. They repeated my words again and again, tagging on sneers and merriments. I frowned, and tried not to cry. This was at dusk, the saddest time of day. We’d been marched back for miles, to some sort of reserve concentration in a tiny town.

“We’ve robbed the cradle for sure,” the big blue cavalryman was saying to friends he met. “This little shaver—no sweetheart, he says!”

A new gale of laughter from towering captors all around me. It hushed suddenly at a stern voice:

===Chinese===

“小叛军约翰尼（Johnny Reb）养出两撇蛮像样的黑胡子，要给他心上人一个惊喜呢。”他说着，笑了。

“我没有心上人。”我顶了一句，想把嗓门装得像个老兵。可他笑得更响了。

“听见没有，弟兄们？”他冲押送队里其他人喊道，“这个小家伙压根儿没过心上人。”他们把咯咯的哄笑同他的笑声搅作一处，我直后悔自己开了口。他们把我的话翻来覆去地学，还添上奚落和哄笑。我皱紧眉头，忍住不哭。这时正是黄昏，一天里最叫人心里发堵的时辰。我们被押着往回走了好几里地，到了一座小镇上什么后备集结的所在。

“这回真是从摇篮里抓兵了，”高大的蓝衣骑兵正对撞见的熟人说，“这个小毛头——他居然说没有心上人！”

我四周高大的看守又掀起一阵狂风似的哄笑。一声严厉的断喝，笑声蓦地哑了：

===Original===

“Bring that prisoner to me.”

He rolled out from between two sheds, as heavily and smoothly as a gun-limber.

He was a short, thick man in a dragoon jacket and one of those little peaked Yankee caps. There was just enough light to show me his big beard and the sergeant’s stripes on his sleeve.

“Bring him along,” he ordered again. “March the others to the stockade.” A moment later, he and I stood alone in the gloom. “What’s your name?” he asked.

===Chinese===

“把那个俘虏带到我跟前来。”

他从两座棚屋之间滚了出来，沉甸甸、顺溜溜，像一辆炮车。

他是个矮壮汉子，穿一件龙骑兵上衣，戴一顶北军那种小尖顶帽。光线刚好够我看清他的大胡子和袖上的中士军阶条纹。

“把他带上。”他又下令道，“其余的人押去战俘营。”片刻之后，只剩他和我两个站在昏暗里。“你叫什么名字？”他问。

===Original===

“High Private Cole Wickett,” I replied. A prisoner could say that much. If he asked about my regiment, or the conditions of the army—But he didn’t. His next question was: “How old are you?”

“Fifteen next birthday.” Again no reason to lie, though I’d told the recruiting sergeant eighteen.

“Fourteen years, and some months,” the big man figured it out. “Come with me.”

He put a hand the size of a hayfork on my shoulder, and steered me into a back yard full of soldiers playing cards by firelight. He paused, and scolded them for gambling. Any sergeant in Forrest’s command who had tried that would have been hooted at, maybe struck at—we Confederates respected God and General Johnston and Bedford Forrest, and scorned everyone else. But these men put away the cards and said, “Yes sir,” as if he had been an officer. He marched me on into the house beyond the yard, and sat me in a chair in what had been the kitchen.

There he left me. I could hear him talking to someone in the next room. There was a window through which I might have climbed. But it was dark, and I was tired, hungry, sick, and not yet fifteen. I couldn’t have fought my way back through Grant, Sherman and the rest of the Yankees. I waited where I was until the sergeant opened the door and said, “Come in here, Wickett.”

===Chinese===

“高阶列兵科尔·威基特（High Private Cole Wickett）。”我答道。一个俘虏能说的，也就这一句。他要是问我的团，问部队的境况——可他没问。他接着问的是：“你多大了？”

“过了下个生日就十五岁。”这回也犯不着撒谎，虽然当初我对募兵的中士报的是十八岁。

“十四岁，零几个月。”大个子心里一合计。“跟我来。”

他把一只干草叉大小的手搭上我的肩，把我领进后院——满院子士兵正就着火光打牌。他停下脚，申斥他们赌博。在福雷斯特的队伍里，哪个中士敢这么干，准得挨嘘，弄不好还要挨揍——我们邦联兵只敬上帝、约翰斯顿将军和贝德福德·福雷斯特，其余的人一概不放在眼里。可这些人收起纸牌，答一声“是，长官”，倒好像他是个军官。他领我穿过院子进了屋，让我在原先做厨房的那间屋里的一把椅子上坐下。

他把我撂在那里。我听得见他在隔壁屋里同什么人说话。有一扇窗，我本可以打那儿爬出去。可是天黑，我又累、又饿、又难受，还没满十五岁。要我一路杀回去，穿过格兰特、谢尔曼和其余那些北军，是办不到的。我原地等着，一直等到中士开了门，说：“到这边来，威基特。”

---

===Original===

The front room was lighted by one candle, stuck in its own grease on a table. There sat a tall, gray officer with a chaplain’s cross for insignia. He was eating supper—bread, bacon and coffee. My eyes must have been wolfish, for he asked if I’d have some. I took enough to make a sandwich, and thanked him kindly. Then the chaplain said, “My boy, is it true what Sergeant Jaeger heard? That you’re only a child, and never had a sweetheart?”

I stuck my chin out and stood up straight. The Yankees must be worse than all our Southern editors and speechmakers claimed, if even a preacher among them made jokes about such things. “Sir,” I said, keeping my voice deep in my chest, “it’s none of your business.”

“But it is my business,” he replied solemnly, “and the business of many people. Upon your answer, Cole, depends an effort to help some folk out of awful trouble—northern and southern both—and to right a terrible wrong. Now will you reply?”

“I don’t know what you mean,” I returned, “but I never even thought much about girls. What’s wrong with that?”

===Chinese===

前屋点着一支蜡烛照明，蜡烛插在桌上自己凝出的一摊蜡油里。屋里坐着一位高个子、头发花白的军官，徽记是随军牧师的十字。他正在吃晚饭——面包、咸肉，还有咖啡。我的眼神想必饿狼似的，因为他问我要不要吃一点。我拿了够做一个三明治的分量，诚恳地道了谢。随后随军牧师开了口：“孩子，杰格中士（Sergeant Jaeger）听来的话是真的吗？说你不过是个娃娃，还从来没过心上人？”

我扬起下巴，站得笔直。要是连他们中间的传道人都拿这种事打趣，那北军只怕比我们南方所有的主笔和演说家骂的还要坏。“长官，”我说，把嗓音沉在胸腔里，“这可不关您的事。”

“可这事偏偏归我管，”他郑重其事地答道，“也归许多人管。科尔，有人正陷在可怕的祸殃里等搭救——北方的、南方的都有——还有一桩天大的冤屈等着昭雪，全看你怎么回答。现在，你肯答了吗？”

“我不明白您这话什么意思，”我回嘴道，“可我连姑娘都不曾多想过。这有什么不对？”

===Original===

“Nothing’s wrong with it,” answered the big sergeant named Jaeger. “You should be proud to say that thing, Wickett, if it’s really true.”

“Sergeant,” I sputtered, “I’m a southern gentleman. If you and I were alone, with horses and sabers, I’d teach you to respect my word.”

His face grew as dark as his beard, and he said, “Respect your elders and betters, youngster. So says the Bible.”

“The catechism, not the Bible, Sergeant,” corrected the chaplain. “Cole, it’s only that we must be dead sure.” He pushed a black-bound book across the table toward me. “This is the Bible. Do you believe in the sanctity of an oath.”

“My word’s good, sir, sworn on the Bible or not,” I told him, but I put my hand on the book. “Must I swear something?”

===Chinese===

“这没什么不对，”那位姓杰格的大个子中士答道，“威基特，这话若当真，你该挺光彩地说出来才是。”

“中士，”我气急败坏，“我是南方绅士。就咱俩单挑，有马有刀，我定要教教你，该怎么敬重我的话。”

他的脸沉得跟他的胡子一样黑。“要敬长辈、敬上官，小子。圣经上是这么说的。”

“那是教理问答，不是圣经，中士。”随军牧师纠正道。“科尔，我们只是必须十拿十稳。”他把一本黑面的书顺着桌子推到我面前。“这是圣经。你信不信誓言是神圣的？”

“我的话作数，长官，不用对圣经起誓也作数。”我对他说，可我还是把手放到了书上。“我得起个誓么？”

===Original===

“Only that you told the truth about never having a sweetheart,” he said, and I did so. The chaplain put away the book, and looked at Sergeant Jaeger.

“Something tells me that we have the help we needed, and couldn’t be sure of in our own forces,” he said. “Take care of this boy, for we’re lost without him.”

He went out. Sergeant Jaeger faced me. He was no taller than I, even then, but about twice as broad.

“Since you’re a man of your word, will you give your parole?” he asked.

I swallowed the last bite of bacon, and shook my head. “I’ll escape,” I announced, “as soon as there’s light enough.”

===Chinese===

“只起誓你说的从没过心上人这话是真的。”他说，我便起了誓。随军牧师把书收起来，望向杰格中士。

“我心里有个声音说，我们缺的帮手这回有了——这帮手在我们自家队伍里，原是求不稳的。”他说。“照应好这孩子，没了他，我们就全完了。”

他出去了。杰格中士朝我转过身来。就是那年头，他也不比我高，宽窄倒差不多抵我两个。

“既然你是个说话作数的汉子，肯不肯给我一句假释保证（parole）？”他问。

我咽下最后一口咸肉，摇了摇头。“我要逃跑，”我宣布，“等天色亮得够了就跑。”

===Original===

“Will you give me your parole until sunrise?” he almost pleaded.

Wondering, I gave it. He put his hand on my shoulder again, steered me to a narrow stairway and up to a little room the size of a pantry. There was a cot with a gray blanket, Union army issue, on it.

“Sleep here,” he said. “No, no questions—I won’t answer them. Be ready for orders at an hour before dawn.”

He left me. I took off my tunic and boots, and stretched out on the cot. Still puzzling over things, I went to sleep.

===Chinese===

“那到日出为止，肯不肯给我这句保证？”他几乎是在恳求了。

我满心纳罕，还是给了。他的手又搭上我的肩，把我领到一道窄楼梯跟前，上了楼，进了一间食品储藏室大小的小屋。屋里有一张行军床，床上搭着一条北军发的灰毯子。

“睡在这儿。”他说。“不，别问——问了我也不会答。黎明前一个钟头，整装听令。”

他走了。我脱了军上衣和靴子，在行军床上躺平。事情仍旧一团乱麻，我也就睡着了。

===Original===

I woke to the touch of a hand, cold as a washrag, on my brow. Somehow there was light enough to see a woman standing there. She wore a frosty white dress and veil, like a bride’s. Her face was still whiter.

I saw a straight, narrow-cut nose, a mouth that must be very red to be so darkly alive, and eyes that glowed green. Perhaps the eyes gave the light. I sat up, embarrassed.

“I was told to sleep here, ma’am,” I said. “Is this your house?”

“Yes,” she whispered, “it is my house.” She sat on the edge of the cot. Her hand moved from my face to my shoulder. Her grip was as strong as Sergeant Jaeger’s. “Your name is Cole Wickett. You are a brave soldier, but you never had a sweetheart.”

===Chinese===

一只手落到我脑门上，冷得像块拧过的湿抹布，把我弄醒了。不知怎么，光线竟够我看清一个女人立在跟前。她穿一身结霜似的白裙，罩着白纱，像个新娘。她的脸比裙纱还要白。

我看见一只端正的窄鼻子，一张嘴——必是红得很了，才显出那股暗沉沉的活气——还有一双绿莹莹放光的眼睛。那点光，兴许就是眼睛发出来的。我坐起身来，好生尴尬。

“有人叫我睡在这儿的，夫人。”我说。“这是您的家吗？”

“是，”她悄声说，“这是我的家。”她在床沿坐下。她的手从我脸上挪到我肩上，那手劲同杰格中士的一样大。“你叫科尔·威基特。你是个勇敢的士兵，可你从来没过心上人。”

===Original===

I was tired of hearing about it. I said nothing, and she went on:

“I will be your sweetheart.” And she put her arms around me.

She was beautiful, more than anyone I had ever seen. But when she came that close I felt a horrible sick fear. Perhaps it was the smell of deadness, as of a week-old battlefield. Or all of them.

I wriggled loose and jumped off the cot. She laughed, a little gurgle like water in a cave.

“Do not be afraid, Cole. Stand where you are.”

===Chinese===

这话我都听腻了。我没做声，她接着说：

“我来做你的心上人。”说着，她张开双臂搂住了我。

她美，超过我见过的任何人。可她挨那么近的时候，我心里涌起一种叫人发呕的恐惧。多半是那股死气，像搁了一个礼拜的旧战场。又或许，是所有旧战场的气味一齐到了她身上。

我扭身挣开，从行军床上蹦了下来。她笑了，咕噜一声轻响，像岩洞里的水泡。

“别怕，科尔。站在原地。”

===Original===

She, too, rose. She was taller than I. Her eyes fixed mine, and I could not move. If you want to know how I felt, stare for a while at some spot on the wall or floor. After a moment, you’ll have trouble looking away. It’s called hypnotism, or something. She came near again, and this time I did not shrink when she put her hands on my shoulders.

“Now,” she said.

Then Sergeant Jaeger opened the door, took one look, and began to say something, very rapidly and roughly. It sounded like Bible verses: “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God—”

The woman shrieked, high and ear-tingling, like a bat. She let go of me.

===Chinese===

她也起身了。她比我高。她的眼睛钉住我的眼睛，我便动弹不得。想知道我当时的滋味，就请你盯着墙上或地上的一个点看上一阵——过不了一会儿，你就很难挪开眼了。人家管这个叫催眠术（hypnotism），还是别的什么名堂。她又凑近来，这一回她的双手搭上我肩头时，我没有缩。

“好了。”她说。

就在这时，杰格中士推开了门，看了一眼，便急速地、狠狠地念起一句什么来。听着像圣经的经文：“太初有道，道与神同在——”

那女人尖叫一声，又尖又刺耳，像蝙蝠叫。她松开了我。

===Original===

She was gone. It was like a light being blown out, or a magic-lantern image switched from a screen.

I stared stupidly, like a country idiot. Jaeger cleared his throat, and tugged his beard. “That was close,” he said.

“Who was she?” I asked, and the words had a hard time forming in my throat.

“Somebody whose call we’ll return,” he put me off gruffly. “She thought she’d destroy the one power we’re counting on. It’s time to strike back.”

===Chinese===

她不见了。像一盏灯被吹熄，又像幻灯的影像从幕布上撤去。

我傻瞪着眼，像个乡下痴汉。杰格清了清嗓子，捋了捋胡子。“好险。”他说。

“她是谁？”我问，那几个字在喉咙里好半天才挣出来。

“一个我们回头自会登门回访的人。”他粗声粗气地把我打发了。“她当自己能毁掉我们指望的那股力量。该还手了。”

---

===Original===

I followed him outside. The night was black, but the early-morning stars had wheeled up into heaven. We passed two different sentries, and came through the sleeping street of the little town to a church, either ruined or shell-smashed. Beyond was a burying ground, grown up in weeds and walled around with stone. At the broken-down gate stood the chaplain. He held the bridle of a chunky black stallion colt, not quite full grown.

“I can vouch for the beast,” he greeted Sergeant Jaeger. “It is sad that we watch our animals so much more carefully than our own children.”

“This night I almost failed in my own duty of watching,” replied Jaeger in a tired voice. To me he said, “crawl out of those clothes. Don’t stare. Do as I say.”

By this time there had been so much strangeness and mystery that I did not argue. I shucked my uniform, and the pre-dawn air was cold on my bare skin. The chaplain motioned for me to mount. I did, and he led the colt into the burying ground.

===Chinese===

我跟着他出了门。夜黑如漆，凌晨的星星已经转上了半天。我们过了两道岗哨，穿过小镇沉睡的街道，来到一座教堂跟前——不是坍塌了，就是叫炮弹轰塌了。教堂后头是一片墓地，荒草齐腰，四围砌着石墙。塌坏的墓门前站着随军牧师，手里牵着一匹矮壮的黑色公马驹，那驹子还没长足个头。

“这牲口，我打包票。”他同杰格中士招呼道。“我们把牲口看得比自家孩子还上心，想想也真可悲。”

“今夜我自己这份看护的差事，险些没尽到。”杰格用疲惫的声音答道。又对我说：“把那身衣裳扒了。别瞪眼。照我说的做。”

到了这步田地，怪事和玄虚见得多了，我也就不争辩。我剥下军服，黎明前的空气贴着我赤裸的身子，冷飕飕的。随军牧师示意我上马。我上了，他牵着马驹进了墓地。

===Original===

There were wreaths and wrappings of mist. Through them I saw pale, worn-out tombstones. We tramped over them. It wasn’t polite nor decent, but I saw that the chaplain and the sergeant—he came behind, carrying some shovels and a mattock—meant business. I kept my mouth closed. Riding the colt, I was steered across that burying ground, and across again.

In the middle of the second crossing, the colt planted his hoofs and balked.

Jaeger, bringing up the rear, struck with the handle of a shovel. The colt stood firm. The chaplain tugged in front, Jaeger flogged behind. The colt trembled and snorted, but he did not move.

The chaplain pointed. A grave-mound, a little naked wen of dirt among the weeds, showed just in front of the planted hoofs.

“Your book tells the truth,” he said, strangely cheerful. “Here is a tomb he will not cross.”

===Chinese===

雾气一圈一圈地裹着。透过雾，我看见一块块灰白破败的墓碑。我们打墓碑上踏了过去。这既无礼，也不体面，可我看得出随军牧师和中士——他跟在后头，扛着几把铁锹和一把鹤嘴锄——是来干正经事的。我便闭紧了嘴。我骑着马驹，被人牵着穿过那片墓地，又穿了一遍。

第二回穿到一半，马驹四蹄钉地，说什么也不肯挪了。

押在后头的杰格用锹柄抽它。马驹纹丝不动。前头随军牧师拉，后头杰格抽。马驹打着颤，喷着响鼻，就是不动。

随军牧师伸手指了指。荒草中间一颗光秃秃的小肉疣似的坟包，正露在马驹钉住的四蹄前头。

“你那本书上说得不假。”他说，语气出奇地轻快。“有这么一座坟，它就是不肯跨。”

===Original===

“Get down, Wickett,” commanded Jaeger. “Dress, and help dig.”

I hurried to the gate, threw on my clothes anyhow, and returned. The chaplain was scraping with a shovel. Jaeger swung a mattock. I grabbed another spade and joined in.

As the first moment of gray dawn was upon us, we struck a coffin lid. Jaeger scraped earth from it. “Get back!” he grunted, and I did so; but not before he heaved up the lid with his mattock.

Inside lay the woman who had come to my cot, in her bridal dress.

===Chinese===

“下马，威基特。”杰格下令。“穿好衣裳，帮忙挖。”

我赶紧跑到墓地门口，胡乱套上衣裳，跑了回来。随军牧师正挥锹铲土，杰格抡着鹤嘴锄。我抓起另一把铁锹，加入了进去。

灰蒙蒙的曙色初临那一刻，我们磕上了棺材盖。杰格把盖上的土扒开。“退后！”他低喝一声，我便退了——可还没等我退开，他已用鹤嘴锄把棺盖撬了起来。

里头躺着的，正是到过我床前的那个女人，还穿着那身新娘的裙子。

===Original===

“The stake,” said the chaplain, and passed down a sharp stick like a picket-pin. I judged it was of hawthorn, cut from a hedge somewhere. “Strike to the heart,” went on the chaplain, “while I strike at the throat.”

He suited action to word, driving down the blade of his shovel. At the same moment Jaeger made a strong digging thrust with the stick. I heard again the bat-squeaking; and then, was made faint by a horrid stink of rottenness.

Jaeger slammed down the lid—I heard it fall—and scrambled out of the grave. He and the chaplain began tumbling clods into the hole.

===Chinese===

“桩子。”随军牧师说着，递下来一根削尖的木棍，像根拴马桩钉。我掂量那是山楂木的，从哪道树篱上砍来的。“照心口扎，”随军牧师接着叮嘱，“我来扎喉咙。”

他言出即行，一锹劈了下去。同一刹那，杰格握着木棍狠狠一记直扎。我又听见了那声蝙蝠似的尖叫；跟着，一股腐臭直冲上来，熏得我险些晕倒。

杰格砰地把棺盖砸落回去——我听见它落了下去——手脚并用地爬出坟坑。他和随军牧师便动手，大块大块往坑里丢土。

===Original===

Jaeger looked at me over his shoulder, haggard but triumphant.

“I give you back your parole,” he panted. “Jump on that colt and clear out. To the west there’ll be none of our troops. If you ever tell what was done here, nobody will believe you!”

I needed no second permission.

===Chinese===

杰格回头看了我一眼，形容憔悴，却是一副得胜的神气。

“你的假释保证，我还给你了。”他喘着气说。“跳上那匹马驹，快跑。往西去，不会有我们的人马。今天这里的事，你日后就是说了，也没人肯信！”

我用不着人催第二遍。

## It All Came True in the Woods / 林中一切都成了真

===Original===

> When the Horror passing speech
 

 Hunted us along,
 

 Each laid hold on each, and each
 

 Found the other strong.
 

 In the teeth of things forbid
 

 And reason overthrown,
 

 Helen stood by me, she did,
 

 Helen all alone.

===Chinese===

> 当那言语难状的恐怖
> 一路追猎我们，
> 人人相挽相扶，人人
> 觉出彼此的坚强。
> 任凭犯禁之事迎面逼迫，
> 理智尽已倾覆，
> 海伦站在我身旁，真的，
> 海伦孤身一人。

===Original===

Helen took long steps to keep up with her father. Her chubby face, solemn in its pointed blue hood, turned up to him. “What are these woods called, Daddy?” she asked for the fifth time.

“The Indians called them A-ma-soo-kit,” Clay said patiently, blowing out blue smoke on the brisk autumn air. “Can’t you remember, Helen? You’re six now, and you recited ‘Horatius’ for the people at the cabin last night.”

“ ‘Horatius’ is easy,” Helen explained, as her short legs in ski-pants made hoppy haste to stay beside her father. “A-ma-soo-kit,” and she achieved it at last, “is not. That’s foreign.” She was silent, catching her breath, and Clay slowed to a saunter.

No sense in wearing the child out, and the walk along this trail was too beautiful to hurry, anyway; the brush that fenced them in on either hand offered every fall tint that was richest and brightest﻿—lemon, peach, orange, scarlet, royal purple. As for the trees of the wood, taller and more distant, they might have been a seafloor garden with their welters of warm red and gold clumped on their boughs.

===Chinese===

海伦迈开大步，好跟上父亲。她那张胖乎乎的小脸罩在尖顶的蓝色兜帽里，一本正经地仰起来望着他。“爸爸，这片树林叫什么名字呀？”她已经问了五回了。

“印第安人管它叫阿马苏基特（A-ma-soo-kit），”克莱（Clay）耐心地说，朝清冽的秋风喷出一口蓝烟。“记不住吗，海伦？你都六岁了，昨天晚上还在小屋里给大伙儿背《贺雷修斯》（Horatius）呢。”

“《贺雷修斯》好背，”海伦解释道；她那两条裹在滑雪裤里的短腿一颠一颠，紧赶慢赶才贴在父亲身边。“阿马苏基特嘛，”她好歹把这个音念囫囵了，“就不好背了。那是外国话。”她不作声了，喘匀了气，克莱放慢脚步，踱起步来。

犯不着把孩子累坏，再说这条小径一路的景致美得叫人不忍赶路；两旁围拢的灌木丛，把秋天最浓艳的颜色全数摆了出来——柠檬黄、蜜桃粉、橙红、猩红、绛紫。至于林子里的树，更高也更远，枝头堆着一簇簇暖红金黄，斑斓交叠，倒像是一座海底花园。

===Original===

“No, it isn’t foreign, Helen,” laughed Clay. “Why, the Indians were the first Americans﻿—they lived here for ages before our people ever thought about the *Mayflower*.”

“Before George Washington?” That was Helen’s ultimate conception of antiquity.

“Ever so long before. They had a right to name these woods.”

“What does the name mean. Daddy?”

Clay drew on his pipe. It was a favorite of his, a big-bowled English briar. Quite a time would pass, he mused, before he’d be buying any more pipes in England, what with the war and all﻿—Helen was tugging at his hand to hurry the answer. “Why, I’ve told you that, too,” he reminded. “A-ma-soo-kit﻿—the Trees of Truth. Because the Indians believed that any words spoken here came true.”

===Chinese===

“不，那不是外国话，海伦，”克莱笑道。“要知道，印第安人才是最早的美国人——咱们的人还压根儿没想起‘五月花号’（Mayflower）的时候，他们就在这儿住了不知多少年代。”

“比乔治·华盛顿（George Washington）还早？”在海伦心里，这已经是古老到了头的事了。

“早得多得多。这片林子，本来就该由他们起名字。”

“那这名字是什么意思呀，爸爸？”

克莱吸了口烟斗。这是他的心爱之物，一只斗钵大大的英国石楠木烟斗。他心里盘算，且不说眼下正打着仗，往后再想上英国买烟斗，总得等上好一阵子——海伦正拽着他的手，催他快些回答。“咳，这个爸爸也讲过啦，”他提醒道。“阿马苏基特——真理之树（the Trees of Truth）。因为印第安人相信，在这片林子里说出口的话，句句都会成真。”

===Original===

“Oooo!” Helen was again intrigued. She liked outings of any kind, and had danced when her parents took the cabin on the edge of this forest reserve for fall weekends. Now, if the place had a story attached﻿—“All words come true,” she repeated with relish. “You mean, like fairies and dwarfs?”

“Exactly,” nodded Clay with the utmost gravity. Imaginative himself, he encouraged when he could any romancing his little daughter might attempt. “Fairies and dwarfs.”

“Oooo!” squealed Helen again, and glanced around searchingly. “Daddy, there was a dwarf right there﻿—with a long beard and a red cap﻿—peeking out from under that bush.”

She pointed excitedly. Clay smiled down at her, and led the way around a bend of the trail. The shrubbery was thicker here, and its coloring even richer. Moss made the earth green under their boot-soles.

“It was something to see, then,” Clay remarked, still grave. He knew some parents, and despised them, who would call any child who claimed to see dwarfs a liar. But Clay remembered his own childhood, the vividness of his games and imaginings.

===Chinese===

“哦——！”海伦又来了兴致。她顶喜欢出门游玩；父母在林区边上租下那座小屋过秋日周末时，她欢喜得直蹦。如今这地方要是还连着一个故事——“句句都成真，”她咂着滋味又念了一遍。“你是说，就像仙女、小矮人那样？”

“一点不错，”克莱点头，一脸郑重。他自己就富于想象，小女儿但凡想编点什么，他总是尽量成全。“仙女和小矮人。”

“哦——！”海伦又叫起来，四下里搜寻地张望。“爸爸，那儿就有一个小矮人——长胡子，红帽子——正从那棵灌木底下往外偷看呢。”

她激动地指着那边。克莱低头朝她笑了笑，领路绕过小径的一道弯。这里的灌木更密，颜色也更浓。青苔在他们的鞋底下面铺出一地碧绿。

“那可真值得一看。”克莱说，神情依旧郑重。他认得一些做父母的，谁家孩子说看见小矮人，就骂孩子撒谎——这种人他打心眼儿里瞧不起。可克莱记得自己的童年，记得那时的游戏和幻想何等鲜活。

===Original===

Helen put her hands in her jacket pockets, imitating her father. “Are there witches here too?” she pursued her inquiries.

“Mmmmm﻿—no.” Her father would like to rule such thoughts out, ever since a night of awful dreams after Betty had heard some Halloween tales. “Whatever witches there were in this country got driven away by Cotton Mather, you know.”

“Is Cotton Mather here?” she asked at once.

“Cotton Mather is in heaven,” said Clay. “Let’s stop while Daddy fills his pipe.”

Waiting, Helen glanced up at the half-hazy sky, as though she expected to see the old Puritan divine look out at her, dwarf fashion. “Well,” she said, “are there giants in these woods?”

Tamping down the tobacco in the bowl, Clay held a match to it. He shrugged in defeat﻿—what can you do with kids? They simply throve on stories of excitement and danger and terror. He and Mrs. Clay had tried to hold Helen down to gentle fantasies like Peter Rabbit, but Helen’s taste ran stubbornly to Red Riding Hood’s wolf and even grimmer gentry. It came to Clay that in keeping the stories mild he might be frustrating an instinct. That would be bad﻿—what did J. G. Fraser have to say about such childhood tastes? Or Irvin S. Cobb? Or Freud? Clay decided not to deprive Helen further of giants. Bad dreams tonight would be on his own head.

===Chinese===

海伦把两只手插进外套口袋，学她父亲的样子。“这儿也有巫婆吗？”她追问个不停。

“唔——没有。”做父亲的巴不得把这路念头统统岔开——打从贝蒂（Betty）听了几段万圣节的故事、接着闹了一夜噩梦之后，他就留了神。“这个国家里但凡有过巫婆，也都叫科顿·马瑟（Cotton Mather）给赶跑了，你知道吧。”

“科顿·马瑟在这儿吗？”她马上问。

“科顿·马瑟在天堂呢，”克莱说。“咱们停一停，让爸爸把烟斗装上。”

等着装烟斗的工夫，海伦抬头望了望半阴半晴的天，仿佛指望那位清教老教士会像小矮人似的，从天上探出头来瞧她一眼。“那么，”她说，“这片林子里有没有巨人呀？”

克莱把斗钵里的烟丝按紧，划火柴点着了。他认输似的耸耸肩——拿孩子有什么法子？惊险、危险、吓人的故事，他们偏偏最来劲。他和克莱太太一直想把海伦圈在《彼得兔》那样温和的童话里，可海伦的口味死死认准了小红帽的狼，还有比那更阴森的角色。克莱忽然想到，把故事讲得这样温吞，说不定反倒憋屈了孩子的天性。那可不妙——J. G. 弗雷泽（J. G. Fraser）对这种儿童趣味是怎么说来着？欧文·S·科布（Irvin S. Cobb）呢？弗洛伊德（Freud）呢？克莱打定主意，往后再不剥夺海伦听巨人的乐子了。今夜若做噩梦，责任全在他自己头上。

---

===Original===

“Oh, yes, indeed,” he nodded. “The Trees of Truth would be full of giants. Big ones.”

“Bigger than you?” suggested Helen, who considered her father to be of tremendous stature.

“Much bigger, darling. And bigger than Uncle Frank, or the football boys you saw last week. Twice﻿—three or four times as big. Taller than those trees yonder.”

Helen glanced at the trees, and shivered. “How many eyes do they have?” she almost whispered.

“Only one eye apiece,” improvised Clay promptly, remembering the Cyclops who imprisoned Ulysses. “One eye in the middle of the forehead. But, on the other hand, they have each two or three rows of teeth﻿—sometimes more, and as sharp as swords. And shaggy beards.”

===Chinese===

“哦，有，当然有，”他点头。“真理之树里准保满是巨人。大家伙。”

“比你还大吗？”海伦试探着问——在她看来，父亲的个头已经大得不得了。

“大得多呢，宝贝。也比弗兰克（Frank）叔叔大，比你上礼拜见的那些打橄榄球的小伙子大。大上一倍——大上三四倍。比那边那些树还高。”

海伦瞥了那些树一眼，打了个寒噤。“他们长几只眼睛？”她几乎是耳语。

“一人只长一只眼，”克莱随口就编，心里想起囚禁尤利西斯（Ulysses）的独眼巨人（Cyclops）。“一只眼，长在脑门正当中。不过话说回来，他们每人倒有两三排牙——有时还更多，跟宝剑一样锋利。还有乱蓬蓬的胡子。”

===Original===

She glanced over her shoulder at the trees, and grew pale.

“I’m afraid of them,” she said.

But Clay had been thinking hard and fast, to deal with just such a contingency. “Don’t worry,” he told her. “The giants can’t hurt us﻿—not when Daddy smokes this magic pipe all the way from England.” He blew out a great cloud of blue vapor by way of punctuation. “Because giants are afraid of it.”

“Really truly?” And Helen squinted hopefully at the pipe.

“Oh, yes. Terribly afraid. They’d never dare come near enough to touch us.” Clay began to mix in fragments of half-forgotten Indian lore, learned a generation ago in Boy Scout camp. “You see, the old Indians used tobacco for a charm. Their medicine men smoked it to drive away bad things﻿—spirits and witches and so on. Giants are bad things, just about the worst. They hate the smell of tobacco. Especially,” and he exhaled a bigger cloud, “when it’s in a magic pipe, like Daddy’s.”

===Chinese===

她回头望了望那些树，脸色发白了。

“我怕他们。”她说。

好在克莱早飞快地动了一番脑筋，专为应付这样的意外。“别担心，”他对她说。“巨人伤不了咱们——只要爸爸抽着这只从英国老远带来的魔法烟斗。”他喷出一大团蓝烟，权当句号。“因为巨人怕它。”

“真的呀？”海伦满眼指望，眯起眼睛瞅那烟斗。

“哦，真的。怕得厉害。他们绝不敢凑得近了来碰咱们。”克莱开始搀进些半记不清的印第安掌故，那还是一代人以前在童子军营地里学来的。“你瞧，老印第安人拿烟草当护符使。他们的巫医抽它，好把坏东西驱走——精灵啦，巫婆啦，等等。巨人就是坏东西，还是顶坏的那种。他们恨烟草的气味。尤其是，”他呼出一大口更浓的烟，“装在魔法烟斗里的时候，就像爸爸这只。”

===Original===

Helen glanced at the trees once more, but not pallidly this time. Her chin was squared.

“Well, then, I won’t be afraid of those old giants over there,” she announced sturdily.

At her games again, thought Clay. “Good girl!” he applauded. “Let’s sit down here, on this nice soft rock, and I’ll tell you more things.”

He had meant to turn the conversation to the autumn colors of the leaves, discussing them in simple terms Helen’s six-year-old mind might digest. But, as she took a seat beside him, she had no such idea.

“What do giants wear. Daddy?” she continued on the subject which just now preoccupied her.

===Chinese===

海伦又看了那些树一眼，这一回脸色却不发白了。她的小下巴绷得方方正正。

“那么，我才不怕那边那些老巨人呢。”她挺起小胸脯宣布。

又玩起她的把戏了，克莱心想。“好孩子！”他喝彩。“咱们在这儿坐下吧，坐这块又软和又舒服的石头上，爸爸再给你讲点儿别的。”

他本打算把话头引到秋叶的颜色上，用些简单字眼讲讲，好让海伦六岁的脑子消化得了。可她在身边一坐下，满脑子却全不在这上头。

“巨人穿什么呀，爸爸？”她接着盘问眼下占住她心思的题目。
