===Original===

“They wear skins,” he smiled down at her. “Deerskins and bearskins, sewed together like patchwork, to make a piece big enough to cover them.”

Her eyes were still fixed on the middle distance beyond the brush. “What kind of shoes?”

“No shoes, of course. Their feet are too big for shoes, aren’t they now?”

“I suppose so,” agreed Helen seriously. “I can’t see their feet from here﻿—What do giants eat?”

“Men,” said Clay impressively.

===Chinese===

“他们穿兽皮，”他低头朝她微笑。“鹿皮呀，熊皮呀，像拼布似的一块块缝起来，才够一块遮得住他们身子的。”

她的眼睛还盯着灌木丛那头不远不近的地方。“穿什么样的鞋？”

“当然不穿鞋。他们的脚太大，穿不进鞋，对不对呀？”

“大概是吧，”海伦一本正经地表示同意。“我在这儿看不见他们的脚——巨人吃什么呀？”

“吃人。”克莱一字一顿地说。

===Original===

“Men who don’t have magic pipes?”

“Yes.” To comfort her, Clay blew smoke. “Nobody had better come among the giants without tobacco.”

“They look scared,” announced Helen, and stood up as if to get a better view of something. “Look at them. Daddy.”

“Yes, yes, you’re right,” nodded Clay, stooping to pick up a very brilliant maple leaf. “Now, take this to Mummy when we get back, dear. Say that it’s a souvenir of this walk in the woods.”

“You’re not looking, Daddy. Not looking at the giants.” Helen was pointing insistently.

To fall in with her humor, Clay lifted his head and looked.

===Chinese===

“吃没有魔法烟斗的人吗？”

“对。”为了安慰她，克莱喷了口烟。“谁不带烟草，顶好别往巨人堆里去。”

“他们像是吓坏了，”海伦说着站起身，仿佛要把什么东西看得更清楚些。“你看他们呀，爸爸。”

“是，是，你说得对，”克莱点头，弯腰拾起一片鲜艳夺目的枫叶。“喏，回去的时候把这个带给妈妈，好孩子。就说这是咱们林中散步的纪念品。”

“你没看呀，爸爸。没看巨人。”海伦一个劲儿地指着。

为了凑她的趣，克莱抬起头来，看了看。

---

===Original===

There were three of them, and not more than thirty yards away, taller than the topmost branches of the gorgeous red-and-gold trees.

Clay could see them from the waist upward, above the thick trailside brush﻿—lumpy, hairy, skin-clad, like the ugliest of colossal statutes come to life. Two had beards﻿—one black, the other grizzled and blond﻿—and the third wore long gun braids hanging like lengths of cable at either side of a gross, hairless face the width of a bureau.

So Helen had not been imagining giants, after all.

In the back of a brain that throbbed and whirled, Clay fenced off a tiny corner for what sensible thoughts he could summon. No time now, he told himself, to wonder how or why. The necessity was to get away﻿—get Helen away, at least. Without rising from where he sat, he caught Helen’s shoulder and pressed her back and back, sliding her off the stone.

===Chinese===

一共三个，就在不出三十码开外，比那些红金斑斓的树最高的枝梢还要高。

隔着小径旁浓密的灌木，克莱看得见他们的腰部以上——疙瘩累赘、毛茸茸、裹着兽皮，像最丑陋的巨型雕像活了过来。两个长着胡子——一个黑胡子，另一个灰白里掺着金黄——第三个没长胡子，一张横肉脸有五斗柜那么宽，脸两侧垂着长长的辫子，像一截截缆绳。

这么说，海伦看见巨人，终究不是凭空编出来的。

在那颗突突狂跳、天旋地转的脑袋深处，克莱隔出一个小小的角落，收拢起还能召来的那点清明念头。眼下没工夫，他告诉自己，去想这是怎么回事、为什么。要紧的是脱身——至少让海伦脱身。他没有从坐处起身，一把攥住海伦的肩膀，把她往后按、再往后按，让她的身子从石头上滑下去。

===Original===

“Darling,” he said, wondering why his voice remained steady, “there are bushes near us. Crawl under them. Far under. If there are thorny branches, get under those. Don’t move or make a noise, until Daddy has made those﻿—those things go away from here. When you can’t see or hear them any more, get up and run back along this trail we came. Get to the cabin. Once you start running, don’t stop﻿—”

He heard the dry leaves rustle as she silently obeyed. She was doing her part. Now he must do his.

Springing up suddenly, as high into the air as he could, he flourished both arms over his head. At the top of his voice he yelled:

“Hi! Hi! Hi!”

From his gaping mouth the pipe dropped, bouncing on the mossy ground beside the rock. Clay turned and ran his fastest down trail.

===Chinese===

“宝贝，”他说，自己也奇怪嗓音怎么还这样稳，“咱们旁边就有灌木丛。往底下爬。使劲往里爬。要是有带刺的枝条，就钻到带刺的底下。别动，也别出声，等爸爸把那些——把那些东西从这儿引开。等你再也看不见、听不见他们了，就站起来，顺着咱们来的这条小径往回跑。跑回小屋去。一旦跑起来，就别停——”

干树叶沙沙一响，她悄悄照办了。她尽了她的本分。现在该他尽他的了。

他猛地纵身跃起，跳得越高越好，双臂在头顶上乱挥，扯开最大的嗓门喊：

“嘿！嘿！嘿！”

烟斗从他大张的嘴里掉了下去，在石头旁青苔地上蹦了一蹦。克莱转身顺着小径，撒开腿没命地跑。

===Original===

At almost the same moment he heard a mighty crashing, as of elephants among the timber. He permitted himself one backward look. The three great towers of flesh had sprung through the brushy hedge and were lumbering after him. All three. None had paused to hunt for Helen.

He felt a thrill of elation. Thus the parent partridge saves its hidden young﻿—by diverting danger and pursuit after itself. He flourished his arms and yelled again. Then he saved his breath for running.

No use.

Their legs were longer than his entire body. One of their strides made four of his. Behind, then above, he heard a furnacelike panting. A grip fastened upon him﻿—fingers as long as his arms. He was lifted from the ground in mid-career. The air rang with a deep stormy growl﻿—a concert of prodigious laughter.

After one convulsive struggle, like a chicken in the jaws of a fox, Clay made himself go slack. He might have a chance later, had best save his strength for that. If there was no chance, a doomed man should have dignity﻿—The three gathered and exulted grossly over him, the two giants and the giantess. To them he was smaller than Helen would be to him like a doll or a baby. He saw at close hand their eyes﻿—each had but the one, deep set in the middle of the low forehead﻿—as large as tennis balls; their mouths like open satchels, all studded inside with rows of pointed sharky teeth; their hairiness, their patchwork skin garments, their bare feet like toe-fringed bolsters. Dangling in midair, Clay recognized his own handiwork. Among the A-ma-soo-kit, the Trees of Truth, any spoken word became fact. The idle improvisations of a father diverting his little one had taken shape, flesh, life.

===Chinese===

几乎同一瞬间，他听见一阵天崩地裂的巨响，像大象闯进了树林。他容许自己回头望了一眼。三座肉砌的高塔已经破开灌木的藩篱，轰隆隆追了上来。三个全来了。没有一个停下来找海伦。

他心头一阵狂喜。鹧鸪亲鸟搭救窝里的雏鸟，用的就是这个法子——把危险和追兵都引到自己身上。他又挥臂喊了一声。随后便省下气力，专顾奔跑。

没有用。

他们的腿比他整个人还长。人家跨一步，顶他迈四步。先是身后，继而是头顶，他听见了像鼓风炉似的喘息。一只巨掌攥住了他——指头就有他的胳膊长。他正跑在半路，就被凌空提离了地面。空气被一阵深沉如雷的咆哮震得嗡嗡作响——那是一场惊天动地的狂笑大合奏。

他猛挣了一下，随即像狐狸嘴里的鸡，让自己瘫软下来。说不定往后还有机会，气力最好留着那时候使。倘若没有机会，一个注定要死的人也该死得有尊严——三个巨人把他围在当中，两个男的，一个女的，丑态百出地得意个不了。在他们眼里，他比海伦之于他还小——像个洋娃娃，或是个婴孩。他近在咫尺看清了他们的眼睛——每只果然只有一只，深深陷在低平的额头正中——有网球那么大；他们的嘴像敞开的挎包，里头密密排着一行行鲨鱼般尖利的牙；还有那一身毛发，那拼布似的兽皮衣，那光脚板活像缀了一圈脚趾的长枕头。吊在半空，克莱认出了自己的手笔。在阿马苏基特，这真理之树之中，凡是说出口的话都会变成事实。一位父亲哄小女儿开心的随口瞎编，就这样有了形状，有了血肉，有了性命。

===Original===

The one who held him was the oldest, with a mat of buckskin beard turning gray. Some of his myriad teeth were broken. This captor grunted to the younger black-beard and the terrible woman-mountain with the braids. The dark giant drew from his girdle-thong a stone knife as long as a scythe, with which he began to whittle.

First he uprooted a cedar sapling, and pared away its branches with powerful slashing digs of his blade. Then he sharpened the tip, like a pencil. Beside the trail lay a fallen trunk of pine, dead and beginning to rot. Setting down his pole, the young giant caught this log in his huge hands and with a single humping of his muscles wrenched it in two lengthwise. Kneeling, he set the sharpened point of cedar upon the exposed inside of one slab, and began twirling the stake briskly between his palms.

Thus spun, the point drilled a hole. Wood meal crawled out. It smoked from the friction, glowed. The giantess, with a fistful of shredded bark, evoked a flame that greatened and put forth smudge﻿—fire made by rubbing sticks. Fire for what?

Beneath big skillful hands the fuel quickly caught. The flames grew and climbed. The grizzled monster that held Clay nodded his big bushy head in approval. It was a cooking fire.

===Chinese===

抓着他的那个年纪最大，毡片似的鹿皮色胡子已经泛灰。他那满口密密的牙，有几颗已经断了。这个捕获者朝年轻的黑胡子和那个拖辫子的可怕女巨人咕哝了几声。黑巨人从腰带上抽出一把石刀，镰刀那么长，动手削了起来。

他先把一棵雪松幼树连根拔起，一刀一刀有力地砍净枝杈，又把尖端削尖，像削铅笔似的。小径旁躺着一段倒伏的松树干，早已枯死，开始朽烂。年轻的巨人放下手里的杆子，一双巨手抓住这段木头，肌肉一鼓，便顺着长势掰成了两半。他跪下来，把削尖的雪松尖抵在其中半片露出的新茬上，双掌夹住木桩，飞快地搓转起来。

这样一搓，尖头钻出了孔。木屑一点一点爬出来。摩擦处冒烟了，发红发亮。女巨人抓起一把撕碎的树皮，引出一簇火苗；火苗旺起来，吐出浓烟——钻木取的火。取火做什么？

在那双灵巧的大手底下，柴火很快烧着了。火苗蹿起来，越爬越高。抓着克莱的那个灰胡子怪物点了点毛蓬蓬的大脑袋，表示赞许。这是一堆做饭的火。

===Original===

His huge captor lifted him. The cedar point turned toward his stomach. The other two giants watched with relish, and the tongue of the giantess, like a red banner, came into view to moisten her lips.

This, Clay thought with the corner of his brain kept sensible, was an end that nobody would ever believe. He would never be seen again. Helen, back at the cabin, would tell a story that would not make sense. Even if searchers found his bones, stripped and crushed﻿—

“You let my Daddy alone!” commanded someone close at hand.

===Chinese===

捕获他的巨物把他举了起来。雪松尖转过来，对准了他的肚子。另外两个巨人津津有味地瞧着；女巨人的舌头伸了出来，像一面红旗，舔了舔自己的嘴唇。

这样的下场，克莱用脑中尚存清明的那一角想着，是任谁也永远不会相信的。他从此再不会被人看见。海伦回到小屋，会讲出一个没人听得懂的故事。就算搜寻的人找见他的骸骨，剔得精光、压得粉碎——

“不许你们碰我爸爸！”近旁有人厉声喝道。

---

===Original===

The voice was young, shrill and indignant. Clay bracing himself to feel the crushing impalement of the cedar stake, knew that voice, and in despair counted his sacrifice as useless﻿—Helen hadn’t hidden, hadn’t escaped. She had followed his abhorrent captors, was coming among them.

“Let him alone,” she was repeating, “or I’ll﻿—” There was deadly, confident menace in her little-girl voice.

The grizzled giant lowered both his hands, with the spit in one and Clay in the other.

His huge single eyes widened and protruded grossly. The firelight made it gleam like a very nasty jewel. The fulvid tangle of beard parted, the open mouth writhed over the rows of broken fangs.

===Chinese===

嗓音稚嫩、尖利、气鼓鼓。克莱正咬紧牙关，等着雪松桩子穿身的那一记，却听出了这嗓音，绝望中只道自己的牺牲全白费了——海伦没有藏起来，没有逃出去。她竟一路跟着他那可憎的捕获者，走进他们中间来了。

“放开他，”她一遍遍重复，“不然我就——”小丫头的话音里，带着一股笃定要命的威吓。

灰胡子巨人垂下双手，一手提着烤叉，一手提着克莱。

他那颗巨大的独眼睁大了，丑恶地凸出来。火光映得它像一颗腌臜的宝石似的发亮。土黄色的乱髯分开了，张开的大嘴在一排排断牙上头扭动。

===Original===

Clay managed to turn his head as he hung in the prisoning grip.

He had never realized how small Helen was, how frail. She seemed barely as long as any one of the great bare feet among which she had planted herself. Her arms were set akimbo, her head flung back so that the hood drooped from it, her eyes glittered. So Clay had seen her often before when her young temper was up. Only one thing was really strange about Helen.

In a corner of her mouth, clamped tight between her six-year molars, rode the English briar pipe he had dropped. Above and around Helen’s ruffled hair whirled a wreath of tobacco smoke. Even as he saw all this, she puffed out a bigger, bluer cloud.

“Why did you﻿—” Clay tried to begin, but no words came. He was done for, unable to move or speak. Helen looked, not at him, but at the giants.

===Chinese===

克莱吊在那钳子似的掌握里，总算把头转了过去。

他从没意识到海伦竟这么小，这么单薄。她站在一只只巨大的光脚板中间，身量似乎还不及其中随便哪一只长。她双手叉腰，头往后仰，兜帽从脑后耷拉下来，两眼灼灼闪光。海伦小孩子脾气上来的时候，克莱常见她这副模样。只有一样东西，真正透着古怪。

她嘴角上叼着他掉落的那只英国石楠木烟斗，被六岁孩子的臼齿咬得死死的。海伦蓬乱的头发上面和四周，旋绕着一环烟草的烟雾。就在他看清这一切的当口，她又喷出一团更大更蓝的烟。

“你怎么——”克莱想开口，却一个字也吐不出来。他完了，动弹不得，也说不出话。海伦看的不是他，是巨人们。

===Original===

“I guess I’ll show you!” she squealed at the three staring hulks, just as she might have defied the biggest and roughest boy in her school.

One giant, the black-bearded one that seemed youngest, was first to move. Very raptly he lifted one foot and set it down again, well behind the other. Then he retreated a second pace. A third. The giantess, who had crouched to blow upon the fire, also moved backward on all fours, rather like a tremendous and revolting crab. Helen favored these fugitives with no more than a flick of her bright eyes. She wheeled toward the grizzled one who still stood his ground, holding Clay like a trapped frog.

Rising on tiptoe, Helen hooked one hand in her father’s trouser-cuff. “You put him down,” she ordered terribly, “or I’ll blow some more smoke, and you’ll wish you had.”

She suited action to word.

===Chinese===

“我这就让你们瞧瞧！”她冲那三个呆看的大块头尖叫，那口气，就像向她学校里顶大顶横的男生叫阵。

一个巨人——那个黑胡子、看着最年轻的——头一个动弹。他屏神静气地抬起一只脚，又轻轻放下，落在另一只脚后面老远。接着退了第二步。第三步。先前蹲在地上吹火的那个女巨人，也手脚并用地朝后爬，活像一只大得吓人、丑得作呕的螃蟹。对这些逃跑的，海伦亮晶晶的眼睛只轻轻一扫。她转过身，对准那个还钉在原地不动、攥着克莱像攥着一只落网青蛙的灰胡子。

海伦踮起脚尖，一只手钩住父亲的裤脚。“把他放下来，”她威风凛凛地命令，“不然我再喷你几口烟，叫你后悔莫及。”

她说着便见行动。

===Original===

Above Clay sounded a great hacking cry, as the giant choked and strangled. He felt himself released, falling heavily to the ground. The odor of burning tobacco smote his nostrils. He heard the heaviest of feet scrambling and stumbling away. He heard Helen laugh, in harsh triumph, as Deborah might have laughed over the fall of Sisera’s army.

“They’re gone. Daddy,” she said brightly. “You shouldn’t have dropped your pipe in the first place. But I remembered﻿—giants hate tobacco. I came to save you.”

Mist swallowed Clay and he fainted gratefully.

When he awoke, Helen was sitting beside the great dying fire, quite unconcerned. “Did you have a nice sleep?” she asked.

===Chinese===

克莱头顶上响起一声呛咳似的凄厉大吼，那巨人噎得透不过气来。他觉得自己被松开了，重重摔在地上。燃烧烟草的气味直冲他的鼻孔。他听见世上最沉的脚步连滚带爬地远去。他听见海伦在笑，笑声里是狠狠的得意——底波拉（Deborah）当年看着西西拉（Sisera）全军覆没，笑起来想必也是这样。

“他们走啦，爸爸。”她欢快地说。“你头里就不该把烟斗掉在地上。不过我想起来了——巨人恨烟草。我就来救你了。”

一团迷雾吞没了克莱，他满怀感激地晕了过去。

他醒来时，海伦正坐在那堆将熄的大火旁边，若无其事。“睡得好吗？”她问。

===Original===

He rose on his elbow. “How long was I like that?”

“Not very long. About five minutes, I guess.” She offered him his pipe. “It didn’t make me sick a bit.”

Clay got up, shakily. Helen took his hand, as though it represented to her the surest pledge of safety. They turned homeward on the trail. “Helen,” he said, “what has happened today? Before I﻿—slept?”

“Oh, you mean about those giants? Why, just what you said.” She looked up at him with a little wonder that he should not be sure.

===Chinese===

他撑着胳膊肘抬起上身。“我那样儿躺了多久？”

“没多久。我猜，大概五分钟吧。”她把烟斗递给他。“我一点儿也不难受。”

克莱摇摇晃晃地站起身。海伦拉起他的手，仿佛那只手就是最牢靠的平安凭证。他们顺着小径，朝家的方向走去。“海伦，”他说，“今天出什么事了？在我——睡觉以前？”

“哦，你是说那些巨人呀？嗐，就是照你说的那样呗。”她抬头望着他，有点纳闷，他怎么连这个都拿不准。

===Original===

Then it had been true, among the Trees of Truth. She, too, had seen and known. “Helen, how were they driven away?”

“With the magic pipe. Daddy. You know. I smoked it.”

This as carefully and clearly as though she were the adult speaking to the child. “Why, Helen,” he said, “this isn’t a ma﻿—”

Then he broke off. Better to be careful about talking away any protection. He asked another question. “You weren’t afraid?”

===Chinese===

这么说，在真理之树中间，这一切当真发生过。她也看见了，也知道了。“海伦，他们是怎么被赶走的？”

“用魔法烟斗呀，爸爸。你知道的。我抽了它。”

她说得又仔细又清楚，倒像大人嘱咐孩子似的。“哎呀，海伦，”他说，“这可不是什么魔——”

话到这儿他收住了。凡是能护身的东西，还是别拿嘴说破为妙。他另问了一句：“你不害怕吗？”

===Original===

“Not with the magic pipe. You told me they hated it. And everything comes true in these woods﻿—whether it’s about giants or pipes.”

Clay agreed in his heart that it was a thing not to be explained﻿—only to tremble over his whole life long. Helen was more fortunate. Six years old in a world of wonders and importance, to her three hungry giants were no more wondrous or important than many another thing.

“Don’t tell Mummy about this, Helen,” he said. “We’ll have it for our secret.”

She smiled and nodded, pleased by the word “secret.” Clay felt better. That would help matters now. Some time when she was older, and mentioned the business as a childhood memory, he could get her to agree that it was a dream﻿—grotesque, frightening, but only a dream.

“Daddy,” said the little girl, “are there squirrels here, too? Because I think I see one.”

===Chinese===

“有魔法烟斗就不怕。你说过，他们恨它。这片林子里什么都成真——说的是巨人也好，是烟斗也好。”

克莱打心底里承认，这件事没法解释——只能让他一辈子想起来就打颤。海伦却有福气。六岁的年纪，活在一个处处新奇、事事要紧的世界里；对她来说，三个饿肚子的巨人也并不比许多别的东西更新奇、更要紧。

“这事别告诉妈妈，海伦，”他说。“咱们把它当个秘密。”

“秘密”两个字让她欢喜，她笑着点头。克莱心里松快了些。眼下这能稳住事态。等她长大些，把这件事当童年旧事提起的时候，他可以说动她承认那是一场梦——荒诞，吓人，但不过是一场梦。

“爸爸，”小姑娘说，“这儿也有松鼠吗？我好像看见一只了。”

## The Dreadful Rabbits / 可怕的兔子

===Original===
> “… there are a hundred things one has to know, which we understand and you don’t, as yet. I mean passwords, and signs, and sayings which have power and effect, and plants you carry in your pocket, and verses you repeat, and dodges and tricks you practise; all simple enough when you know them, but they’ve got to be known. …”

===Chinese===
> “……有上百样事体，是你非知道不可的；这些我们懂，你们呢，眼下还不懂。我说的是口令，是暗号，是那些有灵有验的老话；是揣在口袋里的草，是念了顶用的诗句，还有你平日使的种种闪转腾挪的巧技门道；你知道了，样样都简单透顶——可偏偏，这些事就是非知道不可。……”

===Original===

At a point about four miles out of Crispinville, a lean-looking rabbit, with black-and-white smudgings on the gray of his ears and long hind legs, came flopping out on the pavement and paused in full way of the car. Morgan Pitts put on the brakes, drew out a handkerchief and mopped the summer heat from his flushed, seamed brow. He said, with casual courtesy; “Howdy, Mister Rabbit!”

The animal immediately finished its crossing of the road, and sat up in a tussock of grass, gazing while Pitts started the car again. Judge Keith Hilary Pursuivant, big and blond and bespectacled, returned the gaze of those bulging black eyes. They seemed to have a green flash in them. He made no remark, but appeared deeply interested, and he was. He had come all the way to Crispinville for tire very purpose of learning about the custom of rabbit-greeting.

After they rounded a curve and left the little town well out of sight, Judge Pursuivant ventured his query:

“How old is that custom of speaking to rabbits, Mr. Pitts? And how did it start?”

===Chinese===

在离克里斯平维尔（Crispinville）约莫四英里的地方，一只瘦精精的兔子——灰耳朵和两条长后腿上带着黑白晕斑——蹦蹦跳跳地窜上路面，径直停在了车子正前方。摩根·皮茨（Morgan Pitts）踩下刹车，抽出一条手帕，抹了抹那张泛红多皱的脑门上的暑汗。他随口客气了一句：“你好啊，兔子先生！”

那畜生当即过了马路，在一丛草里直起身子坐着，盯着皮茨重新发动车子。基思·希拉里·普瑟文特法官（Judge Keith Hilary Pursuivant）——高大，金发，戴一副眼镜——也回望着那双鼓凸的黑眼睛。那双眼睛里似乎闪着一星绿光。他没作声，却显得兴致十足——他也确实兴致十足。他千里迢迢来到克里斯平维尔，为的正是专门来考察这向兔子问好的风俗。

等车子拐过一道弯，把小镇远远甩出视线之外，普瑟文特法官才试着开了口：

“跟兔子打招呼这个风俗，有多久了，皮茨先生？又是怎么兴起来的？”

===Original===

Pitts scratched his grizzled head. He was little and spry-looking, with a face as red as a rooster’s comb. “Dunno, Judge Pursuivant. Ain’t kept up much on things, been pretty busy with my work. But I guess it’s been goin’ on since the Year One. …” He took a hand from the wheel and pointed ahead. “There’s my place, up yonder, next to Hungry Hill. Your friend’s rented a room there for you. You and him are my only boarders this summer.”

A phrase had caught the judge’s ear. “Hungry Hill?” he repeated, and gazed at the great green swelling, with its thatchy covering of evergreen brush and thicket. “It doesn’t look hungry.”

“I think that’s the old Injun name. And there’s a cave or pit, like an open mouth—” The driver broke off. “Well, here we are, getting there.”

The house nestled comfortably at the foot of the big hill, with plump-looking trees around it—a house old and modest, but well built and well kept, with a stable and barn and rail-enclosed stock pen behind.

===Chinese===

皮茨挠了挠他那花白的脑袋。他是个小个子，看着挺利索，一张脸红得像鸡冠。“说不上来，普瑟文特法官。我这人不大留心这些事儿，活计忙得很。不过我琢磨着，打从元年那会儿起，就有这规矩了。……”他腾出一只手来，往前面一指。“我家就在那边高处，挨着饥饿山（Hungry Hill）。您那位朋友替您在里头租了间屋。今年夏天，房客就您和他两位。”

有个词儿钻进了法官的耳朵。“饥饿山？”他重复了一遍，望着那座绿茸茸隆起的大山——山上覆着一层茅草似的常绿灌木和杂树林。“瞧着可不像挨饿的样子。”

“我想那是老印第安土名儿。那儿有个洞，还是个坑，像一张张开的大嘴——”开车人把话头收住了。“呃，到啦，这就到家了。”

那所房子舒舒服服地偎在大山脚下，四周围着一圈圆滚滚的树——房子古老而朴素，却造得结实、拾掇得齐整，后头还有马厩、谷仓，和一个围了栏杆的牲口圈。

===Original===

As the car stopped, someone came out on the porch and waved a long arm, then hurried down to shake hands with Pursuivant.

It was Ransome. He looked much improved in health and spirits since Pursuivant had last seen him, at New York in early spring. The doctors had apparently sent him to the right part of the country to get over his nervous breakdown. He was still gaunt, but there was color in his flat checks and sparkle in the dark eyes set deep under the bushy brows. Ransome was forty and looked younger, with a square, shallow jaw and black hair and mustache like curls of astrakhan.

“I saw your train come in, over yonder along the horizon,” he told the judge, “and I sat out here to wait for Mr. Pitts to bring you back. Come on, both of you, and have a drink.”

===Chinese===

车子停稳时，有人从门廊里出来，挥了挥一条长胳膊，随即匆匆迎下来，同普瑟文特握手。

是兰塞姆（Ransome）。自打上回见面——年初开春，在纽约——他的气色和精神都好了许多。医生们显然把他送到对路的地方来养他的神经衰弱了。人还是清瘦，可塌陷的两颊有了血色，浓眉下深陷的黑眼睛里也闪着光彩。兰塞姆四十岁，看着还更年轻些，下颌方而单薄，一头黑发、两撇黑胡子，卷得像羔羊皮。

“我瞧见您的火车进站了，就在地平线那一头，”他对法官说，“我就坐在这儿外头，等皮茨先生把您接回来。来吧，二位，喝一杯。”

---

===Original===

They followed him into a pleasant front room, with ancient flower-figured paper and white-painted woodwork, and massive old furniture that was older and better preserved than any of them. Ransome had set out a tray, with bottles, glasses, and a bowl of cracked ice.

“I thought that rabbit legend would fetch you when I wrote to you about it,” he said to Pursuivant. “You collect such things, don’t you? Hard to believe—but I’ve seen the bunny greeted on every road and path in Crispinville Township.”

“Mr. Pitts here told me something about it,” said Pursuivant. “Not much, though—not as much as I’d like to hear.”

“Nobody seems to know much about it,” Ransome said, pouring. “It’s pretty well a local thing. Over in the next county, people hadn’t even heard of it—said I was making it up. There’s ice here, gentlemen. Take it or leave it?”

“Take it,” said Pitts, with relish.

===Chinese===

他们随他走进一间舒适的前屋，墙上贴着古旧的碎花壁纸，木活儿漆成白色，屋里那几件笨重的老家具，比他们谁的岁数都大，也都保存得更好。兰塞姆已经摆出一只托盘，上头有酒瓶、酒杯，还有一碗碎冰。

“我写信跟您提起那桩兔子传说，就知道准能把您勾来，”他对普瑟文特说。“您专收这类东西，不是吗？说来叫人难信——可我亲眼见过，克里斯平维尔镇区的每条大路小径上，人人见了兔子都要打招呼。”

“这位皮茨先生跟我说了点，”普瑟文特道。“不多——没有我想听的那么多。”

“这事儿好像谁也说不出个所以然，”兰塞姆一边斟酒一边说。“差不多就是本地一桩事。隔壁县里的人连听都没听说过——还说我瞎编。这儿有冰，二位。加还是不加？”

“加，”皮茨说得津津有味。

===Original===

“Leave it,” said Pursuivant, “and not much soda. … If you haven’t any information, Ransome, you must have a theory. You skeptics always have theories.”

Ransome poured whiskey and spurted soda into the glasses. “Hnnn,” he said, “might be a Negro thing—this used to be slave territory. One storekeeper in Crispinville thinks it may have come from the first English colonists; again, it might be Indian. But what keeps it so local? Can’t you tell us, Pitts?”

“Not me,” said Pitts, his eyes on the dewy glass held out to him.

They all drank. Pursuivant wiped his blond mustache. His spectacles were full of thoughtful lights.

“The rabbit’s a great figure in folklore,” he observed. “A witch named Julian Cox was tried in England in the 1660’s, for turning into a rabbit. And Jules de Grandin once told me that southern French will turn back from a day’s work because a hare hopped across their trail—bad luck, like a black cat.”

===Chinese===

“不加，”普瑟文特说，“苏打也少搁。……兰塞姆，你手上就算没有实证，总归有一套理论。你们这些怀疑派，向来人手一套理论。”

兰塞姆斟上威士忌，又往杯子里激起苏打。“唔嗯，”他说，“没准儿是黑人的风俗——这地方从前是蓄奴的地界。克里斯平维尔有个开铺子的倒觉得，兴许是头一批英国殖民者带来的；要么，就是印第安人的。可它怎么就只在这一带流行呢？皮茨，你说说？”

“我说不上，”皮茨道，眼睛盯着递到面前那只挂满水珠的杯子。

三人都喝了。普瑟文特抹了抹他的金胡子。他的眼镜片上满是沉思的光。

“兔子在民俗里是个大角色，”他说。“1660 年代，英格兰有个名叫朱利安·考克斯（Julian Cox）的女巫受审，罪名就是变身兔子。儒勒·德·格朗丹（Jules de Grandin）还跟我说过，法国南方人会因为一只野兔蹦过脚前的路，就掉头收工，一天的活儿不干了——晦气，跟黑猫一样。”

===Original===

“Never heard that,” rejoined Ransome. “Of course, de Grandin’s a fable-collector, like you. Of course, I read Uncle Remus when I was a boy—plenty of rabbit stuff there.”

“And I used to carry the left hind foot of a graveyard rabbit for luck,” contributed Pitts, sipping at his highball.

Pursuivant was also turning over the Uncle Remus tales in his mind. They were impressive and sometimes grim, for all the bright humor of Joel Chandler Harris. Br’er Rabbit, seemingly so harmless and plausible, had tricked all the larger and fiercer creatures in self-defense, or for profit, or for mere cruel fun; hadn’t Br’er Wolf been deluded into killing his own children, and Br’er Fox shunted into a fire so that all his progeny looked singed, down into the present day?

“Don’t you think,” Ransome was saying, “that you’re paying too much attention to a silly little custom—a triviality?”

===Chinese===

“这个倒没听说过，”兰塞姆应道。“当然啦，德·格朗丹跟您一样，是个传说收藏家。雷默斯大叔（Uncle Remus）嘛，我小时候倒是读过的——那里头兔子的事儿多得很。”

“我还随身带过坟场兔子的左后脚呢，图个吉利，”皮茨啜着杯里的威士忌苏打，添了一句。

普瑟文特心里也在翻检雷默斯大叔那些故事。别看乔尔·钱德勒·哈里斯（Joel Chandler Harris）笔下透着明快的幽默，那些故事其实很有分量，有时还相当冷酷。布勒兔（Br’er Rabbit），瞧着那么无害、那么乖巧，却把一切比它大、比它凶的生灵都骗了个遍——有时为自卫，有时为占便宜，有时纯为残忍的取乐；布勒狼不是曾受骗杀了自己的亲崽，布勒狐不是被搡进了火堆，害得他的子子孙孙直到今天看上去都像燎过毛的？

“你不觉得吗，”兰塞姆正说着，“你对这么个犯傻的小风俗——这么件鸡毛蒜皮——太上心了？”

===Original===

“Hey,” protested Pitts, taking his nose from his glass, “it ain’t silly when it’s a township ordinance—you can’t even hunt rabbits.”

“And there are no trivialities in life, as Sherlock Holmes or somebody said,” added Pursuivant. “As Mr. Pitts suggests, there must be a good reason for making the rule, and for observing it as well.”

Ransome laughed loudly. His own drink had been long and strong, and he was at the bottom of it. “Time for me to do some missionary work,” said he. Rising, he took two objects from the table.

They were the stock and barrels of an excellent shotgun, and they snicked neatly together in his knowing hands. He grinned above the weapon. “It’s summer, and rabbit’s aren’t fit to eat, but just for the sake of smashing a superstition—” And he fed two shells into the double breech.

===Chinese===

“嘿，”皮茨抗议道，把鼻子从杯口抬起来，“这可不是犯傻——这是镇区立的法令，连打兔子都不许。”

“生活里没有鸡毛蒜皮的小事，夏洛克·福尔摩斯——还是哪位什么人——说过这话，”普瑟文特补了一句。“正如皮茨先生所言，立下这条规矩，又人人遵行，必有其充分的道理。”

兰塞姆放声大笑。他自己那杯调得又长又凶，这会儿已经见了底。“该我做点传教士的功课了。”他说着站起身，从桌上拿起两样东西。

那是一支上好双管猎枪的枪托和枪管，在他内行的手里咔哒一声利落合拢。他在枪上方咧嘴一笑。“眼下是夏天，兔子肉不中吃。可就是为了砸碎一个迷信——”说着，他往双管枪膛里压进了两发弹。

===Original===

Pitts got up. “Better not do it, Mr. Ransome. It’s ’gainst the law.”

“I’ll pay any fine, or whatever,” laughed Ransome.

Pursuivant also rose, and set down his empty glass. “I want to go back to town and look into the community records. I’ll leave my suitcase, and be back before sundown.”

“Shall I run you back in the car?” offered Pitts.

“No, thanks. It’s fine weather and lovely country, and only four miles. I’ll walk.” Pursuivant turned to Ransome. “Promise me you won’t go rabbit-hunting until I return.”

“Oh, all right,” Ransome agreed, and stood the gun in a corner. He saw the judge to the door.

===Chinese===

皮茨站起来。“最好别去，兰塞姆先生。这是犯法的。”

“罚多少款我认，怎么着都行，”兰塞姆笑道。

普瑟文特也站起身，放下空杯子。“我要回镇上去查查社区的档案。手提箱先搁在这儿，日落前准回来。”

“要不要我开车送您回去？”皮茨提议。

“不必了，谢谢。天气正好，乡下又这么可看，才四英里地。我走着去。”普瑟文特转向兰塞姆。“答应我，我回来之前，你不去打兔子。”

“哦，行吧，”兰塞姆应承下来，把枪立在墙角。他把法官送到门口。

---

===Original===

Crispinville was not the county seat, but Pursuivant knew that there would be a township trustee, a clerk and a constable. When he reached the hamlet, he approved once again the well-painted old houses and the quaint little stores with canopy-like arcades jutting out over the wooden sidewalk, admired the square-steepled church that dominated all. He estimated that what Pitts called “the Year One” for this community would be well before the middle of the Eighteenth Century.

“There were settlers here before Daniel Boone’s time,” he thought, and inquired for the home of the township clerk. Finding it, he introduced himself.

The clerk was a frail ancient named Simmons, who prided himself on having most of his teeth and needing no spectacles. He was vague about old records, and only when Pursuivant pleaded did he pry into the clutter of files and trunks that jammed a rear room of his house.

“I been the Crispinville clerk for forty-four years,” he grumbled, “and nobody never asked to see them original papers. Huh, they must be in this here oldest chest.”

The oldest chest was very old indeed, made of unpainted hard wood from which a covering of rawhide was all but rotted away. Mr. Simmons probed and fiddled in the rusty lock with a brass key that might have gone with Noah’s strong box, once or twice calling on heaven to witness his displeasure that the guards did not turn; but then Pursuivant stepped to his side and lifted the lid with a creak of the hinges—the lock had never been fastened. Inside lay papers, yellow and dusty, tied into bundles with antediluvian-looking twine. Simmons examined one handful, then another.

“Yep, these is the old records. Huh, the oldest bunch will be on the bottom, I expect.” He dug down, and brought up a sheaf. “This is what you’ll want. Judge.”

===Chinese===

克里斯平维尔不是县治所在，但普瑟文特知道，镇上总该有一名镇区理事、一名文书，还有一名治安官。他走进这座村子时，再一次赏识起那些油漆齐整的老房子，和那些古色古香的小铺子——凉棚似的廊檐探出木铺的人行道；也再一次赞赏那座方塔尖顶的教堂，它高踞全镇之上。他估摸着，皮茨所说的这个社区的“元年”，该远远早于十八世纪中叶。

“早在丹尼尔·布恩（Daniel Boone）之前，这儿就有移民落脚了，”他心里想着，一路打听镇文书家的住处。找到了，他自报了家门。

文书是个孱弱的古稀老人，名叫西蒙斯（Simmons），颇为自得的是：一口牙还剩了大半，看书读字还用不着眼镜。他对老档案的事含含糊糊，直到普瑟文特再三恳求，他才动手去翻检塞满他家后屋的杂乱文件箱柜。

“我当克里斯平维尔的文书，当了四十四年，”他嘟囔道，“从没有一个人来要瞧过那些原始文件。哼，八成就搁在这只顶老的箱子里。”

那只最老的箱子果真老得很，是不上漆的硬木打的，外头蒙的一层生牛皮几乎烂光了。西蒙斯先生拿一把黄铜钥匙在锈锁眼里又拨又戳——那钥匙没准儿跟挪亚的钱箱是一套的——其间还一两声唤老天爷来见证锁栓不肯转的不快；这时普瑟文特却踱到他身边，一抬手，铰链吱呀一声掀开了箱盖——那锁压根儿就没锁上。里面躺着一些纸，黄黄的，积满尘土，用看着像大洪水之前的细绳捆成一扎一扎。西蒙斯检视了一撮，又检视一撮。

“没错儿，这些就是老档案。哼，顶老的一捆，我估摸着准在底下。”他往下一掏，掏出一沓来。“您要找的，法官，八成就是这个。”

===Original===

Pursuivant took the papers, unfastened the string, and carefully unfolded them to avoid breaking at the creases. They were covered with writing in rusty ink. At the head of the first was printed in block letters, crude and archaic but forceful:

> **Recordes of** 

 **Y**e **Town Council of Crispinville**, 

 **Founded Y**is **Day Y**e 14 **June**, 

 **Anno Regnii Georgii II** 

 **Nono**

The ninth year of the region of George II; Pursuivant computed that it would be 1735 when Crispinville was founded as a formal community. The clerk let him carry the documents into the dining-room and spread them on the top of the table.

===Chinese===

普瑟文特接过纸页，解开绳结，小心翼翼地摊开来，生怕在折痕处断裂。纸上是铁锈色墨水写成的字。头一页的开头，是用印刷体大写字母写成的一行行字，粗陋古拙，却遒劲有力：

> **克里斯平维尔镇政务会** 

 **记事录**， 

 **创于本日，6 月 14 日**， 

 **当乔治二世在位** 

 **第九年**

乔治二世在位第九年——普瑟文特一推算，克里斯平维尔建为正式社区，应在 1735 年。文书由他把这些文件捧进饭厅，摊开在桌面上。

---

===Original===

The paper on which the records had been written was not of the best, and two centuries had made it brittle and tea-tan; but the first clerk of the township had written fluently and in a good bold hand, with all the underlinings and capitalizations of his age. There was a list of names, with official titles opposite, some half-dozen members of that original council. Then, as the first item of history:

> This day we, the Chosen Council of the Town of *Crispinville*, did pay to certain Indians the Price agreed upon for the Lands whereon our Company will live and plant and reap. …

The price was itemized, and Pursuivant saw that, as usual in such matters, the Indians had all the worst of it—gaudy cheap cloth, beads, rickety hatchets and knives, one or two muskets and a horn of powder, and certain bottles of raw New England rum. The screed went on, and suddenly Pursuivant was aware that, upon the very threshold of his researches, he had found the origin of the custom he was tracing:

===Chinese===

写档案的纸质并不上乘，两百年的光阴把它变得又脆又黄，像浸过浓茶；但那位首任镇文书下笔流利，一手好字遒劲饱满，满纸都是他那个时代惯用的下划线和大写。有一份名单，名字对面注着官职——最初那届政务会的成员，约莫半打人。接下来，便是头一件史事：

> 本日，我等*克里斯平维尔*（*Crispinville*）镇民选政务会，依前议向若干印第安人付讫地价，购得我等同仁将于其上居住、耕种、收获之土地。……

地价开列得一项一项，普瑟文特一看便知，这类买卖向来是印第安人吃大亏——花哨的贱价布匹、玻璃珠子、摇摇晃晃的短柄斧和刀子，一两杆滑膛枪、一角筒火药，还有几瓶新英格兰的生酿朗姆酒。这份文书接着往下写，普瑟文特猛然发觉：自己的研究才刚迈过门槛，就已经找到了他追踪的那风俗的源头：

===Original===

> … The Indians engaging on their Part to respect our Rights and Boundaries and to keep the Peace, asking only that we observe their Manner of (as our Interpreter putteth) *Greeting the Hare*; that is, we shall not hunt Hares nor snare them, but upon meeting them, salute and bespeak them as apertly as it were a *Christian Man*, and not a silly Hare.

> To this last, certain of our Company did take Exception, and notably *Capt. Scadlock*, that such Custom was Childish and Fond; but the Chief Person of the Savages, him they call *King Mosh*, did bide firm, saying that the Rabbit was the *Ototemon* of their People and sacred; and further that if we pledged not our Word to continue their Custom, they would never sell the Land, be the Price paid Ten Times Over. And finally the *Rev. Mr. Horton*, our Minister of *God*, did earnestly pray us to give over, shewing that we had Precedent in that the First Missionaries to *Britain* did respect and observe certain Festivals and Useages of the old Heathen; saying further that, right so as we took pity of these simple Indians their Beliefs, right so would they incline to stand our Friends. And so it was agreed upon both Sides, we all signing our Names, saving only *Capt. Scadlock*, and the Matter placed of Record and made a Rule whereby to Govern and guide the Town henceforth.

===Chinese===

> ……印第安人方面则立约尊重我等之权利与疆界，并恪守和平，惟求我等遵行其（如我等通译所译）*向野兔致意*之礼俗；即：我等不得猎兔、网兔，惟于途中相遇时，须坦然向其致意问询，视之俨如*基督徒*（*Christian Man*），而非区区蠢兔。

> 至于末项，我等同仁中颇有持异议者，尤以*斯卡德洛克上尉*（*Capt. Scadlock*）为甚，谓此等习俗幼稚糊涂；然蛮人之首领，即彼等所称之*莫什王*（*King Mosh*），坚执不让，言兔乃其族之*奥托特蒙*（*Ototemon*），神圣不可亵渎；又言我等若不立誓永守其俗，彼等断不出售此地，纵十倍其价亦不可得。末后，我等*上帝*（*God*）之牧师*霍顿先生*（*Rev. Mr. Horton*）恳切劝谕我等依从，并申明前例可援：昔日初至*不列颠*（*Britain*）之传教士，亦曾尊重并遵行古异教徒之若干节期与习俗；又言，我等既宽待此等印第安人之信仰，彼等亦必倾心为我等之友。于是两厢合议定约，我等俱签名画押，惟*斯卡德洛克上尉*独否；此事载入记录，立为章程，以资日后治理导引本镇。

===Original===

Pursuivant smiled in his mustache as he read, a smile of scholarly relish. He could see in his mind’s eye that meeting, the stark jack-booted colonists and the brown, insistent savages. King Mosh—he had spoken out well for his people and faith, even against Captain Scadlock, who undoubtedly was the chief of the colony’s armed forces; and the minister, Mr. Horton, had shown rare tact and liberality—perhaps, good man, he had hoped for converts among those Indians on whose behalf he spoke.

But that hope had been in vain, Pursuivant saw as he read further in the records. Less than a year later there had been a fight, and it had gone against the Indians. The same clerk wrote:

> … and a Searching Party, following the tracks of *Captain Scadlock* upon the Second Day after his Vanishment, did trace him to that Hill which the Indians do call *Gontolah* (that is, the *Hungry Hill*). …

===Chinese===

普瑟文特读着读着，胡须底下浮起一丝笑意，学者读到妙处的那种欣然。他心里望得见那场会面的光景：长筒马靴、身板挺直的殖民者，和皮肤棕褐、态度坚决的土人。莫什王——他为自己的族人和信仰挺身陈词，顶住的是斯卡德洛克上尉——那无疑是殖民地武装的头目；还有那位牧师霍顿先生，显出难得的圆通与开明——兴许，这位好人，还盼着能在他代为陈情的那些印第安人里发展些教徒吧。

可是那指望落了空——普瑟文特往下读档案，便看明白了。不到一年，就起了冲突，印第安人败下阵来。还是那位文书写道：

> ……一队搜索人马，于*斯卡德洛克上尉*失踪后第二日循其足迹而行，追踪至一座山丘，即印第安人所谓*贡托拉*（*Gontolah*）者（意即*饥饿山*）。……

===Original===

“Hello!” muttered Pursuivant, half aloud. “That’s the hill back of Pitt’s place!”

> … and did find him, at the Mouth of the Cave near the Summit; and he had perished miserably, of many small Wounds, so thick upon him that no Inch of his Skin remained whole, nor did any Jot of his Blood remain unto him. And the Indians swore by their false gods that he came to his Death for failing to *greet the Hare*, rather pursuing and slaying Hares upon the Hill; which we took as meaning to say, that they themselves had slain the Captain. Wherefore, falling to our Arms. …

The remainder of the account was unsavory, and dealt with a one-sided conflict. The dead Indians were scalped, it seems, and the prisoners taken all hanged. A few survived and escaped the carnage. That had finished the savages in the vicinity. Only the name of the hill, and the rabbit-greeting, remained to memorialize them.

===Chinese===

“嚯！”普瑟文特半出声地咕哝了一句。“那不就是皮茨家房后那座山嘛！”

> ……于近山顶处一洞之口寻获其人；其人死状极惨，身负细小创口无数，密密匝匝，致周身肌肤无一处完好，体内血液亦涓滴无存。印第安人指其伪神起誓，谓上尉之死，乃因不曾*向野兔致意*，反在山上追猎屠戮野兔所致；我等度其言外之意，实谓彼等亲手杀害了上尉。于是，我等操起兵器。……

这段记述的下文不堪入目，写的是一场一面倒的杀戮。阵亡的印第安人看来都被剥了头皮，抓到的俘虏一律绞死。少数人死里逃生，躲过了那场屠戮。自此，四近的土人绝了迹。留到今天的，只剩那座山的名字，和向兔子问好这个风俗，算是对他们的纪念。

===Original===

At this moment, the clerk came in and tapped his shoulder.

“Judge,” he said, “here’s Morgan Pitts come to find you.”

Pursuivant looked up, his big forefinger marking the place on the old sheet of paper. Pitts came in, his eyes wide with serious wonder. “Judge Pursuivant,” he said, “Mr. Ransome hasn’t come back.”

“Hasn’t come back from where?”

“He went hunting for a rabbit—”

===Chinese===

正在这时，文书进来碰了碰他的肩膀。

“法官，”他说，“摩根·皮茨来找您了。”

普瑟文特抬起头，大食指还压在发黄的旧纸页上标着地方。皮茨走了进来，眼睛瞪得老大，满是严肃的惊奇。“普瑟文特法官，”他说，“兰塞姆先生没回来。”

“没回来？从哪儿没回来？”

“他打兔子去了——”

===Original===

Simmons made a choking sound of protest, and Pursuivant sprang to his feet, quick as a cat for all his bulk. “Hunting for a rabbit? He promised me—”

Pitts nodded glumly. “Yes, sir, I know he did. But when you left, Mr. Ransome, he took his gun and went out. Said he’d be back in fifteen minutes. But”—the man’s lips were quivering—“but he ain’t. I think. Judge, you better come.”

The old records of Crispinville, telling of superstition and pioneering and grim battle, had cracked and crumbled in Pursuivant’s clenching hands. He laid down the remains.

“Have you brought your car, Mr. Pitts? All right, we’ll drive back together.”

===Chinese===

西蒙斯发出一声呛住似的抗议，普瑟文特一跃而起——以他那样的块头，敏捷得像只猫。“打兔子去了？他答应过我——”

皮茨阴沉沉地点头。“是，长官，我知道他答应过。可您一走，兰塞姆先生就拎着枪出去了。说是十五分钟就回。可是”——这汉子的嘴唇直哆嗦——“可是他没回来。我看哪，法官，您最好来一趟。”

克里斯平维尔的老档案，那些讲着迷信、拓荒与恶战的档案，在普瑟文特越攥越紧的手里迸裂成了碎屑。他把残页放了下来。

“车开来了吗，皮茨先生？好，我们一起开车回去。”

---

===Original===

The house was still empty when they got there. Pursuivant moved away through the back yard, across a meadow and among brush and small trees at the foot of the hillside. It was as bright and hot as a tropical seashore. The judge’s blue eyes had found and followed the trail of Ransome’s tennis shoes. Pitts followed just behind.

“It’s bad stuff, hunting rabbits,” he chattered. “Folks around here don’t believe in it—and when people don’t believe—”

“It’s best to string along with such beliefs, I agree,” finished Pursuivant for him. “Look, Mr. Pitts. He found a rabbit trail here—fresh.”

They could see that Ransome had squatted down above the pattern of little paw-prints in the leaf-mold; his toes only made deep depressions, and beside them was the narrow oval where he had rested the gunstock. Then he had risen and followed the game slantwise up the hill. Pursuivant and Pitts went up after him, through dragging belts and tangles of brush, some of it thorny. Pitts spoke again:

“Look, Judge.” He pointed with a knobby old forefinger to a whole clutter of tracks. “More rabbits—Mr. Ransome’s hunting a mess of them.”

The judge’s shaggy head shook. “I’m afraid not. See here—some of the paw-prints fall over Mr. Ransome’s shoe-marks. This bunch—flock—whatever you call a number of rabbits—it came along later. Mr. Ransome is hunting only that first one that made the lone trail.”

“I see,” said Pitts softly. “I see; and these other rabbits—are—*hunting* Mr. Ransome!”

===Chinese===

他们赶到时，屋子还是空的。普瑟文特穿过后院，越过一片草场，走进山坡脚下灌木和小树丛中。天光明亮暑热，像热带的海滩。法官的蓝眼睛已经找到并跟上了兰塞姆网球鞋的足迹。皮茨紧跟在后面。

“打兔子这事儿，邪性着呢，”他喋喋地说。“这一带的人不兴这个——人家要是不信这个邪——”

“这种忌讳，顺着大伙儿总没错，这我同意，”普瑟文特替他把话说完。“瞧，皮茨先生。他在这儿发现了一条兔子道——还是新的。”

他们看得出，兰塞姆曾在腐叶土上那一串小爪印旁蹲下过；他的鞋尖只留下深深的印坑，旁边还有一个窄窄的椭圆印子，是枪托搁过的地方。随后他起身，斜着朝山上追那猎物去了。普瑟文特和皮茨跟着往上走，穿过一道道牵衣拽裤的灌木带和乱树丛，有些还长着刺。皮茨又开了口：

“瞧，法官。”他用疙疙瘩瘩的老食指，指着一大片乱糟糟的足迹。“兔子更多了——兰塞姆先生这是要打一大群呢。”

法官那颗毛发蓬乱的脑袋摇了摇。“恐怕不是。瞧这儿——有几串爪印压在兰塞姆先生的鞋印上头。这一伙——一群——随你怎么叫法——是后头才来的。兰塞姆先生追的，只是先前那只独自留下一条道的。”

“我明白了，”皮茨轻声说。“我明白了；这些别的兔子——是在——*猎*兰塞姆先生！”

---

===Original===

It was hotter than they had thought, as they pushed through one more clump of brambly growth, and came to where hunters and hunted had met.

They had not the time nor the wish to read more than the essentials of the story written in large tracks and small upon the soft, spurned earth. Pursuivant began talking swiftly, pointing here and there.

“Look! Ransome stopped and, probably, aimed his gun. He was looking yonder, perhaps at that dark hollow place among those vine-grown saplings. The rabbit must have stopped there.” He crossed over and peered. “Yes—see! The tracks were turned toward Ransome. It stopped and turned on its heels, to look at him.”

“Like it was mocking him,” said Pitts, and swallowed hard.

Pursuivant looked at the leaves behind the tracks. They were cut to pieces by shot—Ransome must have fired both barrels at that rabbit as it sat up to gaze at him. And then—

===Chinese===

比他们想的还要热。他们又穿过一丛带刺的荆棘，到了猎人与猎物相遇的地方。

软土被踏得凌乱，大大小小的足迹在上头写成了一个故事；他们既没工夫、也没心思细读，只拣要紧的看。普瑟文特开始飞快地讲起来，指指这里，指指那里。

“瞧！兰塞姆停下了，多半是端起了枪。他望着那边，也许是望着藤蔓缠绕的小树苗间那处幽暗的空当。兔子准是在那儿停住的。”他走过去眯眼细看。“没错——看！足迹朝着兰塞姆这边转了。它停下脚，转过身子，正望着他呢。”

“倒像是在奚落他，”皮茨说，狠狠咽了口唾沫。

普瑟文特查看足迹后头的树叶。树叶被霰弹打得稀碎——那只兔子直起身子望着他的时候，兰塞姆准是两管齐发了。接下来——

===Original===

Pitts was down on one knee. “They swarmed over him as he fired!” he cried shakily. “Look, Judge—they rushed him from behind, right here!”

Pursuivant made a step and bent to pick up something from a patch of leafy weeds. “His gun!” he said, and snapped open the breech. “Both barrels were fired—he must have thrown it at them. Then he was unarmed.”

He returned to where Pitts kneeled. The flurry of tracks seemed to say that Ransome had fallen, as under the impact of many missiles; what those missiles were could be deduced from the strength of certain hind-leg marks, telling of how rabbits had sprung straight upward and at the face or chest. The gun still in his hand, Pursuivant stooped to make out what had happened to Ransome then.

===Chinese===

皮茨单膝跪了下去。“他开枪的时候，它们一拥而上！”他声音发颤地喊。“瞧，法官——它们从背后扑他，就在这儿！”

普瑟文特跨上一步，弯腰从一片杂草里捡起一样东西。“他的枪！”他说着，啪地掰开枪膛。“两管都打空了——他准是把枪朝它们砸了过去。这下他手里就没了家伙。”

他回到皮茨跪着的地方。那一片纷乱的足迹像是在说：兰塞姆倒下了，像是被许多投掷物砸倒的；那些投掷物是什么，从几处后腿印的力度便能推想出来——兔子们曾笔直向上跃起，扑向人的脸和胸口。手里仍旧提着枪，普瑟文特俯下身去，辨认这之后兰塞姆的遭遇。

===Original===

Here were hand-prints, deeply driven, as though weight had been supported upon the palms. Here was the scrape of a dragged knee, and another, with repetitions beyond—yes, Ransome had crept upon his hands and knees, stunned, wretched, driven. For at either flank of his trail were the trails of his little adversaries, herding and harrying him, toward the dark opening among the vines where he had seen and fired upon the quarry that was really a decoy.

“Poor Mr. Ransome,” Pitts was saying. “He should have obeyed the law—you got to respect things like that, or—”

“Stay behind me,” commanded Pursuivant, and bent, thrusting with the muzzle of the shotgun into the space among the vines.

Within was empty gloom, for here the hill rose abruptly under a masking of herbage, and in it was a cave.

===Chinese===

这里有手印，深深压进土里，像是全身的分量都撑在了手掌上。这里有一条拖蹭的膝盖印，又一条，还有更多的——不错，兰塞姆是手脚并用地爬行过的，晕头转向，狼狈不堪，被驱赶着。因为他足迹的两翼，都有那些小小对头的足迹，一路圈围着他、袭扰着他，逼向藤蔓间那个幽黑的洞口——他先前在那里看见并向之开枪的那只猎物，其实是个诱子。

“可怜的兰塞姆先生，”皮茨正说着。“他该守法的——这种事你可得敬着，不然——”

“躲我后头。”普瑟文特喝道，随即弯下身，用猎枪口往藤蔓间的空当里捅。

里面是一团空荡荡的昏黑，原来山在这里披着一层草木陡然隆起，里头是一个山洞。

===Original===

“Gontolah—the Hungry Hill,” remembered Pursuivant. Yes, as Pitts had said, this place looked like an open, starved mouth, a lune-shape hole with a flat rim of rock above and another below, like gaping lips. And something was wedged in that mouth-like cavern.

He forced himself to touch it. His fingers closed on a slack, damp wrist. With a heave and a scrape, he dragged the body into view.

Yes, it was Ransome, or what had been Ransome. Pursuivant knew him by the contours of that pounded, lacerated head, by the leanness of the blood-boltered body inside chopped-up rags.

Pitts whimpered as the thing came into the light.

“Poor Mr. Ransome,” he said again. “Now I know how—*oh*!”

===Chinese===

“贡托拉——饥饿山。”普瑟文特记了起来。不错，正如皮茨说过的，这地方像一张饿坏了的张开的大嘴：一个新月形的洞，上下各有一道平直的岩缘，像两片咧开的嘴唇。有样东西楔在那张嘴似的洞穴里。

他逼着自己去碰。手指扣上了一只松弛的、潮湿的手腕。他猛一使劲，一路拖拽着，把那具尸身拖到了亮处。

不错，是兰塞姆，或者说，是兰塞姆剩下的东西。普瑟文特认出了他——凭那颗被捣烂、撕裂的脑袋的轮廓，凭破成碎条的衣裳里那具血污凝结的瘦削身躯。

那东西被拖进天光里，皮茨呜咽了一声。

“可怜的兰塞姆先生，”他又说了一遍。“这下我知道是怎么——*哦！*”

===Original===

Pursuivant whirled like a top at that final gasp of horror. He saw, too, what Spencer had seen.

The spaces among the bushes along their back trail were full of rabbits, all lean and gray with black and white blazings on legs and ear-tips, and all a trifle larger than ordinary. Every eye in that horde was turned upon the two men, and the eyes of meat-eating animals. They were an army, moving concertedly and purposefully upon the judge and Pitts, who stood cut off with their backs to the cave.

Pursuivant’s big fists tightened on Ransome’s shotgun. He would not throw it, he told himself at once—clubbed, its metal-shod butt would smash these little assailants to rags. But Pitts was trying another weapon.

With eyes and outstretched hands he addressed himself to the foremost of the rabbits, the one that moved cautiously but steadily ahead of the press, like an officer leading troops in an orderly advance. He spoke, audibly and with a tremble of fear:

===Chinese===

这一声惊骇的抽气，让普瑟文特像陀螺似的转过身去。他也看见了皮茨看见的东西。

来路两旁灌木的空隙间，满都是兔子，只只精瘦灰皮，腿上和耳朵尖带着黑白花斑，只只都比寻常的略大一号。那一大群里，每一双眼睛都转向这两个人——那是食肉兽的眼睛。它们是一支军队，协同一致、目标明确地朝法官和皮茨进逼，把两人堵得背靠山洞，无路可退。

普瑟文特的大拳头在兰塞姆的猎枪上攥紧了。不能把枪扔出去，他当即告诫自己——倒握了抡起来，那包铁的枪托能把这群小攻击手砸成肉泥。可皮茨在试另一件武器。

他用目光和伸出的双手，向着兔群最前头的那只致辞——那一只走在大队前头，谨慎却沉稳，像一位带领队伍有序推进的军官。他开了口，声音听得见，带着恐惧的颤抖：

===Original===

“H‑howdy, Mister Rabbit!”

There was a momentary pause in the oncoming torrent of fur. A little eddy showed, then a parting in the ranks. They were making a way for Pitts to retreat through them, and he needed not a moment to make up his mind. He fairly darted along that open lane, which closed behind him. The expanse of fuzzy backs and upturned green eyes resolidified, and above it Pitts looked back at Pursuivant.

“Better say the words,” he advised huskily. “They’re closing in on you.”

===Chinese===

“你……你好啊，兔子先生！”

涌来的那股皮毛洪流迟滞了一瞬。队形里起了一点小小的涡旋，随后分开一道口子。它们是在给皮茨让出一条退路，他也用不着半秒犹豫，撒腿就顺着那条空当飞奔过去，空当随即在身后合拢。毛茸茸的脊背和仰起的绿眼睛重新凝成一片，皮茨在那片脊背的上方，回头望着普瑟文特。

“还是念那句话吧，”他嗓音沙哑地劝道。“它们朝你围上来了。”

---

===Original===

They converged slowly and smoothly, flowing like a puddle of grease—but grease scummed over with fur and green-black eyes, sprouting a meadow of ears. Pursuivant lifted the clubbed shotgun and set himself to strike. The leader-rabbit sprang suddenly at him. Pursuivant swung the gun, as a batter strikes at a ball. He could not miss—but the weapon swished thinly in the air, and the little sinewy body struck him at the base of the throat. A moment later more rabbits were springing at him—a dozen, a score, hundreds. His flailing with the gun did not find a single mark. He swayed under the bombardment, but kept his feet—he was stronger and bulkier than Ransome, he would take more battering to bring down—

“Say the words. Judge!” Pitts’s voice pleaded with him from beyond. “They ain’t real rabbits—they’ll finish you!”

Fighting, clawing at the rain of buffets, Pursuivant found his mind turning from the struggle to consideration of something else. What had the Indian, King Mosh, called the rabbit? *Ototemon.* Strange word. But with a familiar sound … suddenly he saw blue expanse, fringed with green. The sky among the treestops looked into his face, for he had come down upon his back. The rabbits had felled him. They were swarming around and upon him, their feet striking like great raindrops, incessantly and with precision—a rhythm that sapped his strength and his consciousness—again and again, on the same places.

How could he escape these airy blows and kicks? There seemed one way to crawl along—but it would lead to the cave, where Ransome had been. And once caught there, they’d have him. They’d dance upon him forever and forever, until he died, torn and bled to death by uncountable strokes—it would be like the falling of water upon a Chinese victim of the old drop-death—

“Say the words!” beseeched Pitts tearfully, his voice faint as an echo. “Say the words—howdy—”

===Chinese===

它们缓缓地、平滑地合围过来，流动如一摊油脂——只是这油面上浮满了皮毛和绿黑的眼睛，还长出一片草场似的耳朵。普瑟文特举起倒握的猎枪，摆好架势要砸。领头兔猛地朝他跃来。普瑟文特抡起枪，像击球员挥棒打球。这一击断无落空之理——可枪在空里嗖地掠过，细细一声，那具筋腱结实的小小躯体已经撞上了他的喉底。片刻之后，更多的兔子朝他扑来——十来只，几十只，成百只。他抡着枪乱扫，一下也没碰着。他在那阵轰炸之下摇晃，却站住了脚——他比兰塞姆更强壮、更魁伟，要打倒他，还得再费一番捶打——

“念那句话，法官！”皮茨的声音从圈外恳求他。“它们不是真兔子——它们会要了你的命！”

一边招架，一边抓挠着那阵拳脚如雨的捶打，普瑟文特发觉自己的心思从搏斗中转开，去琢磨另一件事了。那印第安人，莫什王，管兔子叫什么来着？奥托特蒙。怪词。可听着耳熟……忽然他看见一片蔚蓝，镶着绿边。树梢间的天空正俯视他的脸——他已经仰面倒在地上了。兔子们把他放倒了。它们在他周身和身上蜂拥，脚掌像大雨点一样落下，不停歇、不含糊——一种节奏，一寸寸抽干他的气力和知觉——一次又一次，落在同一些地方。

他怎么逃开这些无形的拳脚？看来只有一条道可以爬——可那条道通向山洞，兰塞姆就是栽在那儿的。一旦在那儿被堵住，它们就有他了。它们会在他身上永远永远地跳下去，直到他死了，被数不清的捶击撕烂、放干鲜血而死——就像旧时中国那种滴水酷刑，水一滴一滴落在受刑人身上——

“念那句话呀！”皮茨哭着哀告，声音微弱得像回声。“念那句话——你好啊——”

===Original===

*Ototemon*—the term meant something sacred to the Indians. And the minister, Mr. Horton, had gone on record as saying that the honest faith of savages could be respected, must be respected—

Somehow he got upon his feet, and lifted his hands as Pitts had done.

“Howdy,” he mumbled thickly. “Howdy, Mister Rabbit.”

And he stumbled and staggered away. Nothing prevented him. Pitts’s hand caught his arm, supporting him. He was safe, being led downhill.

“Who’ll believe?” he was saying to himself. “Who’ll believe? …”

===Chinese===

奥托特蒙——这个词在印第安人那里，指的是神圣之物。还有那位牧师，霍顿先生，档案里白纸黑字：蛮人的诚挚信仰可以尊重，必须尊重——

他不知怎么撑起了身，像皮茨方才那样举起了双手。

“你好啊，”他口齿含混地咕哝。“你好啊，兔子先生。”

于是他跌跌撞撞、踉踉跄跄地走开去。没有什么拦他。皮茨的手搀住他的胳膊，扶着他。他安全了，被人领着下山。

“谁会信呢？”他自言自语着。“谁会信呢？……”

===Original===

“Don’t worry. Judge,” Pitts replied. “We’re all right now. And this has happened before—all the folks say that the rabbits kill people near that cave. When some stranger drops out of sight, the folks go look for them and bury them—it ain’t thought strange any more—I’ll get a couple of men from town to help me bring back Mr. Ransome—”

Pursuivant was content to leave it at that. Later he would write and make an inquiry of Dr. Trowbridge, de Grandin’s friend and fellow-scholar of the occult.

===Chinese===

“别担心，法官，”皮茨答道。“咱们这会儿没事了。这种事从前也出过——乡亲们都说，靠近那洞的兔子会杀人。有外乡人没了踪影，乡亲们就去找他们、把他们埋了——谁也不觉得奇怪了——我去镇上找两个人，帮我把兰塞姆先生抬回来——”

事情就这样了结，普瑟文特也乐得由它去。回头他要写封信，向特罗布里奇医生（Dr. Trowbridge）讨教一二——他是德·格朗丹的朋友，也是钻研灵异之学的同好。

---

===Original===

Trowbridge’s letter came after the judge had returned to New York.

> My Dear Pursuivant:
> The meaning of the word *ototemon* should betray itself because of the familiarity of its corruption—*totem*. It’s Algonquin and, as well as I can establish, means a local sacrosanctity, generally embodied in some animal. A tribe or clan or community would claim that such animals were in reality the reincarnated spirits of dead ancestors, and full of supernatural power for good or evil.
> I was sorry to hear about Ransome’s death. Why are you so mysterious? De Grandin joins me in inviting you out to Huntingdon, to tell us about it. We have a strange story or two of our own that might intrigue you.
> Yours, etc.,

And Trowbridge’s almost indecipherable signature wound it up.

===Chinese===

法官回到纽约之后，特罗布里奇的信到了。

> 亲爱的普瑟文特：
> “奥托特蒙”（ototemon）一词的含义本该不点自破，因为由它讹变而来的那个词——“图腾”（totem）——人人耳熟。这是个阿尔冈昆词，据我所能考订，意指一种地方性的神圣之物，通常体现为某种动物。一个部落、氏族或社区会宣称，这类动物实为亡故祖先转世之灵，满载超自然的力量，为善为恶皆有可能。
> 惊闻兰塞姆之死，深为哀悼。你何必这样讳莫如深？德·格朗丹与我一同邀你来亨廷顿（Huntingdon）做客，讲给我们听听。我们自己手里也有一两个离奇的故事，或许能勾起你的兴致。
> 专此，余不一一。

信尾，是特罗布里奇那笔几乎没法辨认的签名。

===Original===

Pursuivant laid down the letter and reasoned himself out of any sense of defeat. He had wanted to respect the custom from the first, had blamed Ransome for defying it. Mr. Horton, the long-dead minister of Crispinville, had felt the same. *“We had precedent in that the first Missionaries to Britain did respect and observe certain festivals. …”* It might be heathen to greet a rabbit, yet it was part of formal and sincere religion. And when you were in Crispinville, you should do what the Crispinvillagers did.

Judge Pursuivant decided not to feel fouled by his experience. Only he would never look at a rabbit again, and keep his heart from thumping nervously.

===Chinese===

普瑟文特放下信，一番自我开导，把挫败感排解掉了。他从头一天起就想尊重那风俗，还怪过兰塞姆不把它放在眼里。克里斯平维尔那位早已作古的霍顿牧师，当年也是这个想法。“我们有先例可援：最先到不列颠去的传教士，也曾尊重并遵行某些节期。……”向一只兔子问好，这兴许是异教行径，可它是一套正式而虔诚的宗教的一部分。到了克里斯平维尔，就得随克里斯平维尔人。

普瑟文特法官打定主意，不为这段遭遇觉得自己脏了。只不过，他从此再也不看兔子一眼，还要按住心口，不让它咚咚地慌跳。

## The Half-Haunted / 半闹鬼之屋

===Original===
> I went into a house, and it wasn’t a house.

===Chinese===
> 我走进一座房子，而它已不是一座房子。

===Original===
For six months Judge Pursuivant had intended to visit that old dwelling with the strange history, but Judge Pursuivant often has trouble finding time to do what he most wants. The fall passed, the winter came. He spent Christmas, not very joyfully, helping the widow of a friend repossess some property at Salem. New Year’s Eve found him at Harrisonville, where de Graudin and Towbridge wanted his word on translating certain old Dutch documents better left untranslated. Heading west and south toward his home, he passed Scott’s Meadows. And, though it was nearly dark and snowy, he could not resist the opportunity to visit Criley’s Mill then and there.

A druggist on the little main street gave him directions. The judge drove up a steep ill-paved road, then between hills crowned with naked trees. Eventually he came to an old quarry road and followed it to here, across a rapid brown brook, a creaky bridge led to the place.

By the last rays of the sun, he decided he had either come the wrong way or come too late.

He had heard of a tall, gaunt building, the ruins of a mill house﻿—a place two hundred years old, that looked two thousand. This was almost the opposite﻿—quite new, of brown shingles, low and rambling, with a screened porch and wide windows. The place should have been cheerful, but it was not.

===Chinese===
六个月来，普瑟文特法官（Judge Pursuivant）一直打算去拜访那座有着离奇来历的老宅，可他最想做的事，偏偏总是挤不出工夫。秋天过去了，冬天来了。圣诞节那天他过得并不怎么快活——帮一位朋友的遗孀去塞勒姆（Salem）收回一处房产。新年前夜，他在哈里森维尔（Harrisonville），德格罗丹（de Graudin）和托布里奇（Towbridge）两位请他拿主意，说有几份旧荷兰文档案要不要译出来——那种东西，照理还是不译为妙。他朝西再折南往家里去，路过斯科特草甸（Scott's Meadows）。天色将暮，又落着雪，他还是禁不住这个机会，当场就要去一趟克里利磨坊（Criley's Mill）。

小街上一位药剂师给他指了路。法官驱车上一条陡峭的破路，穿过山顶只剩光秃秃树冠的丘陵。最后他驶上一条老采石场路，一路跟到这儿；跨过一道湍急的褐色溪流，一座吱呀作响的桥通向那地方。

借着落日最后一抹余光，他断定：自己不是走错了路，就是来得太迟。

他听人说的是一座高瘦嶙峋的屋宇，磨坊宅的残骸——两百年的老屋，看着足有两千年。眼前这座几乎恰恰相反——崭新，棕色墙板，低矮而铺展，带纱窗门廊和大窗户。这地方本该叫人心头一亮，偏偏不是。

===Original===
Pursuivant drove across, got out and knocked at the door. Snow began to shimmer down. Lights went on in the front room, and a man opened the door. He was small and slim, with a gray forelock and a lined, shrewd face, reminiscent of the late Will Rogers. He wore a smoking jacket and slippers.

“Yes?” he half challenged.

“Excuse me,” replied Pursuivant, hunching his massive shoulders, “but is this Criley’s Mill? The haunted house?”

“Haunted?” echoed the man on the threshold. “Why, I﻿—I don’t know.”

There seemed to be only one thing to say. Pursuivant shook snowflakes from his tawny mustache and said it. “I’m sorry to have troubled you. I seem to have made a mistake.”

===Chinese===
普瑟文特把车开过桥去，下车敲门。雪片开始纷纷扬扬地闪落。前屋亮起灯，一个男人开了门。他瘦小利落，一绺灰色的额发，一张皱纹纵横、精明机警的脸，让人想起已故的威尔·罗杰斯（Will Rogers）。他穿着家常吸烟服和拖鞋。

“有什么事？”他半带挑衅地问。

“冒昧打扰，”普瑟文特耸起宽厚的肩膀答道，“请问这里是克里利磨坊吗？就是那座凶宅？”

“凶宅？”门口的人跟着念了一遍。“这个，我——我说不上来。”

看来只有一句话好说了。普瑟文特抖落黄褐色胡子上的雪花，把这句话说了出来。“对不起，打扰您了。看来我是弄错了。”

===Original===
At once the other changed his manner “Oh, no, sir. No mistake. This *was* the place. You see, I built where Criley’s Mill was﻿—just finished and moved in on Thanksgiving﻿—look here, won’t you come in? I’m sorry if I was abrupt. Just nerves. I didn’t know who might be coming to my door﻿—so far away from everything﻿—”

His gaunt little hand caught at Pursuivant’s big one. “Come in, sir. Or﻿—wait. It’s putting on to snow. I’ve got a double garage around back. Want to slide your car in with mine? Then we’ll have a drink. Maybe a bite to eat.”

He wanted Pursuivant to stay. The judge gazed at him with big blue eyes, deceptively innocent. Then he nodded and said, “Thanks. I’ll be very glad to stay.”

===Chinese===
对方立刻换了副神气。“哦不，先生，没错。这里从前正是那地方。您瞧，我就在克里利磨坊的旧址上盖的房——感恩节刚盖完搬进来——请进来坐坐，好吗？方才是我失礼了。纯粹是神经紧张。这地方前不着村后不着店，我真不知道上门的会是什么人——”

他那只瘦小干枯的手一把攥住普瑟文特的大手。“请进，先生。哦——等等，眼看雪要下大了。我后头有个双车位车库。把您的车挪进来跟我的并排放？然后咱们喝一杯，再随便吃点。”

他是想留普瑟文特住下。法官用那双蓝汪汪的大眼睛打量他，眼神天真得骗人。随后他点头道：“多谢。我很乐意住下。”

---

===Original===
After stowing the car, he returned through the snow. The little man still waited at the door to usher him in. “What did you say your name was?”

The judge had not said, but he replied, “Pursuivant. Judge Keith Pursuivant. I’m interested in haunted houses.”

“And I’m Alvin Scrope﻿—country editor, retired, bachelor.” They were in the front room now, a room designed to answer a man’s prayer for comfort. It had cushioned furniture, thick rugs, bright pictures, plenty of light, a shelf of books. But, as outside, the cheer was somehow lacking. “You’ll have to pardon me,” said Alvin Scrope. “My house boy left here New Year’s eve, and I’m running the place alone for a day or so.”

===Chinese===
停好车后，他冒雪回来。小个子仍在门口候着，引他进屋。“您方才说贵姓来着？”

法官其实没说过，但他答道：“普瑟文特。基思·普瑟文特法官。我对凶宅感兴趣。”

“我叫阿尔文·斯克罗普（Alvin Scrope）——乡间小报的编辑，退了休，光棍一条。”此刻他们已进了前屋；这间屋子，简直照着男人对安逸的全部祈愿设计出来。软垫家具，厚地毯，明快的画，充足的光线，还有一书架的书。可是跟外头一样，那股亮堂劲儿不知怎的就是欠着。“您得多担待，”阿尔文·斯克罗普说，“我家男仆新年前夜走了，这一两天就我一个人张罗。”

===Original===
From a side table he lifted a bottle of scotch and a syphon. Mixing two highballs, he gave one to Pursuivant. “Snow’s coming down harder. You’d better plan to stay the night.”

Pursuivant laid aside overcoat and black hat. “You are very kind,” he said, wondering why he had been half-rebuffed at first, then almost wheedled into entering. Alvin Scrope dabbed at his forelock.

“Yes, sir,” he said, trying to sound hearty, “I built this right where the old mill stood. How d’you like it?”

The judge fitted his big body into an armchair, and sipped. “I don’t quite know yet. I’ve only come. How do you like it yourself, Mr. Scrope?”

===Chinese===
他从一张边桌上拿起一瓶苏格兰威士忌和一个苏打水瓶，调了两杯威士忌苏打，递一杯给普瑟文特。“雪越下越紧了。您最好打定主意今晚就住这儿。”

普瑟文特脱下大衣，摘下黑帽子。“您太客气了，”他说，心里纳闷：先前为何吃了半个闭门羹，随后又被半哄半劝地让进门来。阿尔文·斯克罗普按了按额前的头发。

“是啊，先生，”他说，竭力做出热络的口气，“我就在老磨坊立的地方盖了这房子。您瞧着怎么样？”

法官把他硕大的身躯安进一张扶手椅，抿了一口。“一时还说不上。我才刚到。您自己觉得怎么样，斯克罗普先生？”

===Original===
Another dab at the forelock. “To tell the truth, I don’t know either.” He, too, drank before continuing. “Maybe because I’ve never had a place of my own before. And I’ve been used to working, always on the go with my paper﻿—now I’m a little lost with all the slack time on my hands. You know how that is. But when I first saw the spot, with the ruined mill and all, stuck away here, I thought it was as nice a building site as I’d ever heard of.”

“I’ve been told a little about the mill and its legend,” ventured Pursuivant, rummaging a pocket for his pipe. At once his host began the tale, as Pursuivant had hoped:

“The place, I understand, was built before the War of Independence. It was owned and run by a man named Criley. He had a wife, a son and a daughter.”

“Mind if I take notes?” asked Pursuivant, producing notebook and pen. “Go on, Mr. Scrope.”

“Well, the war came. The miller and his son joined Washington’s army. The British took New York, and there was a long, hard scrap to see whether they’d stop there or take the rest of the country, too.”

===Chinese===
又按了按额发。“说实话，我也说不上来。”他也喝了一口，才接着说。“许是我从前从来没一处自己的房子。我干活干惯了，整天围着报纸转——如今闲工夫一多，反倒有些没着没落。您懂那滋味。可我头一回瞧见这地方——废磨坊立在这儿，四下里僻静得很——我就想，这是我听说过的最好的宅基地。”

“关于这磨坊和它的传说，我也略有所闻，”普瑟文特一面在口袋里摸烟斗，一面试探着说。主人立刻讲起故事来，正中普瑟文特下怀：

“据我所知，这宅子建于独立战争之前。主人叫克里利（Criley），磨坊也是他开的。他有老婆，一儿一女。”

“我记记笔记您不介意吧？”普瑟文特掏出笔记本和钢笔。“请讲，斯克罗普先生。”

“呃，后来打起仗来了。磨坊主带着儿子投了华盛顿的军队。英国人占了纽约，接着是一场漫长艰苦的恶斗，要看他们是就在那儿收手，还是索性把全国其余的地方也一并吞掉。”

---

===Original===
Pursuivant nodded. He knew that dark, desperate phase of his nation’s history. After the first disaster to American arms, the fighting had taken on the somber complexion of raids, ambuscades, betrayals. Considerable savagery on both sides. Nathan Hale and John Andre﻿—two fine gentlemen﻿—hanged like felons. Thousands of other tragedies. All the New York area﻿—including this part of New Jersey﻿—stock full of grim deeds, giving rise to creepy tales.

Scrope went on:

“New York had quite a few Hessian soldiers stationed around﻿—hired to fight the Americans, you know.”

Again Pursuivant nodded. His Virginian ancestor had followed Washington in the battle of Trenton. “The Hessians weren’t very fierce fighters,” he commented.

===Chinese===
普瑟文特点点头。本国历史上那段黑暗绝命的岁月，他了然于胸。美国军队初战失利之后，战事便换了一副阴沉的面孔：奔袭，伏击，出卖，双方都凶残得很。内森·黑尔（Nathan Hale）、约翰·安德烈（John Andre）——两位堂堂君子——竟像重罪犯一样被送上绞架。此外还有成千上万桩惨剧。整个纽约地区——包括新泽西这一带——满满当当塞着惨酷的勾当，也就生出种种阴森森的传说。

斯克罗普接着讲：

“纽约一带驻了不少黑森兵（Hessian）——雇来打美国人的，您知道。”

普瑟文特又点了点头。他的一位弗吉尼亚先祖，当年追随华盛顿打过特伦顿（Trenton）战役。“黑森兵算不得多么能打，”他评了一句。

===Original===
“There was an exception to that rule,” Scrope declared pithily. “Still taking notes, Judge?﻿—I can’t tell you this particular Hessian’s name, but it comes down in the story how he looked. Big as you, I figure. Burly. He was a famous hunter back home in Germany. Maybe a criminal, joining the army to escape﻿—Anyway, he could beat the Americans at their own game of hunt and shoot.”

“That’s hard to believe,” rejoined Pursuivant. “Some of Washington’s men were hard-set old Indian fighters.”

“This Hessian outdid the Indians. He’d strip naked﻿—even in winter﻿—and paint himself like a Mohawk and sally out to kill. He was a dead shot, and a devil with sword or hatchet or knife.” Scrope paused to bite the end off a cigar. “He could track or stalk anything, and he’d fight two soldiers at a time. Sometimes more. He raided farms and murdered civilians, even women and children. Quite a score he ran up.”

Scribbling in his book, the judge could see in his mind one of those fancy-portraits so often vivid﻿—a naked colossus, streaked with red and black, a heavy-boned face, thick, pale brows over slitted eyes. A belt stuck full of weapons. Had the Hessian looked like that? Pursuivant filled his pipe and thrust it under his mustache. “Go on,” he prompted.

===Chinese===
“这条规矩有个例外，”斯克罗普说得干脆利落。“法官，笔记还记着呢？——这名黑森兵姓甚名谁，我说不上来，可他生得什么模样，故事里一代代传了下来。块头跟您不相上下，我估摸。虎背熊腰。他在德国老家就是个远近闻名的猎户。没准儿还是个罪犯，当兵是为躲官司——反正，美国人自己那套打猎放枪的把式，他玩得比美国人还精。”

“这可难叫人相信，”普瑟文特应道。“华盛顿麾下有些人是跟印第安人摸爬滚打出来的老硬汉。”

“这个黑森兵比印第安人还厉害。他能剥个精光——大冬天也照剥不误——浑身涂上莫霍克人（Mohawk）的花纹出外杀人。他是百步穿杨的神枪手，使起刀剑、战斧、匕首来更是个煞星。”斯克罗普停下来，咬掉雪茄的烟头。“论追踪潜伏，什么猎物也逃不过他，他一个人能同时对付两个兵，有时候更多。他袭掠农场，屠戮平民，连女人孩子也不放过。他手上血债累累。

法官一面在本子上刷刷地记，脑海里已浮出那类往往鲜活如真的想象画像——一个赤裸的巨汉，涂着红黑相间的条纹，颧骨粗重的一张脸，浓白的眉毛压着眯缝的眼。腰带上插满兵器。那黑森兵就是这副模样吗？普瑟文特装上烟斗，把烟嘴送到胡子底下。“讲下去。”他催道。

===Original===
“The two women left here at the mill hated and feared that Hessian. They plotted against him. Pretended to be British sympathizers, and scraped an acquaintance.”

“That was nervy of them,” commented the judge. His mind’s eye showed him new pictures. Probably the daughter made the overture﻿—buxom, rosy-cheeked on a chill afternoon, she managed to encounter the man of blood on a country lane. The Hessian would be a heavy-handed gallant. His broad, tough face grinned admiringly. The rural beauty, trying not to tremble, would venture a return smile, a curtsey.

“They invited him to dinner,” Scrope continued. “He put on his best uniform﻿—”

Strange that Hessian butcher would look in full dress﻿—white small-clothes and gaiters modelling his brawny legs, the red coat with white facings and shiny buttons cramping his barrel torso. How out of place the powdered hair, the tall grenadier cap! But Scrope was getting on to the climax:

===Chinese===
“留在磨坊的两个女人恨那黑森兵，又怕他。她们密谋对付他。装成亲英派，好歹攀上了交情。”

“这胆子可真够大的，”法官评道。他心里的眼睛又映出新的画面。多半是女儿先递的话——一个凉飕飕的午后，姑娘丰腴的脸蛋红扑扑的，设法在乡间小路上“偶遇”那个杀人不眨眼的凶神。黑森兵做殷勤客，想必笨手笨脚。他那张宽大粗硬的脸咧开一副倾倒的笑。乡下的美人儿强撑着不让身子发抖，才敢回他一笑，行一个屈膝礼。

“她们请他吃饭，”斯克罗普继续道。“他穿上了最好的军礼服——”

那黑森屠夫盛装起来该是什么光景——白色的紧身裤袜和绑腿箍着两条粗壮的腿，红呢军装配白领亮扣，紧箍着桶一样的胸膛。扑了粉的头发，掷弹兵的高帽，多么不伦不类！

===Original===
“When he sat down at the table, one of the women﻿—mother or daughter, the stories disagree﻿—stuck a serving knife into his back. They got rid of the body somehow﻿—walled it up or buried it in the cellar. But the spirit returned.”

“How many saw it?” demanded Pursuivant.

“Many. The mother died of fright, and the daughter of jumping from an attic window, before the year was out. The son committed suicide before he’d been long back from the war﻿—nobody says anything about the father, I guess he was killed in some battle. Well, that disposed of the family. The mill went out of use. There’s lots of newer yarns. A girl from Scott’s Meadows yonder stayed one night ten years ago, on a dare. Next morning she was roaming around, too crazy to talk.”

“And you bought the place?”

“Yes. Tore down the old mill house, and rebuilt on its foundations. Shouldn’t that lay any ghosts, Judge Pursuivant?”

===Chinese===
“他在桌边刚坐下，两个女人里的一个——当妈的还是当女儿的，说法不一——一把餐刀捅进了他后心。尸首她们想法子处理了——或是砌进墙里，或是埋进地窖。可那魂灵回来了。”

“多少人见过？”普瑟文特追问。

“多着呢。不出一年，当妈的活活吓死，当女儿的从阁楼窗户跳了下去。儿子从战场回来没多久也自尽了——当爹的事谁也没提过，我猜是死在哪一仗里了。得，这一家子算是了账了。磨坊也废了。往后新编的段子还多得很。十年前，那边斯科特草甸的一个姑娘跟人打赌，在这儿住了一夜。第二天一早就在四处乱转，疯得连话都说不成了。”

“然后您把这块地买下来了？”

“买了。拆了老磨坊宅，在旧地基上重新盖的。这总该能送走鬼魂了吧，普瑟文特法官？”

===Original===
“Most rebuilders prefer to burn the haunted place entirely,” said the judge. “However, that depends on how much they believe in ghosts. I take it that you don’t laugh at these stories.”

Scrope almost bit his cigar in two. “Would you laugh,” he asked, “if two houseboys quit on you inside of six weeks? If something followed you all around your cellar, something cold and sneaky, that wasn’t ever there when you turned around? If you fidgeted all the time, like at a play by Ibsen or a story by Poe? It’s no laughing matter, Judge.”

Pursuivant leaned forward. “You imagine disturbing sights and sounds?”

“Right. Never quite see or hear them﻿—just a whisper, a shadow in dim places, when I’m all alone here. I wish,” and Scrope grew somber, “that I belonged to some classical old church. A priest, with bell, book and candle, would be mighty comforting.”

===Chinese===
“多数重建的人，宁可把闹鬼的宅子一把火烧个干净，”法官说。“不过，这也得看他们信鬼信到几分。我看，您对这些故事并不是一笑了之的吧。”

斯克罗普险些把雪茄咬成两截。“换了您，您笑得出来吗，”他问，“要是六个礼拜里头，两个男仆先后撂了挑子？要是有个东西在地窖里一路缀着您，又冷又贼，可您一转身它又没了影？要是您成天心惊肉跳，像看易卜生（Ibsen）的戏、读爱伦·坡（Poe）的小说？法官，这可不是闹着玩的事。”

普瑟文特俯身向前。“您想象出了搅扰人的光影和声响？”

“正是。从来瞧不真、听不真——只有一声低语，昏暗角落里一道影子，在我一个人守着这屋子的时候。我倒盼着，”斯克罗普神色沉了下来，“自己归的是个老派教会。有位教士，捧着钟、书、蜡烛来，那才叫人安心呢。”

===Original===
“Just so,” agreed Pursuivant. “It so happens that I know an old formula of exorcism. I’m not a clergyman, but I offer it for what it’s worth, as charm or psychological clearance.”

Scrope frowned, then smiled. The subject was new to him. Pursuivant made haste to be logical: “I’m not trying to make an occult convert out of you, Mr. Scrope. But it seems that a symbol or ceremony might serve as rationalization﻿—a psychological peg to hang your worries on and forget them entirely﻿—”

“Right as a rabbit!” cried Scrope, almost explosively. “Go ahead. Judge. Do it.”

Pursuivant set down glass and pipe, and stood up. Scrope also rose from his chair. In so doing he moved backward and stood almost by the darkened door that led to the rear of the house. Pursuivant began solemnly:

===Chinese===
“正是这话，”普瑟文特赞同道。“碰巧我知道一道古老的驱邪咒文。我不是神职人员，但这咒文权当它是一道护符，或者一回心理大扫除，值多少算多少，我奉献出来。”

斯克罗普皱起眉，随即又笑了。这个题目于他是件新鲜事。普瑟文特赶紧把话讲得在理：“我并不是要把您度进神秘教门，斯克罗普先生。不过，一个象征、一场仪式，也许正好充当一回‘合理化’——给您的心事一个挂钩，往上一挂，就此忘个干净——”

“对极啦！”斯克罗普几乎炸雷般喊出来。“来吧，法官。念吧。”

普瑟文特放下杯子和烟斗，站起身。斯克罗普也从椅子里起身；起身的当口往后一让，几乎站到那扇通往屋子后部的黑黢黢的门跟前。普瑟文特庄重地开口：

===Original===
“All ye evil spirits, I forbid you this man’s bed, his couch; I forbid you, in heaven’s name, his house and home; I forbid you, in the name of God, his blood and flesh, his body and soul. Let all evil return from him and his, unto you and yours, in the name of the Trinity.”

He finished, and Scrope’s face showed a sudden thankful relief﻿—which went out like a light. Scrope’s thin body suddenly gyrated, reeled. His mouth opened, shouting:

“Let go! Let go!”

===Chinese===
“一切恶灵，我禁绝你们染指这人的床榻卧铺；我以天国之名，禁绝你们染指他的家宅居所；我奉上帝之名，禁绝你们染指他的血肉、他的身躯与灵魂。愿一切邪祟，从他与他的一切，退还你们与你们的一切，奉三位一体之名。”

他念完了，斯克罗普脸上顿时露出感激的宽慰——那神情又像一盏灯似的倏然熄灭。斯克罗普瘦削的身子猛地拧了一下，踉跄摇晃。他张大了嘴，狂喊：

“放开我！放开我！”

---

===Original===
He staggered backward to the door, turned halfway and braced himself against the jamb. He seemed to struggle with something beyond. Pursuivant sprang toward him, and at that instant Scrope was walking shakily back toward the center of the room. His eyes were glassy, his lips slack, his face pale.

“Thought it had me,” he panted.

“What?” demanded Pursuivant, quickly pouring whiskey.

“Didn’t you see? That big thing with a naked arm﻿—and no eyes﻿—”

“Drink this. I saw nothing.”

===Chinese===
他跌跌撞撞退到门边，半侧过身，撑在门框上。他像是在跟什么看不见的东西角力。普瑟文特朝他扑过去，而就在那一瞬，斯克罗普已摇摇晃晃地朝屋子中央走回来了。他眼神发直，嘴唇松垂，脸色灰白。

“还当它抓住我了。”他喘着气说。

“什么？”普瑟文特追问，赶紧斟威士忌。

“您没瞧见吗？那个大家伙，光着一条胳膊——还没有眼睛——”

“把这喝了。我什么也没瞧见。”

===Original===
Scrope drank obediently. Color returned a little. He spoke rapidly, as one who convinces himself of a hopeful fact: “My imagination ran away with me, didn’t it?”

“Did it?”

Pursuivant filled Scrope’s glass again. Plainly Scrope was trying to save his nerves by chatter. “Oh, it’s quite clear, Judge. I’ve keyed up my imagination to what seems like reality. I was sure some sort of boogey﻿—but if you didn’t see it﻿—”

“If I didn’t see it,” Pursuivant took up Scrope’s words, “there is still no proof that it doesn’t exist.”

Scrope looked blank, and Pursuivant continued, “I take nothing for granted. This looks like the beginning of one of my adventures.”

===Chinese===
斯克罗普顺从地喝了。脸色稍稍回暖。他急急地说，像一个人硬要说自己信下一件有望的好事：“是我自己的想象脱了缰，对不对？”

“是吗？”

普瑟文特又给斯克罗普的杯子满上。明摆着，斯克罗普是想靠说话来稳住神经。“哦，这再清楚不过了，法官。我把自己的想象力越绷越紧，绷出了跟真的一样。我方才满心以为有什么鬼东西——可既然您没瞧见——”

“就算我没瞧见，”普瑟文特接过斯克罗普的话头，“也仍旧证明不了它不存在。”

斯克罗普一脸茫然。普瑟文特接着说：“我从不把任何事当成理所当然。看来，我的一场历险就要开场了。”

===Original===
“But look here!” Scrope suddenly went a little wild in his speech. “You were reciting a spell against just that sort of thing. Why should﻿—it﻿—dare to tackle﻿—”

“Desperation. To stave off defeat. Wait here.”

He went to the inner door and peered. There was a dim hallway to a kitchen, an open doorway for a bathroom at the left, and two closed doors to the right. He asked about them.

“Bedroom,” replied Scrope, steadying his voice. “Want a light?”

“No, thanks.” Pursuivant entered the hall.

===Chinese===
“可是您瞧！”斯克罗普的话音忽然带上了狂气。“您念的那道咒语，克的正是这一路东西。它凭什么——敢——扑上来——”

“那是绝路里的拼命。为的是不让败局落定。在这儿等着。”

他走到内门前往里张望。一条昏暗的过道通往厨房，左手一扇敞开的门是浴室，右手两扇关着的门。他问起那两间。

“卧室，”斯克罗普答道，勉强稳住嗓音。“要开个灯吗？”

“不必了，谢谢。”普瑟文特走进过道。

---

===Original===
It was like stepping into a fog﻿—into the vapor, for instance, of many damp, filthy coats in a sealed closet. Pursuivant snorted, and walked quickly through to the kitchen, turning on a light. Breathing was comfortable there. The sweat dried on his brow and his tawny mustache.

“All clear?” Scrope was asking.

“So far.” The judge gazed around the clean white kitchen, with automatic refrigerator and electric range. It was the most reassuring room so far. He walked back into the hall, then into the rear bedroom.

“That’s my room,” Scrope informed him, from the parlor door.

Pursuivant waited only a moment in the chamber, which filled the rear quarter opposite the kitchen. Then into the hall yet again, to glance into the bathroom. It was a fight to throw off the smothering spiritual weight hanging in the dim atmosphere. Finally to the closed door of the front bedroom. “Who sleeps here?” he asked, hand on knob.

===Chinese===
像一步跨进了浓雾——打个比方，像跨进一只密闭衣柜里许多件潮乎乎的脏外衣蒸出的那股水汽。普瑟文特哼了一声，快步径直穿到厨房，开了灯。在那里呼吸才畅快。他额头和黄褐色小胡子上的汗这才收干。

“没事吧？”斯克罗普在问。

“到目前为止还好。”法官环视这间白净的厨房，有自动冰箱，有电灶。这是到那时为止最叫人踏实的一间屋。他走回过道，再进了后卧室。

“那是我的屋，”斯克罗普从客厅门口告诉他。

普瑟文特在那间屋子里只略站了站——那屋子占着与厨房斜相对的后侧一角。随后又回到过道，往浴室里望了一眼。要把弥漫在这昏暗空气里的、压得人透不过气来的阴重之气抖落掉，简直是一场角力。最后他来到前卧室那扇关着的门前。“这儿谁睡？”他手搭在门钮上问。

===Original===
“You will, if you stay tonight,” Scrope replied, and the judge entered.

In the first instant he thought he had been struck﻿—his knees wavered, his brain swam and darkened. The walls﻿—weren’t they ruinous, flaking away?﻿—whirled around him in the gloom. But he kept his feet and his head, groped for the light switch, turned it.

He had been wrong. The room was quite modern, cream-papered, and should be bright; but the light was as murky as though it shone through smoke. A neat single bed, a bureau, an armchair﻿—how could that arrangement cause such a deep shadow in the far corner? Or was it a shadow?

The weight he had felt in the hall was doubled here, crushing him as a diver is crushed by sea-bottom pressures. The switch clicked, though Pursuivant had not touched it. The light went out abruptly.

===Chinese===
“您啊，要是您今晚住下的话，”斯克罗普答道。法官推门进去。

头一瞬他还当自己挨了打——膝盖发晃，脑子发晕发黑。四壁——那不是朽坏剥落了吗？——在幽暗里绕着他打转。可他站住了脚，也稳住了神，摸索到电灯开关，拧亮了。

是他错了。这屋子十分新式，贴着奶油色墙纸，本该亮堂堂的；可灯光浑浊晦暗，像是隔着烟照过来似的。一张整洁的单人床，一个五斗柜，一把扶手椅——这么点陈设，怎会在远处的墙角投下那么深的一团黑影？还是说，那不是影子？

他在过道里就感到的那股重压，在这儿翻了一倍，压得他像潜水人被海底的水压挤扁。开关咔哒一响——普瑟文特根本没碰它。灯蓦地灭了。

===Original===
Something pawed at him through the darkness. A hand﻿—he saw it dimly, but not its arm. Was there an arm? Pursuivant jerked away, but refused to retreat. Now a face hung in the thick dusk﻿—a head, anyway, for he made out the contour only, not the features. But it must have a mouth. For he felt a fanning of tepid breath, heard a mumble that became a word of sorts:

“*Raus.*﻿ ﻿…”

German. Get out!

Pursuivant stared at the hanging oval, trying to find eyes to fix with his own. Now another touch, at his shoulder. Light this time. Fluffy. Another voice, so soft as to be felt rather than heard:

“No﻿ ﻿… stay﻿ ﻿… you came to save.﻿ ﻿…”

The featureless head became more solid, and a suggestion of body was visible beneath﻿—thick, as big as Pursuivant’s own body. Wide-planted columns that might be mist-moulded legs. Again: “*Raus!*”

===Chinese===
黑暗里有什么东西在抓挠他。一只手——他影影绰绰看见了，却看不见胳膊。到底有没有胳膊？普瑟文特猛地一挣，但拒不后退。这时一张脸悬在浓稠的昏暝里——至少是一颗头，因为他只辨得出轮廓，辨不出眉目。可它必定有嘴。因为他觉出一股温吞吞的气息扇上脸来，听见一阵含混的咕哝，渐渐拼成了一个词：

“*Raus*。”……

德语，是“滚出去”的意思。

普瑟文特盯着那悬着的椭圆，想找出一双眼睛来跟自己对视。这时又是一触，落在他肩上。这一回很轻，毛茸茸的。另一个声音，轻得与其说是听见，不如说是感觉到：

“不……别走……你是来救的……”

那颗没有五官的头凝实了几分，底下隐约透出身子的轮廓——粗壮，块头赶得上普瑟文特本人。两根分得很开的柱子，怕是雾气塑成的两条腿。又是一声：“*Raus*！”

===Original===
Pursuivant backed from the room, leaving the door open. He was in the parlor again, wiping his face. He felt better.

Scrope, mixing more drinks, looked at him questioningly. “You felt it too, huh?”

“I felt something. For a moment I saw.” The judge paused to marshal his findings. “Who has ever slept in that front bedroom?”

“Nobody. The house-boy﻿—before he left﻿—had a lean-to off the kitchen. You’re inaugurating my guest room tonight, judge. Here, have a drink.”

They touched glasses and drank. Then they crossed the heavy-aired hall to the kitchen. Scrope quickly cooked a meal, simple but hearty﻿—ham, eggs, home-fried potatoes, strong coffee. They ate at a white-topped table. Pursuivant acted as though fear had not come to him that night.

===Chinese===
普瑟文特退出房去，让房门敞着。他回到客厅，抹了一把脸。他觉得好受些了。

斯克罗普正调着酒，疑问地望着他。“您也觉出它了，嗯？”

“我觉出了点什么。有一阵子，我也看见了。”法官顿了顿，把所见所闻理出头绪。“从前有谁在那间前卧室里睡过？”

“没人。原先那个男仆——走以前——睡厨房外头搭的披屋。您今晚是给我这间客房开张，法官。来，喝一杯。”

两人碰杯对饮。然后他们穿过那间空气沉滞的过道进厨房去。斯克罗普很快烧出一顿饭，简单而管饱——火腿、鸡蛋、家常煎土豆、浓咖啡。他们在一张白面小桌旁吃起来。普瑟文特的神气，仿佛今夜恐惧压根没沾过他的边。

===Original===
“I suggested that the Hessians weren’t good fighters,” he observed, holding out his cup, “but they were Germanic﻿—and Germany has been the home of witches and devils. Read *Faust*, read *Phantasmagoria*, read the Brothers Grimm. And in a file of *Old New York*﻿—out of print now﻿—I found a story of how two Hessian soldiers bewitched a Manhattan farmer.”

“True story?”

“It’s in the reminiscences of George Rapaelje. That’s a respected name in old New York history. Rapaelje claims to have seen it happen. Yes, and other Hessians﻿—settling in Pennsylvania and New Jersey﻿—worked magic.”

“Of course. Look at that Headless Horseman yarn of Irving’s,” contributed Scrope. “Judge, you’ve got something. If that spell you recited﻿—I wish you hadn’t, for it didn’t work.”

Pursuivant looked earnestly at Scrope. “I didn’t finish. It must be said three times, an hour apart.” He drew out a thick gold watch. “And an hour has passed, or nearly.”

===Chinese===
“我方才说黑森兵不善战，”他一面把杯子递过去一面说，“可他们是日耳曼人——而德国向来是女巫和魔鬼的家乡。去读读《浮士德》（Faust），读读《幻影集》（Phantasmagoria），读读格林兄弟。我还在一册《老纽约》（Old New York）——如今早已绝版——的旧刊合订里，读到过一个故事，讲两个黑森兵怎样迷住了一个曼哈顿农场主。”

“真事？”

“载在乔治·拉帕尔杰（George Rapaelje）的回忆录里。在老纽约的历史上，这可是个受人敬重的名字。拉帕尔杰自称亲眼看见。是啊，还有些黑森兵——在宾夕法尼亚和新泽西落户的——也行过法术。”

“可不是嘛。瞧瞧欧文那个无头骑士的故事，”斯克罗普附和道。“法官，您摸着门道了。要是您方才念的那道咒语——唉，我真宁愿您没念，因为它不灵。”

普瑟文特恳切地望着斯克罗普。“我没有念完。这咒必须念三遍，每隔一个钟头一遍。”他掏出一只厚实的金表。“一个钟头过去了，差不多。”

===Original===
Quite steadily, if not casually, he walked into the hall. Scrope came just behind. Again Pursuivant felt the baleful weight and closeness. Undaunted, he began to recite for the second time:

“All ye evil spirits, I forbid you this man’s bed, his couch; I forbid you, in heaven’s name, his house and home; I forbid﻿—”

It had come heavily, noiselessly, out of the front bedroom. A hunchbacked hulk of it, that straightened and showed itself as tall and powerful as Pursuivant.

The judge knew amazement, complete but rational. Even in the half-light, he made out only a silhouette, roughly human, vague at the edges﻿—clothed or naked, he could not say. As before, a faceless head lifted itself on broad shoulders. Only the fingers of the hand were distinct. They spread, advanced. Thus his eyes summed up, while he kept reciting the exorcism, down to its end:

“﻿—all evil return from him and his unto you and yours, in the name of the Trinity.”

It blundered forward, clutching.

===Chinese===
他迈步走进过道，脚步平稳，近乎闲庭信步。斯克罗普紧跟在后。普瑟文特又感到那股凶邪的重压和逼仄。他毫不畏缩，开始念第二遍：

“一切恶灵，我禁绝你们染指这人的床榻卧铺；我以天国之名，禁绝你们染指他的家宅居所；我禁绝——”

那东西从前面的客房里出来了，沉甸甸的，没有一丝声息。先是佝偻的一大团，随即直起身来，显出与普瑟文特不相上下的高大和强悍。

法官心头掠过惊愕——完完全全，却不失清明。即便在半明半暗里，他也只辨出一个剪影，粗具人形，四缘模糊——穿着衣裳还是赤身露体，说不上来。和先前一样，一颗没有脸的头颅昂在宽阔的双肩上。只有手上那几根指头看得真切。指头张开，进逼过来。他的眼睛就这样把一切收拢在案，嘴里仍旧把那道驱邪咒一路诵到底：

“——愿一切邪祟，从他与他的一切，退还你们与你们的一切，奉三位一体之名。”

它跌跌撞撞扑上来，五指乱抓。

===Original===
The doorway was no place to fight in, not even if the foe were normal. Pursuivant retreated, quickly and lightly for all his bearlike weight. Behind him, Scrope had run whimpering to the back door, tried to tear it open without unlocking.

“Come on!” Scrope was crying. “We’ll get out of here!”

“Wait!” called Pursuivant in reply. “Look!” And Scrope paused and turned back.

“The thing’s gone,” said Pursuivant. “It vanished before my eyes as I retreated.”

He clasped his big hands behind his back, scowling. Something was wrong here; absolutely unconventional﻿—for there is a certain unconventionality about demons and their ways.

===Chinese===
门洞里不是动手的地方，哪怕对头是个寻常人也一样。普瑟文特向后退去——以他熊一样的吨位，退得又快又轻。在他身后，斯克罗普呜咽着奔向后门，忘了开锁就硬去撕拽。

“快来！”斯克罗普在喊。“咱们逃出去！”

“等等！”普瑟文特回喊。“看哪！”斯克罗普一怔，回过头来。

“那东西没了，”普瑟文特说。“就在我退开的时候，它当着我的眼睛消散了。”

他把两只大手背到身后，眉头紧锁。这里头有什么不对头；全然不合章法——须知魔鬼及其做派，自有它一套章法。

===Original===
How often did the old books say that the best way to quell a specter is to face it dauntlessly? Yet here was the exact reverse. The foe had faded only when he and Scrope fled. He glared at the empty hall, as though to read there an answer to the enigma.

But the hall was not empty. In it was another pale suggestion of shape, slender this time. And the softer voice he had sensed in the bedroom:

“*Again﻿—again﻿—*”

It, too, vanished.

Scrope drew alongside of Pursuivant, peering. “Judge, were you and I seeing things? Both of us?”

Pursuivant actually grinned, and shook his tawny head. “No chance of that, Scrope. People who see things don’t see the same things at the same time.”

===Chinese===
古书里说过多少回：镇服幽灵的上策，就是毫无惧色地直面它？可眼下恰恰相反。他和斯克罗普一逃，那对头才隐去的。他瞪着空荡荡的过道，仿佛要在那上头读出谜底。

可过道并不空。里面又浮起一片淡淡的形影，这回是苗条的一缕。还有他在卧室里觉察到的那个更温柔的声音：

“再来——再来——”

它也消散了。

斯克罗普凑到普瑟文特身旁，眯眼张望。“法官，方才是咱们俩眼花了吗？两个人一道？”

普瑟文特竟咧嘴笑了，摇了摇他黄褐色的头。“没那回事，斯克罗普。眼花的人，不会两个人在同一时刻花出同一副景象。”

===Original===
“Group-hypnotism,” began Scrope, as though the word might be a comfort, but again Pursuivant gestured a demur.

“I believe in many strange things, Scrope, but not in that. Don’t go back into the hall. Sit here, in the kitchen. I begin to understand﻿—to guess, at least.”

They sought their chairs. Pursuivant faced the door.

“The old familiar situation, worn threadbare by writers of fantasy,” he pronounced. “The murdered one haunts the place of his destruction.” He stared hard into the hallway, wondering if he had really seen a stir of movement there. “Anyway, it’s here﻿—spiteful and harmful, able to attack﻿—”

“That’s right,” nodded Scrope, sighing. “He appeared to me, then you, then to both of us.”

===Chinese===
“集体催眠，”斯克罗普开口道，仿佛这个词本身能带来安慰，普瑟文特却又摆手表示异议。

“我信许多离奇的事，斯克罗普，可不信这个。别再回过道去。坐在这儿，坐厨房里。我开始明白了——至少猜出几分了。”

两人归座。普瑟文特面对着那扇门。

“写奇幻的作家们磨秃了的老套子，”他宣示道。“被杀者作祟于自己毙命之地。”他死死盯住过道，拿不准自己是不是真又瞥见那里面有什么动了一动。“不管怎样，它就在这儿——怨毒而有害，还能出手伤人——”

“正是，”斯克罗普点头叹道。“它先显给我看，又显给你看，后来咱们俩一起看见了。”

===Original===
“Which brings us to point number two. The spell is going to work.” Scrope glanced up in almost prayerful eagerness. “You’re sure?”

“Not quite sure of anything in life or death, but this thing’s desperate. It’s trying to fight us. I gather, from what you tell me, that it never manifested itself so strongly before﻿—”

Scrope was nodding eagerly. “Sure. It’s been around here, a sort of edgy atmosphere that drove my houseboys away﻿—but nothing like this. As you say, it’s playing the game for keeps now.”

“It’s in danger,” replied Pursuivant, his blue eyes remaining fixed on the hallway. “So are we. But it’s alone in its fight, and we have friends.”

“Friends?” echoed Scrope.

“I saw another shape, or near shape. Twice. It doesn’t threaten. It pleads. It wants us to go ahead and win.”

===Chinese===
“这就说到第二点。这道咒语就要见效了。”斯克罗普抬起眼，急切得近乎祈祷。“您有把握？”

“生死之间的事，我从没有十拿九稳的，可这东西是在拼命。它想跟咱们斗。据您方才所讲，它从前从没显形显到这么凶——”

斯克罗普热切地点头。“可不是。它一直就在这儿盘桓，一股紧绷绷的邪气，把我的男仆一个个赶跑了——可从没有今晚这样。就像您说的，这回它是玩真的了。”

“它处境危险，”普瑟文特答道，蓝眼睛始终盯住过道。“咱们也一样。可它是孤军作战，咱们却有朋友。”

“朋友？”斯克罗普跟着问。

“我看见了另一个形影，或者说近乎形影的东西。两次。它不威胁人。它在恳求。它盼着咱们干到底，赢下来。”

===Original===
Scrope gazed at Pursuivant. “I think I saw it, too. But if it’s a ghost﻿—”
