“Don’t you realize that a ghost might want release? And others beside the Hessians found a tragic death here. Two women, didn’t you say﻿—I heard a voice ask for the final repetition of my spell. *Again*, it said.”

“We‑ell﻿—” began Scrope uncertainly.

“The spirits of those two women are here, too,” said Pursuivant confidently. “The evil of the place is too strong to let them escape, even though they’re dead.”

“Judge!” gasped Scrope, very pale. He swallowed twice, and continued:

===Chinese===
斯克罗普凝视着普瑟文特。“我想我也看见它了。可它要是鬼魂——”

“您不明白吗，鬼魂也可能盼着解脱？再说，在这儿惨死的又不止黑森兵一个。两个女人，您方才不是说了——我听见有个声音央求我把咒语再念最后一遍。‘再来’，它是这么说的。”

“呃——”斯克罗普没把握地开了腔。

“那两个女人的魂灵也在这儿，”普瑟文特笃定地说。“这地方的邪气太强，镇着她们，人死了也脱不了身。”

“法官！”斯克罗普倒抽一口气，脸白得很。他咽了两口唾沫，接着说：

===Original===
“You realize something? If something happens to us﻿—”

“Exactly,” agreed Pursuivant, very steadily. “We’d be caught, too. For all eternity. I realize it perfectly. That is why we must push this thing through to the end﻿—and win.”

He rose once again and went to the door. Foot on the sill, he leaned ever so narrowly in. Then he drew quickly back, like a spectator from the cage of an angry beast.

“Still here,” he reported. “Ready for us. It, too, knows that the showdown’s at hand.”

Scrope studied the doorway, eyes and lips hard. “I’ve got a theory. It stays in that part of the house, the middle part. Might it live in the cellar?”

“Why?” asked Judge Pursuivant.

===Chinese===
“您意识到了吗？要是咱们俩遭了什么——”

“一点不错，”普瑟文特应道，语气镇定极了。“咱们也会被摄在这儿。直到永远永远。这一点我想得明明白白。正因为如此，咱们才必须把这桩事推到头——并且赢下来。”

他再次起身，走到门边。一只脚踏上门槛，把身子只探进去窄窄一线，随即猛地缩回，像看客从盛怒的兽笼前退开。

“还在，”他通报。“正候着咱们呢。它也知道，摊牌的时辰到了。”

斯克罗普端详着那道门洞，眼睛和嘴唇都绷得狠狠的。“我有个想法。它守着屋子当中那一段，别处不去。它会不会就住在地窖里？”

“为什么这么问？”普瑟文特法官问。

===Original===
“Because the cellar﻿—the old basement﻿—lies only under the bathroom and the hall and that guestroom, with only a bit lapping under parts of the kitchen and﻿—”

“By thunder, you have it!” interrupted Pursuivant excitedly.

While Scrope stared, the judge fished his pen from his vest pocket. He began to sketch, on the tabletop.

“See here,” he lectured as he drew. “Your house is sprawling﻿—great big rooms, making a wide base, like this.” He outlined a square. “And the cellar is rather centrally located, so.” He marked in a smaller rectangle, which took a middle slice of the square.

“Yes. That’s about like it,” nodded Scrope. “What are you getting at?”

===Chinese===
“因为那地窖——老宅的地下层——只钻在浴室、过道和那间客房底下，只有一小角伸到厨房这一片底下，还有——”

“天哪，你说着了！”普瑟文特激动地打断。

斯克罗普还怔着，法官已从背心口袋里摸出钢笔，开始在桌面上画。

“您瞧，”他一面画一面讲。“您这房子摊得开——间间都是大屋，铺出一个宽宽的底盘，像这样。”他勾出一个方形。“地窖差不多落在正当中，这样。”他在方形里填进一个窄些的长方形，占下正中的一条。

“对，差不多就是这样，”斯克罗普点头。“您这是想到什么了？”

===Original===
“Don’t you see, man?” cried Pursuivant, almost roughly. “That basement shows the limits of the old house﻿—narrow and high, just as this new one is broad and low. The spirit haunted the old place. Your house takes in that original territory, and some new ground as well.”

He threw down the pen.

“You’re only half haunted, Scrope.”

Understanding dawned into the little man’s face. He sprang to his feet. He began a glad jabber:

“That changes everything. We’re safe. If we don’t go in there﻿—”

===Chinese===
“您还不明白吗，老兄？”普瑟文特几乎是粗声喊起来。“那地下层划出的，正是老宅的边界——又窄又高，恰如您这座新宅又宽又矮。作祟的是老宅的幽灵。您的房子把原来的地界圈了进来，也把新辟的地皮圈了进来。”

他把笔一撂。

“您这宅子不过是半闹鬼，斯克罗普。”

小个子脸上豁然开了窍。他一跃而起，欢欢喜喜地叽里呱啦起来：

“这一下全变了。咱们安全了。只要咱们不进去——”

===Original===
“Oh, but we’re going in there.”

Scrope looked wide-eyed, scared. Pursuivant elaborated:

“The last recital of the spell will take place right in that thing’s den﻿—right on his own dunghill, so to speak. We’ll destroy him forever, where he can’t seek refuge from us.”

===Chinese===
“哦，可咱们偏要进去。”

斯克罗普瞪圆了眼睛，吓坏了。普瑟文特细细说道：

“咒语的最后一遍，就到那东西的巢穴里去念——可以说是念到它自家的粪堆上去。咱们要把它就地永远除掉，叫它没法从咱们手底下躲出去。”

---

===Original===
Again an hour was passed. The two rose from their chairs in the kitchen.

“It’s time,” said Scrope, looking at his wrist watch. “Judge, must I come in there with you?”

“You must,” Pursuivant assured him. “Into that front bedroom. The creature must face his final exorcism.”

He walked to to the hall, and in. Scrope kept close behind, on feet that sounded amazingly heavy for so small a body. They stood together in the hall’s dimness.

It was no longer the hall, new and narrow and fresh-painted in light color. It was a corner of something else.

===Chinese===
又过了一个钟头。两人在厨房里从椅子上站起身。

“到时候了，”斯克罗普看着腕表说。“法官，我一定得跟您一块儿进去吗？”

“一定得，”普瑟文特给他吃定心丸。“进那间前卧室。那孽障必须直面它最后的驱邪。”

他朝过道走去，进了门。斯克罗普紧跟在后面，那样小的身量，脚步声却沉得出奇。两人并肩立在过道的昏暝里。

这已不再是那条过道——那条崭新的、窄窄的、漆着浅色新漆的过道。这是别的什么所在的一角。

===Original===
Despite the gloom, Pursuivant could see plainly that the walls had somehow fallen away. He stood as in a wide and ruinous apartment, with shattered windows extending almost to the high ceiling. The half-rotted floor boards were strewn with rubbish, like plaster fallen away from ancient laths. Wind﻿—there was surely wind here, in the very center of Scrope’s snug home. Yes, wind, blowing through the cracks in this big wrecked place to which they had somehow been wafted.﻿ ﻿…

“Judge,” breathed Scrope, “I know. This is the old mill﻿—it looked like this before they tore it down﻿—”

“Quiet,” bade Pursuivant. He moved in the direction where he remembered the front bedroom’s door to be. It was before him now﻿—he felt its knob under his hand though he could not see it. Hinges creaked. They could walk farther into the room that had been part of the razed mill.

Again things were changed to their eyes.

A sort of blue-green light, such as filters down to the bottom of deep water, showed them spacious floor, high ceiling, great windows﻿—but no more in ruins. The room was suddenly fresh, solidly built, a room for living. Painted plaster, broad white sills and jambs, some furry pelts spread like rugs﻿—and furniture. Even in the weird soft glimmer, Pursuivant knew valuable antiques when he saw them. Yonder table was such﻿—dark, stout, gleaming. The chairs, too. The table was spread with white linen, set with silver and china. And somebody﻿—something﻿—was seated there, as if to eat.

===Chinese===
尽管幽暗，普瑟文特仍看得明白：四壁不知怎么已经塌没了。他像是立在一间宽大破败的屋子中央，破碎的窗洞几乎直通到高高的顶棚。朽了半截的地板上散着垃圾，像从老墙筋上剥落的灰泥。风——这里头分明有风，就在斯克罗普温暖安乐的家宅正中央。是啊，风，从这座破败大屋的裂罅里灌进来——他们不知怎么被送到了这里。……

“法官，”斯克罗普低声说，“我明白了。这就是老磨坊——他们拆它之前，它就是这副样子——”

“别作声，”普瑟文特吩咐。他朝记忆中前卧室房门的方位走去。那门如今就在眼前——他手底下摸到了门钮，虽然眼睛看不见。合页吱呀一响。他们得以往里再走，走进这间曾是磨坊残骸一部分的屋子。

眼前的光景又是一变。

一种青绿色的光，像滤到深水海底的那种，照出宽敞的地板、高高的顶棚、巨大的窗子——却不再是断壁残垣。屋子忽然焕然一新，造得结结实实，是一间过日子的屋子。粉刷过的墙面，宽大的白窗台和窗框，几张毛皮摊开当地毯——还有家具。即便在这片诡异的柔光里，普瑟文特也识得货：那边那张桌子便是值钱的古董——色深、敦实、莹然生光。椅子也是。桌上铺着白麻布，摆着银器瓷器。有谁——有什么——坐在那里，像是要用餐。

===Original===
The Hessian﻿—of course. Or what had been the Hessian.

It faced them across the table. Now Pursuivant knew where the watery glow come from.

That semi-shape exuded it, like touchwood. He could dimly make out a clarification of outline and detail﻿—a dress coat of ancient British style, powdered hair, elegance strangely out of place upon such a brute body. The most light came from around the head, which still did not have a face.

Pursuivant began to recite once more:

“All ye evil spirits, I forbid you this man’s bed, his couch﻿—”

===Chinese===
那黑森兵——当然。或者说，当过黑森兵的那个东西。

它隔着桌子面对他们。至此普瑟文特才明白，那汪水底似的幽光从何而来。

那半具形影周身发着光，像一块引火的火绒。他隐约辨出轮廓和细部渐渐清晰——一件英式古装礼服呢上衣，扑了粉的头发，一派优雅，罩在那副蛮横的身躯上，错位得说不出的古怪。光最亮的地方围着那颗头——那头上，依旧没有脸。

普瑟文特开始再诵：

“一切恶灵，我禁绝你们染指这人的床榻卧铺——”

===Original===
The blue light dimmed. The shape rose and came toward them.

“Scrope,” muttered Pursuivant, between phrases of his formula. “Lights﻿—turn them on﻿—”

He put himself where the approaching shape would find him. “I forbid you, in heaven’s name﻿—” he continued.

Strong hands seized him, hands as cold as marsh ice. He had a sense of filth and ferocity being hurled at him. He fought back.

===Chinese===
幽幽的蓝光暗了下去。那形影站起身，朝他们逼来。

“斯克罗普，”普瑟文特在咒文的句缝里低声说。“灯——把灯打开——”

他把自己横到那逼近的形影必经的路上。“我以天国之名，禁绝——”他继续念。

一双强有力的大手扼住了他，冷得像沼泽里的冰。他觉出一股污秽和凶暴劈头砸来。他回手还击。

---

===Original===
Judge Keith Pursuivant was big, strong and cunning, but here was his match. It worked those cold hands to his throat, striving to shut off his breath and the words he spoke. He heard it panting and snarling, like the unknown animals of which one dreams. His own fists struck for that featureless face, battering it backward upon its cloudy shoulders, but the thing wrestled closer and closer, trying to throttle him.

“The lights﻿—won’t work!” Scrope was screaming. He struck a match, set it to a scrap of paper he whisked out of his pocket. This little torch he held aloft.

The rosy light dominated the blue, and Scrope saw plainly the thing that Pursuivant grappled. He screamed louder, and dropped the blazing paper. It floated sidewise, into some sort of a wall hanging. A stronger flame leaped up. Pursuivant caught the hard, chill wrists of his enemy and tore himself free.

“﻿—unto you and yours, in the name of Trinity!” he finished.

===Chinese===
基思·普瑟文特法官高大、强健、有心计，可这一回他遇上了对手。那东西把冰冷的双手摸向他的咽喉，要掐断他的呼吸，掐断他口中的咒词。他听见它呼哧带喘、低吼咆哮，像人梦里那些叫不出名字的野兽。他自己的拳头朝那张没有五官的脸捣去，把它打回它那云雾般的肩头上去，可那东西越缠越紧，非要扼死他不可。

“灯——不管用！”斯克罗普在尖叫。他划亮一根火柴，点燃一张从口袋里飞快抽出来的纸片，把这小小的火把高高举起。

玫瑰色的火光压倒了幽蓝。斯克罗普把普瑟文特缠住的那东西看了个满眼真切。他叫得更凶，燃着的纸片脱了手。纸片飘飘荡荡斜落下去，落进一幅什么壁挂里。更旺的火苗腾地蹿起。普瑟文特攥住敌人坚硬冰冷的腕子，奋力挣脱出身来。

“——退还你们与你们的一切，奉三位一体之名！”他念完了。

===Original===
Then he wheeled abruptly, seized and lifted Scrope, and hurried him away. They found themselves in the parlor, the room they had known before. Behind them flames gushed and roared, like a blast furnace.

Scrope, set on his feet again, seemed ready to faint. Pursuivant shook sense and steadiness back into him.

“Come on,” he ordered. “Keep moving. Outside. This place is burning like a wicker basket.”

===Chinese===
随即他猛地转身，一把抱起斯克罗普，架着他就走。两人发觉已回到客厅——他们先前熟识的那间屋子。身后烈火奔涌咆哮，像一座鼓风炉。

斯克罗普重新落了地，眼看就要晕过去。普瑟文特连摇带撼，把理智和镇定摇回他身上。

“走，”他下令。“别停下。到外头去。这地方烧得像只柳条筐。”

---

===Original===
They reached the outside, and Pursuivant let Alvin Scrope lean against a tree for support. He himself hurried to the double garage. He started and brought out first one, then the other of the cars, parking them at a point safe from any flying sparks or embers.

He returned to his companion. The flames now burst from the open parlor windows, licking at the clapboards and shingles outside. Snow fell but scantily, barely enough to make a hissing in the heat.

Scrope shook himself, like a dog coming out of water. He was getting command over his fear-crumbling spirit.

“Hadn’t we better get to a phone somewhere?” he suggested. “There’s a volunteer fire department in town﻿—”

===Chinese===
两人到了屋外，普瑟文特让阿尔文·斯克罗普倚着一棵树支住身子。他自己赶去双车位车库，先后把两辆车发动着开了出来，停到一处不惧火星飞迸的安全地方。

他回到同伴身边。此刻火焰已从客厅敞开的窗口喷涌而出，舔着外面的墙板和屋面板。雪下得稀稀拉拉，刚够在热浪里咝咝响几声。

斯克罗普抖了抖身子，像刚从水里上岸的狗。他渐渐拿稳了那副险些被恐惧泡酥的精神。

“咱们是不是该找个电话？”他提议。“镇上有支义务消防队——”

===Original===
“No,” said Pursuivant. “No fire departments. Let that house burn to the ground.”

“To the ground?” Scrope’s face looked stronger in the red light. “Yes, of course. You’re exactly right. No more ghosts after fire. I can build again.”

“Build, and be at peace. Let it burn, I say. We’ll drive the cars to Scott’s Meadows, and stay at the little inn there. And tomorrow you can come and stay with me at my home until you catch hold of your affairs again.”

“Thanks. I will.”

===Chinese===
“不，”普瑟文特说。“不叫消防队。让那房子烧个精光。”

“烧个精光？”斯克罗普的脸在红光里显得刚强了些。“对，当然。您说得一点不错。大火过后，再无鬼魂。我还可以再盖。”

“盖起来，从此太太平平。我说，让它烧。咱们把车开到斯科特草甸，住那边的小客栈。明天您就随我回家去住，等您把自家的事务重新抓上手。”

“谢谢。我会的。”

===Original===
They fell silent. In the darkness, no longer so chilly, came a rustle of passing. A semi-shape﻿—two semi-shapes﻿—glided swiftly by, like puffs of smoke from the house.

Thank you, Pursuivant felt gentle cries of joy, more in his heart than in his ears. Thank you﻿—

They were gone.

Scrope, too, had been aware of that passing. “I guess,” he ventured, “that the spirits of those poor women are set free.”

===Chinese===
两人都静了下来。黑地里——夜已不那样砭骨——传来一阵掠过的窸窣。一团半形半影——两团半形半影——飞快滑了过去，像从屋子里飘出的两股烟。

谢谢你们。普瑟文特感到一阵温柔的、欢欢喜喜的呼喊，与其说落进耳朵，不如说落进心里。谢谢你们——

她们去了。

斯克罗普也觉察到了那一掠而过。“我猜，”他大着胆子说，“那两个苦命女人的魂灵，这就得了自由。”

===Original===
From the heart of the red rage of flame that now possessed all the house came suddenly a sound﻿—a shout, a roar, a scream﻿—recognizeable as human and masculine.

Scrope faltered and swore. “That﻿—was the Hessian?”

“It is what was the Hessian,” agreed Pursuivant, gazing at the fire.

Another peal of sound. Full of horror﻿—full of agony.

===Chinese===
从此刻盘踞了整座屋子的红色狂怒的烈焰深处，陡然迸出一个声音——一声呐喊，一声吼，一声惨叫——听得出是人的声音，男人的声音。

斯克罗普一个趔趄，骂了一句。“那——是那黑森兵？”

“那就是当过黑森兵的东西，”普瑟文特望着火，表示同意。

又是一声长嚎冲天。满是恐怖——满是苦痛。

===Original===
“Why does he stay?” quavered Scrope. “Those others thanked us for setting them free﻿—why does he hang on there until he’s burned clear loose from﻿—” He broke off. “I know,” he said, gaining command of himself again.

Pursuivant turned toward him. “What, then?”

“The women were killers﻿—yes. But they killed for a good purpose. They knew they’d find some kind of happiness now that they’re not held here. But,” and Scrope, too, faced the fire, “the other thing has nothing like that to expect. He hangs onto the burning den. Because, when he leaves, it’ll be for﻿—for﻿—”

“Something much worse,” finished Pursuivant for him.

===Chinese===
“他为什么赖着不走？”斯克罗普声音发颤。“那两位谢咱们放了她们——他为什么死抱着那儿，非要烧得……烧得脱开了才——”他说不下去。“我懂了，”他说，重新拿稳了自己。

普瑟文特转向他。“那么，是为什么？”

“那两个女人是杀人凶手——不错。可她们杀人是为了正当的目的。她们知道，如今不再被拘在这儿，总还能寻到几分幸福。可是，”斯克罗普也面向火场，“那另一个东西，没有这样的指望。他死抱着燃烧的巢穴。因为他一旦离开，去的就是——去的就是——”

“比这糟得多的去处，”普瑟文特替他说完。

===Original===
Once again the suffering voice mounted up and shook the night. Then it died to a wail, a rattle, it died to nothing. It was silent.

The flames flapped like banners of victory. They seemed cleaner and more joyous.

Pursuivant and Scrope suddenly shook hands.

===Chinese===
那受苦的声音再一次拔起来，震得夜空发颤。随后它沉为一声哀号，一阵咯咯的气音，沉为一片虚无。寂静了。

火焰像得胜的旌旗一般翻卷。它们仿佛更明净，也更欢畅了。

普瑟文特和斯克罗普突然握起手来。

## The Liers in Wait / 伺伏者

===Original===
> Here lies our Sovereign Lord the King,
 

 Whose word no man relies on,
 

 Who never said a foolish thing,
 

 Nor ever did a wise one.

===Chinese===
> 这里长眠着我们的君主大王，
> 他说的话无人肯信；
> 他从不曾说一句蠢话，
> 也从不曾干一件聪明事。

===Original===
Yes, Jack Wilmot wrote so concerning me, and rallied me, saying these lines he would cut upon my monument; and now he is dead at thirty-three, while I live at fifty, none so merry a monarch as folks deem me. Jack’s verse makes me out a coxcomb, but he knew me not in my youth. He was but four, and sucking sugarplums, when his father and I were fugitives after Worcester. Judge from this story, if he rhymes the truth of me.

===Chinese===
是的，杰克·威尔莫特（Jack Wilmot）就是这样写我、这样打趣我的，还说要把这几行诗刻到我的墓碑上去；如今他三十三岁上就死了，我倒活到了五十岁，做起人们以为的那样一位快活君主。杰克的诗把我写成个自命不凡的蠢货，可他并不认识年轻时的我。他父亲和我在伍斯特（Worcester）败后亡命的时候，他不过四岁，还在咂蜜饯糖块呢。我讲的这段故事里，他的诗到底押没押对真相，请诸位自己评判。

===Original===
I think it was then, with the rain soaking my wretched borrowed clothes and the heavy tight plough-shoes rubbing my feet all to blisters, that I first knew consciously how misery may come to kings as to vagabonds. Egad, I was turned the second before I had well been the first. Trying to think of other things than my present sorry state among the dripping trees of Spring Coppice, I could but remember sorrier things still. Chiefly came to mind the Worcester fight, that had been rather a cutting down of my poor men like barley, and Cromwell’s Ironside troopers the reapers; How could so much ill luck befall—Lauderdale’s bold folly, that wasted our best men in a charge? The mazed silence of Leslie’s Scots horse, the first of their blood I ever heard of before or since who refused battle? I remembered too, as a sick dream, how I charged with a few faithful at a troop of Parliamentarian horse said to be Cromwell’s own guard; I had cut down a mailed rider with a pale face like the winter moon, and rode back dragging one of my own, wounded sore, across my saddle bow. He had died there, crying to me: “God save your most sacred Majesty!” And now I had need of God to save me.

“More things than Cromwell’s wit and might went into this disaster,” I told myself in the rain, nor knew how true I spoke.

===Chinese===
我想正是在那时，雨水浸透我那身借来的破衣裳，沉重的耕田鞋又紧又硬，把两脚磨得满是水泡，我这才头一回清清楚楚地明白：倒霉这东西，国王摊得上，流浪汉也摊得上。老天爷，我头一把交椅还没坐热，就给打落到第二流去了。我立在斯普林矮林（Spring Coppice）滴水的树间，想撇开眼前的狼狈境地去想点别的，可想起来的尽是更狼狈的事。压上心头的主要是伍斯特那一仗——那与其说是打仗，不如说是把我可怜的士卒当大麦收割，克伦威尔（Cromwell）的铁甲军（Ironsides）骑兵就是刈麦人。怎么会倒这么大的霉？劳德代尔（Lauderdale）匹夫之勇，一次冲锋折尽了我们的精兵？莱斯利（Leslie）的苏格兰骑兵那阵茫然的死寂——他们那种人，我此前此后都再没听说过有临阵拒战的？我还像记起一场病中噩梦似的记起：我带着几名忠心部下，朝一支号称克伦威尔亲随卫队的国会派骑兵冲杀过去；我砍翻了一个披甲的骑士，那张脸白得像冬天的月亮，随后又驮着自己一名重伤的部下，把他横搭在鞍桥上驰回。他就在那儿断了气，临终还朝我喊：“上帝保佑至圣的陛下！”而如今，倒轮到我需要上帝保佑了。

“这场大祸里头，掺的可不只是克伦威尔的智谋和兵力。”我在雨里对自己这样说，却不知道自己说得多灵验。

===Original===
After the battle, the retreat. Had it been only last night? Leslie’s horsemen, who had refused to follow me toward Cromwell, had dogged me so close in fleeing him I was at pains to scatter and so avoid them. Late we had paused, my gentlemen and I, at a manor of White-Ladies. There we agreed to divide and flee in disguise. With the help of two faithful yokels named Penderel I cut my long curls with a knife and crammed my big body into coarse garments—gray cloth breeches, a leathern doublet, a green jump-coat—while that my friends smeared my face and hands with chimney-soot. Then farewells, and I gave each gentleman a keepsake—a ribbon, a buckle, a watch, and so forward. I remembered, too, my image in a mirror, and it was most unkingly—a towering, swarthy young man, ill-clad, ill-faced. One of the staunch Penderals bade me name myself, and I chose to be called Will Jones, a wandering woodcutter.

Will Jones! ’Twas an easy name and comfortable. For the nonce I was happier with it than with Charles Stuart, England’s king and son of that other Charles who had died by Cromwell’s axe. I was heir to bitter sorrow and trouble and mystery, in my youth lost and hunted and friendless as any strong thief.

===Chinese===
仗打完，便是逃亡。那不过是昨天夜里的事吧？莱斯利的骑兵，先前不肯跟我去打克伦威尔，逃起来却把我盯得死紧，我费了好大的劲才把他们甩散、躲开。深夜，我同我的绅士们在白衣修女院（White-Ladies）的一处宅院里稍作停留，商定分头化装潜逃。靠两个姓潘德雷尔（Penderel）的忠厚庄稼汉帮忙，我用刀割去长鬈发，把高大的身躯硬塞进粗布衣裳——灰布马裤、皮紧身上衣、一件绿色短外套——伙伴们又拿烟囱里的煤灰抹黑我的脸和手。然后是道别，我赠给每位绅士一件纪念物——一条缎带、一枚带扣、一块怀表，如此种种，各自上路。我还记得镜子里我的模样，实在没有半分君王气概——一个高大黝黑的年轻人，衣衫寒酸，面容憔悴。一个牢靠的潘德雷尔要我给自己起个名字，我便选了威尔·琼斯（Will Jones），一个四处流浪的伐木工。

威尔·琼斯！这名字简单顺口，安适自在。眼下我顶着它，竟比顶着查理·斯图尔特（Charles Stuart）还快活些——那可是英格兰的国王，是死在克伦威尔斧头下的老查理的儿子。我继承的是辛酸、祸乱与疑云，年纪轻轻便亡命天涯，遭人追捕，孤苦无依，同任何一个江洋大盗没什么两样。

===Original===
The rain was steady and weary. I tried to ask myself what I did here in Spring Coppice. It had been necessary to hide the day out, and travel by night; but whose thought was it to choose this dim, sorrowful wood? Richard Penderel had said that no rain fell elsewhere. Perhaps that was well, since Ironsides might forbear to seek me in such sorry bogs; but meanwhile I shivered and sighed, and wished myself a newt. The trees, what I could see, were broad oaks with some fir and larch, and the ground grew high with bracken reddened by September’s first chill.

Musing thus, I heard a right ill sound—horses’ hoofs. I threw myself half-downways among some larch scrub, peering out through the clumpy leaves. My right hand clutched the axe I carried as part of my masquerade. Beyond was a lane, and along it, one by one, rode enemy—a troop of Cromwell’s horse, hard fellows and ready-seeming, with breasts and caps of iron. They stared right and left searchingly. The bright, bitter eyes of their officer seemed to strike through my hiding like a pike-point. I clutched my axe the tighter, and swore on my soul that, if found, I would die fighting—a better death, after all, than my poor father’s.

But they rode past, and out of sight. I sat up, and wiped muck from my long nose. “I am free yet,” I told myself. “One day, please our Lord, I shall sit on the throne that is mine. Then shall I seek out these Ironsides and feed fat the gallows at Tyburn, the block at the Tower.”

===Chinese===
雨下个不停，下得人心里发疲。我试着问自己：我到斯普林矮林里来做什么？白日藏身、夜里赶路，这固然必要，可挑中这么一片阴沉凄惶的林子，又是谁的主意？理查德·潘德雷尔（Richard Penderel）说过，别处一滴雨也没落。这或许倒是好事，铁甲军未必肯到这等凄惨的泥沼里来搜我；只是眼下我又冷又乏，唉声叹气，恨不得自己变成一只蝾螈。凭目力所及，林中尽是高大的橡树，间杂几株冷杉、落叶松，齐膝的蕨草被九月最初的寒气染得通红。

正这样胡思乱想，我听见一种极不妙的声音——马蹄声。我半截身子扑进一片落叶松丛，从密簇簇的枝叶间向外窥探，右手攥住那把作伪装用的斧头。林子外是一条小路，路上鱼贯驰来敌人——一队克伦威尔的骑兵，个个面目剽悍、杀气腾腾，胸甲和头盔都是铁的。他们左右逡巡，搜索张望。那军官一双明亮的苦毒眼睛，像长枪尖似的，仿佛要扎透我的藏身处。我把斧头攥得更紧，在心里对天起誓：一旦被发觉，我就战斗而死——怎么说，这也比我可怜父王的死法强。

但他们径直驰了过去，消失不见。我坐起身，抹去长鼻子上的泥浆。“我暂时还是自由的，”我对自己说，“托上帝的福，总有一天，我要坐回属于我的王座。到那时，我要把这些铁甲军一个个找出来，让泰伯恩（Tyburn）的绞架吃个饱，让伦敦塔的断头台不得闲。”

---

===Original===
For I was young and cruel then, as now I am old and mellow. Religion perplexed and irked me. I could not understand nor like Cromwell’s Praise-God men of war, whose faces were as sharp and merciless as, alas, their swords. “I’ll give them texts to quote,” I vowed. “I have heard their canting war-cries. ‘Smite and spare not!’ They shall learn how it is to be smitten and spared not.”

For the moment I felt as if vengeance were already mine, my house restored to power, my adversaries chained and delivered into my hand. Then I turned to cooler thoughts, and chiefly that I had best seek a hiding less handy to that trail through the trees.

The thought was like sudden memory, as if indeed I knew the Coppice and where best to go.

===Chinese===
须知那时我年轻气盛、心肠狠硬，如今老了，才变得温厚。宗教这东西叫我困惑，也叫我不耐烦。我实在弄不懂、也喜欢不了克伦威尔那帮“赞美上帝”的武士——他们的脸孔同他们的刀剑一样又冷又刻毒，呜呼。“我要送他们几段经文引用引用，”我发起誓来，“他们那套伪善的杀喊声我听够了。‘击杀勿赦！’好，也让他们尝尝被人击杀勿赦的滋味。”

一时间，我恍惚觉得大仇已报，我王家重掌大权，仇人们锁上镣铐，交到我手里发落。随即我转念想到更冷静的事，头一件便是：最好另找个藏身之处，离林中那条小路别这么近。

这念头来得像骤然想起旧事，仿佛我当真熟悉这片矮林，知道哪里最该去。

===Original===
For I mind me how I rose from among the larches, turned on the heel of one pinching shoe, and struck through a belt of young spruce as though I were indeed a woodcutter seeking by familiar ways the door of mine own hut. So confidently did I stride that I blundered—or did I?—into a thorny vine that hung down from a long oak limb. It fastened upon my sleeve like urging fingers. “Nay, friend,” I said to it, trying to be gay, “hold me not here in the wet,” and I twitched away. That was one more matter about Spring Coppice that seemed strange and not overcanny—as also the rain, the gloom, my sudden desire to travel toward its heart. Yet, as you shall see here, these things were strange only in their basic cause. But I forego the tale.

“So cometh Will Jones to his proper home,” quoth I, axe on shoulder. Speaking thus merrily, I came upon another lane, but narrower than that on which the horsemen had ridden. This ran ankle-deep in mire, and I remember how the damp, soaking into my shoes, soothed those plaguey blisters. I followed the way for some score of paces, and meseemed that the rain was heaviest here, like a curtain before some hidden thing. Then I came into a cleared space, with no trees nor bush, nor even grass upon the bald earth. In its center, wreathed with rainy mists, a house.

===Chinese===
我记得自己就这样从落叶松间站起身，就着一只夹脚的鞋跟转过方向，径直穿过一带云杉幼林，仿佛我真是个伐木工，正循着熟路走向自家小屋的门口。我走得那样笃定，一头撞上了——或者说，是撞上了吗？——一根从橡树长枝上垂下来的带刺藤蔓。它攀住我的衣袖，像几根催促的手指。“不啦，朋友，”我强作轻松地对它说，“别把我留在这儿挨雨淋，”说着挣脱开去。这又是斯普林矮林里一桩古怪得邪性的小事——同样古怪的还有这场雨、这片阴沉，还有我那股突然生出的、要往林子深处去的念头。不过，往下您自会看到，这些事情古怪，只古怪在根由上。这里且按下不表。

“威尔·琼斯就这样回他正该回的家啦，”我说，斧头扛在肩上。一路这样说说笑笑，我走到另一条小路上，比骑兵们驰过的那条更窄。路面泥浆没过脚踝，我记得湿气渗进鞋里，反倒让那些可恶的水泡觉出几分舒缓。我沿路走了二十来步，只觉此处雨势最猛，像一道帘幕，前头遮着什么隐匿的东西。随后我走进一片空地，光秃秃的地面上没有树，没有灌木，连草也不长。空地中央，裹在雨雾里，立着一座房子。

===Original===
I paused, just within shelter of the leaves. “What,” I wondered, “has my new magic of being a woodcutter conjured up a woodcutter’s shelter?”

But this house was no honest workman’s place, that much I saw with but half an eye. Conjured up it might well have been, and most foully. I gazed at it without savor, and saw that it was not large, but lean and high-looking by reason of the steep pitch of its roof. That roof’s thatch was so wet and foul that it seemed all of one drooping substance, like the cap of a dark toadstool. The walls, too, were damp, being of clay daub spread upon a framework of wattles. It had one door, and that a mighty thick heavy one, of a single dark plank that hung upon heavy rusty hinges. One window it had, too, through which gleamed some sort of light; but instead of glass the window was filled with something like thin-scraped rawhide, so that light could come through, but not the shape of things within. And so I knew not what was in that house, nor at the time had I any conscious lust to find out.

I say, no conscious lust. For it was unconsciously that I drifted idly forth from the screen of wet leaves, gained and moved along a little hard-packed path between bracken-clumps. That path led to the door, and I found myself standing before it; while through the skinned-over window, inches away, I heard noises.

===Chinese===
我停在枝叶的荫蔽之内。“奇怪，”我暗想，“莫非我当伐木工的这门新法术，凭空变出了一座伐木工的棚屋？”

可这座房子绝非正经工匠的住处，这我半只眼就看得出。说它是凭空变出来的，倒很可能是，而且是被最肮脏的邪法变出来的。我兴致索然地打量它，见它不算大，却因屋顶陡峭而显得瘦长高挑。屋顶的茅草湿得发黑、脏得厉害，仿佛一整块蔫塌塌的东西，像一顶深色毒菌的伞盖。墙壁也是潮的，是在枝条编的骨架上糊的泥。屋子有一扇门，厚得出奇，一整块深色厚木板，吊在沉重的锈铰链上。它还有一扇窗，透出些许光亮；可窗上嵌的不是玻璃，倒像一层刮薄的生牛皮，光透得进来，屋内东西的模样却透不出去。因此我不知道那屋里有什么，而当时，我也没有丝毫想要探个明白的自觉欲望。

我说的是，没有自觉的欲望。因为我是不知不觉地从湿叶的荫蔽里飘荡出去的，踏上蕨丛之间一条踩得坚实的小径，顺径而行。小径通向那扇门，我发觉自己已站在门前；隔着那层蒙皮的窗，近在咫尺，我听见了响动。

===Original===
Noises I call them, for at first I could not think they were voices. Several soft hummings or purrings came to my ears, from what source I knew not. Finally, though, actual words, high and raspy:

“We who keep the commandment love the law! Moloch, Lucifer, Bal-Tigh-Mor, Anector, Somiator, sleep ye not! Compel ye that the man approach!”

It had the sound of a prayer, and yet I recognized but one of the names called—Lucifer. Tutors, parsons, my late unhappy allies the Scots Covenentors, had used the name oft and fearfully. Prayer within that ugly lean house went up—or down, belike—to the fallen Son of the Morning. I stood against the door, pondering. My grandsire, King James, had believed and feared such folks’ pretense. My father, who was King Charles before me, was pleased to doubt and be merciful, pardoning many accused witches and sorcerers. As for me, my short life had held scant leisure to decide such a matter. While I waited in the fine misty rain on the threshold, the high voice spoke again:

===Chinese===
我管它叫响动，因为起初我不肯相信那是人声。几阵低低的嗡嗡声、咕噜声传进我耳朵，不知来自何处。到后来，终于有了字句，又尖又哑：

“守诫命的我等热爱律法！摩洛（Moloch）、路西法（Lucifer）、巴尔提格莫（Bal-Tigh-Mor）、阿内克托（Anector）、索米亚托（Somiator），你们莫睡！逼那男人近前来！”

听上去像一篇祷文，可那些名号里我只认得一个——路西法。我的师傅们、牧师们，还有我先前那些不幸的盟友苏格兰誓约派（Covenentors），都常带着惧色提起这个名字。那座丑陋瘦屋里做的祷告，是升上天去——多半是沉下地去——献给那位堕落的早晨之子的。我倚门而立，暗自思量。我的祖父詹姆斯王（King James）就信并且怕这路人的伎俩。我的父王，我之前的查理王，倒宁肯存疑并且仁慈，赦免过许多被告发的女巫和术士。至于我，短短半生还腾不出闲工夫对这种事拿定主意。正当我立在门前的细雨里等待，那尖嗓子又开了口：

===Original===
“Drive him to us! Drive him to us! Drive him to us!”

Silence within, and you may be sure silence without. A new voice, younger and thinner, made itself heard: “Naught comes to us.”

“Respect the promises of our masters,” replied the first. “What says the book?”

And yet a new voice, this time soft and a woman’s: “Let the door be opened and the wayfarer be plucked in.”

===Chinese===
“把他驱到我们这儿来！把他驱到我们这儿来！把他驱到我们这儿来！”

屋里静了，屋外呢，您尽可相信，也静了。一个新的声音开了腔，更年轻，更细弱：“什么也没到我们这儿来。”

“要敬重我们主上们的许诺，”头一个声音答道。“书上怎么说？”

接着又是一个新的声音，这回是软软的女人声：“把门打开，把过路人拽进来。”

---

===Original===
I swear that I had not the least impulse to retreat, even to step aside. ’Twas as if all my life depended on knowing more. As I stood, ears aprick like any cat’s, the door creaked inward by three inches. An arm in a dark sleeve shot out, and fingers as lean and clutching as thorn-twigs fastened on the front of my jump-coat.

“I have him safe!” rasped the high voice that had prayed. A moment later I was drawn inside, before I could ask the reason.

===Chinese===
我发誓，我丝毫没有退却的念头，连闪身让开都没有。仿佛我这条命全系在要多知道一点上。我站在那里，耳朵像猫一样支棱着，那门“吱呀”一声朝里开了三英寸。一条暗色衣袖的胳膊探将出来，几根瘦得像荆棘枝的手指，一把抓住了我短外套的前襟。

“我抓住他了！”那个做祷告的尖嗓子哑声喊道。不等我问一句缘由，眨眼之间我就被拽进了屋内。

===Original===
There was one room to the house, and it stank of burning weeds. There were no chairs or other furniture, and no fireplace; but in the center of the tamped-clay floor burned an open fire, whose rank smoke climbed to a hole at the roof’s peak. Around this fire was drawn a circle in white chalk, and around the circle a star in red. Close outside the star were the three whose voices I had heard.

Mine eyes lighted first on she who held the book—young she was and dainty. She sat on the floor, her feet drawn under her full skirt of black stuff. Above a white collar of Dutch style, her face was round and at the same time fine and fair, with a short red mouth and blue eyes like the clean sea.

Her hair, under a white cap, was as yellow as corn. She held in her slim white hands a thick book, whose cover looked to be grown over with dark hair, like the hide of a Galloway bull.

===Chinese===
这屋子统共只有一间，弥漫着焚烧杂草的恶臭。没有椅子，没有任何家具，也没有壁炉；只在夯实的泥地正中烧着一堆明火，浓浊的烟顺着屋顶尖上的洞口爬出去。火堆四周用白粉画了一个圈，圈外又用红色描了一颗星。紧挨着星外，站着方才出过声的那三个人。

我的目光先落在捧书的那一位身上——年纪轻轻，娇小玲珑。她坐在地上，双脚收在黑色的宽幅呢裙底下。荷兰式白领的上方，她那张脸圆润，却又精致标致，短短的红嘴唇，一双蓝眼睛像明净的海水。

她白色便帽底下的头发，黄得像成熟的玉米。一双纤细白皙的手捧着一部厚书，书皮看上去生满了黑毛，像加洛韦（Galloway）公牛的皮。

===Original===
Her eyes held mine for two trices, then I looked beyond her to another seated person. He was small enough to be a child, but the narrow bright eyes in his thin face were older than the oldest I had seen, and the hands clasped around his bony knees were rough and sinewy, with large sore-seeming joints. His hair was scanty, and eke his eyebrows. His neck showed swollen painfully.

It is odd that my last look was for him who had drawn me in. He was tall, almost as myself, and grizzled hair fell on the shoulders of his velvet doublet. One claw still clapped hold of me and his face, a foot from mine, was as dark and bloodless as earth. Its lips were loose, its quivering nose broken. The eyes, cold and wide as a frog’s, were as steady as gun-muzzles.

He kicked the door shut, and let me go. “Name yourself,” he rasped at me. “If you be not he whom we seek—”

===Chinese===
她的目光同我的对了两瞬，随后我越过她，看向另一个坐着的人。他小得足当个孩子，可瘦脸上那双细窄明亮的眼睛，比我生平见过的最老的人还要苍老；扣着嶙峋膝盖的那双手粗糙多筋，骨节粗大，看着就叫人觉得作痛。他头发稀疏，眉毛也同样稀疏。脖颈肿胀着，瞧着就疼。

说来奇怪，我最末才打量把我拽进来的那个人。他身材高大，几乎同我不相上下，灰白的头发披散在丝绒紧身上衣的肩头。一只爪子还扒着我不放，他的脸离我不过一英尺，暗沉无血色，像泥土一样。嘴唇松垮，颤动个不停的鼻子断过。那双眼睛又冷又凸，像青蛙眼，纹丝不动，稳得如同两支枪口。

他把门踢上，松开我。“报名来，”他哑着嗓子冲我说，“倘若你不是我们等的那个人——”

===Original===
“I am Will Jones, a poor woodcutter,” I told him.

“Mmmm,” murmured the wench with the book. “Belike the youngest of seven sons—sent forth by a cruel step-dame to seek fortune in the world. So runs the fairy tale, and we want none such. Your true name, sirrah.”

I told her roundly that she was insolent, but she only smiled. And I never saw a fairer than she, not in all the courts of Europe—not even sweet Nell Gwyn. After many years I can see her eyes, a little slanting and a little hungry. Even when I was so young, women feared me, but this one did not.

===Chinese===
“我叫威尔·琼斯，一个穷伐木工，”我告诉他。

“唔，”捧书的姑娘喃喃道。“多半是七兄弟里最小的那个——叫狠毒的后娘赶出家门，去外头闯运气。童话里都这么讲，这种人我们可不要。报你的真名来，小子。”

我直截了当地说她放肆无礼，她只是微笑。我从未见过比她更美的女子，走遍欧洲各国的宫廷也不曾——连甜姐儿奈尔·格温（Nell Gwyn）也比不上。隔了这么多年，我仍能想见她那双眼睛，微微带斜，透着一点贪婪。即便在我那么年轻的时候，女人们见了我也有几分畏怯，唯独这一个没有。

===Original===
“His word shall not need,” spoke the thin young-old fellow by the fire. “Am I not here to make him prove himself?” He lifted his face so that the fire brightened it, and I saw hot red blotches thereon.

“True,” agreed the grizzled man. “Sirrah, whether you be Will Jones the woodman or Charles Stuart the king, have you no mercy on poor Diccon yonder? If ’twould ease his ail, would you not touch him?”

That was a sneer, but I looked closer at the thin fellow called Diccon, and made sure that he was indeed sick and sorry. His face grew full of hope, turning up to me. I stepped closer to him.

“Why, with all my heart, if ’twill serve,” I replied.

===Chinese===
“用不着他开口，”火边那个又年轻又老态的瘦子说话了。“不是有我在，让他自己证明自己么？”他抬起脸，火光照亮了它，我看见上面有一块块火红的斑。

“说的是，”灰白头发的男人表示赞同。“小子，不管你是伐木工威尔·琼斯，还是国王查理·斯图尔特，你瞧那边可怜的迪肯（Diccon），你就一点不怜悯他么？倘若摸一摸能减轻他的病痛，你肯不肯摸他一下？”

这话带着讥诮，可我细看那个叫迪肯的瘦子，断定他确实病得不轻、可怜巴巴。他仰起脸望我，满脸生出指望。我朝他走近了些。

“嗐，只要管用，我十分愿意，”我答道。

===Original===
“ ’Ware the star and circle, step not within the star and circle,” cautioned the wench, but I came not near those marks. Standing beside and above Diccon, I felt his brow, and felt that it was fevered. “A hot humor is in your blood, friend,” I said to him, and touched the swelling on his neck.

But had there been a swelling there? I touched it, but ’twas suddenly gone, like a furtive mouse under my finger. Diccon’s neck looked lean and healthy. His face smiled, and from it had fled the red blotches. He gave a cry and sprang to his feet.

“ ’Tis past, ’tis past!” he howled. “I am whole again!”

But the eyes of his comrades were for me.

===Chinese===
“当心星形和圆圈，别踏进星圈里去，”姑娘提醒道，我却没有靠近那些线条。我站在迪肯身侧，居高临下，摸了摸他的额头，摸出一片滚烫。“朋友，你血里烧着热毒，”我对他说，又按了按他脖子上那块肿处。

可那里方才有过肿块么？我摸过去，它却倏地没了，像一只躲闪的老鼠从我指头底下溜走。迪肯的脖子看上去瘦而健康。他脸上绽开笑，红斑也逃得无影无踪。他欢呼一声，蹦了起来。

“好了，好了！”他高声喊道。“我又是个囫囵人了！”

可他两个同伴的眼睛，都盯在我身上。

===Original===
“Only a king could have done so,” quote the older man. “Young sir, I do take you truly for Charles Stuart. At your touch Diccon was healed of the king’s evil.”

I folded my arms, as if I must keep my hands from doing more strangeness. I had heard, too, of that old legend of the Stuarts, without deeming myself concerned. Yet, here it had befallen. Diccon had suffered from the king’s evil, which learned doctors call scrofula. My touch had driven it from his thin body. He danced and quivered with the joy of health. But his fellows looked at me as though I had betrayed myself by sin.

===Chinese===
“这种事，只有国王做得到，”年长的那个道。“年轻的老爷，我如今当真信你是查理·斯图尔特了。你一伸手，迪肯的国王病（the king’s evil）就好了。”

我抱起双臂，仿佛非这样管住双手不可，免得它们再干出什么怪事来。斯图尔特家的这桩古老传说我也听说过，只是从不觉得同自己相干。可如今，它就在我眼前应验了。迪肯害的是国王病——有学问的医生管它叫瘰疬（scrofula）。我的触摸把这病从他瘦弱的身子里赶了出去。他乐得又蹦又颤，浑身是重获健康的欢喜。可他那两个同伴望着我，倒像我犯下一桩罪过，自己招认了自己。

===Original===
“It is indeed the king,” said the girl, also rising to her feet.

“No,” I made shift to say. “I am but poor Will Jones,” and I wondered where I had let fall my axe. “Will Jones, a woodcutter.”

“Yours to command, Will Jones,” mocked the grizzled man. “My name is Valois Pembru, erst a schoolmaster. My daughter Regan,” and he flourished one of his talons at the wench. “Diccon, our kinsman and servitor, you know already, well enough to heal him. For our profession, we are—are—”

===Chinese===
“果然是国王，”姑娘说着，也站起身来。

“不，”我勉强说出话来。“我不过是穷伐木工威尔·琼斯，”心里正纳闷我把斧头丢在了哪儿。“威尔·琼斯，一个伐木工。”

“但凭吩咐，威尔·琼斯，”灰发男人讥讽道。“在下名叫瓦卢瓦·彭布鲁（Valois Pembru），从前是个教书先生。我女儿里根（Regan），”他朝那姑娘挥了挥一只爪子。“迪肯，我们的亲戚兼仆从，您已经认识了——认得够深，深到能给他治病了。至于我们的行当嘛，我们是——我们是——”

---

===Original===
He seemed to have said too much, and his daughter came to his rescue. “We are liers in wait,” she said.

“True, liers in wait,” repeated Pembru, glad of the words. “Quiet we bide our time, against what good things comes our way. As yourself, Will Jones. Would you sit in sooth upon the throne of England? For that question we brought you hither.”

I did not like his lofty air, like a man cozening puppies. “I came myself, of mine own good will,” I told him. “It rains outside.”

“True,” muttered Diccon, his eyes on me. “All over Spring Coppice falls the rain, and not elsewhere. Not one, but eight charms in yonder book can bring rain—’twas to drive your honor to us, that you might heal—”

===Chinese===
他像是说漏了嘴，他女儿赶来解围。“我们是伺伏的人，”她说。

“对，伺伏的人，”彭布鲁如获至宝地接过话头。“我们安安静静地守株待兔，等好东西自己撞上门来。比如您，威尔·琼斯。您果真愿意坐上英格兰的王座么？为着这个问题，我们才把您请到这儿来。”

我不喜欢他那副高高在上的做派，像个哄弄小狗崽的人。“我是自己来的，出于自愿，”我告诉他，“外头在下雨。”

“不错，”迪肯咕哝道，眼睛望着我。“雨只落在斯普林矮林，别处一滴不下。那本书里能唤雨的符咒不是一道，是八道——就是为了把老爷您驱到我们这儿来，好让您治病——”

===Original===
“Silence,” barked Valois Pembru at him. And to me: “Young sir, we read and prayed and burnt,” and he glanced at the dark-orange flames of the fire. “In that way we guided your footsteps to the Coppice, and the rain then made you see this shelter. ’Twas all planned, even before Noll Cromwell scotched you at Worcester—”

“Worcester!” I roared at him so loudly that he stepped back. “What know you of Worcester fight?”

He recovered, and said in his erst lofty fashion: “Worcester was our doing, too. We gave the victory to Noll Cromwell. At a price—from the book.”

He pointed to the hairy tome in the hands of Regan, his daughter. “The flames showed us your pictured hosts and his, and what befell. You might have stood against him, even prevailed, but for the horsemen who would not right.”

===Chinese===
“住口，”瓦卢瓦·彭布鲁朝他喝道。又转向我：“年轻的老爷，我们又念咒、又祷告、又焚香烧物，”他瞥了一眼火堆里暗橙色的火苗。“就这样，我们引着您的脚步走进矮林，那场雨又教您望见了这座栖身之所。一切都是早就安排下的，早在老诺尔·克伦威尔在伍斯特挫败您之前——”

“伍斯特！”我冲他吼得那么响，逼得他倒退了一步。“伍斯特那一仗，你们知道些什么？”

他定住神，又端起先前那副高高在上的腔调：“伍斯特那一仗，也是我们做下的。胜利是我们送给老诺尔·克伦威尔的。付了代价的——照那本书办的。”

他指了指他女儿里根手里那部生毛的书。“火焰把您和他两边的兵马、还有后来的种种变故，都照成影像放给我们看。您本可以同他抗衡，甚至打赢的，要不是那些不肯交战的骑兵。”

===Original===
I remembered that bitter amazement over how Leslie’s Scots had bode like statues. “You dare say you wrought that?”

Pembru nodded at Mistress Regan, who turned pages. “I will read it without the words of power,” quoth she. “Thus: ‘In meekness I begin my work. Stop rider! Stop footman! Three black flowers bloom, and under them ye must stand still as long as I will, not through me but through the name of—’ ”

She broke off, staring at me with her slant blue eyes. I remembered all the tales of my grandfather James, who had fought and written against witchcraft. “Well, then, you have given the victory to Cromwell. You will give me to him also?”

===Chinese===
我想起莱斯利的苏格兰人怎样如石像般呆立不动，当时那份惊骇又苦又涩。“你们竟敢说，那是你们干的？”

彭布鲁朝里根小姐点点头，她便翻动书页。“我略去那些有法力的字眼，念给您听，”她说。“是这样：‘我谦卑地开始我的功课。止住吧，骑马人！止住吧，步行人！三朵黑花开放了，在花下你们须站立不动，我愿意多久便多久——不凭我，只凭某位尊名——’”

她收住不念了，用那双斜斜的蓝眼睛盯着我。我想起我祖父詹姆斯王的种种旧事，他生前既兴兵讨伐巫术，又著书痛斥巫术。“好啊，这么说，胜利是你们送给克伦威尔的。那你们也要把我送给他么？”

===Original===
Two of the three laughed—Diccon was still too mazed with his new health—and Pembru shook his grizzled head. “Not so, woodcutter. Cromwell asked not the favor from us—’twas one of his men, who paid well. We swore that old Noll should prevail from the moment of battle. But,” and his eyes were like gimlets in mine, “we swore by the oaths set us—the names Cromwell’s men worship, not the names we worship. We will keep the promise as long as we will, and no longer.”

“When it pleases us we make,” contributed Regan. “When it pleases us we break.”

Now ’tis true that Cromwell perished on third September, 1658, seven year to the day from Worcester fight. But I half-believed Pembru even as he spoke, and so would you have done. He seemed to be what he called himself—a lier in wait, a bider for prey, myself or others. The rank smoke of the fire made my head throb, and I was weary of being played with. “Let be,” I said. “I am no mouse to be played with, you gibbed cats. What is your will?”

===Chinese===
三人里有两个笑了——迪肯还沉浸在新得的健康里，晕晕乎乎——彭布鲁摇着灰白的脑袋。“那倒不至于，伐木工。求我们行这个方便的不是克伦威尔——是他手底下的一个人，出手很阔绰。我们起了誓：老诺尔自开战那一刻起便稳操胜券。不过，”他的眼睛像两支手钻，直旋进我的眼底，“我们起的誓，是按人家给我们定下的誓词起的——拜的是克伦威尔一伙崇奉的名号，不是我们自己崇奉的名号。这诺言，我们想守多久便守多久，不想守了，随时可以撒手。”

“我们高兴了就立约，”里根插话道。“高兴了就毁约。”

如今人人都知道，克伦威尔死于 1658 年 9 月 3 日，同伍斯特开战那天整整隔了七年。可彭布鲁说话的当口，我已经信了一半——换了您，也会信的。他看上去确是他自称的那种东西——一个伺伏者，一个守候猎物的，猎物么，或是我，或是旁人。火堆浓浊的烟熏得我脑仁发胀，这种被人戏耍的滋味我也受够了。“住手吧，”我说，“我不是给你们耍着玩的老鼠，你们这群吊死鬼猫儿。你们到底想要什么？”

===Original===
“Ah,” sighed Pembru silkily, as though he had waited for me to ask, “what but that our sovereign should find his fortune again, scatter the Ironsides of the Parliament in another battle and come to his throne at Whitehall?”

“It can be done,” Regan assured me. “Shall I find the words in the book, that when spoken will gather and make resolute your scattered, running friends?”

I put up a hand. “Read nothing. Tell me rather what you would gain thereby, since you seem to be governed by gains alone.”

“Charles Second shall reign,” breathed Pembru. “Wisely and well, with thoughtful distinction. He will thank his good councillor the Earl—no, the Duke—of Pembru. He will be served well by Sir Diccon, his squire of the body.”

===Chinese===
“啊，”彭布鲁油腔滑调地叹道，仿佛就等着我这一问，“还能有什么？无非是让我们至尊的君主时来运转，在另一场大战里把国会的铁甲军杀个星散，然后登上白厅（Whitehall）的御座。”

“这是办得到的，”里根向我保证。“要不要我从书里找出那些字句？一念出口，就能把您那些溃散奔逃的朋党重新聚拢，叫他们同心壮胆？”

我抬起一只手。“什么也别念。倒不如先告诉我，你们从中图什么好处——看这情形，你们行事只认好处二字。”

“查理二世将临朝执政，”彭布鲁低声道。“英明有道，思虑周全，恩威并施。他将感激他贤明的辅弼重臣——彭布鲁伯爵——不，是彭布鲁公爵。迪肯爵士将做他的御前贴身扈从，把他伺候得妥妥帖帖。”

===Original===
“Served well, I swear,” promised Diccon, with no mockery to his words.

“And,” cooed Regan, “are there not ladies of the court? Will it not be said that Lady Regan Pembru is fairest and—most pleasing to the king’s grace?”

Then they were all silent, waiting for me to speak. God pardon me my many sins! But among them has not been silence when words are needed. I laughed fiercely.

“You are three saucy lackeys, ripe to be flogged at the cart’s tail,” I told them. “By tricks you learned of my ill fortune, and seek to fatten thereon.” I turned toward the door. “I sicken in your company, and I leave. Let him hinder me who dare.”

===Chinese===
“伺候得妥妥帖帖，我发誓，”迪肯应承道，话里没有半点讥诮。

“还有呢，”里根柔声说，“宫廷里难道没有女眷么？到时人人岂不都要说，里根·彭布鲁小姐生得最美，而且——最得国王陛下的欢心？”

随后他们一齐沉默，等着我开口。上帝饶恕我这一身的罪过！可我的罪过里，没有一条是该说话时闷不吭声。我放声狂笑。

“你们是三个欠教训的狂奴，正好绑到大车尾巴后头吃鞭子，”我对他们说。“你们靠诡计刺探了我的厄运，又想借它把自己养肥。”我转身朝门口走去。“同你们待在一处我恶心，我走了。有胆子的就来拦我。”

===Original===
“Diccon!” called Pembru, and moved as if to cross my path. Diccon obediently ranged alongside. I stepped up to them.

“If you dread me not as your ruler, dread me as a big man and a strong,” I said. “Step from my way, or I will smash your shallow skulls together.”

Then it was Regan, standing across the door.

“Would the king strike a woman?” she challenged. “Wait for two words to be spoken. Suppose we have the powers we claim?”

“Your talk is empty, without proof,” I replied. “No, mistress, bar me not. I am going.”

“Proof you shall have,” she assured me hastily. “Diccon, stir the fire.”

===Chinese===
“迪肯！”彭布鲁喊道，抬身像要拦住去路。迪肯乖乖地并到他身边。我迎着他们逼上前去。

“你们不敬畏我，就别把我当你们的君王敬畏；那就把我当一条彪形大汉来怕，”我说。“滚开，不然我就把你俩那两颗薄脑壳撞到一块儿去。”

这回拦在门口的，是里根。

“国王难道会动手打女人么？”她挑衅道。“且等两句话说完。倘若我们真有自称的那些法力呢？”

“空口无凭，你们的话都是白说，”我答道。“不行，小姐，别拦我。我走了。”

“凭证自然会有的，”她急忙向我保证。“迪肯，把火拨旺。”

---

===Original===
He did so. Watching, I saw that in sooth he was but a lad—his disease, now banished by my touch, had put a false seeming of age upon him. Flames leaped up, and upon them Pembru cast a handful of herbs whose sort I did not know. The color of the fire changed as I gazed, white, then rosy red, then blue, then again white. The wench Regan was babbling words from the hair-bound book; but, though I had learned most tongues in my youth, I could not guess what language she read.

“Ah, now,” said Pembru. “Look, your gracious majesty. Have you wondered of your beaten followers?”

In the deep of the fire, like a picture that forebore burning and moved with life, I saw tiny figures—horsemen in a huddled knot riding in dejected wise. Though it was as if they rode at a distance, I fancied that I recognized young Straike—a cornet of Leslie’s. I scowled, and the vision vanished.

===Chinese===
他照办了。看着他拨火，我才看清他其实不过是个少年——那场被我触摸驱走的病，给他脸上罩了一层虚假的老相。火焰蹿起来，彭布鲁朝火里撒了一把香草，什么品种我认不得。火色在我眼前变来变去，白，玫瑰红，蓝，又转回白。里根姑娘对着那本生皮裹绑的书念念有词；可我年轻时虽学过好多种语言，也猜不出她念的是哪一国话。

“啊，来了，”彭布鲁说。“看啊，尊敬的陛下。您可曾挂念您那些吃了败仗的追随者？”

在火的深处，像一幅烧不着、还会活动的画，我看见一些小小的人影——一小簇缩作一团的骑兵，垂头丧气地赶路。他们仿佛远在天边，我却觉得认出了小斯特雷克（Straike）——莱斯利麾下的一名旗手。我一皱眉，幻象便消失了。

===Original===
“You have prepared puppets, or a shadow-show,” I accused. “I am no country hodge to be tricked thus.”

“Ask of the fire what it will mirror to you,” bade Pembru, and I looked on him with disdain.

“What of Noll Cromwell?” I demanded, and on the trice he was there. I had seen the fellow once, years agone. He looked more gray and bloated and fierce now, but it was he—Cromwell, the king rebel, in back and breast of steel with buff sleeves. He stood with wide-planted feet and a hand on his sword. I took it that he was on a porch or platform, about to speak to a throng dimly seen.

“You knew that I would call for Cromwell,” I charged Pembru, and the second image, too, winked out.

===Chinese===
“你们预先备好了傀儡，不然就是一场皮影戏，”我指斥道。“我又不是乡巴佬，这么好糊弄。”

“要火照什么给您看，您只管开口，”彭布鲁吩咐道；我轻蔑地瞪了他一眼。

“那就照照老诺尔·克伦威尔，”我点了名。转瞬之间，他就在那儿了。多年前我见过此人一面。如今他看上去更灰败、更虚胖、更凶悍了，可正是他——克伦威尔，那个窃国的叛逆，钢铁的背甲胸甲，水牛皮的袖子。他叉开两腿站着，一手按剑。我估摸他是站在一处门廊或高台上，正要对一群影影绰绰的民众训话。

“你们早料到我会点克伦威尔，”我质问彭布鲁；第二幅影像也随之熄灭了。

===Original===
He smiled, as if my stubbornness was what he loved best on earth. “Who else, then? Name one I cannot have prepared for.”

“Wilmot,” I said, and quick anon I saw him. Poor nobleman! He was not young enough to tramp the byways in masquerade, like me. He rode a horse, and that a sorry one, with his pale face cast down. He mourned, perhaps for me. I felt like smiling at this image of my friend, and like weeping, too.

“Others? Your gentlemen?” suggested Pembru, and without my naming they sprang into view one after another, each in a breath’s space. Their faces flashed among the shreds of flame—Buckingham, elegant and furtive; Lauderdale, drinking from a leather cup; Colonel Carlis, whom we called “Careless,” though he was never that; the brothers Penderel, by a fireside with an old dame who may have been their mother; suddenly, as a finish to the show, Cromwell again, seen near with a bible in his hand.

The fire died, like a blown candle. The room was dim and gray, with a whisp of smoke across the hide-spread window.

===Chinese===
他微微一笑，仿佛我这股倔劲是他在世上最心爱的东西。“那么，还点谁？点一个我没法预先备下的。”

“威尔莫特，”我说，顷刻之间便看见了他。可怜的贵族！他到底不如我年轻，经不住化了装、徒步奔走小路。他骑着一匹马——也是匹可怜巴巴的马——苍白着脸，垂着头。他在哀恸，也许是哀恸我。望着朋友的这幅影像，我想笑，也想哭。

“还有谁？您的那些绅士？”彭布鲁提议道。不待我点名，他们便一个接一个跳了出来，一个个只在须臾之间。那些面孔在碎焰里闪现——白金汉（Buckingham），风雅而鬼祟；劳德代尔，正捧着皮杯饮酒；卡利斯上校（Colonel Carlis），我们都唤他“没心事”，其实他从不曾没心事；潘德雷尔兄弟，围在一处炉边，陪着一位老妇人，许是他们的母亲；陡然地，作为这场戏的收梢，又是克伦威尔，近在眼前，手里捧着一册《圣经》。

火熄了，像一支被吹灭的蜡烛。屋子灰暗下来，一缕烟横过蒙着生皮的窗。

===Original===
“Well, sire? You believe?” said Pembru. He smiled now, and I saw teeth as lean and white as a hunting dog’s.

“Faith, only a fool would refuse to believe,” I said in all honesty.

He stepped near. “Then you accept us?” he questioned hoarsely. On my other hand tiptoed the fair lass Regan.

“Charles!” she whispered. “Charles, my comely king!” and pushed herself close against me, like a cat seeking caresses.

===Chinese===
“怎么样，陛下？您信了么？”彭布鲁说。他这会儿笑了，我看见一口瘦长的白牙，像猎狗的牙。

“说实话，只有傻子才不肯信，”我说的是真心话。

他凑近一步。“那么，您肯接纳我们了？”他哑声问道。我另一侧，美丽的里根姑娘踮着脚尖凑了过来。

“查理！”她悄声唤道。“查理，我俊美的国王！”整个人贴上来，像一只讨人抚弄的猫。

===Original===
“Your choice is wise,” Pembroke said on. “Spells bemused and scattered your army—spells will bring it back afresh. You shall triumph, and salt England with the bones of the rebels. Noll Cromwell shall swing from a gallows, that all like rogues may take warning. And you, brought by our powers to your proper throne—”

“Hold,” I said, and they looked upon me silently.

“I said only that I believe in your sorcery,” I told them, “but I will have none of it.”

You would have thought those words plain and round enough. But my three neighbors in that ill house stared mutely, as if I spoke strangely and foolishly. Finally: “Oh, brave and gay! Let me perish else!” quoth Pembru, and laughed.

===Chinese===
“您这抉择是明智的，”彭布鲁接着说下去。“咒语迷乱了您的军队、打散了您的军队——咒语也会把它重新聚拢如初。您将得胜，将用叛贼的白骨给英格兰撒上盐。老诺尔·克伦威尔将吊上绞架荡个来回，好叫天下同类的歹徒引以为戒。而您呢，借我们的法力，将被扶上本该属于您的御座——”

“慢着，”我说；他们三人默然望着我。

“我只说了我信你们的妖术，”我告诉他们，“可我一丝一毫也不要沾它。”

这两句话，您总当够明白、够直截了当的了。可那凶宅里我的三位邻居只是干瞪眼，一言不发，仿佛我的话说得又古怪又愚蠢。半晌，彭布鲁才道：“嚯，好胆量，好兴致！不然叫我不得好死！”说着大笑起来。

===Original===
My temper went, and with it my bemusement. “Perish you shall, dog, for your saucy ways,” I promised. “What, you stare and grin? Am I your sovereign lord, or am I a penny show? I have humored you too long. Goodbye.”

I made a step to leave, and Pembru slid across my path. His daughter Regan was opening the book and reciting hurriedly, but I minded her not a penny. Instead, I smote Pembru with my fist, hard and fair in the middle of his mocking face. And down he went, full-sprawl, rosy blood fountaining over mouth and chin.

“Cross me again,” quoth I, “and I’ll drive you into your native dirt like a tether-peg.” With that, I stepped across his body where it quivered like a wounded snake, and put forth my hand to open the door.

There was no door. Not anywhere in the room.

===Chinese===
我的火气冲了上来，连带着那股晕乎劲儿也散了。“不得好死的会是你，狗东西，就为你这副放肆样，”我回敬他。“怎么，还瞪着眼龇着牙笑？我是你们的君王主宰，还是一场一个铜板的把戏？我迁就你们迁就得太久了。再见。”

我举步要走，彭布鲁滑身又挡到路中。他女儿里根翻开书匆匆念诵起来，我却半个子儿也没把她放在心上。我一拳砸将过去，端端正正，正中彭布鲁那张讥诮脸孔的正中间。他直挺挺地摊身扑倒，鲜红的血从嘴巴和下巴上泉涌而出。

“再敢拦我，”我说，“我就把你像根拴马桩似的，钉进你老家的土里去。”说罢，我从他身上跨过去——那身子还像条受伤的蛇似的抽动——伸手去开门。

门没有了。整间屋子，哪儿都没有门。

===Original===
I turned back, the while Regan finished reading and closed the book upon her slim finger.

“You see, Charles Stuart,” she smiled, “you must bide here in despite of yourself.”

“Sir, sir,” pleaded Diccon, half-crouching like a cricket, “will you not mend your opinion of us?”

“I will mend naught,” I said, “save the lack of a door.” And I gave the wall a kick that shook the stout wattlings and brought down flakes of clay. My blistered foot quivered with pain, but another kick made some of the poles spring from their fastenings. In a moment I would open a way outward, would go forth.

===Chinese===
我回过身；里根正好念完，一根纤细的手指还夹在合上的书页间。

“看见了吧，查理·斯图尔特，”她微笑道，“您只好违背着自己的心意，在这儿待下去了。”

“老爷，老爷，”迪肯半蹲着，像只蟋蟀似的哀求，“您就不能改变对我们的看法么？”

“我什么也不改，”我说，“只改一改这屋里缺门的毛病。”说着一脚踹在墙上，踹得坚实的枝条骨架直晃，震下片片泥皮。我那只满是水泡的脚疼得直颤，可再一脚，就有几根木杆从绑扎处迸脱出来。再过片刻，我就能在墙上开出一条道，闯出去了。

---

===Original===
Regan shouted new words from the book. I remember a few, like uncouth names—Sator, Arepos, Janna. I have heard since that these are powerful matters with the Gnostics. In the midst of her outcry, I thought smoke drifted before me—smoke that stank like dead flesh, and thickened into globes and curves, as if it would make a form. Two long streamers of it drifted out like snakes, to touch or seize me, I gave back, and Regan stood at my side.

“Would you choose those arms,” asked she, “and not these?” She held out her own, fair and round and white. “Charles, I charmed away the door. I charmed that spirit to hold you. I will still do you good in despite of your will—you shall reign in England, and I—and I—”

Weariness was drowning me. I felt like a child, drowsy and drooping. “And you?” I said.

“You shall tell me,” she whispered. “Charles.”

===Chinese===
里根又对着书高声念起新的字句。我记得其中几个，像些古怪的名号——萨托（Sator）、阿雷波斯（Arepos）、詹纳（Janna）。后来我听说，这些在诺斯替派（Gnostics）里都是厉害的东西。她喊念到一半，我觉得有烟在我眼前浮动——一股死尸般恶臭的烟，渐渐凝成球状和弧形，像要拼出一个形体。两股长长的烟缕像蛇一样游出来，要碰我、抓我。我退了一步，里根已站到我身边。

“您宁可要那样的臂膀，”她问，“也不要这样的么？”她伸出自己的双臂，丰润白皙。“查理，门是我用符咒收去的。缠住您的那个精灵，也是我符咒召来的。哪怕违背您的心意，我还是要给您好处——您将在英格兰登基为王，而我——而我——”

倦意像水一样漫过我的头顶。我像个孩子似的，昏昏沉沉，直往下坠。“那你呢？”我说。

“您自己说给我听，”她耳语道。“查理。”

===Original===
She shimmered in my sight, and bells sang as if to signal her victory. I swear it was not I who spoke then stupidly—cunsult Jack Wilmot’s doggerel to see if I am wont to be stupid. But the voice came from my mouth: “I shall be king in Whitehall.”

She prompted me softly: “I shall be duchess, and next friend—”

“Duchess and next friend,” I repeated.

“Of the king’s self!” she finished, and I opened my mouth to say that, too. Valois Pembru, recovering from my buffet, sat up and listened.

===Chinese===
她在我眼前微微发亮，铃铛叮咚作响，像在为她的胜利报信。我发誓，接下来那句蠢话并不是我说的——不信去查杰克·威尔莫特的打油诗，看我平日是不是个蠢人。可那声音偏偏从我嘴里出来：“我将在白厅登基为王。”

她轻轻提词：“我将做公爵夫人，做头号近臣——”

“公爵夫人，头号近臣，”我跟着念。

“——国王陛下跟前的！”她补完了这一句；我张开口，也要跟着把这句说出来。瓦卢瓦·彭布鲁从我那一拳底下缓过劲来，坐起身听着。

===Original===
But—

“**Stop!**” roared Diccon.

===Chinese===
可是——

“**住口！**”迪肯大吼一声。

---

===Original===
We all looked—Regan and I and Valois Pembru. Diccon rose from where he crouched. In his slim, strong hands was the foul hairy book that Regan had laid aside. His finger marked a place on the open page.

“The spells are mine, and I undo what they have wrought!” he thundered in his great new voice. “Stop and silence! Look upon me, ye sorcerers and arch-sorcerers! You who attack Charles Stuart, let that witchcraft recede from him into your marrow and bone, in this instant and hour—”

He read more, but I could not hear for the horrid cries of Pembru and his daughter.

The rawhide at the window split, like a drumhead made too hot. And cold air rushed in. The fire that had vanished leaped up, its flames bright red and natural now. Its flames scaled the roof-peak, caught there. Smoke, rank and foul, crammed the place. Through it rang more screams, and I heard Regan, pantingly:

===Chinese===
我们全都望过去——我、里根，还有瓦卢瓦·彭布鲁。迪肯从他蹲伏的地方站起身。他那双瘦而有力的手里，捧着里根撂下的那本肮脏的生毛的书。手指点在摊开的书页上。

“那些咒语是我的，我来解除它们做成的一切！”他用大病初愈后的洪亮新嗓门雷鸣般喝道。“止住，静默！看着我，你们这些巫师、大巫师！你们向查理·斯图尔特下的手，就让那巫术从他身上退回来，钻进你们的骨髓里去，就在此时此刻——”

他还往下念，可彭布鲁父女的惨叫太骇人，我什么也听不见了。

窗上的生皮绷裂了，像一面烤得过热的鼓皮。冷风灌了进来。方才熄掉的火重新蹿起，火舌如今是明亮的、正常的红色。火舌攀上屋脊，在那里烧牢了。浓浊恶臭的烟挤满了整间屋子。烟里惨叫连连，我听见里根上气不接下气地喊：

===Original===
“Hands—from—my—throat—!”

Whatever had seized her, it was not Diccon, for he was at my side, hand on my sleeve.

“Come, sire! This way!”

Whither the door had gone, thither it now came back. We found it open before us, scrambled through and into the open.

===Chinese===
“手——放开——我的——喉咙——！”

无论掐住她的是什么，反正不是迪肯——迪肯就在我身边，手扯着我的衣袖。

“快，陛下！这边！”

门先前消失到了哪里，如今便从哪里回来。只见它就在我们面前敞开着，我们手脚并用爬出去，到了露天底下。

---

===Original===
The hut burnt behind us like a hayrick, and I heard no more cries therefrom. “Pembru!” I cried. “Regan! Are they slain?”

“Slain or no, it does not signify,” replied Diccon. “Their ill magic retorted upon them. They are gone with it from earth—forever.” He hurled the hairy book into the midst of the flame. “Now, away.”

We left the clearing, and walked the lane. There was no more rainfall, no more mist. Warm light came through the leaves as through clear green water.

“Sire,” said Diccon, “I part from you. God bless your kind and gracious majesty! Bring you safe to your own place, and your people to their proper senses.”

===Chinese===
小屋在我们身后烧得像一座干草垛，里面再没有传出任何喊声。“彭布鲁！”我喊道。“里根！他们死了么？”

“死没死，都无关紧要了，”迪肯答道。“他们的邪法反噬了他们自己。他们连人带法，从这世上消失了——永远。”他把那本生毛的书掷进火焰中央。“走吧。”

我们离开空地，走上小路。雨不下了，雾也没有了。暖融融的光穿过树叶洒下来，像穿过澄澈的绿水。

“陛下，”迪肯说，“我就在此与您分手了。愿上帝保佑仁慈宽厚的陛下！愿他领您平安归位，也让您的人民回过神来，恢复清明。”

===Original===
He caught my hand and kissed it, and would have knelt. But I held him on his feet.

“Diccon,” I said, “I took you for one of those liers in wait. But you have been my friend this day, and I stand in your debt as long as I live.”

“No, sire, no. Your touch drove from me the pain of the king’s evil, which had smitten me since childhood, and which those God-forgotten could not heal with all their charms. And, too, you refused witch-help against Cromwell.”

===Chinese===
他捧起我的手吻了吻，还要下跪。我把他扶住，没让他跪下去。

“迪肯，”我说，“我先前把你错当成了伺伏者一流的人物。可这一天里，你做了我的朋友，只要我活着一日，就欠你一分情。”

“不，陛下，不。您一触摸，就驱走了折磨我自幼到今的国王病的苦痛——那病，那两个被上帝忘掉的人使尽浑身符咒也治不好。还有，您也不肯借巫术之力去对付克伦威尔。”

===Original===
I met his round, true eye. “Sooth to say, Cromwell and I make war on each other,” I replied, “but—”

“But ’tis human war,” he said for me. “Each in his way hates hell. ’Twas bravely done, sire. Remember that Cromwell’s course is run in seven years. Be content until then. Now—Godspeed!”

He turned suddenly and made off amid the leafage. I walked on alone, toward where the brothers Penderel would rejoin me with news of where next we would seek safety.

===Chinese===
我迎着他那双圆而真诚的眼睛。“实话说，克伦威尔同我是在互相开战，”我答道，“不过——”

“——不过是人间的战争，”他替我说了下去。“各人用自己的法子憎恨地狱。这一手干得漂亮，陛下。记住，克伦威尔的气数七年便尽。在那之前，安心等待。此刻——愿上帝与您同行！”

他蓦地转身，钻进枝叶间去了。我独自前行，朝潘德雷尔兄弟将要同我会合的地方走去——他们会带来消息，说我们下一处该往哪里觅安身之地。

---

===Original===
Many things churned in my silly head, things that have not sorted themselves in all the years since; but this came to the top of the churn like fair butter.

The war in England was sad and sorry and bloody, as all wars. Each party called the other Godforsaken, devilish. Each was wrong. We were but human folk, doing what we thought well, and doing it ill. Worse than any human foe was sorcery and appeal to the devil’s host.

I promise myself then, and have not since departed from it, that when I ruled, no honest religion would be driven out. All and any such, I said in my heart, was so good that it bettered the worship of evil. Beyond that, I wished only for peace and security, and the chance to take off my blistering shoes.

===Chinese===
许多事情在我这颗蠢脑袋里翻腾，此后这么多年也没理出个头绪；可有一样东西翻到了最上头，像搅乳桶里澄出来的好黄油。

英格兰这场战争同一切战争一样，悲惨、凄惶、血腥。两边都骂对方遭上帝弃绝、是魔鬼一党。两边都骂错了。我们不过是凡人，做自己认为对的事，却做得一团糟。比任何人间的仇敌都更坏的，是妖术，是向魔鬼军团的祈求。

我在那时对自己许下诺言，此后从未背离：有朝一日临朝执政，任何正派的信仰都不容驱逐。我在心里说，这一切信仰、任何一种信仰，都好——好到足以胜过敬拜邪祟。除此以外，我所愿的唯有太平与安稳，还有脱下这双磨脚的鞋的机会。

===Original===
“Lord,” I prayed, “if thou art pleased to restore me to the throne of my ancestors, grant me a heart constant in the exercise and protection of true worship. Never may I seek the oppression of those who, out of tenderness of their consciences, are not free to conform to outward and indifferent ceremonies.”

And now judge between me and Jack Wilmot, Earl of Rochester. There is at least one promise I have kept, and at least one wise deed I have done. Put that on my grave.

===Chinese===
“主啊，”我祈祷道，“你若愿将我重新扶上祖先的宝座，求你赐我一颗恒定的心，去力行并护卫真正的敬拜。对于那些出于良心的柔软、未能苟从于无关紧要的外在仪文的人，愿我永不寻求压迫他们。”

现在，请在我和罗切斯特伯爵（Earl of Rochester）杰克·威尔莫特之间做个裁断吧。至少有一个诺言，我守住了；至少有一件聪明事，我做了。把这句刻到我的墓碑上去。

===Original===
(From the *Smith City Mirror*, June 26, 1927)

Police are searching today for Dr. J. E. Lawlor, well-known physician and scientist, following a report from his secretary, James Brock, that he had disappeared from his home at 2100 Van Ness Avenue.

According to Brock, Dr. Lawlor locked himself into his private laboratory twelve days ago, ordering his servants not to disturb him, and to send food down by means of a dumbwaiter. As he had followed this plan several times before while working on experiments, Brock complied with his request. The time set was ten days and when there had been no response from the laboratory during the two days following the elapse of this period, Brock feared some accident and, with the help of Georges Dmitri, Dr. Lawlor’s cook, and Emil Bonner, his chauffeur, he forced the door this morning and found that the doctor was gone.

===Chinese===
（载《史密斯城镜报》（*Smith City Mirror*），1927 年 6 月 26 日）

警方今日正在搜寻知名医生、科学家 J. E. 劳洛尔（Dr. J. E. Lawlor）的下落——据其秘书詹姆斯·布罗克（James Brock）报告，劳洛尔医生已从他位于范尼斯大道（Van Ness Avenue）2100 号的住所中失踪。

据布罗克所述，劳洛尔医生十二天前把自己锁进了私人实验室，吩咐仆人们不得打扰，饭食一律用食物升降机送下去。由于他从前埋头做实验时也曾几度照此办理，布罗克便依言照办。约定的期限是十天；期满之后接连两天，实验室里毫无动静，布罗克生怕出了意外，今晨在劳洛尔医生的厨师乔治·德米特里（Georges Dmitri）和司机埃米尔·邦纳（Emil Bonner）的协助下撬开了房门，发现医生已不见踪影。

===Original===
A weird angle is added to the incident by the dead body of a large ape which Brock found in a corner of the disordered laboratory. Although Dr. Lawlor was known to be interested in natural history and to have conducted several experiments with animals recently, Brock stated that he was sure the ape was not in the laboratory when it was closed twelve days ago. The table was covered with papers, which have been turned over to the police. Brock, Dmitri and Bonner are held for questioning by Chief of Police John Walton.

Dr. Lawlor has no immediate family. A brother, Stanley Lawlor, of Topeka, Kansas, has been notified.

===Chinese===
布罗克在凌乱不堪的实验室一角发现了一头大猿的尸体，给这桩事件平添了一层诡异的色彩。尽管众所周知，劳洛尔医生素来爱好博物学，近来也做过几回动物实验，但布罗克声称，十二天前实验室闭门之时，他确信里面并没有这头猿。桌上摊满了纸张文件，已悉数移交警方。布罗克、德米特里和邦纳三人现被警察局长约翰·沃尔顿（John Walton）扣留问话。

劳洛尔医生并无直系亲属在世。他有一位兄弟斯坦利·劳洛尔（Stanley Lawlor），住在堪萨斯州托皮卡（Topeka, Kansas），已获通知。

## The Song of the Slaves / 奴隶之歌

===Original===
Gender paused at the top of the bald rise, mopped his streaming red forehead beneath the wide hat-brim, and gazed backward at his forty-nine captives. Naked and black, they shuffled upward from the narrow, ancient slave trail through the jungle. Forty-nine men, seized by Gender’s own hand and collared to a single long chain, destined for his own plantation across the sea. … Gender grinned in his lean, drooping mustache, a mirthless grin of greedy triumph.

For years he had dreamed and planned for this adventure, as other men dream and plan for European tours, holy pilgrimages, or returns to beloved birthplaces. He had told himself that it was intensely practical and profitable. Slaves passed through so many hands—the raider, the caravaner, the seashore factor, the slaver captain, the dealer in New Orleans or Havana or at home in Charleston. Each greedy hand clutched a rich profit, and all profits must come eventually from the price paid by the planter. But he, Gender, had come to Africa himself, in his own ship; with a dozen staunch ruffians from Benguela he had penetrated the Bihé-Bailundu country, had sacked a village and taken these forty-nine upstanding natives between dark and dawn. A single neck-shackle on his long chain remained empty, and he might fill even that before he came to his ship. By the Lord, he was making money this way, fairly coining it—and money was worth the making, to a Charleston planter in 1853.

So he reasoned, and so he actually believed, but the real joy to him was hidden in the darkest nook of his heart. He had conceived the raider-plan because of a nature that fed on savagery and mastery. A man less fierce and cruel might have been satisfied with hunting lions or elephants, but Gender must hunt men. As a matter of fact, the money made or saved by the journey would be little, if it was anything. The satisfaction would be tremendous. He would broaden his thick chest each day as he gazed out over his lands and saw there his slaves hoeing seashore cotton or pruning indigo; his forty-nine slaves, caught and shipped and trained by his own big, hard hands, more indicative of assured conquest than all the horned or fanged heads that ever passed through the shops of all the taxidermists.

===Chinese===
根德在一道光秃秃的山坡顶上停住脚，在宽宽的帽檐底下抹了一把汗涔涔的红脑门，回头打量他那四十九名俘虏。他们赤裸着乌黑的身子，正沿着丛林里那条狭窄的古奴隶小道，拖着脚步一路往上蹭。四十九个男人，全是根德亲手掳来的，脖颈上套着项圈，锁在同一条长长的锁链上，注定要发往海那一边他自己的种植园。……根德那两撇耷拉着的瘦髭须里绽开一丝笑——那是贪婪得逞的笑，没有半点欢意。

多少年来，他一直为这趟冒险又梦想又盘算，就像别的男人梦想盘算欧洲游历、圣地朝拜，或是回心爱的故乡走一遭。他对自己说，这营生实惠得很，油水也足。奴隶要经过多少道手——掠奴的、押商队的、海边代理的、贩奴船的船长，还有新奥尔良、哈瓦那，或者本乡本土查尔斯顿（Charleston）的贩子。每一只贪婪的手都要攥走一份厚利，而所有这些利，到头来都得出自种植园主付出的价钱。可他根德，亲自跑了一趟非洲，坐的是自己的船；带着十二个从本格拉（Benguela）招募的结实的恶棍，一路打进比耶-拜伦杜（Bihé-Bailundu）地方，荡平了一座村子，在天黑到天亮之间抓来了这四十九条好身板的原住民。他那条长链上还空着一只颈枷，回到船上之前，兴许还能把它也填满。老天在上，他这法子简直是在铸钱——而对一个 1853 年的查尔斯顿种植园主来说，钱是值得这么挣的。

他这样盘算，也当真信这一套，可他真正的快活，藏在心底最黑的旮旯里。他生出这份掠奴的计谋，是因为他这天性，就靠凶残和役使他人滋养。换个凶横差些的男人，打打狮子猎猎大象也就满足了，可根德非要猎人来不可。说实在的，这趟买卖挣的省的钱，即便真有，也只是蝇头小利；那份满足感才叫惊天动地。往后每天，他眺望着自家的田产，看见奴隶们在那儿锄海岛棉、修剪靛青，胸膛就会挺得愈发宽厚——他那四十九个奴隶，是他亲手捕获、亲手装船、亲手调教出来的，比历来挂满各家标本师铺子的所有带角带牙的兽头，都更能昭示一场稳稳到手的征服。

===Original===
Something hummed in his ears, like a rhythmic swarm of bees. Men were murmuring a song under their breath. It was the long string of pinch-faced slaves. Gender stared at them, and mouthed one of the curses he always kept at tongue’s end.

“Silva!” he called.

The lanky Portuguese who strode free at the head of the file turned aside and stood before Gender. “*Patrao?*” he inquired respectfully, smiling teeth gleaming in his walnut face.

“What are those men singing?” demanded Gender. “I didn’t think they had anything to sing about.”

“A slave song, *Patrao*.” Silva’s tapering hand, with the silver bracelet at its wrist, made a graceful gesture of dismissal. “It is nothing. One of the things that natives make up and sing as they go.”

===Chinese===
耳畔有什么东西嗡嗡作响，像一窝合着节拍振翅的蜜蜂。是有人在屏着气低哼一支歌。正是那一长串面容枯槁的奴隶。根德瞪着他们，嘴唇无声地翕动，骂出一句他随时挂在舌尖上的咒骂。

“西尔瓦（Silva）！”他喊道。

那个大步流星走在队伍最前头、身上不带锁链的瘦高葡萄牙人侧身出列，站到根德面前。“*Patrao*？（葡语「老板」）”他恭恭敬敬地问，一笑之间，牙齿在核桃色的脸上闪闪发亮。

“那些人在唱什么？”根德质问，“我倒没想到，他们还有什么可唱的。”

“一支奴隶歌，*Patrao*。”西尔瓦抬起那双修长的手——腕上戴着银镯——做了个轻盈的、不以为意的手势。“没什么要紧的。不过是土人赶路时自己编了唱唱的东西。”

---

===Original===
Gender struck his boot with his coiled whip of hippopotamus hide. The afternoon sun, sliding down toward the shaggy jungle-tops, kindled harsh pale lights in his narrow blue eyes. “How does the song go?” he persisted.

The two fell into step beside the caravan as, urged by a dozen red-capped drivers, it shambled along the trail. “It is only a slave song, *patrao*,” said Silva once again. “It means something like this: ‘Though you carry me away in chains, I am free when I die. Back will I come to bewitch and kill you.’”

Gender’s heavy body seemed to swell, and his eyes grew narrower and paler. “So they sing that, hmm?” He swore again. “Listen to that!”

The unhappy procession had taken up a brief, staccato refrain:

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”

===Chinese===
根德用盘起的河马皮鞭抽了抽靴筒。午后的太阳正向着毛茸茸的丛林顶上滑落，在他狭窄的蓝眼睛里点起两道白硬的光。“那歌是怎么唱的？”他追问不休。

两人跟在驼队旁边并步而行——那支队伍被十二个戴红帽的赶奴工驱赶着，顺着小道踉踉跄跄地挪。“不过是支奴隶歌罢了，*Patrao*，”西尔瓦又说了一遍，“意思差不多是这样：‘你虽用锁链把我带走，我一死便得了自由。我还要回来，蛊死你，杀掉你。’”

根德那沉重的身躯似乎涨了起来，眼睛眯得更细、颜色更淡了。“他们唱的就是这个，嗯？”他又咒骂了一句。“你听听！”

那支苦兮兮的队伍已经唱起了一段短促的叠句：

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”（海洛瓦——根达！海帕纳——根达！）

===Original===
“Genda, that’s my name,” snarled the planter. “They’re singing about me, aren’t they?”

Silva made another fluid gesture, but Gender flourished his whip under the nose of the Portuguese. “Don’t you try to shrug me off. I’m not a child, to be talked around like this. What are they singing about me?”

“Nothing of consequence, *patrao*,” Silva made haste to reassure him. “It might be to say: ‘I will bewitch Gender, I will kill Gender.’”

“They threaten me, do they?” Gender’s broad face took on a deeper flush. He ran at the line of chained black men. With all the strength of his arm he slashed and swung with the whip. The song broke up into wretched howls of pain.

“I’ll give you a music lesson!” he raged, and flogged his way up and down the procession until he swayed and dripped sweat with the exertion.

===Chinese===
“根达，那是我的名字，”种植园主咆哮道。“他们这是在唱我，对不对？”

西尔瓦又做了个圆滑的手势，根德却把鞭子一抖，甩到那葡萄牙人的鼻子底下。“少给我打马虎眼。我不是小孩子，由不得你这么哄来哄去。他们唱我什么？”

“没什么了不得的，*Patrao*，”西尔瓦赶紧安抚他。“大概是说：‘我要蛊死根德，我要杀掉根德。’”

“他们敢威胁我，是吗？”根德那张宽脸涨得更红了。他朝锁成一串的黑人队伍冲过去，抡圆了胳膊，一鞭一鞭狠抽猛挥。歌声散了，碎成一片凄惨的痛嚎。

“我给你们上一堂音乐课！”他怒气冲冲地吼着，顺着队伍从头抽到尾、又从尾抽到头，直到自己都晃晃悠悠、挥汗如雨。

===Original===
But as he turned away, it struck up again:

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”

Whirling back, he resumed the rain of blows. Silva, rushing up to second him, also whipped the slaves and execrated them in their own tongue. But when both were tired, the flayed captives began to sing once more, softly but stubbornly, the same chant.

“Let them whine,” panted Gender at last. “A song never killed anybody.”

Silva grinned nervously. “Of course not, *patrao*. That is only an idiotic native belief.”

===Chinese===
可他刚一转身，那歌又响了起来：

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”

他猛地旋回身，又是一顿鞭雨。西尔瓦赶上来帮腔，也抽打奴隶，还用土话咒骂他们。可等两人都打乏了，那些皮开肉绽的俘虏又唱了起来，还是那支歌，声音轻轻的，却倔得很。

“随他们哼哼去，”根德终于喘着气说。“歌又没杀过人。”

西尔瓦赔出一个不安的笑。“当然杀不死，*Patrao*。那不过是土人的蠢迷信。”

===Original===
“You mean, they think that a song will kill?”

“That, and more. They say that if they sing together, think together of one hate, all their thoughts and hates will become a solid strength—will strike and punish for them.”

“Nonsense!” exploded Gender.

But when they made camp that night, Gender slept only in troubled snatches, and his dreams were of a song that grew deeper, heavier, until it became visible as a dark, dense cloud that overwhelmed him.

===Chinese===
“你是说，他们当真以为一支歌能杀人？”

“这个，还不止。他们说，只要齐声唱、齐心恨同一桩恨，所有的念头和仇恨就会凝成一股结结实实的力气——替他们去打击，去讨还。”

“一派胡言！”根德炸了。

可是当晚扎营之后，根德只断断续续睡了几觉，觉觉不安稳；梦里总有那支歌，越唱越深沉，越压越沉重，末了竟化作一团又黑又密的乌云，把他整个人吞没了。

---

===Original===
The ship that Gender had engaged for the expedition lay in a swampy estuary, far from any coastal town, and the dawn by which he loaded his goods aboard was strangely fiery and forbidding. Dunlapp, the old slaver-captain that commanded for him, met him in the cabin.

“All ready, sir?” he asked Gender. “We can sail with the tide. Plenty of room in the hold for that handful you brought. I’ll tell the men to strike off those irons.”

“On the contrary,” said Gender, “tell the men to put manacles on the hands of each slave.”

Dunlapp gazed in astonishment at his employer. “But that’s bad for blacks, Mr. Gender. They get sick in chains, won’t eat their food. Sometimes they die.”

“I pay you well, Captain,” Gender rumbled, “but not to advise me. Listen to those heathen.”

===Chinese===
根德为这趟远征雇的那艘船，泊在一片沼泽化的河口湾里，离哪座沿海城镇都远远的；他往船上装货的那天拂晓，天色火红得邪性，一副拒人的凶相。替他管船的老贩奴船长邓拉普（Dunlapp），在舱房里迎住他。

“都齐备了，先生？”他问根德。“趁涨潮就能开船。您带来的那一小撮人，货舱里宽敞得很。我这就吩咐伙计们把那些铁家伙卸了。”

“恰恰相反，”根德说，“吩咐伙计们，给每个奴隶的手都戴上镣铐。”

邓拉普愕然望着自家雇主。“可那样对黑奴不好，根德先生。戴着链子他们会闹病，不肯吃东西。有时候会死。”

“我给你的工钱不薄，船长，”根德瓮声瓮气地说，“可不是雇你来给我出主意的。听听那些异教徒。”

===Original===
Dunlapp listened. A moan of music wafted in to them.

“They’ve sung that cursed song about me all the way to the coast,” Gender told him. “They know I hate it—I’ve whipped them day after day—but they keep it up. No chains come off until they hush their noise.”

Dunlapp bowed acquiescence and walked out to give orders. Later, as they put out to sea, he rejoined Gender on the after deck.

“They do seem stubborn about their singing,” he observed.

“I’ve heard it said,” Gender replied, “that they sing together because they think many voices and hearts give power to hate, or to other feelings.” He scowled. “Pagan fantasy!”

===Chinese===
邓拉普侧耳听。一缕呜咽似的乐声飘进舱来。

“一路上到海边，他们唱的就是那支咒我的歌，”根德对他说。“他们知道我恨它——我一天接一天地抽他们——可他们偏不停嘴。他们不闭嘴，铁链就不卸。”

邓拉普躬身应承，出去传令。稍后，船出了海，他又到船尾甲板上来寻根德。

“他们唱起来倒真是犟，”他说。

“我听人讲过，”根德答道，“他们齐声唱，是因为他们以为，许多嗓音、许多颗心，能给仇恨、或者别的什么情感添力气。”他眉头一皱。“异教徒的胡思乱想！”

===Original===
Dunlapp stared overside, at white gulls just above the wave-tips. “There may be a tithe of truth in that belief, Mr. Gender; sometimes there is in the faith of wild people. Hark ye, I’ve seen a good fifteen hundred Mohammedans praying at once, in the Barbary countries. When they bowed down, the touch of all those heads to the ground banged like the fall of a heavy rock. And when they straightened, the motion of their garments made a swish like the gust of a gale. I couldn’t help but think that their prayer had force.”

“More heathen foolishness,” snapped Gender, and his lips drew tight.

“Well, in Christian lands we have examples, sir,” Dunlapp pursued. “For instance, a mob will grow angry and burn or hang someone. Would a single man do that? Would any single man of the mob do it? No, but together their hate and resolution becomes—”

“Not the same thing at all,” ruled Gender harshly. “Suppose we change the subject.”

===Chinese===
邓拉普朝舷外望去，望着浪尖上方的一群白鸥。“这说法里兴许也有一星半点的真东西，根德先生；野蛮人的信仰，有时还真有。您听好了，我在巴巴里诸国，亲眼见过足足一千五百个穆斯林一齐祷告。他们俯身下拜的时候，那么多颗脑袋磕在地上，咚的一声，像一块巨石砸落；他们直起身来，衣衫翻卷，飒飒作响，像刮过一阵狂风。我不由得想，他们那祷告，是有力道的。”

“又是异教蠢话，”根德厉声说，嘴唇抿得紧紧的。

“呃，在基督教地界，咱们也有现成的例子，先生，”邓拉普接着说。“比如说，一伙暴民会越聚越怒，烧死人，吊死人。单个人会干这个吗？暴民里随便挑一个出来单干，他会干吗？不会。可凑到一块儿，他们的仇恨和狠劲就变成了——”

“根本不是一回事，”根德疾言厉色地一锤定音。“咱们换个话题吧。”

===Original===
On the following afternoon, a white sail crept above the horizon behind them. At the masthead gleamed a little blotch of color. Captain Dunlapp squinted through a telescope, and barked a sailorly oath.

“A British ship-of-war,” he announced, “and coming after us.”

“Well?” said Gender.

“Don’t you understand, sir? England is sworn to stamp out the slave trade. If they catch us with this cargo, it’ll be the end of us.” A little later, he groaned apprehensively, “They’re overtaking us. There’s their signal, for us to lay to and wait for them. Shall we do it, sir?”

===Chinese===
第二天下午，一片白帆从他们身后的地平线上悄悄爬了上来。桅顶闪着一小块颜色。邓拉普船长眯眼从望远镜里一瞧，骂了一句水手的老粗话。

“一艘英国军舰，”他宣布道，“正咬着咱们追过来。”

“那又怎样？”根德说。

“您还不明白吗，先生？英国人立了誓要剿绝贩奴这行当。要是让他们连这船货截住，咱们就算到头了。”过了一小会儿，他提心吊胆地哼哼起来，“他们追上来了。那是他们打的信号，叫咱们停船候着。照办不照办，先生？”

===Original===
Gender shook his head violently. “Not we! Show them our heels. Captain.”

“They’ll catch us. They are sailing three feet to our two.”

“Not before dark,” said Gender. “When dark comes, we’ll contrive to lessen our embarrassment.”

And so the slaver fled, with the Britisher in pursuit. Within an hour, the sun was at the horizon, and Gender smiled grimly in his mustache.

“It’ll be dark within minutes,” he said to Dunlapp. “As soon as you feel they can’t make out our actions by glass, get those slaves on deck.”

===Chinese===
根德狠狠摇头。“咱们偏不停！让他们追咱们的脚后跟去吧，船长。”

“他们会追上的。人家走三尺，咱们才走两尺。”

“天黑之前追不上，”根德说。“等天一黑，咱们自有法子，把这份尴尬减一减。”

于是贩奴船一路逃，英国船一路追。不到一个钟头，太阳已落到天边，根德髭须底下露出一丝狞笑。

“不出几分钟天就黑透了，”他对邓拉普说。“你一估摸出他们隔着望远镜已瞧不清咱们的动静，就把那些奴隶统统押上甲板。”

===Original===
In the dusk the forty-nine naked prisoners stood in a line along the bulwark. For all their chained necks and wrists, they neither stood nor gazed in a servile manner. One of them began to sing and the others joined, in the song of the slave trail:

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”

“Sing on,” Gender snapped briefly, and moved to the end of the line that was near the bow. Here dangled the one empty collar, and he seized it in his hand. Bending over the bulwark, he clamped it shut upon something—the ring of a heavy spare anchor, that swung there upon a swivel-hook. Again he turned, and eyed the line of dark singers.

“Have a bath to cool your spirits,” he jeered, and spun the handle of the swivel-hook.

The anchor fell. The nearest slave jerked over with it, and the next and the next. Others saw, screamed, and tried to brace themselves against doom; but their comrades that had already gone overside were too much weight for them. Quickly, one after another, the captives whipped from the deck and splashed into the sea. Gender leaned over and watched the last of them as he sank.

===Chinese===
暮色里，四十九个赤条条的俘虏沿着舷墙站成一排。虽说脖子上、手腕上都拴着链子，他们站着不见奴态，望着也不带媚色。一个开了腔，其余的跟着唱起来，正是奴隶小道上的那支歌：

“*Hailowa—Genda! Haipana—Genda!*”

“接着唱，”根德简短地喝了一声，朝队伍靠近船头的那一头走去。那只空着的颈枷就在那儿晃荡，他一把抓在手里。他探身过舷墙，把枷口钳合在一个东西上——一只沉重备用锚的锚环，那锚就挂在那儿的旋转吊钩上晃着。他又转过身，打量那排黑黢黢的歌者。

“洗个澡，败败你们的火气，”他讥嘲道，随即扳动了旋转吊钩的把手。

铁锚坠了下去。最近的那个奴隶被它猛地拽了下去，接着一个，又一个。旁人看见了，尖叫起来，想撑住身子抵挡厄运；可已经翻出船外的同伴分量太沉，他们撑不住。眨眼间，俘虏一个接一个被拽离甲板，扑通扑通栽进海里。根德俯下身，眼看着最后一个人沉下去。

===Original===
“Gad, sir!” exclaimed Dunlapp hoarsely.

Gender faced him almost threateningly.

“What else to do, hmm? You yourself said that we could hope for no mercy from the British.”

===Chinese===
“天哪，先生！”邓拉普嘶声道。

根德朝他转过身，几乎是一脸威胁。

“不这样又怎么办，嗯？你自己说的，英国人不会饶咱们。”

---

===Original===
The night passed by, and by the first gray light the British ship was revealed almost upon them. A megaphoned voice hailed them; then a shot hurtled across their bows. At Gender’s smug nod, Dunlapp ordered his men to lay to. A boat put out from the pursuer, and shortly a British officer and four marines swung themselves aboard.

Bowing in mock reverence. Gender bade the party search. They did so, and remounted the deck crestfallen.

“Now, sir,” Gender addressed the officer, “don’t you think that you owe me an apology?”

The Englishman turned pale. He was a lean, sharp-featured man with strong, white teeth. “I can’t pay what I owe you,” he said with deadly softness. “I find no slaves, but I smell them. They were aboard this vessel within the past twelve hours.”

===Chinese===
一夜过去，借着最初的灰色天光，那艘英国船显出了身影，几乎已逼到跟前。扩音喇叭里的嗓音朝他们喊话；接着一发炮弹呼啸着掠过船头。根德沾沾自得地点了点头，邓拉普便吩咐手下停船。追兵放下一艘小艇，不多时，一名英国军官带着四个陆战队员翻上了甲板。

根德装模作样地躬身行礼，请这一行人搜船。他们搜了，又一副垂头丧气的样子回到甲板上。

“好了，先生，”根德对那军官说，“您不觉得欠我一个道歉吗？”

英国人脸色发白。他是个瘦削、轮廓锋利的男人，一口结实的好白牙。“我欠您的，怕是还不清，”他说，语气温软，却透着杀气。“我没找到奴隶，可我闻得出。过去十二个钟头里，他们在这条船上待过。”

===Original===
“And where are they now?” teased Gender.

“We both know where they are,” was the reply. “If I could prove in a court of law what I know in my heart, you would sail back to England with me. Most of the way you would hang from my yards by your thumbs.”

“You wear out your welcome, sir,” Gender told him.

“I am going. But I have provided myself with your name and that of your home city. From here I go to Madeira, where I will cross a packet bound west for Savannah. That packet will bear with it a letter to a friend of mine in Charleston, and your neighbors shall hear what happened on this ship of yours.”

“You will stun slave-owners with a story of slaves?” inquired Gender, with what he considered silky good-humor.

“It is one thing to put men to work in cotton fields, another to tear them from their homes, crowd them chained aboard a stinking ship, and drown them to escape merited punishment.” The officer spat on the deck. “Good day, butcher. I say, all Charleston shall hear of you.”

===Chinese===
“那他们如今在哪儿呢？”根德戏弄道。

“你我心知肚明，”对方答道。“要是我能把心里明白的事在法庭上坐实，你就得随我乘船回英国去了。而且这一路上大半程，你都会吊在我的桅桁上，吊着大拇指。”

“你招人嫌了，先生，”根德对他说。

“我这就走。不过我已经记下了你的名字和你家乡的名字。我从这儿去马德拉（Madeira），在那儿搭一艘西去萨凡纳（Savannah）的邮船。那船会捎上一封我写给我查尔斯顿朋友的信，你的乡邻们会听说你在这条船上干的好事。”

“讲一段奴隶的事，就能把奴隶主们吓破胆？”根德问道，自以为口气俏皮和气。

“让人下棉花地干活是一回事，把他们从家园里生生扯走、戴着锁链塞上一条臭气熏天的船、为了逃脱罪有应得的惩办再把他们淹死，那是另一回事。”军官朝甲板上啐了一口。“再见，屠夫。听好了，整个查尔斯顿都会听说你的事。”

---

===Original===
Gender’s plantation occupied a great, bluff-rimmed island at the mouth of a river, looking out toward the Atlantic. Ordinarily that island would be called beautiful, even by those most exacting followers of Chateaubriand and Rousseau; but, on his first night at home again. Gender hated the fields, the house, the environs of fresh and salt water.

His home, on a seaward jut, resounded to his grumbled curses as he called for supper and ate heavily but without relish. Once he vowed, in a voice that quivered with rage, never to go to Charleston again.

At that, he would do well to stay away for a time. The British officer had been as good as his promise, and all the town had heard of Gender’s journey to Africa and what he had done there. With a perverse squeamishness beyond Gender’s understanding, the hearers were filled with disgust instead of admiration. Captain Hogue had refused to drink with him at the Jefferson House. His oldest friend, Mr. Lloyd Davis of Davis Township, had crossed the street to avoid meeting him. Even the Reverend Doctor Lockin had turned coldly away as he passed, and it was said that a sermon was forthcoming at Doctor Lockin’s church attacking despoilers and abductors of defenseless people.

What was the matter with everybody? savagely demanded Gender of himself; these men who snubbed and avoided him were slaveholders. Some of them, it was quite possible, even held slaves fresh from raided villages under the Equator. Unfair! … Yet he could not but feel the animosity of many hearts, chafing and weighing upon his spirit.

===Chinese===
根德的种植园占着一座大河河口的大岛，四围尽是断崖，面朝大西洋。寻常日子里，这座岛是谁都要赞一声美的，就连夏多布里昂（Chateaubriand）和卢梭（Rousseau）最挑剔的追随者也挑不出话来；可是重新到家的头一夜，根德却恨透了这些田地、这座宅子，恨透这片淡咸水交错的周边。

他的宅子坐落在一处探向海面的凸崖上；他叫晚饭、吃晚饭的当口，屋里只回荡着他咕咕哝哝的咒骂。他吃得很多，却全无滋味。有一刻他发誓——嗓音气得直打颤——再也不进查尔斯顿城了。

论理，他还真不如避一阵子风头。那英国军官说到做到，全城都已听说了根德的非洲之行，和他在那里干的勾当。听众们非但不喝彩，反倒满心厌恶——这份别扭的恶心劲儿，实在超出根德的理解。霍格船长（Captain Hogue）在杰斐逊旅馆（Jefferson House）拒绝与他共饮；他最老的朋友、戴维斯镇区（Davis Township）的劳埃德·戴维斯先生（Lloyd Davis），隔着马路绕道走，不肯与他打照面；就连洛金牧师（Reverend Doctor Lockin）与他擦肩而过时，也冷冷地扭过头去。据说洛金牧师的教堂里还要布一场道，痛斥那些蹂躏、掳掠手无寸铁之人的恶徒。

这都是怎么了？根德恶狠狠地自问；这些冷落他、躲着他的人，个个都是奴隶主。他们当中有些人，很可能手里就使唤着刚从赤道底下被荡平的村子里掳来的新奴隶。太不公道！……可他分明感觉得到，许多颗心的怨毒正磨着他、压着他，压在他心头上。

===Original===
“Brutus,” he addressed the slave that cleared the table, “do you believe that hate can take form?”

“Hate, Marsa?” The sooty face was solemnly respectful.

“Yes. Hate, of many people together.” Gender knew he should not confide too much in a slave, and chose his words carefully. “Suppose a lot of people hated the same thing, maybe they sang a song about it—”

“Oh, yes, Marsa,” Brutus nodded. “I heah ’bout dat, from ole gran-pappy when I was little. He bin in Affiky, he says many times day sing somebody to deff.”

===Chinese===
“布鲁图斯（Brutus），”他唤那个收拾桌子的奴隶，“你信不信，仇恨能成个形儿？”

“恨，玛萨（Marsa，黑人口语里的「老爷」）？”那张乌黑的脸上是端端正正的恭顺。

“对。恨，许许多多人凑在一处、拧成一股的恨。”根德知道对奴隶不可交心太多，字句拣着说。“比方说，好多人恨同一样东西，兴许还编一支歌唱唱它——”

“哦，信咧，玛萨，”布鲁图斯直点头。“俺听说过这宗事，俺小时候听俺老爷爷讲的。他去过阿弗里卡（非洲）咧，他讲过好多回，那边的人能把活人唱死。”

===Original===
“Sing somebody to death?” repeated Gender. “How?”

“Dey sing dat dey kill him. Afta while, maybe plenty days, he die—”

“Shut up, you black rascal!” Gender sprang from his chair and clutched at a bottle. “You’ve heard about this somewhere, and you dare to taunt me!”

===Chinese===
“把人唱死？”根德又问了一遍。“怎么个唱法？”

“他们唱，唱着要把他弄死。过些日子，兴许好多天，他就死咧——”

“闭嘴，你这黑奴才！”根德从椅子上蹦起来，一把抓住个酒瓶。“你这是打哪儿听来的风声，竟敢来刺挠我！”

===Original===
Brutus darted from the room, mortally frightened. Gender almost pursued, but thought better and tramped into his parlor. The big, brown-paneled room seemed to give back a heavier echo of his feet. The windows were filled with the early darkness, and a hanging lamp threw rays into the corners.

On the center table lay some mail, a folded newspaper and a letter. Gender poured whisky from a decanter, stirred in spring water, and dropped into a chair. First he opened the letter.

“Stirling Manor,” said the return address at the top of the page. Gender’s heart twitched. Evelyn Stirling, he had hopes of her … but this was written in a masculine hand, strong and hasty.

===Chinese===
布鲁图斯魂都吓飞了，一溜烟窜出屋去。根德差点追出去，又想想不值得，蹬蹬蹬走进了客厅。这间宽大的褐木板壁屋子，回他脚步的声音似乎格外沉。窗户里塞满了早降的黑夜，一盏吊灯把光投进屋角。

当中的桌上搁着几件邮件，一份折起的报纸和一封信。根德从细颈瓶里倒出威士忌，兑上泉水，一屁股坐进椅子。他先拆了那封信。

“斯特林庄园（Stirling Manor），”信纸顶端的回信地址写着。根德的心抽了一下。伊芙琳·斯特林（Evelyn Stirling）——他对她原本存着指望……可这信是男人的笔迹，遒劲而潦草。

===Original===
> “Sir:
